# 📚 Pico 2500: Documentation (Projects 1401-1500)

## Batch 141: Advanced Radar Screen 4 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1401: Advanced Radar Screen Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Echo Optimizer"
At the end of this project, you will be able to optimize the scanning efficiency of a radar system by tuning the mechanical wait-states between the Servo movement and the Ultrasonic sensor ping to find the minimum delay where the sensor still returns valid echoes without signal interference.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Ultrasonic Physics**: Sound waves travel at approximately 343 meters per second. A pulse must travel to an object and back before the next one is sent; otherwise, "Phantom Echoes" occur where the sensor hears the previous pulse's reflection.
*   **Mechanical Latency**: Servo motors require physical time to transition between angles. If a sensor pings while the servo is still vibrating or moving, the "beam" is smeared across multiple degrees, reducing accuracy.
*   **Wait-State Optimization**: Engineering a balance between "Speed" (fast sweeps) and "Sample Integrity" (allowing components to settle) is a critical skill in robotics.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **SG90 Servo Motor**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**
*   **3D Printed or Cardboard Sensor Mount** (to hold sensor on servo)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo (Signal)** | GP15 | PWM Control |
| **HC-SR04 (Trig)** | GP16 | Digital Out |
| **HC-SR04 (Echo)** | GP17 | Digital In |
| **Servo/Sensor VCC**| 5V (VBUS) | High power source |
| **GND** | GND | Common ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag `servo_write` (set angle).
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `sonar_read` (measure distance).
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`.
*   From **Loops**, drag `repeat_forever` and `count_with`.

### 7. Variables
*   **scan_angle**: Number (Stores current degree from 0-180).
*   **step_delay**: Number (Tuning parameter for speed in milliseconds).
*   **distance**: Number (Stores the result of the ultrasonic ping).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Mechanical Setup**
1.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms` to the top of the loop.
2.  Set **step_delay** to `50` initially. This is our "Safe Mode" speed.

**Phase B: The Scanning Sweep**
3.  From **Loops**, snap a `count_with` block into the `repeat_forever`. Set range from `0` to `180`.
4.  From **Motion & Motors**, snap `servo_write` inside the loop. Set pin to `GP15` and value to the `scan_angle` variable.
5.  From **Wait**, snap `pico_wait_ms` and set it to the `step_delay` variable. This allows the servo to move and vibration to stop.

**Phase C: Data Acquisition**
6.  From **Variables**, create `distance`. 
7.  From **Smart Sensors**, snap `sonar_read` and assign it to `distance`. Set Trig to `GP16` and Echo to `GP17`.
8.  From **Text**, snap a `print` block to show the results in the terminal.

**Phase D: Tuning**
9.  Run the code. Slowly reduce **step_delay** by 5 in each iteration.
10. Observe the data. When the readings become erratic (sensing the floor or missing walls), you have found the "Stability Limit."

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The system initializes the Servo at 0 degrees.
2.  **Process**: The loop increments the angle by 1 degree.
3.  **Output**: The servo moves, waits exactly `step_delay` ms, and then fires a sound pulse.
4.  **Process**: The sensor calculates distance based on the return time.
5.  **Repeat**: The process repeats until 180 degrees are covered, then restarts or reverses.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
from servo import Servo # Hypothetical standard library
from sonar import Sonar # Hypothetical standard library

# Phase A: Hardware Init
scanner_servo = Servo(15)
radar_sensor = Sonar(trigger=16, echo=17)
step_delay = 20 # Optimized target value

# Phase B: Main Scanning Loop
while True:
    # Forward Sweep
    for scan_angle in range(0, 181, 1):
        # Phase C: Mechanical Settle
        scanner_servo.write_angle(scan_angle)
        utime.sleep_ms(step_delay)
        
        # Phase D: Data Capture
        distance = radar_sensor.distance_cm()
        print(f"Angle: {scan_angle} | Dist: {distance} cm")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Too Fast**: Setting `step_delay` < 15ms. The servo is a physical motor; it cannot move instantly. It will lag behind the software command, leading to "Skewed" radar mapping.
*   **Power Sag**: Using the 3.3V pin for the servo. High-speed movement draws current spikes that can crash the Pico. Always use VBUS (5V).
*   **Vibration**: If the sensor isn't mounted tightly, the movement vibration will trigger false echoes.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Precision Sweep**: Can you modify the code to use **Logic** and **Variables** to only scan between 45 and 135 degrees but with 0.5-degree steps for higher detail?

## 1. Project 1402: Advanced Radar Screen Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Polar Plot V2"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an advanced radar-style display on an OLED by drawing concentric distance circles (range rings) and plotting detection data relative to those rings using polar coordinates.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Polar Coordinate System**: Unlike Cartesian (X,Y) grids, Polar coordinates identify points using an **Angle** (θ) and a **Radius** (r). This is the natural coordinate system for anything that rotates, like a radar dish.
*   **UI Overlay**: In professional displays, "Static" elements (the grid/rings) are often drawn once, while "Dynamic" elements (the targets) are updated every frame.
*   **Coordinate Mapping**: Converting a 200cm physical distance to a 64-pixel screen radius requires a scaling factor (e.g., `ScreenRadius = PhysicalDist / 3`).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **0.96" SSD1306 OLED Display (I2C)**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data |
| **OLED SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock |
| **HC-SR04 Trig** | GP16 | Trigger |
| **HC-SR04 Echo** | GP17 | Echo |
| **VCC/GND** | 3V3 / GND | Power |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `draw_circle`, `draw_pixel`, and `show_display`.
*   From **Math**, drag `sin`, `cos`, and `multiply`.
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `sonar_read`.

### 7. Variables
*   **radar_angle**: Number (The current scanning angle).
*   **object_dist**: Number (Distance measured by the sensor).
*   **target_x / target_y**: Number (Calculated pixel coordinates on the screen).
*   **scale_factor**: Number (Ratio to fit 200cm into 32 pixels).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Drawing the Range Rings**
1.  From **Smart Display**, drag `clear_display`.
2.  From **Loops**, use a `count_with` block to draw three circles. Set radii to `10`, `20`, and `30` pixels. Use center point `(64, 63)` (bottom-center of the screen).
3.  This establishes our "Range Rings" (e.g., 50cm, 100cm, 150cm).

**Phase B: Data Mapping**
4.  From **Variables**, set `scale_factor` to `0.2` (32 pixels / 160 cm).
5.  Get `object_dist` from **Smart Sensors**.
6.  Calculate screen radius: `r = object_dist * scale_factor`.

**Phase C: Polar to Cartesian Conversion**
7.  To draw on the screen, we must convert `(r, angle)` to `(x, y)`:
    *   `target_x = 64 + (r * cos(angle))`
    *   `target_y = 63 - (r * sin(angle))`
8.  From **Smart Display**, snap `draw_pixel` at `target_x` and `target_y`.

**Phase D: Updating**
9.  Call `show_display` to push the grid and the new target to the screen.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The OLED initializes and clears.
2.  **Process**: The radar grid (concentric circles) is drawn into the buffer.
3.  **Input**: The sensor reads a distance (e.g., 80cm) at a specific angle (e.g., 45°).
4.  **Math**: The code converts 80cm ➔ 16 pixels, then converts (16, 45°) ➔ (75, 52).
5.  **Output**: A single dot appears on the OLED halfway between the first and second rings.
6.  **Repeat**: The screen updates as the radar sweeps.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import math
import ssd1306

# HW Setup
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = ssd1306.SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
scale = 0.2

def update_radar(angle_deg, distance_cm):
    # Phase A: Static Grid
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.circle(64, 63, 10, 1) # Ring 1
    oled.circle(64, 63, 20, 1) # Ring 2
    oled.circle(64, 63, 30, 1) # Ring 3
    
    # Phase B: Math Conversion
    angle_rad = math.radians(angle_deg)
    r = distance_cm * scale
    
    # Phase C: Plotting
    x = int(64 + r * math.cos(angle_rad))
    y = int(63 - r * math.sin(angle_rad))
    
    if r < 32: # Stay on screen
        oled.pixel(x, y, 1)
    
    oled.show()

# Example Call
update_radar(45, 100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Rad vs Deg**: Using degrees directly in `sin()` or `cos()`. MicroPython math functions require **Radians** (`Rad = Deg * π / 180`).
*   **Coordinate Origin**: Forgetting that OLED (0,0) is the top-left. To draw a radar "sweep" that goes up, you must **subtract** the vertical component from the bottom coordinate (63).
*   **Flicker**: Clearing the screen inside a high-speed loop. Draw the grid first, then show the buffer.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Text Annotations**: Use the `text()` block to label the Rings as "0.5m", "1.0m", and "1.5m" so the user knows exactly how far the targets are.

## 1. Project 1403: Advanced Radar Screen Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Dynamic Range"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an Automatic Gain Control (AGC) simulation by dynamically adjusting detection thresholds based on object distance to filter out signal clutter and prevent sensor saturation.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Automatic Gain Control (AGC)**: In real radar systems, signals from far-away objects are very weak due to the "Inverse Square Law" (signal strength drops rapidly over distance). AGC boosts weak signals and damps strong ones to maintain a consistent output.
*   **Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR)**: By increasing sensitivity for distant objects, we also risk increasing "Noise" (false readings). Dynamic thresholding helps maintain a high SNR by only being sensitive when necessary.
*   **Logical Normalization**: Creating a mathematical relationship between distance and sensitivity (e.g., `Threshold = 1 / Distance`) allows the system to behave intelligently without manual tuning for every scenario.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **Buzzer or LED** (to indicate valid detection)
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **HC-SR04 Trig** | GP16 | Out |
| **HC-SR04 Echo** | GP17 | In |
| **Buzzer (Positive)**| GP18 | PWM/Digital |
| **VCC/GND** | 3V3 / GND | Power |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else` and comparison blocks (`<`, `>`).
*   From **Math**, drag `division` (to calculate dynamic threshold).
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `sonar_read`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output` (for the LED/Buzzer).

### 7. Variables
*   **raw_dist**: Number (The actual distance reading).
*   **min_sensitivity**: Number (The constant noise floor).
*   **dynamic_threshold**: Number (The calculated limit).
*   **is_target_locked**: Boolean (Result of the logic).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Defining the Noise Floor**
1.  From **Variables**, set `min_sensitivity` to `10`. This prevents the radar from triggering on tiny dust particles or air currents.

**Phase B: Dynamic Calculation**
2.  From **Smart Sensors**, read the `raw_dist`.
3.  From **Logic**, create an `if_else` block.
4.  **Condition**: `If raw_dist > 100`:
    *   Set `dynamic_threshold` to `5`. (Be very sensitive for far objects).
5.  **Else**:
    *   Set `dynamic_threshold` to `20`. (Be less sensitive for close objects to avoid floor reflections).

**Phase C: Validation Logic**
6.  Compare the sensor signal (simulated as the stability of the reading) against the `dynamic_threshold`.
7.  If the signal passes the threshold, set `is_target_locked` to **True**.

**Phase D: Feedback**
8.  From **Smart IO**, turn on the buzzer at `GP18` only if `is_target_locked` is True.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The radar pings the environment.
2.  **Process**: The system checks the distance.
3.  **Process**: If the object is far (e.g., 150cm), the threshold drops to 5. Even a faint echo is accepted.
4.  **Process**: If the object is near (e.g., 20cm), the threshold rises to 20. Small wobbles are ignored to ensure the "Target" is real.
5.  **Output**: The buzzer beeps only when a "Valid" target is confirmed by the logic.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine

# Hardware Init
sensor = Sonar(16, 17)
indicator = machine.Pin(18, machine.Pin.OUT)

noise_floor = 10

while True:
    raw_dist = sensor.distance_cm()
    
    # Phase B: Dynamic Threshold Logic
    if raw_dist > 100:
        # High sensitivity for long range
        dynamic_threshold = 5
    else:
        # Low sensitivity for short range (Anti-Clutter)
        dynamic_threshold = 25
        
    # Phase C & D: Validation
    # (In this simulation, we check if the reading is stable/meaningful)
    if raw_dist > 0 and raw_dist < 400: # Valid range
        if raw_dist > dynamic_threshold: # Met our logic
            indicator.value(1)
        else:
            indicator.value(0)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Threshold Inversion**: Logic error where high distance results in a High threshold. Remember: For far objects, you need a *lower* bar for the signal to pass (higher sensitivity).
*   **Zero Readings**: Ultrasonic sensors often return `0` if they miss an echo. Ensure your logic ignores `0` before applying thresholds.
*   **Clutter**: Real floors bounce sound. If your sensor is low to the ground, your threshold must be high enough to ignore the floor but low enough to catch a leg.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Time Varying Gain (TVG)**: Create a variable that increases linearly with time after the ping is sent. This is the gold standard for Sonar and Radar signal processing.

## 1. Project 1404: Advanced Radar Screen Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Sweep Line"
At the end of this project, you will be able to animate a rotating "Sweep Line" on an OLED display to simulate a real-time radar scanline, including logic to erase previous scanlines to create a smooth, noise-free animation.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Refresh Strategy**: To create smooth animation, you don't always clear the whole screen (`fill(0)`). Instead, you can "Selective Erase" by drawing the previous object in the background color (Black) before drawing it in its new position in the foreground color (White).
*   **Scanline Rendering**: This project mimics the "Wiper" on a classic radar scope. The line represents the direction the sensor is currently pointing.
*   **Animation Persistence**: Using variables to store the "Previous Position" is the fundamental logic behind all computer graphics and movement.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **0.96" SSD1306 OLED Display (I2C)**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data |
| **OLED SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock |
| **VCC** | 3V3 | Power |
| **GND** | GND | Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `draw_line` and `show_display`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`.
*   From **Math**, drag `sine`, `cosine`, and `to_radians`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_to`.

### 7. Variables
*   **sweep_angle**: Number (0-180 degrees).
*   **line_end_x / line_end_y**: Number (The screen coordinate of the line tip).
*   **prev_x / prev_y**: Number (Stores the old tip to erase it).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Initializing the Center**
1.  Establish the "Radar Hub" at the bottom center of the screen: `(64, 63)`.
2.  Set **sweep_angle** to `0`.

**Phase B: The Erase-Draw Cycle**
3.  Inside a `repeat_forever` loop:
4.  **Erase**: From **Smart Display**, snap `draw_line`. Set color to `Black` (0). Use coordinates `(64, 63)` to `(prev_x, prev_y)`.
5.  **Calculate**: Using **Math**, calculate the new tip:
    *   `line_end_x = 64 + (60 * cos(sweep_angle))`
    *   `line_end_y = 63 - (60 * sin(sweep_angle))`
6.  **Draw**: From **Smart Display**, snap `draw_line`. Set color to `White` (1). Use coordinates `(64, 63)` to `(line_end_x, line_end_y)`.
7.  **Store**: Set `prev_x = line_end_x` and `prev_y = line_end_y`.

**Phase C: Animation Control**
8.  From **Math**, change **sweep_angle** by `2`.
9.  If `sweep_angle > 180`, set it back to `0`.
10. Call `show_display`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The screen is black.
2.  **Process**: The code calculates where the radar "beam" should point.
3.  **Process**: It erases where the beam *used* to be.
4.  **Output**: It draws a new white line in the updated direction.
5.  **Process**: The angle increases, and the loop repeats.
6.  **Repeat**: To the user, it looks like a single green (or white) line is sweeping across the screen like a real radar.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import math
import ssd1306
import utime

# Init OLED
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = ssd1306.SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)

angle = 0
px, py = 64, 63 # Initial prev pos

while True:
    # Phase B: Erase Old Line
    oled.line(64, 63, int(px), int(py), 0)
    
    # Calculate New Line
    rad = math.radians(angle)
    cx = 64 + 60 * math.cos(rad)
    cy = 63 - 60 * math.sin(rad)
    
    # Phase B: Draw New Line
    oled.line(64, 63, int(cx), int(cy), 1)
    
    # Phase C: Update State
    oled.show()
    px, py = cx, cy
    angle += 2
    if angle > 180: angle = 0
    
    utime.sleep_ms(20) # Control Scan Speed
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Flash" Effect**: Calling `fill(0)` every loop. This makes the screen flicker too much and erases all other radar targets. Use the "Dual Drawing" (Black then White) technique instead.
*   **Trig Errors**: Forgetting that `cos()` and `sin()` take radians. If you give it `180`, it won't point left—it will use it as 180 radians! Always convert using `math.radians()`.
*   **Negative Y**: Screen Y-axis goes **down**. To make the line sweep "up," you must subtract the sine result from the Y-coordinate.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Phosphor Fade**: Instead of erasing the line immediately, can you keep the last 5 positions on screen but with decreasing thickness or brightness to simulate the "Glow" of an old CRT radar?

## 1. Project 1405: Automated Advanced Radar Screen Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Sentry Sentry"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an intelligent state-based radar controller that performs automated patrols and switches to a "Target Lock" state when an intruder is detected within a threat sector, holding that position for investigation.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Interest-Based Scanning**: Instead of blindly rotating, an "Intelligent" radar identifies points of interest (POI). If a reading changes significantly at Angle X, that angle becomes a priority for the system.
*   **Simple State Machine**: A "State" is a condition the robot is currently in. This project uses two states:
    1.  **Patrol**: Moving left and right looking for targets.
    2.  **Lock-On**: Staying at a fixed angle because a target was found.
*   **Hysteresis in Robotics**: To prevent the servo from "jittering" between states, we add a delay or a threshold so that once a target is locked, the system doesn't immediately resume patrol if the target disappears for a split second.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **SG90 Servo Motor**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **Active Buzzer** (Threat Alarm)
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo Signal** | GP15 | PWM Control |
| **Sonar Trig** | GP16 | Out |
| **Sonar Echo** | GP17 | In |
| **Buzzer** | GP18 | Digital Out |
| **Power/GND** | VBUS/GND | Common supply |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else` and comparison blocks.
*   From **Loops**, drag `count_with` and `while_true`.
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag `servo_write`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`.

### 7. Variables
*   **is_threat**: Boolean (True if object < 50cm).
*   **current_angle**: Number (0-180).
*   **stare_timer**: Number (Seconds to stay locked).
*   **detected_dist**: Number (Current distance reading).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Normal Patrol**
1.  Snap a `while_true` block. Inside, create a `count_with` loop from `0` to `180` (stepping by 5 for faster patrol).
2.  Set `current_angle` to the loop variable.
3.  Move the servo to `current_angle`.

**Phase B: Detection Logic**
4.  Inside the sweep loop, read the distance from the sensor.
5.  Check: `If distance < 50 and distance > 0`:
    *   Set `is_threat` to **True**.
6.  If `is_threat` is True, snap a `break` block to stop the patrol loop immediately.

**Phase C: Target Lock-On**
7.  After the sweep loop ends (via break), the code will "Fall through" to the Lock-On logic.
8.  From **Smart IO**, turn the buzzer `ON`.
9.  From **Wait**, snap `pico_wait` and set it to `5` seconds. This is the investigation phase.
10. Turn the buzzer `OFF`.
11. Reset `is_threat` to **False** so the patrol can restart in the next main loop iteration.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The radar begins its left-right patrol.
2.  **Process**: It constantly checks the sonar distance at every 5-degree step.
3.  **Process**: An object appears at 45 degrees.
4.  **Interaction**: The patrol loop "Breaks." The servo stops exactly at 45 degrees.
5.  **Output**: The buzzer sounds, and the system "Stares" at the intruder for 5 seconds.
6.  **Repeat**: After 5 seconds, the system resumes patrolling, looking for new threats.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware Init
servo = Servo(15)
sonar = Sonar(16, 17)
alarm = machine.Pin(18, machine.Pin.OUT)

while True:
    # State 1: Patrol Mode
    target_lock = False
    for angle in range(0, 181, 5):
        servo.write(angle)
        utime.sleep_ms(30)
        
        dist = sonar.read()
        if 0 < dist < 50:
            target_lock = True
            lock_angle = angle
            break # Exit patrol loop
            
    # State 2: Lock-On Mode
    if target_lock:
        print(f"Intruder Detected at {lock_angle} deg!")
        alarm.value(1)
        utime.sleep(5) # Stare for 5 seconds
        alarm.value(0)
        # Loop restarts, returning to Patrol
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Hanging" Loop**: Forgeting to set a `sleep_ms` inside the patrol. Without a small delay, the software will try to read the sensor faster than the servo can actually move to the new angle.
*   **Infinite Lock**: Not resetting the `target_lock` variable or using a `while` instead of an `if`. The robot will get stuck staring at air if the logic isn't clean.
*   **Zero Distance**: Most HC-SR04 sensors return `0` for an error. If you check for `dist < 50` without checking `dist > 0`, the sentry will "lock on" to errors constantly.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Tracking Sentry**: Instead of just staring for 5 seconds and resetting, can you add a small "Sub-Scan" logic? After finding a target at 45 deg, scan 40-50 deg to see if it moved, and update the servo to follow it!

## 1. Project 1406: Advanced Radar Screen Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Proximity Field"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a multi-zone safety trigger system using an RGB LED to provide visual status alerts based on the proximity of detected objects to the radar sensor, utilizing nested logic for "Traffic Light" style safety feedback.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Zone Warning (Safety Buffer)**: In industrial automation, robots use "Safety Envelopes." If a human enters a distant zone, the robot slows down. If they enter the near zone, the robot stops.
*   **Traffic Light Logic**: Uses Red (Danger), Yellow (Warning), and Green (Safe) to communicate complex internal states to a user instantly via visual cues.
*   **RGB Color Mixing**: Digital pins can create compound colors. By turning on both the Red and Green leads of an RGB LED, you create **Yellow**, essential for the warning phase.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Common Cathode RGB LED**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **220Ω Resistors** (3x, to protect the LED leads)
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **RGB Red (Anode)** | GP18 | Pin 24 |
| **RGB Green (Anode)**| GP19 | Pin 25 |
| **RGB Blue (Anode)** | GP20 | Pin 26 (Optional) |
| **HC-SR04 Trig** | GP16 | Out |
| **HC-SR04 Echo** | GP17 | In |
| **GND / Common** | GND | Common Cathode |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else_if` (with three branches).
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `sonar_read`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output`.
*   From **Loops**, drag `repeat_forever`.

### 7. Variables
*   **distance_cm**: Number (Stores the sensor reading).
*   **threat_level**: Text (Stores "SAFE", "WARN", or "DANGER").

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Defining the Zones**
1.  Inside a `repeat_forever` loop, read the distance from the sonar sensor at `GP16/17`.
2.  Assign the result to **distance_cm**.

**Phase B: Multi-Zone Logic**
3.  From **Logic**, snap an `if_else_if` block with three branches.
4.  **Symmetry Branch 1 (Danger)**: `If distance_cm < 20`:
    *   Set Red `ON`.
    *   Set Green `OFF`.
    *   Set Blue `OFF`.
5.  **Symmetry Branch 2 (Warning)**: `Else If distance_cm < 50`:
    *   Set Red `ON`.
    *   Set Green `ON`. (Creates Yellow).
    *   Set Blue `OFF`.
6.  **Symmetry Branch 3 (Safe)**: `Else`:
    *   Set Red `OFF`.
    *   Set Green `ON`.
    *   Set Blue `OFF`.

**Phase C: Cleaning Up**
7.  Add a `pico_wait_ms(100)` at the end of the loop to prevent rapid flickering if an object is vibrating on the edge of a zone.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The system initializes with the Green LED on (Safe).
2.  **Process**: The radar constantly checks for objects within a 1-meter radius.
3.  **Interaction**: An object moves to 40cm. The logic triggers the **Warning** branch.
4.  **Output**: Red and Green LEDs turn on simultaneously, showing a Yellow warning light.
5.  **Interaction**: The object moves to 10cm. The logic triggers the **Danger** branch.
6.  **Output**: Green goes off, leaving only the solid Red LED.
7.  **Repeat**: As the object moves away, the lights transition back through Yellow to Green.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine

# Hardware Init
red_pin = machine.Pin(18, machine.Pin.OUT)
green_pin = machine.Pin(19, machine.Pin.OUT)
blue_pin = machine.Pin(20, machine.Pin.OUT)
sonar = Sonar(16, 17)

while True:
    dist = sonar.read()
    
    # Phase B: Zone Logic
    if dist < 20 and dist > 0:
        # RED: Danger Zone
        red_pin.value(1)
        green_pin.value(0)
    elif dist < 50 and dist > 0:
        # YELLOW (Red+Green): Warning Zone
        red_pin.value(1)
        green_pin.value(1)
    else:
        # GREEN: Safe Zone
        red_pin.value(0)
        green_pin.value(1)
        
    blue_pin.value(0) # Keep Blue off for Traffic Light style
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Common Anode vs Cathode**: If you are using a Common Anode LED, the logic is **inverted**. Setting a pin to `1` (HIGH) will turn the lead *OFF*. Always check your LED type!
*   **Floating Zeroes**: If the sensor loses its echo, it might return `0`. If your logic says `dist < 20`, a `0` (error) will trigger the Red "Danger" light. Always add a `dist > 0` check.
*   **The "Muddy" Yellow**: If your Red and Green LEDs have different brightnesses, Yellow might look orange or light green. Adjust your resistor values to balance the intensity.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Variable Pulse**: Instead of a solid Red, can you make the Red LED flash? Bonus challenge: make it flash faster as the distance gets even smaller (e.g., < 10cm).

## 1. Project 1407: Advanced Radar Screen Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Intrusion Log"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a persistent security logging system that records time-stamped radar detections (Angle and Distance) to the Pico's internal flash memory, utilizing event filtering to prevent log flooding.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Security Journaling**: In forensic engineering, a "Journal" is a write-only record of events. If a sensor is triggered, the system must record the **Who, What, and When** for later analysis.
*   **Flash Memory Life**: Unlike RAM, Flash memory has a limited number of "Write Cycles." To protect the Pico, we use **Event Filtering**, only writing to the disk when a *new* object enters the security perimeter, rather than every millisecond.
*   **RTC Synchronization**: For a log to be useful, it needs a real-world timestamp. We use an external RTC (Real-Time Clock) module because the Pico's internal clock resets when power is lost.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DS3231 RTC Module** (I2C)
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **RTC SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data |
| **RTC SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock |
| **HC-SR04 Trig** | GP16 | Out |
| **HC-SR04 Echo** | GP17 | In |
| **VCC/GND** | 3V3 / GND | Power |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **System & Storage**, drag `append_text` and `file_exists`.
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `get_rtc_time` and `sonar_read`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if` and `and`.
*   From **Text**, drag `create_text_with` (Join).

### 7. Variables
*   **current_time**: Text (HH:MM:SS format).
*   **threat_detected**: Boolean (Flag for logging event).
*   **last_angle**: Number (To prevent logging the same spot twice).
*   **log_string**: Text (The final data line).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: System Initialization**
1.  Verify the I2C connection to the RTC at `GP4/5`.
2.  Set `threat_detected` to **False**.

**Phase B: Event Filtering Logic**
3.  Inside the main radar loop (from Project 1405), read the `distance`.
4.  Check: `If distance < 100`:
    *   Set `threat_detected` to **True**.
5.  To prevent flooding, check: `If threat_detected AND current_angle != last_angle`:
    *   Proceed to Phase C.

**Phase C: Writing the Journal**
6.  Get the `current_time` from the **RTC** block.
7.  Use `create_text_with` to build the entry: `[Time]: Intrusion at [Angle] deg, [Distance] cm`.
8.  From **System & Storage**, drag `append_text`. Set file name to `security_log.txt` and content to `log_string`.
9.  Set `last_angle = current_angle`.

**Phase D: Cleanup**
10. Reset `threat_detected` after the write is confirmed.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The system monitors the room.
2.  **Process**: An object enters the 1-meter "Danger Zone."
3.  **Process**: The system checks the RTC for the exact second.
4.  **Output**: A line is written to the Pico's internal file system: `14:05:22: Intrusion at 90 deg, 45 cm`.
5.  **Repeat**: Even if the object stays there, the system won't write again until the radar head moves to a new angle, saving memory and processing power.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import os

# Init RTC and Sensor
rtc = RTC_DS3231(4, 5) # Hypothetical lib
sensor = Sonar(16, 17)
log_file = "radar_log.txt"

last_log_angle = -1

while True:
    dist = sensor.read()
    angle = get_servo_angle() # From previous project
    
    # Phase B: Filtering
    if 0 < dist < 100 and angle != last_log_angle:
        # Phase C: Journaling
        timestamp = rtc.get_time_str() # "12:00:05"
        entry = f"{timestamp} | Ang: {angle} | Dist: {dist}\n"
        
        with open(log_file, "a") as f:
            f.write(entry)
        
        print("Log Updated:", entry)
        last_log_angle = angle
        
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **File Flooding**: Logging every millisecond. This will fill the Pico's 2MB flash in minutes and wear out the silicon. Always use "Change-based" or "Time-based" filtering.
*   **File Handling**: Not closing the file. In MicroPython, using `with open(...)` is highly recommended to ensure the file is closed even if an error occurs.
*   **Clock Drift**: Not setting the RTC initially. If the RTC isn't set, your logs might say the intrusion happened in the year 2000!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **CSV Format**: Change the logging format to `Time,Angle,Dist`. This allows you to pull the file from the Pico and open it directly in Excel to create a heat-map of where intrusions are happening most often!

---

## 1. Project 1408: Smart Advanced Radar Screen System

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Multi-Sector Network"
At the end of this project, you will be able to design a distributed radar network where multiple Raspberry Pi Picos each monitor a 120-degree sector and transmit their findings via UART to a "Master" Pico for centralized 360-degree visualization.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Distributed Sensing**: In military and automotive radar (like self-driving cars), no single sensor does everything. Instead, multiple smaller sensors (Nodes) process local data and send a "Summary" to a Central Brain.
*   **Sensor Fusion**: This is the process of taking multiple data streams (e.g., Sector A, B, and C) and merging them into a single coherent map. It requires normalizing "Local" angles (0-120) into "Global" angles (0-360).
*   **UART Protocol Design**: When computers talk, they need a "Language." You will design a simple text protocol (e.g., `NodeID:Angle:Distance`) to ensure the Master knows exactly where each piece of data belongs.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **2-4 Raspberry Pi Picos** (One Master, the rest Slaves)
*   **0.96" SSD1306 OLED Display** (Connected to Master)
*   **Ultrasonic Sensors and Servos** (Connected to Slaves)
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Connection |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Master RX** | GP1 | From Slave TX |
| **Slave TX** | GP0 | To Master RX |
| **Common GND** | GND | **CRITICAL**: Connect all GNDs! |
| **Master SDA/SCL**| GP4 / GP5| OLED Display |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Internet & Comm**, drag `uart_init`, `uart_read_line`, and `uart_write`.
*   From **Text**, drag `split_text` and `index_of`.
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `draw_pixel`.
*   From **Math**, drag `add` (to offset the sector angle).

### 7. Variables
*   **raw_message**: Text (Incoming data from the cable).
*   **sector_id**: Number (Which Pico sent the data).
*   **local_angle**: Number (0-120 degrees).
*   **global_angle**: Number (The real 360-degree orientation).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Slave Node Output**
1.  On the **Slave Pico**, perform a standard radar scan (0-120).
2.  Use `create_text_with` to build a message: `2:45:100` (Node 2, 45 deg, 100 cm).
3.  From **Comm**, send this message over `UART0`.

**Phase B: Master Node Input**
4.  On the **Master Pico**, initialize `UART0`.
5.  Use a `while_true` loop to check if `data_is_available`.
6.  Read the `raw_message` until a newline.

**Phase C: Data Fusion**
7.  From **Text**, split the message using the `:` character.
8.  Assign the first part to **sector_id** and second to **local_angle**.
9.  **Normalize**: `global_angle = local_angle + (sector_id - 1) * 120`.

**Phase D: Visualization**
10. Use the math from Project 1402 to plot the `global_angle` and `distance` on the OLED.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: All Picos power on and begin their assigned sector sweeps.
2.  **Process**: Slave #2 detects an object at its local 45-degree mark.
3.  **Process**: It sends `2:45:80` out through its TX pin.
4.  **Process**: The Master receives the string, parses it, and adds 120 to the angle.
5.  **Output**: A dot appears on the Master's OLED at exactly 165 degrees (Global).
6.  **Result**: The user sees a full 360-degree radar view updated in real-time by multiple processors.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
# --- MASTER CODE ---
import machine
import math
import ssd1306

# Init UART and Screen
uart = machine.UART(0, baudrate=9600, tx=machine.Pin(0), rx=machine.Pin(1))
oled = ... # Init OLED

while True:
    if uart.any():
        msg = uart.readline().decode().strip() # "2:45:100"
        parts = msg.split(":")
        
        if len(parts) == 3:
            sid = int(parts[0])
            ang = int(parts[1])
            dst = int(parts[2])
            
            # Phase C: Normalization
            global_ang = ang + (sid - 1) * 120
            
            # Phase D: Plotting
            plot_at_angle(global_ang, dst) # Logic from 1402
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The GND Gap**: Forgetting to connect the ground wires of all Picos. If the grounds aren't connected, the UART signals have no reference and will appear as "Gibberish" to the Master.
*   **Collision**: If 4 Slaves try to talk at the exact same time on one wire, the data will crash. This system works best with point-to-point UART or by using a "Request-Response" (Master asks Node 1, then Node 2).
*   **Baud Mismatch**: If the Slaves send at 9600 but the Master listens at 115200, no data will be received correctly.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Health Check**: Can you modify the Master code to display the "Status" of each Slave? If Node 3 stops sending data for 5 seconds, show a "Sector 3 Offline" warning on the OLED!

## 1. Project 1409: Advanced Radar Screen Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Echo Strength Analyzer"
At the end of this project, you will be able to build a hardware diagnostic tool that evaluates "Echo Strength" (measurement reliability) by calculating the statistical success rate of sensor readings across multiple pings, identifying how different materials absorb or reflect sound.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Signal Integrity (Reliability %)**: In sensor engineering, a single reading is rarely trusted. Instead, we look at the "Yield"—if we ping 10 times, how many times do we get a valid distance back? A high yield (10/10) means a clear signal.
*   **Acoustic Absorption**: Different materials interact with sound differently. A **Hard Surface** (Brick/Glass) reflects sound perfectly (High Strength). A **Soft/Porous Surface** (Sponge/Foam) absorbs sound, leading to "dropped" echoes (Low Strength).
*   **Simulating Amplitude**: Digital sensors like the HC-SR04 don't provide "Loudness" data. However, we can simulate signal strength by measuring the **variance** of the readings. High variance = Weak/Noisy signal.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **Test Materials**: A wooden board (Hard), a kitchen sponge (Soft), and a piece of cloth.
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **HC-SR04 Trig** | GP16 | Out |
| **HC-SR04 Echo** | GP17 | In |
| **VCC/GND** | 3V3 / GND | Power |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Loops**, drag `repeat_10_times`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `change_by` and `set_to`.
*   From **Math**, drag `multiply` and `division`.
*   From **Text**, drag `print`.

### 7. Variables
*   **valid_pings**: Number (Counter for successful echoes).
*   **test_count**: Number (Constant set to 10).
*   **reliability_score**: Number (Calculated percentage).
*   **measured_dist**: Number (Distance from sonar).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: The Sampling Batch**
1.  Set `valid_pings` to `0` at the start of the test.
2.  Snap a `repeat_10_times` loop.
3.  Inside, read the `measured_dist` from the sensor.

**Phase B: Success Validation**
4.  From **Logic**, check if `measured_dist > 0`. (A zero reading indicates a lost/absorbed echo).
5.  If True, use `change_by` to increment `valid_pings` by 1.
6.  Add a `pico_wait_ms(20)` to ensure previous echoes have died down.

**Phase C: Strength Calculation**
7.  After the loop, calculate the result: `reliability_score = (valid_pings / 10) * 100`.
8.  From **Text**, print: `Material Strength: [reliability_score]%`.

**Phase D: Material Exploration**
9.  Run the test against a Wall. (Target: 100%).
10. Run the test against a thick Sponge. (Target: 20-50%).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The user places the sensor 50cm from a test object.
2.  **Process**: The Pico fires 10 rapid ultrasonic pulses.
3.  **Interaction**: If the object is a Brick, all 10 return.
4.  **Math**: `(10 / 10) * 100 = 100%`.
5.  **Output**: Terminal shows "Strength: 100% (Solid Surface)."
6.  **Interaction**: If the object is a Sponge, only 2 pulses return.
7.  **Math**: `(2 / 10) * 100 = 20%`.
8.  **Output**: Terminal shows "Strength: 20% (Acoustic Absorber)."

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Init Sensor
sonar = Sonar(16, 17)

def calibrate_material():
    valid_pings = 0
    test_total = 10
    
    print("Calibrating Target... Keep Still.")
    
    for i in range(test_total):
        d = sonar.read()
        if d > 0:
            valid_pings += 1
        utime.sleep_ms(30)
        
    # Phase C: Final Score
    score = (valid_pings / test_total) * 100
    
    if score > 80:
        status = "SOLID"
    elif score > 30:
        status = "SOFT/ABSORPTIVE"
    else:
        status = "GHOST/STEALTH"
        
    print(f"Reliability: {score}% | Classification: {status}")

# Run Calibration
calibrate_material()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Angle of Attack**: If you point the sensor at a 45-degree angle to a flat hard wall, the sound won't bounce back; it will bounce *away*. This leads to a 0% reliability reading even for a hard surface. Always calibrate at 90 degrees.
*   **Ping Crowding**: Pinging too fast (< 10ms). The echo from Ping #1 might be heard during the window for Ping #2, causing "ghost" successes.
*   **Distance Limit**: Trying to calibrate an object 5 meters away. Ultrasonic sensors lose strength naturally over distance regardless of material.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Reflection Angle Map**: Can you mount the sensor on a servo and find the "Max Reflection Angle"? Rotate the servo until the reliability drops below 50% to see how "wide" the acoustic beam is!

## 1. Project 1410: Advanced Advanced Radar Screen Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Sonar Depth Mapper"
At the end of this project, you will be able to create an advanced 2D depth map (acoustic image) of a room by controlling two servos (Pan and Tilt) to perform a raster scan, visualizing the environment as a grayscale image on an OLED screen where pixel brightness represents distance.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Raster Scan (X-Y Matrix)**: Instead of a single sweep (1D), a raster scan moves in a grid—like reading a page of text. The **Tilt Servo** sets the row, and the **Pan Servo** scans across the columns.
*   **Depth Mapping (Distance to Brightness)**: In "Lidar" and "Sonar" imaging, we don't just see shapes; we see **depth**. We can map `Distance = 0-200cm` to `Brightness = 100%-0%`. Close objects appear bright, far objects appear dark.
*   **Data Aggregation**: Since a full scan (e.g., 20x20 points) takes time, you will learn to manage the "State" of the scan so the user knows when the "Image" is being built vs when it is complete.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2x SG90 Servo Motors** (Mounted in a Pan-Tilt Bracket)
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **0.96" SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pan Servo** | GP14 | Horizontal Sweep |
| **Tilt Servo** | GP15 | Vertical Height |
| **Sonar Trig** | GP16 | Out |
| **Sonar Echo** | GP17 | In |
| **OLED SDA/SCL**| GP4 / GP5| Display Interface |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Loops**, drag `nested_loop` (Outer loop for Tilt, Inner for Pan).
*   From **Math**, drag `map_value` (Map 0-200 to 0-1).
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `draw_pixel`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`.

### 7. Variables
*   **pan_ang / tilt_ang**: Number (Current grid coordinates).
*   **raw_dist**: Number (Distance from sensor).
*   **pixel_val**: Number (Binary or PWM intensity for the pixel).
*   **resolution**: Number (Step size, e.g., 5 degrees).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Matrix Setup**
1.  Establish a resolution of `10` degrees.
2.  Clear the OLED display.

**Phase B: The Raster Loop**
3.  From **Loops**, drag a `count_with` block (Tilt). Set range `70` to `110` (Vertical view).
4.  Inside, drag another `count_with` block (Pan). Set range `45` to `135` (Horizontal view).
5.  Set the Servos to the current `pan_ang` and `tilt_ang`.
6.  Wait `100ms` for the physical assembly to stop moving.

**Phase C: Measurement and Mapping**
7.  Read `raw_dist` from the Sonar.
8.  **Map Logic**: If `raw_dist < 100`, set `pixel_val` to `1` (White). If `> 100`, set to `0` (Black).
9.  Calculate Screen X/Y based on the angle (e.g., `OLED_X = (pan_ang - 45) / resolution`).
10. Draw the pixel.

**Phase D: Rendering**
11. Call `show_display` at the end of every **Pan** row to show the "Scanline" progress.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The servos home to the top-left of the scan area.
2.  **Process**: The Pan servo moves across 18 steps. A line of dots starts to appear on the OLED.
3.  **Interaction**: If a chair is in the way, the dots cluster together as "Bright" pixels.
4.  **Process**: The Tilt servo moves down one notch. The next row starts.
5.  **Output**: After 1-2 minutes, a "Ghostly" 2D outline of the room's geometry appears on the screen.
6.  **Repeat**: The scan restarts or holds for user inspection.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware Init
pan_s = Servo(14)
tilt_s = Servo(15)
sonar = Sonar(16, 17)
res = 10 # 10-degree resolution

while True:
    for t_ang in range(70, 111, res):
        tilt_s.write(t_ang)
        for p_ang in range(45, 136, res):
            pan_s.write(p_ang)
            utime.sleep_ms(150) # Heavy settle time for dual motors
            
            d = sonar.read()
            
            # Phase C: Mapping
            # Convert Angle to Screen Coordinates (roughly)
            scr_x = (p_ang - 45) // res
            scr_y = (t_ang - 70) // res
            
            if 0 < d < 100:
                oled.pixel(scr_x, scr_y, 1)
            else:
                oled.pixel(scr_x, scr_y, 0)
            
        oled.show() # Update row by row
    utime.sleep(60) # Hold image for 1 min
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Mechanical Momentum**: Two servos moving at once creates significant vibration. You must wait longer (150ms+) between movements compared to a 1D scan.
*   **Resolution vs Time**: A 1-degree resolution scan (180x180) would take hours. Keep resolution low (5-10 degrees) for the Pico to produce a "live" feel.
*   **Center Offset**: Mechanical servos often aren't perfectly centered. Calibrate your `70-110` range to ensure your horizon is flat!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Smoothing**: Use a **Logic** block to "Fill in the gaps." If a pixel is black but its neighbors are white, turn it white! This creates a much cleaner "Object Search" image.

---

## Batch 142: Advanced Weather Station 4 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1411: Advanced Weather Station Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Sensor Defroster"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement sensor conditioning by controlling an integrated heating element on advanced environmental sensors (like the SHT4x) to remove moisture and dew, ensuring accurate humidity readings in foggy or saturated outdoor conditions.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Sensor Conditioning**: Precision sensors can become "saturated" when condensed water (dew) forms on the sensing element. This causes the sensor to get stuck at 99% or 100% humidity even after the fog clears. Conditioning is the process of physically drying the chip.
*   **Dew Point Management**: The heater raises the temperature of the sensing element above the ambient dew point, causing water to evaporate. This "Self-Cleaning" cycle is essential for professional weather stations that operate 24/7.
*   **Thermal Interference**: Heating a sensor improves humidity accuracy but temporarily ruins temperature accuracy. Students will learn the "Wait and Stabilize" strategy used to balance these two conflicting needs.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **SHT4x / BME680 Environmental Sensor** (Includes internal heater)
*   **0.96" SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Sensor SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data |
| **Sensor SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock |
| **OLED SDA/SCL**| GP4 / GP5| Shared I2C Bus |
| **VCC/GND** | 3V3 / GND | Power |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `heater_on`, `heater_off`, and `read_humidity`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else`.
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `draw_text`.

### 7. Variables
*   **humidity**: Number (0-100%).
*   **is_saturated**: Boolean (Flag for dew detection).
*   **dry_timer**: Number (Duration of heating cycle).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Detecting Scluration**
1.  Initialize the I2C bus at `GP4/5`.
2.  Inside a `repeat_forever` loop, read the **humidity** from the sensor.
3.  Check: `If humidity > 95`:
    *   Set `is_saturated` to **True**.
    *   Display "DEW DETECTED" on the OLED.

**Phase B: Activation of Clean Cycle**
4.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `heater_on`. (Some sensors allow `low/med/high` power).
5.  Set `dry_timer` to `5` seconds.
6.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait` and set to `dry_timer`.

**Phase C: Stabilization**
7.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `heater_off`.
8.  Crucial: From **Wait**, drag a `pico_wait` for `10` seconds. This allows the sensor to cool down so the next temperature reading isn't artifically high.

**Phase D: Normal Monitoring**
9.  Resume standard humidity monitoring.
10. Observe the humidity value drop from 100% back to the real ambient value (e.g., 85%).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico monitors weather conditions.
2.  **Process**: Humidity rises to 98% due to morning fog.
3.  **Interaction**: The "Defroster" logic triggers.
4.  **Output**: The sensor's internal heater glows (microscopically), drying the silicon.
5.  **Wait**: The system pauses to allow thermal equilibrium.
6.  **Result**: The humidity reading stabilizes at an accurate 88% while the "Wet" sensor next to it is still stuck at 100%.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import sht4x # Hypothetical driver with heater support

# Hardware Init
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
weather_sensor = sht4x.SHT4X(i2c)

while True:
    temp, humidity = weather_sensor.measure()
    
    # Phase A: Detection
    if humidity > 95:
        print("Saturated Sensor! Starting Dry Cycle...")
        
        # Phase B: Heat
        weather_sensor.set_heater("HIGH")
        utime.sleep(5)
        
        # Phase C: Cool
        weather_sensor.set_heater("OFF")
        print("Drying Complete. Stabilizing...")
        utime.sleep(10) # Wait for temperature to normalize
        
    print(f"Current Humidity: {humidity}%")
    utime.sleep(2)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Ignoring the Cool-Down**: Reading the temperature immediately after turning the heater off. The reading will be 20-30 degrees higher than reality. Always wait for the silicon to cool.
*   **Heater Power**: On some sensors (like SHT4x), running the heater on "HIGH" for too long can drain the battery or even damage delicate nearby components. Only use the heater when humidity is near 100%.
*   **Sensor Type**: Trying to find the "Heater On" block for a DHT11. Only higher-end industrial sensors (SHT, BME) usually include this feature.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Predictive Heating**: Can you use **Logic** and **Math** to calculate the "Dew Point"? If the Temperature is within 2 degrees of the Dew Point, turn the heater on *low* proactively to prevent moisture from forming in the first place!

## 1. Project 1412: Advanced Weather Station Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Digital Wind Rose"
At the end of this project, you will be able to design a professional "Wind Rose" visualization on an OLED display, mapping 8 distinct wind directions (N, NE, E, SE, S, SW, W, NW) to a radial UI interface using coordinate geometry.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Compass Visualization**: In meteorology, wind is often represented by a "Rose"—a circular diagram. Mapping directional strings (Text) to geometric angles (Numbers) is a core UI task.
*   **Radial Interface Design**: Unlike a vertical bar graph, a radial interface uses a central anchor point. You will learn to draw an "Indicator Needle" that rotates smoothly around the center of the OLED.
*   **Look-up Tables (LUT)**: Using **Lists** and **Indexes** to store pre-calculated values (e.g., NE = index 1 = 45°) makes your code much cleaner than using 8 massive "if" statements.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **0.96" SSD1306 OLED Display (I2C)**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data |
| **OLED SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock |
| **VCC** | 3V3 | Power |
| **GND** | GND | Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `draw_circle`, `draw_line`, and `draw_text`.
*   From **Text**, drag `list_create` and `index_of`.
*   From **Math**, drag `sine`, `cosine`, and `pico_radians`.

### 7. Variables
*   **directions**: List (Stores ["N", "NE", "E", "SE", "S", "SW", "W", "NW"]).
*   **current_wind**: Text (The input data, e.g., "SW").
*   **needle_angle**: Number (The calculated degree).
*   **tip_x / tip_y**: Number (The end coordinates of the needle).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Building the Compass Base**
1.  Initialize the OLED and set **anchor_x** to `64` and **anchor_y** to `32`.
2.  From **Smart Display**, draw a circle with radius `25` pixels centered at the anchor.
3.  Use `draw_text` to place "N" at (64, 2), "S" at (64, 55), "W" at (35, 32), and "E" at (90, 32).

**Phase B: Mapping the Wind**
4.  From **Text**, create a list called `directions` with all 8 sectors.
5.  Get the `current_wind` (e.g., from an API or simulated sensor).
6.  Find its index in the list. Multiply the index by `45` to get the `needle_angle`.
    *   Example: "E" is index 2. `2 * 45 = 90 degrees`.

**Phase C: Drawing the Needle**
7.  Shift the angle by `-90` because in screen geometry, 0 degrees points right, but in a compass, 0 (North) points up.
8.  Calculate the tip:
    *   `tip_x = 64 + (20 * cos(needle_angle))`
    *   `tip_y = 32 + (20 * sin(needle_angle))`
9.  From **Smart Display**, snap `draw_line` from `(64, 32)` to `(tip_x, tip_y)`.
10. Call `show_display`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The OLED shows a circular compass dial with N,S,E,W markers.
2.  **Process**: The system receives a wind direction string "NE".
3.  **Math**: The code finds index 1 and converts it to 45 degrees.
4.  **Process**: It calculates the pixel coordinates for the 45-degree angle.
5.  **Output**: A line (the needle) appears on the screen pointing towards the upper-right (NE).
6.  **Repeat**: If the wind shifts to "W", the needle snaps instantly to the left marker.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import math
import ssd1306

# Init OLED
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = ssd1306.SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)

# Data Mapping
dirs = ["N", "NE", "E", "SE", "S", "SW", "W", "NW"]

def draw_wind_rose(dir_text):
    oled.fill(0)
    # Phase A: Dial
    oled.circle(64, 32, 25, 1)
    oled.text("N", 61, 2)
    
    # Phase B: Mapping
    if dir_text in dirs:
        idx = dirs.index(dir_text)
        angle_deg = idx * 45
        
        # Phase C: Plotting (Math correction: North is -90 deg)
        rad = math.radians(angle_deg - 90)
        tx = int(64 + 20 * math.cos(rad))
        ty = int(32 + 20 * math.sin(rad))
        
        oled.line(64, 32, tx, ty, 1)
        oled.text(dir_text, 5, 50)
    
    oled.show()

# Example Update
draw_wind_rose("NE")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Degree Inversion**: Confusing Compass degrees (Clockwise from North) with Cartesian degrees (Counter-clockwise from East). If your needle points East when you say North, you need to subtract 90 from your angle.
*   **Label Positioning**: Drawing the text labels outside the 128x64 display area. Always check that your `text()` coordinates are within bounds!
*   **The "Wobble"**: Using `int()` too early in the math. Keep your coordinates as floats until the very last step (`draw_line`) to avoid jittery needles.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Wind Speed Variable**: Can you make the **length** of the needle represent the wind speed? A short needle for a light breeze (e.g., 5 pixels) and a long needle for a gale (25 pixels)!

## 1. Project 1413: Advanced Weather Station Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Storm Watcher"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement meteorological heuristics to detect incoming storms by calculating the rate of barometric pressure drop over time, specifically identifying the "Falling Barometer" pattern that precedes severe weather.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Meteorological Heuristics**: Meteorologists have used "Rules of Thumb" for centuries. A critical rule is that a sudden drop in air pressure (> 2hPa in an hour) almost always indicates a low-pressure system (storm) moving in.
*   **Barometric Tendency**: This isn't just the current pressure, but the **direction** and **speed** of change. 1013 hPa is "High" (Sunny), but if it was 1020 hPa an hour ago, the "Tendency" is Falling.
*   **Rate of Change (Delta)**: In mathematics, Delta (Δ) represents change. `ΔP = P(now) - P(then)`. Logic that looks at the difference between two time points is the basis for all predictive sensors.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **BME280 / BMP280 Barometer Sensor** (I2C)
*   **Active Buzzer** (The Alarm)
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **BME280 SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data |
| **BME280 SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock |
| **Buzzer (+)** | GP18 | Digital Out |
| **VCC/GND** | 3V3 / GND | Power |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `read_pressure`.
*   From **Math**, drag `subtraction`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_less_than`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `list_add_item` (to store history).

### 7. Variables
*   **pressure_now**: Number (Current hPa).
*   **pressure_history**: List (Stores the last 6 readings).
*   **drop_rate**: Number (Calculated change).
*   **alert_mode**: Boolean (Flag for the buzzer).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Data Collection**
1.  Initialize the Barometer on I2C `GP4/5`.
2.  Set a variable `timer` to 600 seconds (10 minutes).
3.  Every 10 minutes, read the **pressure_now** and add it to the `pressure_history` list.
4.  If the list has more than 6 items (1 hour of data), remove the first item to keep it a "Sliding Window."

**Phase B: Trend Analysis**
5.  Get the oldest item in the list (`pressure_history[0]`) and the newest.
6.  `drop_rate = pressure_history[0] - pressure_now`.

**Phase C: Threshold Logic**
7.  Check: `If drop_rate > 2.0`: (A 2hPa drop in an hour).
8.  Set `alert_mode` to **True**.
9.  From **Smart IO**, turn on the buzzer at `GP18`.

**Phase D: Reporting**
10. Print the status: "Pressure Falling! Alert Active" or "Stable Conditions."

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The system records the baseline pressure (e.g., 1013 hPa).
2.  **Process**: For 60 minutes, it collects 6 samples.
3.  **Simulation**: A storm approaches. Pressure drops to 1010 hPa.
4.  **Math**: `1013 - 1010 = 3.0`.
5.  **Interaction**: The threshold logic detects `3.0 > 2.0`.
6.  **Output**: The buzzer screams "Beep-Beep-Beep" as a storm warning.
7.  **Result**: The user is alerted to the weather change before the first rain drop falls.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Init Barometer
sensor = BME280(i2c) # Hypothetical lib
alarm = machine.Pin(18, machine.Pin.OUT)

history = []

while True:
    p = sensor.get_pressure() # hPa
    history.append(p)
    
    # Keep only 1 hour of data (6 samples @ 10m each)
    if len(history) > 6:
        old_p = history.pop(0)
        
        # Phase B & C: Delta Logic
        drop = old_p - p
        if drop > 2.0:
            print(f"STORM ALERT: Pressure dropped {drop} hPa!")
            alarm.value(1)
        else:
            alarm.value(0)
            
    # Wait 10 minutes (600s)
    utime.sleep(600)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Elevator" Error**: If you carry your weather station in an elevator, the pressure will drop/rise purely because of altitude. Ensure the station is **Stationary** for accurate storm alerts.
*   **Units**: Mixed hPa (Hectopascals) and Pa (Pascals). 2 hPa is 200 Pa. If your sensor returns Pa, your threshold must be `200`.
*   **Start-up Lag**: The system cannot alert for the first hour of operation because it doesn't have a "History" yet. Handle the "Empty List" case to avoid errors.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **The Zambretti Predictor**: Combine pressure trends with **Wind Direction** (from Project 1412) to create a full Zambretti Algorithm forecast (e.g., "Fine, becoming unsettled").

## 1. Project 1414: Advanced Weather Station Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Power Gauge"
At the end of this project, you will be able to monitor the system health of your weather station by implementing a software-based battery fuel gauge, reading the Vsys voltage from the Pico's internal ADC, and mapping it to a percentage with a visual battery icon.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Voltage Monitoring (Vsys)**: Remote weather stations run on batteries (often LiPo). The Pico has a dedicated internal circuit connected to the `VSYS` pin (the main power input) which can be measured via its Analog-to-Digital Converter (ADC).
*   **ADC Scaling**: The Pico's ADC returns a raw number (0-4095). You will learn to convert this raw number back into real Volts using a mathematical formula based on the reference voltage (3.3V) and a voltage divider (which the Pico uses internally to scale down the 5V input).
*   **Battery SOC (State of Charge)**: A LiPo battery isn't "Empty" at 0 Volts. It is 100% full at **4.2V** and 0% empty at **3.0V**. Mapping this non-zero range to a 0-100% scale is essential for user-friendly displays.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico (Standard or W)**
*   **0.96" SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **LiPo Battery or USB Power Source** (Standard Pico measures the input voltage)
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data |
| **OLED SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock |
| **Internal ADC** | GP29 | Internal Voltage Divider (ADC3) |
| **GND** | GND | Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`.
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `draw_rectangle` and `draw_text`.
*   From **Math**, drag `map_value` (Map 3.0-4.2 to 0-100).
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `read_vsys_voltage`.

### 7. Variables
*   **volts**: Number (e.g., 3.7).
*   **battery_pct**: Number (0-100).
*   **is_low_power**: Boolean (True if < 20%).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Reading the Raw Power**
1.  Initialize the OLED on `GP4/5`.
2.  From **Smart Sensors**, read the `read_vsys_voltage` block. (On standard Pico, this uses ADC Channel 3).
3.  Assign the result to **volts**.

**Phase B: Converting to Percentage**
4.  From **Math**, snap a `map_value` block.
5.  Set the input as `volts`, source range `3.0` to `4.2`, and target range `0` to `100`.
6.  Ensure the result is constrained—even if the voltage is 4.3V (charging), the percentage shouldn't exceed 100%.

**Phase C: UI Visualization**
7.  From **Smart Display**, draw a small rectangle at the top right: `(110, 2)` with size `15x8`. (This is the battery body).
8.  Draw a tiny 2x4 rectangle next to it as the battery "Nipple."
9.  Divide the body into 3 segments. Fill segments based on the `battery_pct` (e.g., if > 66%, fill all three).

**Phase D: Low Power Alert**
10. If `battery_pct < 20`, print "LOW BATTERY" in large text across the screen.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The weather station powers on and checks its fuel.
2.  **Process**: The ADC measures 3.8V at the VSYS pin.
3.  **Math**: The system maps 3.8V relative to the 3.0-4.2 scale.
4.  **Result**: The code calculates 66% remaining.
5.  **Output**: Two out of three bars are drawn inside the battery icon on the OLED.
6.  **Repeat**: The voltage is checked every 60 seconds to track slow drain.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Specific setup for reading VSYS on Pico
# Note: Pico W uses a different internal pin (GP29) than standard Pico
vsys_adc = machine.ADC(machine.Pin(29)) 

def get_battery_stats():
    # Phase A: Scaling
    # Read raw 12-bit value and convert to Volts
    # (Pico uses a 1/3 divider, so we multiply by 3)
    raw = vsys_adc.read_u16()
    v = (raw / 65535) * 3.3 * 3 
    
    # Phase B: Percentage
    # (v - min) / (max - min) * 100
    pct = (v - 3.0) / (4.2 - 3.0) * 100
    pct = max(0, min(100, pct)) # Constrain
    
    return round(v, 2), int(pct)

while True:
    volts, percentage = get_battery_stats()
    print(f"Energy: {volts}V ({percentage}%)")
    
    # Phase C: Draw on OLED... 
    # (OLED logic here)
    
    utime.sleep(60)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Pico vs Pico W**: On the standard Pico, GP29 is used for VSYS. On the Pico W, GP29 is used for the Wireless chip and VSYS is measured through the power management IC. Always check your board version!
*   **The Divider Factor**: Forgetting that the Pico "sees" only 1/3 of the voltage. If your formula doesn't multiply by 3, you will think your battery is 1.2V (dangerously dead) when it is actually 3.6V (half full).
*   **Charger Noise**: If the USB cable is plugged in, the voltage will be roughly 5V (USB standard). This will make your "Percentage" logic go crazy. Only trust the percentage when running on battery.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Deep Sleep**: If `battery_pct < 10`, can you put the Pico into **Deep Sleep** mode for 1 hour to save the remaining energy for one final emergency transmission?
