## 1. Project 1415: Automated Advanced Weather Station Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Intelligent Awning"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an automated home-protection system that uses multi-sensor logic to control a motorized awning, retracting it during high winds or rain and extending it during direct sunlight to protect a patio while prioritizing safety over comfort.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Multi-Condition Logic**: Real-world automation often involves conflicting data. You will learn to use `OR` and `AND` logic to create a "Priority Hierarchy"—for example, safety (retracting from wind) always overrides comfort (extending for sun).
*   **Hysteresis (Debouncing)**: Sensors can be noisy. If a wind gust lasts 1 second, you don't want the awning to move. If the sun goes behind a cloud for 5 seconds, you don't want it to retract. You will implement "Settle Times."
*   **Fail-Safe States**: Engineering for the worst case. If the wind sensor fails or the power is low, the "Safe State" for an awning is to be **Retracted**.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **High-Torque MG996R Servo** (Awning Motor)
*   **Rain Sensor Module**
*   **Anemometer** (Simulated with a Potentiometer or Pulse counter)
*   **LDR (Light Dependent Resistor)**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo Signal** | GP15 | PWM Control |
| **Rain Sensor** | GP16 | Digital Input (D0) |
| **Wind (Anemometer)**| GP17 | Analog (0-3.3V Sim) |
| **Light (LDR)** | GP26 | Analog (LDR) |
| **Power** | 5V / External | Servos need high current! |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else_if` and `logical_or`.
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag `servo_write`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`.
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `read_analog` and `read_digital`.

### 7. Variables
*   **wind_speed**: Number (0-100 scale).
*   **is_raining**: Boolean (True if wet).
*   **light_level**: Number (0-1023).
*   **is_safe**: Boolean (True if no hazards).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Hazard Detection (Safety First)**
1.  Read the **wind_speed** from `GP17` and **is_raining** from `GP16`.
2.  Assign `is_safe` using **Logic**: `Set is_safe to NOT (wind_speed > 30 OR is_raining == True)`.
3.  This ensures that if *either* hazard is present, the system enters "Safety Mode."

**Phase B: Comfort Detection (Automation)**
4.  Read the **light_level** from `GP26`.
5.  Check: `If light_level > 800`: (Direct Sun).
    *   Set `is_sunny` to **True**.

**Phase C: Actuation Hierarchy**
6.  Inside the loop, use an `if_else_if` block:
7.  **Branch 1**: `If is_safe == False`:
    *   Move Servo to `0` degrees (Retracted).
    *   Wait `30` minutes (Hysteresis - don't extend immediately if a gust ends).
8.  **Branch 2**: `Else If is_sunny == True`:
    *   Move Servo to `180` degrees (Extended).
9.  **Branch 3**: `Else`: 
    *   Move Servo to `0` (Retracted - e.g., at Night).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: It's a sunny day (Light=900). The awning extends.
2.  **Process**: A sudden thunderstorm begins (Rain=True).
3.  **Logic**: Safety logic overrides comfort. Even though it's still bright, the awning retracts to prevent wind damage.
4.  **Wait**: The rain stops, but the system waits 30 minutes to ensure the storm has fully passed.
5.  **Repeat**: After the timer, if it's still sunny, the awning extends again.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware
awning = Servo(15)
rain = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN)
anemometer = machine.ADC(17)
sunlight = machine.ADC(26)

def update_awning():
    # Read Sensors
    is_raining = (rain.value() == 0) # Active Low
    wind = anemometer.read_u16() / 655 # Scale to 0-100
    sun = sunlight.read_u16() / 64 # Scale to 0-1023
    
    # Phase A: Safety Override
    if is_raining or wind > 30:
        print("HAZARD! Retracting Awning.")
        awning.write(0)
        utime.sleep(1800) # 30 min lock-out for safety
        
    # Phase B: Comfort Logic
    elif sun > 800:
        print("High Sun. Extending Awning.")
        awning.write(180)
        
    else:
        print("Night/Cloudy. Retracting.")
        awning.write(0)

while True:
    update_awning()
    utime.sleep(5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Flap" Error**: Moving the awning the instant a cloud passes. Always add a "Confirmation Timer" (e.g., must be sunny for 60 seconds before extending).
*   **Power Hunger**: High-torque servos (MG996R) can pull 2 Amps. If you power it from the Pico's 3.3V pin, the Pico will reset or the chip will burn out. Use an external 5V-6V supply.
*   **Rain Sensor Polarity**: Many rain sensors are "Active Low" (0 means rain). If your awning opens when it rains, flip your logic in Section 8!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Manual Override**: Use a **Pushbutton** to override the automation. If the user presses the button, disable the sunlight logic for 2 hours but **never** disable the safety wind/rain logic!

---

## 1. Project 1416: Advanced Weather Station Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Freeze Protection System"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an agricultural safety system that monitors for frost conditions, triggering a protective heating element via a relay and sounding an alarm when the temperature drops near the freezing point of water.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Agricultural Automation**: In farming, a "Frost Trigger" is used to protect sensitive plants. Even a few minutes at -1°C can kill a crop. Automation ensures the heater turns on at exactly the right time, even in the middle of the night.
*   **Critical Thresholds (Freezing Physics)**: 0°C is the physical threshold where water turns to ice. However, weather stations often use a "Safety Margin"—triggering the heater at **2°C** to account for sensor error or wind chill.
*   **Hysteresis in Heating**: To prevent a relay from "clicking" on and off rapidly when the temperature is exactly 1.99°C, we use a gap. We turn the heater **ON at 2°C** but don't turn it **OFF until it reaches 4°C**.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DS18B20 (Waterproof) or DHT22 Temperature Sensor**
*   **5V Relay Module**
*   **Active Buzzer**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Sensor Data** | GP16 | OneWire or Digital |
| **Relay Signal** | GP17 | Logic Out (High = Activate) |
| **Buzzer (+)** | GP18 | Alarm Output |
| **Power** | VBUS (5V)| Relay needs 5V to click |
| **GND** | GND | Common Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `read_temperature`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`.

### 7. Variables
*   **air_temp**: Number (Degrees Celsius).
*   **frost_threshold**: Number (Set to 2.0).
*   **is_heater_on**: Boolean (Current relay state).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Environment Monitoring**
1.  Initialize the Temperature sensor on `GP16`.
2.  Set `frost_threshold` to `2.0`.
3.  Inside a loop, read the **air_temp**.

**Phase B: Trigger Logic (With Hysteresis)**
4.  From **Logic**, snap an `if_else_if` block.
5.  **Branch 1 (Crisis)**: `If air_temp <= frost_threshold`:
    *   Set Relay `ON`.
    *   Turn Buzzer `ON`.
    *   Set `is_heater_on` to **True**.
6.  **Branch 2 (Recovery)**: `Else If air_temp >= 4.0`:
    *   Set Relay `OFF`.
    *   Turn Buzzer `OFF`.
    *   Set `is_heater_on` to **False**.

**Phase C: Reporting**
7.  Display the state on the OLED or Serial Terminal: `[Temp]C | Heater: [is_heater_on]`.
8.  Wait `10` seconds between checks.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The greenhouse is at 10°C. Everything is off.
2.  **Process**: Night falls. Temperature drops gradually: 5°C... 3.5°C... 2.1°C.
3.  **Interaction**: Temp hits 1.9°C. The logic triggers.
4.  **Output**: The relay "Clicks" closed. The heater starts. The buzzer sounds.
5.  **Feedback**: The temperature begins to rise.
6.  **Control**: Even when it hits 2.5°C, the heater stays ON (due to hysteresis).
7.  **Result**: At 4.1°C, the heater turns off. The plants are safe.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import dht

# Hardware
sensor = dht.DHT22(machine.Pin(16))
relay = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.OUT)
buzzer = machine.Pin(18, machine.Pin.OUT)

heater_active = False

while True:
    try:
        sensor.measure()
        temp = sensor.temperature()
        
        # Phase B: Hysteresis Logic
        if temp <= 2.0:
            relay.value(1)
            buzzer.value(1)
            heater_active = True
            print("FROST ALERT! Heater ON.")
            
        elif temp >= 4.0 and heater_active:
            relay.value(0)
            buzzer.value(0)
            heater_active = False
            print("Temperature stabilized. Heater OFF.")
            
    except OSError as e:
        print("Sensor Error!")
        
    utime.sleep(10)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Relay Logic Inversion**: Many relay modules are "Active Low" (0 turns them on). If your heater turns ON when it's hot and OFF when it's cold, swap your `1` and `0` in the code.
*   **Ground-Level Frost**: Remember that soil temperature can be much colder than air temperature. Place your sensor near the plants, not 2 meters up in the air, for true frost protection.
*   **Missing Hysteresis**: Failing to add the "Off" threshold (4.0C). This causes the relay to chatter (click 5 times a second), which will quickly burn out the mechanical parts of the relay.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual Mode**: Can you add a **Cooling** logic branch? If `temp > 30`, turn on a Fan!

---

## 1. Project 1417: Advanced Weather Station Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Historical Archive"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a long-term environmental data logging system that calculates and stores daily averages (Mean values) for Temperature and Pressure, ensuring data persistence and optimizing storage by aggregating 24 hours of data into a single CSV record.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Data Aggregation**: In big data and meteorology, storing every second's reading is wasteful. Instead, we "Aggregate" data—taking 24 readings (one per hour) and calculating the **Daily Mean**. This preserves the core trend while using 1/24th of the storage space.
*   **Arithmetic Mean**: The mathematical average. `Sum of all samples / Number of samples`.
*   **Persistent CSV Storage**: Comma-Separated Values (CSV) are the universal language of spreadsheets. You will learn to format your logs so they can be opened directly in Excel or Google Sheets for graphing.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DS3231 RTC Module** (For dates and times)
*   **BME280 Environmental Sensor**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **RTC/BME SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data |
| **RTC/BME SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock |
| **VCC/GND** | 3V3 / GND | Power |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **System & Storage**, drag `append_text`.
*   From **Math**, drag `sum` and `division`.
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `get_rtc_date` and `get_rtc_time`.
*   From **Loops**, drag `while_true`.

### 7. Variables
*   **daily_sum**: Number (Total of all readings in a day).
*   **sample_count**: Number (Should reach 24 per day).
*   **current_date**: Text (Used to detect when a new day starts).
*   **mean_temp**: Number (The final result).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Periodic Sampling**
1.  Initialize I2C `GP4/5` for both the RTC and BME sensor.
2.  Get the `current_date` (e.g., "2024-05-10") from the RTC.
3.  Every 1 hour (use a `3600s` wait), read the Temperature.
4.  `Add` the temperature to **daily_sum** and `Increment` **sample_count**.

**Phase B: End-of-Day Detection**
5.  Read the date from the RTC again.
6.  Check: `If current_date != get_rtc_date()`: (A new day has begun!).
    *   Proceed to Phase C.

**Phase C: Calculated Storage**
7.  **Calculate**: `mean_temp = daily_sum / sample_count`.
8.  Use `create_text_with` to build the CSV row: `[current_date], [mean_temp], [sample_count]`.
9.  From **System & Storage**, `append_text` the row to `weather_history.csv`.

**Phase D: Reset**
10. Reset `daily_sum` and `sample_count` to `0`.
11. Update `current_date` to the new today's date.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico begins its 24-hour cycle at midnight.
2.  **Process**: Every hour, it adds the latest temp to a running total.
3.  **Process**: midnight (Day 2) arrives.
4.  **Math**: The Pico divides the total (e.g., 600 degrees) by 24 samples.
5.  **Output**: A line is written to Flash: `2024-05-10, 25.0, 24`.
6.  **Result**: Over a month, the CSV file only reaches 30 lines, even though the Pico checked the temperature 720 times!

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Init
rtc = DS3231(i2c)
sensor = BME280(i2c)

daily_sum = 0
count = 0
last_date = rtc.get_date() # "2024-05-10"

while True:
    # Phase A: Sampling
    t, p, h = sensor.read_all()
    daily_sum += t
    count += 1

    # Phase B: Day Check
    now_date = rtc.get_date()
    if now_date != last_date and count > 0:
        # Phase C: Storage
        avg = daily_sum / count
        log_line = f"{last_date}, {avg:.2f}, {count}\n"

        with open("history.csv", "a") as f:
            f.write(log_line)

        # Reset for new day
        daily_sum = 0
        count = 0
        last_date = now_date

    utime.sleep(3600) # Wait 1 hour
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sample Skew**: If the power goes out at 2:00 PM and comes back at 4:00 PM, you will only have 22 samples. This is why we store the `sample_count` in the CSV, so you know if a day's average is reliable or missing data.
*   **File Extension**: Forgeting the `.csv` extension. While `.txt` works, using `.csv` tells your computer to open it automatically in Excel.
*   **Decimal Precision**: Storing `25.33333333333333` degrees. Use formatting (like `.2f`) to round to two decimal places, which saves space and looks professional.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Min/Max Tracking**: Can you update the logic to also store the **Maximum** and **Minimum** temperatures for the day alongside the average? This provides a much clearer picture of "Thermal Range."

## 1. Project 1418: Smart Advanced Weather Station System

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Agricultural Mesh Network"
At the end of this project, you will be able to design a distributed weather monitoring network where multiple "Sensor Pods" (Slaves) placed across different farm zones transmit their data (Soil moisture, Temp) to a central "Barn Hub" (Master) using an ID-based communications protocol.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Distributed Network Topology**: In precision farming, one sensor isn't enough. You need to know if the "North Field" is dryer than the "South Field." This requires a Hub-and-Spoke model where multiple nodes report to one master.
*   **Data Collision Awareness**: When multiple Slaves share a communication channel, they can "Talk over" each other. You will learn to give each Pod a specific ID and set different "Transmission Windows" to reduce data crashes.
*   **Centralized Visualization**: Merging data from 3 separate locations onto a single OLED dashboard. This requires dynamic UI updating—flipping through "Pages" on the screen for each Pod ID.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **2-3 Raspberry Pi Picos** (One Hub, multiple Pods)
*   **Soil Moisture Sensors** (on the Pods)
*   **0.96" SSD1306 OLED Display** (on the Hub)
*   **UART Cables or LoRa Modules**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pod TX** | GP0 | To Hub RX |
| **Hub RX** | GP1 | From Pod TX |
| **Common GND** | GND | **Required** for UART stability |
| **Hub SDA/SCL**| GP4 / GP5| Hub OLED Interface |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Internet & Comm**, drag `uart_init`, `uart_read_line`, and `uart_write`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else_if` (checking Pod IDs).
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `soil_moisture`.
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `clear_display` and `draw_text`.

### 7. Variables
*   **pod_id**: Number (1, 2, or 3).
*   **moisture_data**: Number (Reading from the field).
*   **hub_msg**: Text (The string `POD:ID:MOISTURE`).
*   **current_display_pod**: Number (Rotation counter).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Pod Transmission (Slave)**
1.  Assign a unique `POD_ID` variable (e.g., `1`).
2.  Read the soil moisture every 60 seconds.
3.  Transmit: `create_text_with` "POD", ID, and Moisture. (Format: `P1:450`).

**Phase B: Hub Collection (Master)**
4.  On the Hub, listen for UART data.
5.  If `data_is_available`, read until newline.
6.  Split the string by `:`. Assign parts to `received_id` and `received_val`.

**Phase C: Hub Dashboard**
7.  Display the data on the OLED.
8.  Use a timer to rotate the screen:
    *   Seconds 0-10: Show "Zone 1 Stats".
    *   Seconds 11-20: Show "Zone 2 Stats".
9.  If a Pod hasn't reported in 10 minutes, show a "POD OFFLINE" warning for that zone.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pods are placed in the garden. The Hub is in the house.
2.  **Process**: Pod 1 (Garden) sends `P1:88%`. Pod 2 (Greenhouse) sends `P2:40%`.
3.  **Process**: The Hub parses the characters "P1" and "P2" to know where the numbers came from.
4.  **Interaction**: The Hub's OLED cycles through the locations.
5.  **Output**: "Greenhouse dry! Turning on pump..." (Logic for future expansion).
6.  **Result**: A single dashboard monitors multiple remote environments.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
# --- HUB CODE ---
import machine
import utime

# Init Comm
uart = machine.UART(0, 9600, tx=machine.Pin(0), rx=machine.Pin(1))
oled = ... # Init OLED

zones = {"P1": "N/A", "P2": "N/A"}

while True:
    # Phase B: Listen
    if uart.any():
        raw = uart.readline().decode().strip() # "P1:88"
        if ":" in raw:
            pid, val = raw.split(":")
            zones[pid] = val
            
    # Phase C: Rotate Display
    for pid in zones:
        oled.fill(0)
        oled.text(f"HUB - Zone {pid}", 0, 0)
        oled.text(f"Moisture: {zones[pid]}%", 0, 20)
        oled.show()
        utime.sleep(5) # Switch every 5s
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Shared Wire Crash**: If two pods send data at the exact same physical moment on the same wire, the data will be garbled. This only works with short wires or wireless modules (LoRa/HC-12) that handle their own timing.
*   **Wait Blocks**: Using long `pico_wait` blocks on the Hub. If the Hub is sleeping for 60 seconds, it might miss the rapid transmission from a Pod. Use `non-blocking` timers instead.
*   **Common GND**: Always connect the GND of all Picos if they are connected by wires!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Direct Action**: Can you add a relay to the Hub? If any Pod reports moisture < 30%, the Hub turns on the main farm irrigation valve!

## 1. Project 1419: Advanced Weather Station Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Altitude Altimeter"
At the end of this project, you will be able to calibrate a barometric pressure sensor by implementing altitude-compensation logic, allowing you to convert local air pressure (Station Pressure) into "Sea Level Pressure" (QNH) using a known reference elevation.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Pressure vs Altitude**: Air is heavier at sea level than on a mountain. To compare weather data between two cities at different heights, scientists "Normalize" the data. This requires the **Barometric Formula**.
*   **Reference Pressure (QNH)**: This is the pressure value used by pilots. Even if you are 1000m up, your weather station should report what the pressure would be if you were at sea level, so your forecast logic (Project 1413) works correctly.
*   **The ISA Standard**: The International Standard Atmosphere assumes that pressure drops by roughly **1 hPa for every 8.5 meters** of ascent (at low altitudes). You will implement a simplified version of this math.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **BME280 / BMP180 Barometer Sensor**
*   **Potentiometer (10kΩ)** (To set your local altitude)
*   **0.96" SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **BME280 SDA/SCL**| GP4 / GP5| I2C Interface |
| **Potentiometer** | GP26 | Analog Input (User Altitude) |
| **VCC/GND** | 3V3 / GND | Power |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **Math**: `math_power`, `math_multiply`, `round`.
*   **Smart Sensors**: `read_pressure`.
*   **Wait**: `pico_wait_ms`.
*   **Smart Display**: `draw_text`.

### 7. Variables
*   **station_p**: Number (Raw reading from sensor).
*   **my_elevation**: Number (Meters above sea level).
*   **sea_level_p**: Number (The calculated result).
*   **adj_factor**: Number (The math constant).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Manual Elevation Input**
1.  Connect a Potentiometer to `GP26`.
2.  Map the analog value `0-1023` to `0-3000` meters. This allows you to "Dial in" the altitude of your city.
3.  Display "Setting Elevation: [meters]m" on the OLED.

**Phase B: The Compensation Formula**
4.  Read the raw **station_p** from the BME280.
5.  Use the simplified international barometric formula:
    *   `sea_level_p = station_p / (1 - (my_elevation / 44330)) ^ 5.255`.
    *   (In Blockly: Use the `power` block from Math).

**Phase C: Verification**
6.  Look up the current "Sea Level Pressure" for your city on a weather website.
7.  Adjust your Potentiometer until the OLED's `sea_level_p` matches the official website.
8.  Lock in this value. Now your weather trends are medically accurate!

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico reads 900 hPa from the sensor (Very low!).
2.  **Process**: The user adjusts the dial to 1000m (the height of their mountain home).
3.  **Math**: The Pico runs the power formula.
4.  **Result**: 900 hPa at 1000m is equivalent to 1013.25 hPa at Sea Level.
5.  **Output**: The OLED shows "Sea Level: 1013 hPa (Clear Skies)."
6.  **Outcome**: Even though you are on a mountain, your station correctly predicts "Fair Weather" instead of "Hurricane" (which 900 hPa would normally imply).

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import math

# Init
sensor = BME280(i2c)
pot = machine.ADC(26)

def get_calibrated_pressure():
    # Phase A: Input
    # Map 0-65535 to 0-3000m
    elev = (pot.read_u16() / 65535) * 3000
    
    # Phase B: Formula
    p_station = sensor.get_pressure()
    # P0 = P / (1 - h/44330)^5.255
    p_sealevel = p_station / math.pow((1 - (elev / 44330.0)), 5.255)
    
    return elev, p_station, p_sealevel

while True:
    h, p_st, p_sl = get_calibrated_pressure()
    print(f"Elev: {h:.0f}m | Local: {p_st:.1f} | SLP: {p_sl:.1f}")
    utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Metres vs Feet**: The constant `44330` is for **Metas**. If you use feet, the formula will fail spectacularly. 
*   **Reference Static**: If you don't adjust for your local altitude, the sensor is only useful for "Change" detection, not for comparing with other weather stations.
*   **Temperature**: Standard formulas assume 15°C air. If it's -20°C or +40°C, the air density changes, and the formula becomes slightly less accurate.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **The Altimeter Mode**: Flip the script! If you know the Sea Level Pressure (from the news), can you calculate your **Altitude**? This is how planes know how high they are flying!

---

## 1. Project 1420: Advanced Advanced Weather Station Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Integrated Forecast Dashboard"
At the end of this project, you will be able to integrate all previous weather station modules into a single synchronized prototype that manages power, predicts storms, controls safety relays, and stores data simultaneously using a round-robin task scheduler.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **System Integration**: This is the "Grand Finale" where you learn to combine multiple independent blocks of code (Modules) into one unified system without them interfering with each other.
*   **Round-Robin Scheduling**: A simple way to multi-task. Instead of one long loop that waits 1 hour for data, you create a loop that runs constantly, checking if it's time to "Save Data" (Task 1), "Update UI" (Task 2), or "Check Safety" (Task 3).
*   **Information Architecture (IA)**: Designing a layout for the 128x64 pixels on your OLED that organizes Temperature, Pressure, Wind, and Battery life into a clear, readable dashboard.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **BME280 (I2C) Sensor**
*   **0.96" SSD1306 OLED**
*   **RTC Module (DS3231)**
*   **Rain Sensor & Anemometer**
*   **5V Relay & Active Buzzer**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Type | Component | Pico Pins |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **I2C Bus** | OLED, BME280, RTC | GP4 (SDA), GP5 (SCL) |
| **Analog** | Wind, Light, Battery | GP26, GP27, GP29 |
| **Digital** | Rain, Buzzer, Relay | GP16, GP17, GP18 |
| **PWM** | Awning Servo | GP15 |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms` (for non-blocking timers).
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_greater_than`.
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `draw_ui_grid`.
*   From **System & Storage**, drag `append_text`.

### 7. Variables
*   **last_log_time**: Number (Timestamp for the 1-hour log).
*   **last_ui_time**: Number (Timestamp for the 1-second screen refresh).
*   **storm_warn**: Boolean (Flag from Project 1413).
*   **system_ready**: Boolean (Init flag).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Initialization**
1.  Initialize all I2C, PWM, and ADC sensors.
2.  Display a "System Booting" progress bar on the OLED.
3.  Set `last_log_time` to the current `ticks_ms`.

**Phase B: The Main Loop (Non-Blocking)**
4.  Calculate the `delta_time = current_ticks - last_log_time`.
5.  **Task 1 (Safety - Fast)**: Every 500ms, check for Storms (Pressure Drop) and Frost (Temp < 2C). If dangerous, trigger the Buzzer and Relay.
6.  **Task 2 (UI - Medium)**: Every 1 second, update the OLED with the dynamic "Wind Rose" and current stats.
7.  **Task 3 (Logger - Slow)**: Every 1 hour, calculate the mean and append to the CSV file.

**Phase C: Logic Synchronization**
8.  Ensure that if the `Heater` is drying the sensor (Project 1411), the `Logger` doesn't record the fake high temperature.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The dashboard appears, showing 25.5°C, 30% Humidity, and a full Battery icon.
2.  **Process**: The system zips through the loop thousands of times per second. 
3.  **Interaction**: It hits the 500ms mark; it checks the sensor. No storm.
4.  **Interaction**: It hits the 1s mark; the wind rose needle moves slightly.
5.  **Event**: Pressure drops significantly. The "STORM" flag flips to TRUE.
6.  **Output**: The OLED flashes a warning, the buzzer beeps, and the Awning retracts automatically.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Master Scheduler
last_fast_task = 0
last_ui_task = 0
last_slow_task = 0

while True:
    now = utime.ticks_ms()
    
    # Fast Task (Safety Logic - Every 500ms)
    if utime.ticks_diff(now, last_fast_task) > 500:
        check_safety_hazard() # Storm/Frost logic
        last_fast_task = now
        
    # Medium Task (OLED Refresh - Every 2000ms)
    if utime.ticks_diff(now, last_ui_task) > 2000:
        update_dashboard_ui() # Drawing stats
        last_ui_task = now
        
    # Slow Task (Data Logging - Every 1 Hour)
    if utime.ticks_diff(now, last_slow_task) > 3600000:
        save_daily_average() # CSV logic
        last_slow_task = now
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Variable Scope**: Forgetting to use the `global` keyword for timers inside sub-functions.
*   **The "Wait" Trap**: NEVER use `pico_wait(600)` in a multi-tasking system. While the Pico waits, it can't check for storms or update the screen. Always use `ticks_diff`.
*   **I2C Bandwidth**: Trying to update the OLED 100 times per second while also talking to the sensors. Limit UI updates to 1-2 times per second.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Remote Dashboard**: Connect your weather station Hub to a **Pico W** and use a `web_server` block to show your dashboard in a browser on your phone!

## Batch 143: Advanced Digital Spirit Level 4 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1421: Advanced Digital Spirit Level Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"The High-Speed Level"
At the end of this project, you will be able to interface with a high-precision IMU (MPU6050) and optimize its sampling rate, configuring the sensor to output gravity-aligned accelerometer data for precise angle measurement in a spirit level application.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Inertial Measurement (Accelerometers)**: An accelerometer doesn't just measure "Speeding up." It measures the force of gravity. Even when sitting still, an IMU "sees" a constant force of **1G (9.8 m/s²)** pointing towards the center of the Earth.
*   **Gravity Vectors**: By measuring gravity across three axes (X, Y, Z), we can calculate the orientation of the sensor. If the sensor is perfectly flat, the Z-axis reads 1G, and X/Y read 0G.
*   **Sampling Frequency (Hz)**: The IMU can provide data at 1000 times per second (1kHz). You will learn why "High-Speed" is necessary for a smooth UI (Spirit Level) but also how to handle the "Noise" (Vibration) that comes with high speeds.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MPU6050 6-Axis Accelerometer/Gyroscope Module**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **MPU6050 SDA** | GP4 | I2C0 Data |
| **MPU6050 SCL** | GP5 | I2C0 Clock |
| **MPU6050 VCC** | 3V3 | Power |
| **MPU6050 GND** | GND | Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `imu_init` and `imu_read_accel`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `create_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **accel_x / accel_y / accel_z**: Number (Force in Gs).
*   **sample_rate**: Number (Delay between readings in ms).
*   **is_stable**: Boolean (Detected by checking variance).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Connecting to the Inertial World**
1.  Connect the MPU6050 via I2C to `GP4/5`.
2.  Initialize the IMU with a sensitivity range of **±2G**. (Lower range means higher resolution for tilt).
3.  Set the `sample_rate` to `10ms` (100Hz).

**Phase B: Reading the Gravity Vector**
4.  Inside the main loop, read the **accel_x**, **accel_y**, and **accel_z** values.
5.  Print these values to the Serial Monitor.
6.  **Observation**: Tilt the breadboard. Notice how as the X-value increases, the Z-value decreases. Gravity is "Flowing" between the axes!

**Phase C: Calibration & Zeroing**
7.  Place the sensor on a known flat surface.
8.  If the readings aren't exactly 0.0 for X and Y, record these as "Offsets."
9.  Subtract these offsets from every new reading to "Zero" the level.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The system initializes the MPU6050.
2.  **Process**: Samples are taken every 10 milliseconds.
3.  **Interaction**: The user tilts the Pico. 
4.  **Math**: The IMU internal MEMS (Micro-Electro-Mechanical System) shifts.
5.  **Output**: The Serial monitor shows values like `X: 0.05 | Y: -1.21 | Z: 9.78`.
6.  **Result**: The Pico now knows exactly which way "Down" is relative to its body.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import mpu6050 # Hypothetical driver for Blockly

# Init I2C and IMU
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
imu = mpu6050.MPU6050(i2c)

# Offsets for Calibration
offset_x = 0.0
offset_y = 0.0

def calibrate():
    global offset_x, offset_y
    print("Calibrating... Keep Level.")
    # Take 10 readings and average them
    ... 

while True:
    # Phase B: High-Speed Read
    accel = imu.get_accel() # Returns {x, y, z} in m/s^2
    
    # Apply Offsets
    clean_x = accel['x'] - offset_x
    clean_y = accel['y'] - offset_y
    
    print(f"X: {clean_x:.2f} | Y: {clean_y:.2f} | Z: {accel['z']:.2f}")
    
    utime.sleep_ms(10) # 100Hz Refresh
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Vibration" Ghost**: If the Pico is near a motor or fan, the accelerometer will show huge spikes even if it's level. This is "High Frequency Noise." Use a `soft_filter` to smooth it out.
*   **Orientation Confusion**: Mounting the sensor upside down. If Z reads -1.0G when it should be +1.0G, your sensor is inverted.
*   **m/s² vs Gs**: Some blocks return m/s² (Earth gravity = 9.8) while others return Gs (Earth gravity = 1.0). Don't mix them up in your math!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **The "Stillness" Detector**: Can you light an LED only when the Pico is perfectly still (no vibration detected)? This is used in trip-wire alarms!

---

## 1. Project 1422: Advanced Digital Spirit Level Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Gravity-Aware UI"
At the end of this project, you will be able to design a dynamic OLED user interface that responds to physical orientation, rotating its text and graphic elements 90 degrees as the device is flipped, ensuring the "Digital Bubble" is always readable regardless of how the tool is held.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Dynamic Screen Rotation**: Modern smartphones rotate the UI when you turn them. You will implement this by detecting the dominant gravity vector (Is gravity pulling on the X-axis or Y-axis?) and calling a `rotate_display` command.
*   **Coordinate Transformation**: When a screen rotates from 128x64 to 64x128, your drawing logic must change. You will learn to use variables to define "Center" so your bubble doesn't get stuck at the old coordinates.
*   **Gravity Thresholds**: To prevent the screen from "Flickering" between modes, we use thresholds. We only rotate if the tilt is greater than **45 degrees**.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MPU6050 IMU**
*   **0.96" SSD1306 OLED Display (I2C)**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **MPU/OLED SDA** | GP4 | Shared I2C Data |
| **MPU/OLED SCL** | GP5 | Shared I2C Clock |
| **VCC / GND** | 3V3 / GND | Power |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `set_rotation` and `draw_circle`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else_if`.
*   From **Math**, drag `map_value` and `abs`.

### 7. Variables
*   **acc_x**: Number (X-axis gravity).
*   **orientation**: Text ("LANDSCAPE" or "PORTRAIT").
*   **bubble_x / bubble_y**: Number (Screen coordinates).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Orientation Switching**
1.  Read **acc_x** and **acc_y** from the IMU.
2.  Use a Logic check: `If abs(acc_x) > 0.8`:
    *   Set `rotation` to `90` (Portrait).
    *   Set `orientation` to "Vertical".
3.  `Else If abs(acc_y) > 0.8`:
    *   Set `rotation` to `0` (Landscape).
    *   Set `orientation` to "Horizontal".

**Phase B: Mapping the Bubble**
4.  In Horizontal mode, **acc_x** tells us how much we are tilted.
5.  Use a `map_value` block to convert **acc_x** (-1.0 to 1.0) into screen coordinates (0 to 128).
6.  Assign this to `bubble_x`. Set `bubble_y` to `32` (Fixed center).

**Phase C: UI Drawing**
7.  Draw a hollow rectangle as the "Level Tube": `(10, 20)` size `108x24`.
8.  Draw a solid circle (the bubble) at `(bubble_x, bubble_y)` with radius `8`.
9.  Inside the circle, display the raw degrees (calculated using `atan2` or simple mapping).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The device is held horizontally. The bubble sits in the center of the long tube.
2.  **Interaction**: The user rotates the device 90 degrees to measure a wall.
3.  **Process**: The system detects that gravity is now pulling on the X-axis.
4.  **Action**: The screen "Flips." The tube redraws vertically.
5.  **Output**: The bubble now slides up and down the vertical tube.
6.  **Result**: The tool remains functional and intuitive in any physical orientation.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import ssd1306
import mpu6050

# Init
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = ssd1306.SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
imu = mpu6050.MPU6050(i2c)

def draw_ui(x_tilt, portrait=False):
    oled.fill(0)
    if portrait:
        oled.rotate(1) # Internal function for rotation
        # Map tilt to 64 pixels (portrait width)
        bx = int((x_tilt + 1) * 32)
        oled.rect(0, 20, 64, 20, 1)
        oled.fill_circle(bx, 30, 5, 1)
    else:
        oled.rotate(0)
        # Map tilt to 128 pixels (landscape width)
        bx = int((x_tilt + 1) * 64)
        oled.rect(10, 20, 108, 20, 1)
        oled.fill_circle(bx, 30, 8, 1)
    oled.show()

while True:
    acc = imu.get_accel()
    if abs(acc['x']) > 8: # MPU returns ~9.8 for 1G
        draw_ui(acc['y']/9.8, portrait=True)
    else:
        draw_ui(acc['x']/9.8, portrait=False)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Flicker Cycle**: If you don't use a large enough threshold, the screen will rotate back and forth if you hold it exactly at a 45-degree angle. This is called "Thrashing."
*   **Coordinate Inversion**: In Portrait mode, up/down and left/right are often swapped. If your bubble moves left when you tilt right, multiply your mapping value by `-1`.
*   **Initialization Order**: Initializing the OLED before the IMU. If the IMU takes a few milliseconds to stabilize, the rotation check might fail on the first frame.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Invert**: Can you make the UI flip **180 degrees** as well? This is useful for measuring the underside of surfaces (ceilings).

## 1. Project 1423: Advanced Digital Spirit Level Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Firefighter's Dead-Man Switch"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an occupancy/immobility detection system that monitors the IMU for constant stillness, triggering a distress alarm if no significant motion is detected for a specific duration (simulating a "Man Down" safety device).

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Activity Monitoring**: Unlike measuring an angle, activity monitoring looks for the **Delta** (Change) between two readings. If `current_gravity` is almost exactly the same as `previous_gravity`, the device is stationary.
*   **Variance Thresholds**: Sensors have noise. Even on a perfectly still table, the numbers will wiggle (e.g., 0.01, 0.02). You will learn to set a "Deadzone" where small wiggles are ignored, but a step or a breath is counted as motion.
*   **Safety Timeouts**: Using non-blocking timers to track how long a specific condition (Stillness) has lasted. If `still_time > 30 seconds`, action is required.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MPU6050 IMU**
*   **Active Buzzer**
*   **Push Button** (For manual reset)
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **MPU6050 I2C** | GP4/5 | Communication |
| **Buzzer (+)** | GP18 | Distress Alarm |
| **Reset Button** | GP16 | Clear Alarm |
| **GND** | GND | Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms` and `pico_ticks_diff`.
*   From **Math**, drag `absolute_value` and `subtraction`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_greater_than`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output`.

### 7. Variables
*   **last_x / last_y**: Number (Comparison samples).
*   **last_motion_ticks**: Number (Timestamp of last movement).
*   **is_alarm_on**: Boolean (Safety state).
*   **still_limit**: Number (30,000ms = 30 seconds).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Detecting Motion**
1.  Read the **current_x** and **current_y** from the IMU.
2.  Calculate the `diff_x = abs(current_x - last_x)`.
3.  If `diff_x > 0.5`:
    *   Motion detected! Set `last_motion_ticks` to the current system time (`pico_ticks_ms`).
4.  Update `last_x = current_x` for the next check.

**Phase B: Timing the Stillness**
5.  Calculate `elapsed = current_time - last_motion_ticks`.
6.  Display the "Countdown" on the OLED: `Seconds Left: [ (30000 - elapsed)/1000 ]`.

**Phase C: Triggering the Alarm**
7.  Check: `If elapsed > 30000` (30 seconds):
    *   Set `is_alarm_on` to **True**.
    *   Turn on the Buzzer at `GP18`.
8.  If the Reset Button at `GP16` is pressed, reset `last_motion_ticks` and turn the buzzer off.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: A firefighter enters a building. The Pico moves with every step; the timer stays at 0.
2.  **Process**: The firefighter stops (e.g., trapped or unconscious). 
3.  **Process**: The sensor readings stop changing. `diff_x` stays below 0.5.
4.  **Action**: The internal timer begins to climb: 1s... 10s... 20s.
5.  **Output**: At 30 seconds of pure stillness, the Buzzer screams to alert rescue teams.
6.  **Interaction**: If the user moves or presses the Reset button, the siren cuts off immediately.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

imu = MPU6050(i2c)
buzz = machine.Pin(18, machine.Pin.OUT)
btn = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

last_x = 0
last_motion = utime.ticks_ms()
LIMIT = 30000 # 30s

while True:
    ax = imu.get_accel()['x']
    
    # Phase A: Change detection
    if abs(ax - last_x) > 0.5:
        last_motion = utime.ticks_ms() # Reset timer
        buzz.value(0)
        
    last_x = ax
    
    # Phase B: Timeout logic
    elapsed = utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), last_motion)
    
    if elapsed > LIMIT:
        # Phase C: Alarm
        buzz.value(1)
        print("ALARM: MAN DOWN!")
        
    if btn.value() == 0: # Manual reset
        last_motion = utime.ticks_ms()
        buzz.value(0)
        
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Vibrating Table"**: If you put this on a table next to a vibrating fan, it will never trigger because the wiggles will be > 0.5. Adjust the `motion_threshold` (0.5) to suit the environment.
*   **Floating Pins**: Forgetting the `PULL_UP` on the reset button. An unpressed button might read 0 by mistake, resetting the safety timer when it shouldn't.
*   **Integer Overflow**: Using non-standard time functions. Always use `ticks_ms` and `ticks_diff` for reliability over long durations.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Visual Warning**: Can you make an LED flash faster as the timer gets closer to 30 seconds? This gives the user a chance to move before the loud buzzer triggers.

---

## 1. Project 1424: Advanced Digital Spirit Level Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"The G-Force Meter"
At the end of this project, you will be able to monitor the dynamic forces acting on your device by implementing a real-time G-Force meter, visualizing instantaneous acceleration peaks ("G-loading") on an OLED display using a dynamic bar-graph interface.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Resultant Force Vector (3D Math)**: Motion doesn't happen on just one axis. To find the "Total Gs" acting on the Pico, you need to combine X, Y, and Z using the 3D Pythagorean theorem: `TotalG = √(X² + Y² + Z²)`.
*   **Static vs. Dynamic Acceleration**: At rest, the sensor measures 1G (gravity). In motion, it measures `Gravity + Acceleration`. You will learn to subtract the constant 1G to find the **True Dynamic Acceleration**.
*   **Peak Holding**: In racing or aerospace, pilots need to know the *highest* G-force they hit during a maneuver. You will implement a "Peak Memory" that stores and displays the `Maximum G` value until a reset button is pressed.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MPU6050 6-Axis IMU**
*   **0.96" SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **Push Button** (For Peak Reset)
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **MPU/OLED SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data |
| **MPU/OLED SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock |
| **Reset Button** | GP16 | Clear Peak G |
| **GND** | GND | Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `square_root`, `power`, and `subtraction`.
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `draw_rectangle` and `draw_text`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_greater_than`.

### 7. Variables
*   **raw_g**: Number (The calculated resultant).
*   **dynamic_g**: Number (Total G - 1.0).
*   **max_g**: Number (Peak memory).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Vector Summation**
1.  Read **accel_x**, **accel_y**, and **accel_z** from the MPU6050.
2.  Use the Math blocks to calculate: `sum_squares = (X*X) + (Y*Y) + (Z*Z)`.
3.  Assign `raw_g = sqrt(sum_squares)`.

**Phase B: Dynamic Filtering**
4.  Subtract the baseline gravity: `dynamic_g = raw_g - 1.0`. (Use `abs()` to ensure values are positive).
5.  If `dynamic_g > max_g`:
    *   Update `max_g` with the new record.

**Phase C: UI Visualization**
6.  Draw a vertical bar on the left of the OLED: `x=5, y=60, width=10, height=-(dynamic_g * 10)`.
7.  Display the `MAX G` value in large text in the center.
8.  If the button at `GP16` is pressed, set `max_g = 0`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico is sitting on the desk. Total G is 1.0. Dynamic G is 0.0.
2.  **Interaction**: The user shakes the Pico rapidly.
3.  **Math**: The system calculates a resultant of 2.5G. 
4.  **Process**: It subtracts the 1G baseline to find the "Thrust" was 1.5G.
5.  **Action**: The `Max G` variable jumps from 0 to 1.5.
6.  **Output**: The OLED bar shoots up and the text says "MAX: 1.50 G".
7.  **Result**: The user can now measure the forces of a car crash, a roller coaster, or a jump.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import math
import ssd1306
import mpu6050

# Init
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = ssd1306.SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
imu = mpu6050.MPU6050(i2c)
btn = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

max_g = 0.0

while True:
    acc = imu.get_accel() # Assumed 1.0 = 1G
    
    # Phase A: Vector Sum
    total = math.sqrt(acc['x']**2 + acc['y']**2 + acc['z']**2)
    
    # Phase B: Dynamic G (Subtract Earth's gravity)
    dyn = abs(total - 1.0)
    
    if dyn > max_g:
        max_g = dyn
        
    # Phase C: Display
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text(f"NOW: {dyn:.2f} G", 20, 10)
    oled.text(f"MAX: {max_g:.2f} G", 20, 30)
    # Bar graph
    height = int(dyn * 20)
    oled.rect(5, 60-height, 10, height, 1)
    oled.show()
    
    if btn.value() == 0:
        max_g = 0
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The baseline 1.0**: Forgetting that Earth is always pulling on the sensor. If you don't subtract 1.0, your "G-meter" will show 1.0G even when nothing is moving!
*   **Calibration Range**: If you use the IMU's ±2G mode, it will "Clip" (max out) at 2G. If you want to measure a high-speed car turn, set the IMU to **±8G** or **±16G** in the setup.
*   **Square Root Cost**: Calculating a square root is mathematically expensive. Don't run this loop 1000 times a second, or the OLED refresh will stutter. 30Hz to 60Hz is perfect.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Directional Gs**: Can you display a "Compass" where a dot moves in the direction of the force? Centered means no force, moving left means a hard right turn!

---

## 1. Project 1425: Automated Advanced Digital Spirit Level Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Self-Leveling Tripod"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an automated stabilization system using a 2-axis servo gimbal, where the Pico uses IMU feedback to counteract physical tilts, keeping a platform perfectly level even on uneven terrain.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Closed-Loop Feedback**: This is the heart of automation. The "Output" (Servo movement) is determined by the "Input" (Sensor reading). If the tilt is +5 degrees, the servo moves -5 degrees to "Close the loop" and return to zero.
*   **Tilt Calculation (Trigonometry)**: To find an angle from gravity, we use the `atan2` function. `Angle = atan2(Y, Z)`. This converts raw G-forces into meaningful Degrees.
*   **Proportional Correction**: Instead of moving the servo to a fixed spot, we move it proportionally to the error. A large tilt causes a large move; a tiny tilt causes a tiny move. This results in smooth, organic-feeling motion.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MPU6050 IMU**
*   **2 x SG90 Serov Motors**
*   **Gimbal Frame** (3D printed or cardboard)
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo 1 (Roll)** | GP14 | PWM Control |
| **Servo 2 (Pitch)**| GP15 | PWM Control |
| **MPU6050 I2C** | GP4/5 | Feedback Sensor |
| **Power** | 5V / VBUS | Servos need 5V! |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `atan2` and `radians_to_degrees`.
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag `servo_write`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`.

### 7. Variables
*   **pitch_deg / roll_deg**: Number (Input angles).
*   **servo_x / servo_y**: Number (Target PWM angles).
*   **center_pos**: Number (90 degrees).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Angle Extraction**
1.  Read the **accel_x**, **accel_y**, and **accel_z** from the IMU.
2.  Calculate the Roll: `roll_deg = atan2(y, z) * 180 / PI`.
3.  Calculate the Pitch: `pitch_deg = atan2(-x, sqrt(y*y + z*z)) * 180 / PI`.

**Phase B: Mapping to Servos**
4.  The neutral position of a servo is `90`.
5.  Set `servo_x = 90 - roll_deg`.
6.  Set `servo_y = 90 + pitch_deg`.
7.  Wait! If the servo reaches 0 or 180, it will "Click" and potentially break. Use a `constrain` block to keep values between **10** and **170**.

**Phase C: Stabilization Loop**
8.  Write the values to the servos at `GP14` and `GP15`.
9.  Add a small `20ms` delay to allow the mechanical parts to move.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The gimbal is powered on. Servos move to 90/90 (Level).
2.  **Interaction**: The user picks up the base and tilts it 20 degrees to the left.
3.  **Math**: The IMU senses the gravity shift. `roll_deg` becomes `20`.
4.  **Process**: The Pico calculates `90 - 20 = 70`.
5.  **Output**: Servo 1 moves to 70 degrees.
6.  **Result**: Because the servo moved *against* the tilt, the platform on top stays level with the horizon.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import math
import utime

# Init Hardware
s_roll = Servo(14)
s_pitch = Servo(15)
imu = MPU6050(i2c)

while True:
    acc = imu.get_accel()
    
    # Phase A: Convert Gs to Degrees
    roll = math.atan2(acc['y'], acc['z']) * (180/math.pi)
    pitch = math.atan2(-acc['x'], math.sqrt(acc['y']**2 + acc['z']**2)) * (180/math.pi)
    
    # Phase B: Inverse Mapping (Move opposite to tilt)
    target_roll = 90 - roll
    target_pitch = 90 + pitch
    
    # Constrain for servo health
    target_roll = max(10, min(170, target_roll))
    target_pitch = max(10, min(170, target_pitch))
    
    # Phase C: Actuation
    s_roll.write(target_roll)
    s_pitch.write(target_pitch)
    
    utime.sleep_ms(20)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Positive vs Negative Error**: If your gimbal "Assists" the tilt (doubling the angle) instead of correcting it, you need to flip the sign in your math (change `90 - roll` to `90 + roll`).
*   **The "Shaking" Loop**: If you don't have a small delay, the Pico will send updates faster than the servo can move, causing it to hum or vibrate. 
*   **Center Offset**: Every gimbal is built slightly differently. You might need to use `92` or `88` as your "Center" instead of exactly `90`.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Joy-Level**: Connect a Joystick. Use the joystick to "Offset" the level. This allows you to remotely control a camera gimbal that stays level with the ground but can still be aimed!
    
---

## 1. Project 1426: Advanced Digital Spirit Level Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Rollover Warning System"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an industrial safety trigger that monitors the roll-angle of a vehicle or machine, activating a staged alert system (Warning and Critical) that triggers a lockout relay to prevent accidents.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Static Stability Limits**: Every vehicle (Tractors, Forklifts, ATVs) has a "Tip-over Point." You will learn how to translate these physical safety limits into code thresholds.
*   **Staged Alerts**: A good safety system doesn't just "Panic." It provides a **Warning** (Yellow Light) when approaching the limit, and only goes to **Critical** (Buzzer + Relay) when the limit is breached.
*   **Digital Lockout**: Using a Relay to break a circuit. In a real vehicle, this might cut the engine's ignition coil or stop a hydraulic pump to freeze the machine's movement before it flips.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MPU6050 IMU**
*   **Active Buzzer**
*   **Yellow and Red LEDs**
*   **5V Relay Module**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Yellow LED** | GP14 | Warning Stage |
| **Red LED** | GP15 | Critical Stage |
| **Relay Signal** | GP16 | Lockout (Power Cut) |
| **Buzzer** | GP17 | Siren |
| **MPU6050** | GP4/5 | I2C Sensor |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `absolute_value` and `atan2`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else_if`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output`.

### 7. Variables
*   **current_roll**: Number (Degrees from horizontal).
*   **warn_limit**: Number (Set to 25.0).
*   **kill_limit**: Number (Set to 40.0).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Tilt Calculation**
1.  Read the **accel_y** and **accel_z** from the IMU.
2.  Calculate the angle: `Set current_roll to abs( atan2(y, z) * 180 / PI )`.
3.  We use `abs()` because a rollover is dangerous whether it is to the Left or Right!

**Phase B: Staged Logic**
4.  From **Logic**, snap a 3-branch `if_else_if` block.
5.  **Branch 1 (Critical)**: `If current_roll > kill_limit`:
    *   Set Relay `ON` (Lockout).
    *   Set Red LED `ON`.
    *   Sound continuous Buzzer.
6.  **Branch 2 (Warning)**: `Else If current_roll > warn_limit`:
    *   Set Yellow LED `ON`.
    *   Pulse the Buzzer (Beep-Beep).
    *   Ensure Relay is `OFF` (Safe to move, but be careful).
7.  **Branch 3 (Safe)**: `Else`:
    *   Turn off all LEDs and Buzzers.
    *   Relay `OFF`.

**Phase C: Filtering for Bumps**
8.  If the vehicle hits a small rock, the tilt might briefly spike. Add a `soft_delay` or average 5 readings to ensure the tilt is sustained before triggering the "Kill" relay.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The tractor is on flat ground. All lights are off.
2.  **Interaction**: The driver drives onto a slope of 30 degrees.
3.  **Process**: `30 > warn_limit (25)`.
4.  **Action**: The Yellow LED begins to flash. The driver knows they are near the limit.
5.  **Event**: The slope steepens to 45 degrees.
6.  **Action**: The Red LED lights up, the siren rings, and the Relay clicks open, stopping the machine.
7.  **Result**: The vehicle is saved from flipping over.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import math
import utime

# Hardware
imu = MPU6050(i2c)
yellow = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
red = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)
relay = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
buzzer = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.OUT)

while True:
    acc = imu.get_accel()
    roll = abs(math.atan2(acc['y'], acc['z']) * (180/math.pi))
    
    # Phase B: Thresholds
    if roll > 40:
        # CRITICAL
        relay.value(1) # Cut power
        red.value(1)
        buzzer.value(1)
        yellow.value(0)
        print("STOP! OVERTURN IMMINENT")
    elif roll > 25:
        # WARNING
        relay.value(0)
        red.value(0)
        yellow.value(1)
        # Pulse buzzer
        buzzer.value(1); utime.sleep_ms(100); buzzer.value(0)
    else:
        # SAFE
        relay.value(0)
        red.value(0)
        yellow.value(0)
        buzzer.value(0)
        
    utime.sleep_ms(200)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Rock" Spike**: A momentary bump on a flat road can look like a 90-degree tilt. Always use a time-based confirm (e.g., must be > limit for 1 second).
*   **Relay Direction**: If your machine only runs when the relay is "OFF," make sure the `kill_limit` logic flips the state correctly.
*   **Angle Confusion**: `atan2(y, z)` is for **Roll**. `atan2(x, z)` is for **Pitch**. For a tractor, checking *both* is best!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual-Axis Safety**: Can you merge the logic to trigger the alarm if **Either** Roll > 40 **OR** Pitch > 40 (going up/down a hill too steep)?

---

## 1. Project 1427: Advanced Digital Spirit Level Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Digital Seismograph"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an earthquake/vibration logging system that monitors the Z-axis for high-frequency accelerations, saving "Event Snapshots" (High-speed bursts of data) to the Pico's flash memory whenever a significant vibration is detected.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Seismic Wave Physics**: Earthquakes produce vibrations in multiple axes. However, a local vibration (someone jumping, a car passing) is most visible in the **Z-axis** (Vertical). You will learn to isolate this axis to detect local "Shocks."
*   **Dynamic Baseline**: Gravity is always 1.0G. But if the floor is slightly sloped, your Z-reading might be 0.98. You will learn to calculate a "Moving Baseline" so your trigger ignores the slope and only reacts to the **Change** in vibration.
*   **Event-Based Snapshots**: Instead of logging everything 24/7 (which would fill the memory in minutes), you will learn to use a "Trigger." The system only opens the file and starts writing when a threshold is breached.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MPU6050 IMU Module**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **MPU6050 SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data |
| **MPU6050 SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock |
| **GND** | GND | Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **System & Storage**, drag `write_to_file`.
*   From **Math**, drag `absolute_value` and `average_of_list`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_greater_than`.

### 7. Variables
*   **z_reading**: Number (Current Z acceleration).
*   **z_avg**: Number (The moving baseline).
*   **vibe_delta**: Number (The current shock intensity).
*   **log_active**: Boolean (Flag to start/stop the snapshot).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Establishing the Baseline**
1.  Read the Z-axis 50 times and calculate the average. Store this in `z_avg`.
2.  In your main loop, continuously read the **z_reading**.
3.  Calculate `vibe_delta = abs(z_reading - z_avg)`.

**Phase B: The Trigger Logic**
4.  Monitor `vibe_delta`. If it exceeds `0.4`G:
    *   Set `log_active` to **True**.
    *   Start a timer for `5 seconds`.

**Phase C: Writing the Snapshot**
5.  While `log_active` is True:
    *   Read the Z-axis as fast as possible (no wait block).
    *   Append the reading to a file named `seismic_event.txt`.
    *   Once 5 seconds have passed, set `log_active` to **False** and "Close" the file.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico is mounted on a wall. It measures a steady baseline of 0.98G.
2.  **Interaction**: Someone slams a door or a minor earthquake occurs.
3.  **Process**: The Z-axis jumps to 1.5G. `vibe_delta` becomes `0.52`.
4.  **Action**: The threshold is breached. The "Recording" light turns on.
5.  **Output**: For the next 5 seconds, every micro-vibration is saved to the internal flash memory.
6.  **Result**: The user can now transfer the file to a PC and see the exact "Signature" of the vibration.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Init IMU
imu = MPU6050(i2c)

# Phase A: Calibration
readings = []
for _ in range(50):
    readings.append(imu.get_accel()['z'])
    utime.sleep_ms(10)
    
z_avg = sum(readings) / len(readings)
print(f"Base Z: {z_avg}")

while True:
    z_now = imu.get_accel()['z']
    delta = abs(z_now - z_avg)
    
    # Phase B: Trigger (Threshold 0.4G)
    if delta > 0.4:
        print("VIBE DETECTED! Logging burst...")
        # Phase C: 5s Snapshot
        end_time = utime.ticks_add(utime.ticks_ms(), 5000)
        
        with open("quakes.csv", "a") as f:
            while utime.ticks_diff(end_time, utime.ticks_ms()) > 0:
                val = imu.get_accel()['z']
                f.write(f"{val}\n")
                # Rapid sampling (no sleep)
                
        print("Logging Complete.")
        utime.sleep(2) # Cooldown to avoid double-trigger
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Slow" Logger**: If you use `print()` or slow OLED commands *during* the loop in Phase C, you will miss the vibration spikes. Keep the logging loop "Lean and Mean."
*   **Baseline Drift**: Temperature changes can make the IMU baseline shift over hours. Consider re-calculating the `z_avg` every 10 minutes.
*   **Storage Exhaustion**: A single 5-second burst at high speed can use multiple kilobytes. If your seismograph triggers 100 times, you might run out of space! Add a check for file size.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Richter Intensity**: Based on the `max_delta` measured during the 5 seconds, can you print a message: "Micro" (< 0.5), "Minor" (0.5-1.0), or "Major" (> 1.0)?

## 1. Project 1428: Smart Advanced Digital Spirit Level System

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Wireless Inspection Hub"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an advanced telemetry system where one Pico (the Sensor Node) sends its real-time tilt data wirelessly to another Pico (the Display Hub), enabling you to level large surfaces or monitor machine stability from a safe distance.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Remote Telemetry**: This is the science of measuring something at Point A and seeing the result at Point B. You will learn why low-latency transmission is critical for a spirit level (so the display feels "Real-time").
*   **Serialized Data Strings**: To send data over a wire or air, we must convert numbers into strings. You will learn to format data into packets (e.g., `ROLL:15.2,PITCH:-2.1`) that can be easily parsed.
*   **Master/Slave Architecture**: One Pico is the "Master" (requesting or coordinating data) and the other is the "Slave" or "Node" (providing the raw sensor data).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **2 x Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MPU6050 IMU** (For Node 1)
*   **0.96" SSD1306 OLED** (For Node 2)
*   **2 x HC-05 Bluetooth or NRF24L01 Modules** (For wireless)
*   *Alternatively: Use a long 3-wire UART cable for simulation.*

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Type | Node 1 (Sensor) | Node 2 (Display) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **I2C** | MPU6050 (GP4/5) | OLED (GP4/5) |
| **UART TX** | GP0 (To RX of Hub) | GP0 (To RX of Node) |
| **UART RX** | GP1 (To TX of Hub) | GP1 (To TX of Node) |
| **GND** | Shared Ground | Shared Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Communication**, drag `uart_write_text` and `uart_read_line`.
*   From **Text**, drag `create_text_with` and `split_text`.
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `clear_display` and `draw_text`.

### 7. Variables
*   **transmit_msg**: Text (The formatted data packet).
*   **received_raw**: Text (The string coming in via UART).
*   **remote_roll**: Number (Parsed from string).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: The Sender (Node 1)**
1.  Initialize the IMU on I2C.
2.  In the loop: Read the **roll** and **pitch**.
3.  Format the string: `Set transmit_msg to ROLL: [roll]`.
4.  Write to UART: `uart_write(transmit_msg + newline)`.

**Phase B: The Receiver (Node 2)**
5.  Initialize the OLED on I2C.
6.  In the loop: `If uart_available()`:
    *   Set `received_raw` to `uart_read_line()`.
7.  Split the string at the `:` symbol.
8.  Take the second part (the number) and convert it to a Number variable `remote_roll`.

**Phase C: Remote Visualization**
9.  Clear the OLED.
10. Draw a "Remote Level" UI using the `remote_roll`. If the number is > 0, draw an arrow pointing Right; if < 0, draw an arrow pointing Left.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Both Picos power up. The Hub OLED shows "Waiting for Data...".
2.  **Interaction**: The user tilts the Sensor Node 10 degrees.
3.  **Process**: Node 1 sends the string `"10.0\n"` across the UART link.
4.  **Action**: Node 2 catches the string, strips the line ending, and turns it back into the number `10.0`.
5.  **Output**: The Hub OLED updates instantly to show a 10-degree tilt.
6.  **Result**: The user can level their camper van or boat while standing outside watching the Remote Display.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
# --- SENDER CODE (NODE 1) ---
import machine
import utime

uart = machine.UART(0, baudrate=9600, tx=machine.Pin(0), rx=machine.Pin(1))
imu = MPU6050(...)

while True:
    roll = get_roll()
    packet = f"{roll:.1f}\n"
    uart.write(packet)
    utime.sleep_ms(100)

# --- RECEIVER CODE (HUB) ---
import machine
import ssd1306

uart = machine.UART(0, baudrate=9600, tx=machine.Pin(0), rx=machine.Pin(1))
oled = ...

while True:
    if uart.any():
        data = uart.readline().decode().strip()
        try:
            val = float(data)
            oled.fill(0)
            oled.text(f"REMOTE TILT:", 0, 0)
            oled.text(f"{val} deg", 0, 20)
            oled.show()
        except:
            pass
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Missing Newline**: If the sender doesn't send `\n` (newline), the receiver's `readline()` function will wait forever, making the UI freeze.
*   **Baud Mismatch**: If one Pico is set to 9600 and the other to 115200, you will receive "Garbage" characters (square boxes and symbols).
*   **The "Buffer Backlog"**: If the sender sends data faster than the receiver can draw it on the OLED, the Hub will fall behind, showing "Old" tilt data. Keep the update rates balanced (e.g., both at 10Hz).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Bidirectional Control**: Can the Hub send a "TARO" command back to the Sensor to remotely zero-out the calibration?

## 1. Project 1429: Advanced Digital Spirit Level Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Zero-Point Calibration"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a persistent calibration system that calculates the accelerometer offsets on a flat surface and saves these "Zero Points" to the Pico's internal flash memory, ensuring the level remains perfectly accurate even after being powered off.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Sensor Offsets**: No sensor is perfect. Even on a perfectly level surface, an IMU might report `0.02` for the X-axis. This is the "Offset." To fix it, we subtract this value from every future reading.
*   **Non-Volatile Storage (NVM)**: Variables in RAM disappear when the Pico loses power. To make settings "Stick," we must write them to the internal Flash memory as a file (e.g., `cal.txt`).
*   **The "Hold-to-Zero" Pattern**: A common User Interface pattern where holding a button for a few seconds triggers a specific "Expert Mode" or "Setup Routine."

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MPU6050 IMU**
*   **Push Button**
*   **0.96" SSD1306 OLED**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Type | Component | Pico Pins |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **I2C Bus** | OLED, MPU6050 | GP4 (SDA), GP5 (SCL) |
| **Digital In**| Zero Button | GP16 |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **System & Storage**, drag `write_to_file` and `read_from_file`.
*   From **Math**, drag `average_of_list` and `subtraction`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_button_held_for`.

### 7. Variables
*   **raw_x / raw_y**: Number (Uncalibrated data).
*   **offset_x / offset_y**: Number (Stored calibration data).
*   **cal_file**: Text ("calibration.dat").

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Loading Stored Cal**
1.  At start-up, check: `If file 'calibration.dat' exists`:
    *   Read the contents and set `offset_x` and `offset_y` from the file.
2.  Otherwise, set offsets to `0`.

**Phase B: The Calibration Routine**
3.  If the button at `GP16` is held for 3 seconds:
    *   Display "Calibrating... DO NOT MOVE" on the OLED.
    *   Take 100 samples of the current X and Y axes.
    *   Calculate the Average.
    *   Save these new averages back to `calibration.dat`.
    *   Update the `offset` variables.

**Phase C: Applying the Offset**
4.  In the main loop, calculate the "Corrected" tilt:
    *   `corrected_x = raw_x - offset_x`.
5.  Display the `corrected_x` on the OLED. It should now show exactly `0.00` on your flat surface.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico boots and loads `offset_x = 0.05` from the file.
2.  **Process**: It reads `0.05` from the sensor, subtracts `0.05`, and displays `0.00`.
3.  **Interaction**: The user decides to re-calibrate. They place the level on a fresh surface and hold the button.
4.  **Action**: The Pico averages 100 readings (finding a new error of `0.07`).
5.  **Output**: It writes `0.07` into the file and updates the variable.
6.  **Result**: The level is now perfectly calibrated to the new surface profile.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import os
import json

# Hardware
btn = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
imu = MPU6050(...)
cal_data = {"x": 0.0, "y": 0.0}

# Phase A: Load
if "cal.json" in os.listdir():
    with open("cal.json", "r") as f:
        cal_data = json.load(f)

def run_calibration():
    print("Calibrating...")
    sum_x = 0
    for _ in range(100):
        sum_x += imu.get_accel()['x']
        utime.sleep_ms(10)
    
    cal_data["x"] = sum_x / 100
    # Phase B: Save
    with open("cal.json", "w") as f:
        json.dump(cal_data, f)
    print("Done!")

while True:
    # Check for Long Press
    if btn.value() == 0:
        start = utime.ticks_ms()
        while btn.value() == 0:
            if utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), start) > 3000:
                run_calibration()
                break
    
    # Phase C: Corrected Read
    raw_x = imu.get_accel()['x']
    level_x = raw_x - cal_data["x"]
    
    print(f"Level X: {level_x:.2f}")
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Unstable Calibration**: Trying to calibrate while the table is shaking or someone is walking nearby. This introduces "Noise" into your zero point.
*   **File Opening Errors**: If you open the file for writing and then the Pico loses power halfway through, the file might become corrupt. Always read the file safely at boot using a `try: except:` block.
*   **Forgetting to Apply**: Calculating the offsets but never subtracting them from the raw IMU data in the main loop.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Factory Reset**: If the calibration is so bad the user wants to start over, can you make a "Double Click" of the button delete the calibration file?

## 1. Project 1430: Advanced Digital Spirit Level Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Multi-Mode Master Level"
At the end of this project, you will be able to synchronize all previous modules into a final professional prototype that features an OLED dashboard, gravity-aware UI, a "Man-Down" safety alarm, and persistent calibration, all accessible via an interactive menu system.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **System Refactoring**: As code grows, it becomes harder to read. You will learn to use **Functions** to organize your code: `def check_safety()`, `def update_ui()`, `def handle_calibration()`.
*   **Menu-Driven Navigation**: You will implement a simple state machine (`mode = 0, 1, 2`) that allows a single button to switch between the "Leveling Mode," "G-Force Mode," and "Seismograph Mode."
*   **User Feedback Loops**: Using the Buzzer and OLED icons to provide "Living" feedback. (e.g., A checkmark appears when perfectly level, a beep sounds when a peak G is hit).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MPU6050 6-Axis IMU**
*   **0.96" SSD1306 OLED**
*   **Active Buzzer**
*   **Push Button**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Type | Component | Pico Pins |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **I2C Bus** | OLED, MPU6050 | GP4 (SDA), GP5 (SCL) |
| **Digital In**| Mode Button | GP16 |
| **Digital Out**| Alarm Buzzer | GP17 |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else_if` (the master mode selector).
*   From **Functions**, create a block for each system mode.

### 7. Variables
*   **mode**: Number (0=Bubble, 1=G-Force, 2=Seismo).
*   **is_level**: Boolean (Center accuracy check).
*   **vibe_peak**: Number (Highest vibration seen).
*   **last_btn_time**: Number (For debounce).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: The Mode Selector**
1.  Initialize all I2C sensors and the Button at `GP16`.
2.  If the button is clicked:
    *   Increment `mode` by 1.
    *   If `mode > 2`: Set `mode = 0`.
    *   Play a short "Mode Change" beep.

**Phase B: Parallel Safety Task**
3.  Regardless of the current UI mode, the "Dead-Man" safety logic from Project 1423 must run in the background.
4.  If the device is stationary for too long, trigger the buzzer alert, overriding the screen with a "STILLNESS ALERT" message.

**Phase C: Implementing the UIs**
5.  **Mode 0 (Spirit Level)**: Draw the gravity-aware bubble UI from Project 1422. If the tilt is < 0.1 degree, turn on the "Level" LED (or draw a star).
6.  **Mode 1 (G-Meter)**: Draw the bar graph from Project 1424.
7.  **Mode 2 (Seismo)**: Show a live Z-axis wave and the number of "Tremors" detected.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The OLED shows a splash screen, then reveals the bubble level.
2.  **Interaction**: The user clicks the button. The screen wipes and shows the G-Meter.
3.  **Process**: The Picos constantly monitors for immobility while calculating G-forces.
4.  **Interaction**: The user hold the button for 3s (from Project 1429); the system enters Calibration mode.
5.  **Output**: The user levels their project, confident that the tool is both accurate and safe.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Master Loop
mode = 0
last_motion = utime.ticks_ms()

while True:
    now = utime.ticks_ms()
    acc = imu.get_accel()
    
    # 1. Background Safety (Project 1423)
    if is_moving(acc): last_motion = now
    
    if utime.ticks_diff(now, last_motion) > 30000:
        trigger_alarm_state()
        continue
    
    # 2. Mode Input (Click to change)
    if btn_clicked():
        mode = (mode + 1) % 3
        
    # 3. Main UI Branching
    if mode == 0:
        draw_bubble_ui(acc) # Project 1422
    elif mode == 1:
        draw_gforce_ui(acc) # Project 1424
    elif mode == 2:
        draw_seismo_ui(acc) # Project 1427
        
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Task Interweaving**: If the `draw_seismo_ui` function takes 500ms to run, the "Dead-Man" timer might glitch. Ensure all UI functions are fast.
*   **Menu Wrap-around**: Forgetting to reset the `mode` variable to 0 when it exceeds the number of available screens.
*   **State Conflict**: Trying to use the Buzzer for "Mode Change Beeps" while it is also screaming for the "Man-Down" alarm. Priority logic is needed for the buzzer!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Battery Saver**: If no button is pressed and no motion for 5 minutes, turn off the OLED and put the Pico into a "Sleep" state until shaken.
