## Batch 144: Advanced Ultrasonic Distance 5 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1431: Advanced Ultrasonic Distance Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Rock-Solid Signal"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement advanced signal processing for the HC-SR04 ultrasonic sensor, using a Median Filter to discard acoustic artifacts and outlier "glitches," resulting in a distance measurement stable enough for high-precision engineering.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Ultrasonic Physics**: Ultrasonic sensors work by bouncing sound waves off objects. However, soft surfaces (curtains) or angled walls can cause "Multipath Interference," where the sound reflects multiple times, creating outlier data points (glitches).
*   **Signal Outliers**: A "Glitch" is a reading that is mathematically impossible (e.g., measuring 10cm, 10cm, 300cm, 11cm). If you average these, the result is ruined.
*   **Median Filtering**: Unlike an "Average" (Mean), which is pulled towards the outliers, a "Median" filter sorts the data and picks the middle value. This effectively deletes the glitch as if it never happened.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Distance Sensor**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **HC-SR04 VCC** | VBUS (5V) | Sensor requires 5V for range |
| **HC-SR04 Trig**| GP14 | Output Trigger |
| **HC-SR04 Echo**| GP15 | Input Echo (5V to 3.3V Divider recommended) |
| **GND** | GND | Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `ultrasonic_read`.
*   From **Lists**, drag `create_empty_list`, `add_to_list`, and `sort_list`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `repeat_times`.

### 7. Variables
*   **samples**: List (Storage for raw readings).
*   **median_dist**: Number (The filtered result).
*   **num_reads**: Number (Set to 5 or 7).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Burst Sampling**
1.  Initialize the sensor on `GP14/15`.
2.  Clear the `samples` list.
3.  Use a `repeat 5 times` loop:
    *   Read the distance and `add_to_list`.
    *   Wait `20ms` between reads to let acoustic echoes die down.

**Phase B: Sorting for Centrality**
4.  Use the `sort samples` block.
5.  **Example**: If your list was `[12, 11, 250, 12, 13]`, it becomes `[11, 12, 12, 13, 250]`.

**Phase C: The Outlier Discard**
6.  Extract the item at index `2` (the middle) and set it to `median_dist`.
7.  Display this value on the OLED or Serial Monitor.
8.  **Observation**: Wave your hand in front of the sensor. Notice how even if a stray echo occurs, the number stays perfectly calm.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The system prepares to take a measurement.
2.  **Process**: 5 sound pulses are sent. One hits an angled chair leg and returns a "fake" 3-meter reading.
3.  **Math**: The Pico sorts the 5 results: `15.1, 15.2, 15.2, 15.4, 300.0`.
4.  **Action**: It ignores the 300.0 and the lower two. It picks `15.2`.
5.  **Output**: The terminal shows a steady "15.2 cm".
6.  **Result**: The "glitch" (300.0) is mathematically deleted before it ever reaches the user.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Sensor Setup
sonar = Sonar(trig=14, echo=15)

def get_median_distance():
    readings = []
    
    # Phase A: Burst
    for _ in range(5):
        d = sonar.read()
        if d > 0: # Ignore timeout errors
            readings.append(d)
        utime.sleep_ms(20)
        
    # Phase B: Sort
    if len(readings) > 0:
        readings.sort()
        # Phase C: Pick Middle
        return readings[len(readings) // 2]
    return 0

while True:
    stable_val = get_median_distance()
    print(f"Filtered Distance: {stable_val:.1f} cm")
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Averaging Outliers**: If you use `(10+10+300)/3`, you get `106`. Your robot will think the wall is a meter away when it is only 10cm away! Always use Median for ultrasonic sensors.
*   **Sampling Rate**: If you don't wait `20ms` between pings, the sound fromPing 1 might bounce off a far wall and return while you are listening for Ping 2. This is called "Ghost Echoing."
*   **Empty Lists**: If the sensor times out 5 times in a row (pointing at the sky), your list will be empty. Sorting an empty list will crash the Pico. Add an `if list_length > 0` check.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **The Signal Confidence**: Can you calculate the **Standard Deviation**? If the 5 readings are very spread out, maybe the data is untrustworthy? Light a red LED if the sensor is "Confused."

---

## 1. Project 1432: Advanced Ultrasonic Distance Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Reverse Parking Assistant"
At the end of this project, you will be able to create a professional reverse parking assistant that uses a non-blocking variable beeper, where the beep frequency increases proportionally as you approach an obstacle, culminating in a solid "Collision" tone.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Auditory Spatial Feedback**: Our brains are very good at judging speed based on sound frequency. By changing the delay between beeps, we can "tell" the driver exactly how close they are to a wall without them needing to look at a screen.
*   **Non-Blocking Logic**: In previous projects, you used `wait`. But if you `wait 1 second` after a beep, you can't read the sensor! You will learn to use `ticks_diff` to handle beeps while the sensor keeps running.
*   **Saturated State**: In car parking systems, when you get extremely close (e.g., < 10cm), the beep becomes a continuous "Solid" tone. This indicates "STOP" rather than "CLOSER."

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **Active Buzzer**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **HC-SR04** | GP14 (Trig), GP15 (Echo) | Distance |
| **Buzzer (+)** | GP18 | Audio Alert |
| **GND** | GND | Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms` and `pico_ticks_diff`.
*   From **Math**, drag `map_value` and `if_less_than`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output`.

### 7. Variables
*   **dist**: Number (Distance from wall).
*   **beep_interval**: Number (Delay in ms between beeps).
*   **last_beep_ticks**: Number (Timer variable).
*   **is_beeping**: Boolean (Flag for beep state).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Distance-to-Interval Mapping**
1.  Read the current **dist** from the sensor.
2.  Use a `map_value` block:
    *   Map `10cm to 100cm` -> `50ms to 1000ms`.
3.  Set this to `beep_interval`. (At 1 meter, it beeps every second. At 10cm, it beeps 20 times per second).

**Phase B: Non-Blocking Pulse Logic**
4.  Check: `If current_ticks - last_beep_ticks > beep_interval`:
    *   Turn on the Buzzer.
    *   Wait exactly `20ms` (short chirp).
    *   Turn off the Buzzer.
    *   Set `last_beep_ticks` to `current_ticks`.

**Phase C: The Solid Tone Transition**
5.  If `dist < 10cm`:
    *   Set `Buzzer` to `ON` constantly.
6.  Else If `dist > 150cm`:
    *   Keep `Buzzer` `OFF` (Silent zone).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The system is active. Distance is 200cm. Silence.
2.  **Interaction**: The user moves a box slowly towards the sensor.
3.  **Process**: At 100cm, the first "BEEP" occurs.
4.  **Action**: As the box moves closer, the speed of the beeps increases: `BEEP... BEEP... BEEP.BEEP.BEEP`.
5.  **Output**: At 9.5cm, the beeping stops and becomes a high-pitched continuous whine.
6.  **Result**: The user knows exactly when to stop without looking at a screen!

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Init
sonar = Sonar(14, 15)
buzz = machine.Pin(18, machine.Pin.OUT)
last_beep = 0

while True:
    d = sonar.read()
    now = utime.ticks_ms()
    
    if d < 10:
        # Phase C: Critical Zone (Solid)
        buzz.value(1)
    elif 10 <= d <= 100:
        # Phase A & B: Warning Zone (Variable)
        # Map 10-100 to 50ms-1000ms
        interval = ((d - 10) / 90) * 950 + 50
        
        if utime.ticks_diff(now, last_beep) > interval:
            buzz.value(1)
            utime.sleep_ms(30) # Short Chirp
            buzz.value(0)
            last_beep = now
    else:
        # Safe Zone
        buzz.value(0)
        
    utime.sleep_ms(10) # Small loop sleep
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Wait Trap**: If you use `time.sleep(1)` for a 1-second beep interval, the sensor isn't checked during that second. If you move fast, you could crash into the wall during the sleep!
*   **Linear Scale**: Sometimes 10-100cm feels too linear. You can use a `square` block on the distance to make the beeps get "Very Fast" only when you are extremely close.
*   **Inverted Logic**: Mapping 10cm to 1000ms and 100cm to 50ms. This would make the beeps *slower* as you get closer!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Visual Guidance**: Can you add an RGB LED that is Green at 1m, Yellow at 40cm, and Red at 10cm?

## 1. Project 1433: Advanced Ultrasonic Distance Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Differential Crossover"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement multi-sensor validation logic, using two ultrasonic sensors facing each other to measure an object's position and verifying the result against a known physical distance (Geometric Validation).

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Differential Measurement**: By using two sensors pointing at each other from a fixed distance (e.g., 100cm apart), we can pinpoint the location of an object between them. If Sensor A sees the object at 30cm and Sensor B sees it at 70cm, we know the reading is accurate.
*   **Geometric Validation**: This is a way of "Self-Correction." If `Dist_A + Dist_B` does not equal `Total_Distance` (100cm), the system knows there is an error (e.g., an echo or a ghosting effect).
*   **Acoustic Cross-Talk**: When using two ultrasonic sensors, Sensor B might "Hear" the ping from Sensor A. This causes total data failure. You will learn to stagger the pings carefully.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2 x HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensors**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Type | Sensor A Pins | Sensor B Pins |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Trig** | GP14 | GP16 |
| **Echo** | GP15 | GP17 |
| **Power**| VBUS (5V) | VBUS (5V) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `sum`, `subtraction`, and `absolute_value`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_less_than`.
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `ultrasonic_read`.

### 7. Variables
*   **dist_a / dist_b**: Number (Readings from sensors).
*   **frame_width**: Number (Set to 100cm).
*   **error**: Number (The difference from 100cm).
*   **is_valid**: Boolean (Truth check).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Staggered Pinging**
1.  Read **dist_a** from `GP14/15`.
2.  Wait `50ms` (Essential! Let the sound die out so Sensor B doesn't hear it).
3.  Read **dist_b** from `GP16/17`.

**Phase B: Summation & Validation**
4.  Calculate `total_calc = dist_a + dist_b`.
5.  Calculate the `error = abs(total_calc - frame_width)`.
6.  If `error < 2.0` (Allowing for 2cm of sensor variance):
    *   Set `is_valid` to **True**.
7.  Else:
    *   Set `is_valid` to **False**. (The reading is wrong or an object is too thin).

**Phase C: Displaying Position**
8.  If `is_valid`:
    *   Show "Position: [dist_a] cm" on the OLED.
9.  Else:
    *   Show "SIGNAL CONFUSED" (to avoid showing fake data).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Two sensors are mounted on a 1-meter rack facing inward.
2.  **Interaction**: A hand is placed between the sensors.
3.  **Process**: Sensor A reads 45cm. Sensor B reads 54cm.
4.  **Math**: `45 + 54 = 99`. Error is `1cm`. 
5.  **Action**: Since 1cm < 2cm threshold, the data is marked TRUE.
6.  **Output**: "Position: 45cm" (Measured from the left wall).
7.  **Error Case**: Someone holds a mirror at an angle. A reads 45, B reads 500 (echo loss). `45 + 500 = 545`. Data REJECTED.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Init
sonar_left = Sonar(14, 15)
sonar_right = Sonar(16, 17)
FRAME_WIDTH = 100.0

while True:
    # Phase A: Staggered pings
    d_left = sonar_left.read()
    utime.sleep_ms(50) 
    d_right = sonar_right.read()
    
    # Phase B: Validation
    total_measured = d_left + d_right
    error = abs(total_measured - FRAME_WIDTH)
    
    if error < 2.0:
        # Phase C: Output
        print(f"Object at: {d_left:.1f} cm (Validated)")
    else:
        print("ERROR: Inconsistent data (Ghost echo?)")
        
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Parallel Pinging**: Trying to read both sensors at the exact same time. One sensor's pulse will travel all the way across the frame and trigger the other sensor's Echo pin instantly. You MUST wait between pings.
*   **Alignment**: If the two sensors aren't perfectly parallel, they will "miss" the object or measure the slant distance.
*   **Width Calibration**: If your `FRAME_WIDTH` in code is 100cm but the physical rack is 102cm, you will always get a persistent error.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Width Measure**: If you place a thick box in the middle, can you use `FRAME_WIDTH - (d_left + d_right)` to calculate the **Width of the Box** itself?

## 1. Project 1434: Advanced Ultrasonic Distance Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Room Occupancy Counter"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an intelligent room occupancy monitor using two ultrasonic sensors (entry and exit "beams") to detect the direction of movement, counting people as they enter or leave a space.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Sequence-Based Logic**: Direction cannot be determined by a single sensor. By checking *which* sensor was triggered first (Sensor A then B = Entry, Sensor B then A = Exit), we can determine vector motion.
*   **Beam Breaking Thresholds**: Instead of measuring distance, we treat the ultrasonic sensor as a "Tripwire." If the distance suddenly drops from 100cm (empty doorway) to 30cm, we know the "Beam" is broken.
*   **State Machine Management**: You will learn to use a `state` variable to track where a person is in the doorway (e.g., State 1: Entering A, State 2: Inside Both, State 3: Leaving B).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2 x HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensors**
*   **0.96" SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Type | Sensor A (GP) | Sensor B (GP) | OLED |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Trig** | 14 | 16 | - |
| **Echo** | 15 | 17 | - |
| **I2C SDA**| - | - | 4 |
| **I2C SCL**| - | - | 5 |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else_if` and `if_and_else`.
*   From **Math**, drag `increment` and `decrement`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`.

### 7. Variables
*   **occupancy**: Number (People currently in room).
*   **beam_a / beam_b**: Boolean (True if someone is blocking).
*   **doorway_state**: Text ("OPEN", "STEP_A", "STEP_B", "MIDWAY").

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Defining the Beam**
1.  Initialize both sensors. 
2.  Set a `threshold = 50`. (If reading < 50cm, someone is in the doorway).
3.  Continuously update `beam_a` and `beam_b` based on this threshold.

**Phase B: Entry Logic**
4.  If `beam_a` is broken and `beam_b` is clear:
    *   Set `doorway_state` to "ENTERING".
5.  If `beam_a` is broken AND `beam_b` is then broken:
    *   The person is now in the middle of the door.
6.  If `beam_a` becomes CLEAR and `beam_b` is still BROKEN:
    *   The person is almost through!
7.  If both are CLEAR and the previous state was "ENTERING":
    *   `increment occupancy`. Play a "Ding" sound.

**Phase C: Exit Logic**
8.  Repeat the logic in reverse (B triggered first) to `decrement occupancy`.
9.  Update the OLED with the message: "People in Room: [occupancy]".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Room is empty. OLED shows "0".
2.  **Interaction**: A person walks through the door from left (A) to right (B).
3.  **Process**: Sensor A spikes (Beam broken). System flags "Potential Entry".
4.  **Process**: Sensor B spikes while A is still blocked. System flags "Confirmed Entry".
5.  **Action**: Person leaves active area. `occupancy` becomes `1`.
6.  **Result**: The counts are accurate even if someone just sticks their head in and pulls back (as long as they don't hit the second beam).

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Sensors
s_a = Sonar(14, 15)
s_b = Sonar(16, 17)
count = 0
last_triggered = "" # A or B

while True:
    d1 = s_a.read()
    d2 = s_b.read()
    
    # Simple Sequence Logic
    if d1 < 50 and last_triggered == "":
        last_triggered = "A" # Someone coming from A
    elif d2 < 50 and last_triggered == "":
        last_triggered = "B" # Someone coming from B
        
    # Resolution
    if d1 > 80 and d2 > 80: # Person has cleared both
        if last_triggered == "A":
            count += 1
            print("Entered. Count:", count)
        elif last_triggered == "B":
            count -= 1
            print("Exited. Count:", count)
            
        last_triggered = "" # Reset for next person
        
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Shoulder-to-Shoulder**: If two people walk in very close together, the sensors will see them as one big object. 
*   **Threshold Height**: Mounting the sensors at knee-height might see two legs as two separate people. Mount them at chest/shoulder height for accuracy.
*   **Cross-Talk**: Ensure the sensors are pointing parallel into the door, not at each other (refer to Project 1433 for more on cross-talk).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Directional Lights**: Light a Green LED for entries and a Red LED for exits.
*   **Anti-Tailgating**: If the beam stays broken for > 3 seconds, trigger an alert for "Crowded Doorway."

## 1. Project 1435: Automated Advanced Ultrasonic Distance Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Socially Distanced Robot"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a Proportional Follower behavior for a mobile robot, where the machine maintains a perfect "safety bubble" by automatically reversing if you get too close and following you if you step away.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Proportional Control (P)**: This is the "P" in PID control. It means that the speed of the motor is determined by how far away it is from its target. If it's 1cm away, it moves slowly. If it's 50cm away, it moves fast.
*   **The Safety Bubble (Setpoint)**: The distance your robot *wants* to be at. In this project, we aim for 20cm.
*   **Oscillation Control (Deadband)**: Real sensors jitter slightly. If we don't include a "Deadband" (e.g., +/- 2cm), the robot will vibrate back and forth even when "level."

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **Dual Motor Robot Chassis** (with Motor Driver)
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Category |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Sonar Trig/Echo** | GP14 / GP15 | Smart Sensors |
| **Motor A (L)** | GP16, GP17 | Motion & Motors |
| **Motor B (R)** | GP18, GP19 | Motion & Motors |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `subtraction` and `map_value`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_less_than` and `abs`.
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag `drive_forward_speed`.

### 7. Variables
*   **dist**: Number (Current distance).
*   **setpoint**: Number (Ideal distance: 20cm).
*   **error**: Number (Difference).
*   **deadband**: Number (Set to 2 - prevents jitter).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Calculating the Gap**
1.  Read the current **dist** from the sensor.
2.  Calculate `error = dist - setpoint`.
    *   If `error` is POSITIVE: Robot is too far (Follow!).
    *   If `error` is NEGATIVE: Robot is too close (Retreat!).

**Phase B: Deadband Logic**
3.  If `abs(error) < deadband`:
    *   Set motor speed to `0`. (The robot is "Happy").
4.  Otherwise, proceed to drive logic.

**Phase C: Scaling Motor Speed**
5.  Multiply the `error` by a `kP` gain (e.g., `5`). 
    *   Example: If error is 10cm, speed is 50.
6.  Use the `drive` block with the calculated speed.
    *   **Pro Tip**: Ensure you clamp the results between -100 and 100.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The robot is stationary.
2.  **Interaction**: You place your hand 10cm from the robot.
3.  **Process**: `10 - 20 = -10`. Error is negative.
4.  **Math**: Speed = `-10 * 5 = -50`.
5.  **Action**: The robot reverses at 50% speed until the distance hits 20cm.
6.  **Result**: The robot maintains a perfect "Personal Space" without human intervention.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Setup
sonar = Sonar(14, 15)
motors = Robot(16, 17, 18, 19) # Hypothetical robot class
SETPOINT = 20.0
KP = 4.0
DEADBAND = 2.0

while True:
    d = sonar.read()
    error = d - SETPOINT
    
    if abs(error) < DEADBAND:
        motors.stop()
    else:
        # P-Control: Speed proportional to error
        speed = error * KP
        
        # Clamp speed
        if speed > 100: speed = 100
        if speed < -100: speed = -100
        
        motors.drive(speed) # Positive = Fwd, Negative = Rev
        
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Hysteresis/Jitter**: Without the `deadband`, the robot will "hunt" for 20.000...cm and never stop moving.
*   **The Runaway**: If your `KP` is too high, the robot will accelerate so fast it slams into you before it can react. Start with a low gain (e.g., 2).
*   **Sensor Blindness**: If you get closer than 2cm, the HC-SR04 can't "see" and might report 0 or 400. This could cause the robot to suddenly lunge forward!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dynamic Setpoint**: Use a Potentiometer to change the "Safety Bubble" size in real-time.
*   **Wall Following**: Can you mount the sensor on the side and make the robot maintain a constant distance from a wall while driving?

## 1. Project 1436: Advanced Ultrasonic Distance Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Acoustic SawStop"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a high-speed industrial safety trigger (similar to SawStop technology) that uses ultrasonic monitoring to detect a human hand entering a hazardous "No-Go" zone and instantly cuts power to machinery via a relay.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Safety-Critical Systems**: These are systems where failure could lead to injury. You will learn to prioritize the "Safety Loop" over all other tasks (like updating a screen).
*   **Relay Power Interruption**: A relay acts as an electrically controlled switch. By putting the motor's power through the "Normally Open" (NO) terminal, we can ensure that if the Pico detects a hazard, it can physically disconnect the power.
*   **Deterministic Latency**: For safety, the time between "Detection" and "Action" must be as short as possible. You will learn to optimize your code to minimize this latency.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Distance Sensor**
*   **Relay Module** (5V Compatible)
*   **DC Motor** (Simulating a blade or drill)
*   **Red LED and Active Buzzer**
*   **Reset Button**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Sonar Trig/Echo** | GP14 / GP15 | Sensor |
| **Relay Signal** | GP18 | Controls Power |
| **Warning LED** | GP19 | Visual Alert |
| **Reset Button** | GP20 | Re-arms the system |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_less_than`.
*   From **Control**, drag `stop_program` or `exit_loop`.

### 7. Variables
*   **safe_limit**: Number (Hazard boundary, e.g., 15cm).
*   **hazard_detected**: Boolean (Trigger state).
*   **system_armed**: Boolean (Readiness state).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: The Armed State**
1.  Initialize the Relay at `GP18`. On startup, it should be **OFF** for safety.
2.  Wait for the user to press the **Reset Button**. Once pressed, turn the Relay **ON** (machine starts) and set `system_armed` to **True**.

**Phase B: High-Speed Monitoring**
3.  Enter a `while system_armed` loop.
4.  Continuously read the distance. 
5.  If `dist < safe_limit` (e.g., 15cm):
    *   **Instantly** turn the Relay **OFF**.
    *   Turn on the Red LED and the Buzzer.
    *   Set `system_armed` to **False**.

**Phase C: The Lockout Pattern**
6.  Once triggered, the machine cannot start again automatically.
7.  The code must exit the monitoring loop and wait for another manual **Reset Button** press.
8.  **Pro Tip**: Display "DANGER: LOCKOUT" on the serial monitor.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: System is idle. Red LED is on. Motor is off.
2.  **Interaction**: User presses Reset. Motor spins. 
3.  **Process**: The Pico pings the area in front of the "blade" 50 times per second.
4.  **Action**: A hand gets within 12cm.
5.  **Output**: The relay clicks OFF instantly. The motor coasts to a stop.
6.  **Result**: An accident is prevented by electronic intervention.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Pins
relay = machine.Pin(18, machine.Pin.OUT)
alert = machine.Pin(19, machine.Pin.OUT)
btn = machine.Pin(20, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_DOWN)
sonar = Sonar(14, 15)

armed = False
LIMIT = 15.0

while True:
    # Wait for re-arm
    if not armed:
        if btn.value():
            relay.value(1) # Machine power ON
            alert.value(0)
            armed = True
            print("System Armed. Running...")
            utime.sleep(0.5)
            
    # Monitoring Loop
    while armed:
        d = sonar.read()
        if 0 < d < LIMIT:
            relay.value(0) # KILL POWER
            alert.value(1)
            armed = False
            print("!!! EMERGENCY STOP TRIGGERED !!!")
        utime.sleep_ms(10) # High frequency check
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Auto-Restart**: Making the motor start again the moment the hand is removed. This is incredibly dangerous in real machines. Always require a physical button press to reset.
*   **Slow Code**: Putting `print()` statements inside the `while armed` loop can slow down the check from 50Hz to 10Hz. Only print during event changes.
*   **Bounce**: The relay might "chatter" if the hand is right on the 15.0cm edge. Use a small buffer (e.g., trip at 15, allow reset only at 20).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Logging the Incident**: Can you record the exact distance and time when the trigger occurred and save it to a file?
*   **Two-Stage Alert**: Sound a quiet "Beep" at 30cm, and only E-Stop at 15cm.

## 1. Project 1437: Advanced Ultrasonic Distance Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Intelligent Traffic Counter"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an overhead traffic counter that uses distance deviations to detect vehicles or pedestrians passing through a gate, logging the timestamps of each event to the Pico's internal flash memory as a CSV file.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Deviation Detection**: Instead of looking for a specific distance, we look for a "Change" from the known floor height. If the floor is 200cm away and the sensor suddenly sees 170cm, something at least 30cm tall has passed underneath.
*   **Event Hysteresis**: A person walking under a sensor might trigger "Broken Beam" multiple times in a millisecond due to their head/shoulders. Hysteresis ensures we wait for the beam to be *completely clear* before counting it as one finished event.
*   **Internal Flash Logging**: You will learn how to open a file (`data.csv`) in "Append" mode, allowing the Pico to remember how many people passed even after the power is turned off.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Distance Sensor**
*   **0.96" SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Category |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Sonar Trig/Echo** | GP14 / GP15 | Smart Sensors |
| **I2C SDA/SCL** | GP4 / GP5 | Smart Display |
| **GND / VCC** | GND / VBUS | Power |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Files**, drag `file_open`, `file_append_line`, and `file_close`.
*   From **Math**, drag `subtraction` and `increment`.
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `show_text`.

### 7. Variables
*   **baseline**: Number (Height of hallway, e.g., 220cm).
*   **total_events**: Number (The count).
*   **is_blocked**: Boolean (State flag).
*   **log_row**: Text (The string to save).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Zeroing the Hallway**
1.  Mount the sensor to the ceiling pointing down.
2.  On boot, take 10 readings and average them to set the `baseline`.
3.  Set `threshold = baseline - 20`. (Anything 20cm taller than the floor is an event).

**Phase B: The Edge Trigger**
4.  Continuously read the distance.
5.  If `dist < threshold` AND `is_blocked` is **False**:
    *   `increment total_events`.
    *   Set `is_blocked` to **True**.
    *   Display "COUNT: [total_events]" on the OLED.
6.  If `dist > baseline - 5` AND `is_blocked` is **True**:
    *   Set `is_blocked` to **False**. (The person has cleared the doorway).

**Phase C: Writing to the Logbook**
7.  Whenever a count occurs, create a string: `Pass #[total_events], [timestamp]`.
8.  Open `log.csv` in **Append** mode.
9.  Write the string and **Close** the file immediately (to ensure data is saved).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico calibrates to the ceiling (2.2m).
2.  **Interaction**: A person walks through.
3.  **Process**: The sensor sees `170cm`. This is less than the `200cm` threshold.
4.  **Math**: `total_events` becomes 1. `is_blocked` becomes True.
5.  **Action**: The Pico writes "Pass #1" to the internal drive.
6.  **Result**: An administrator can plug the Pico into a PC later and download `log.csv` to see the traffic patterns.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import os

# Setup
sonar = Sonar(14, 15)
count = 0
active = False
BASELINE = 220.0 #cm

def log_event(c):
    try:
        with open("traffic.csv", "a") as f:
            f.write(f"Event {c}: {utime.localtime()}\n")
    except:
        print("Logging failed!")

while True:
    d = sonar.read()
    
    # Trigger logic with Hysteresis
    if d < (BASELINE - 20) and not active:
        count += 1
        active = True
        print(f"Person detected! Total: {count}")
        log_event(count)
        
    elif d > (BASELINE - 5) and active:
        active = False # Beam is clear
        
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **File Flood**: If you don't use the `is_blocked` flag, the Pico will try to write to the file 50 times per second while the person is standing under the sensor. This will wear out the flash memory in minutes!
*   **Power Cut**: If you don't use `file_close` (or `with open`), the data is stored in RAM and only saved when the script finishes. If the power is pulled, you lose the whole log.
*   **Noise**: Air currents can cause +/- 2cm jitter. Ensure your threshold is at least 10cm away from the floor height.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Daily Reset**: Can you use an RTC (Project 1416) to automatically reset the count at Midnight?
*   **Peak Time**: Add logic to find which hour of the day had the most traffic.

---

## 1. Project 1438: Smart Advanced Ultrasonic Distance System

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Intelligent Waste Manager"
At the end of this project, you will be able to build an IoT-style bin monitor that measures the "percentage full" of a container using ultrasonic level sensing and transmits the status via UART to a central dashboard.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Level Sensing**: Distance sensors are often used in industry to measure the "Empty Space" (Ullage) in a tank or bin. By subtracting this space from the total depth, we find the level of the contents.
*   **Percentage Mapping**: Converting raw centimeters into a 100-0% scale is tricky because 100cm (Empty) should map to 0%, and 10cm (Full) should map to 100%. You will learn to use inverted mapping logic.
*   **UART Telemetry**: You will learn to send structured data strings (e.g., `BIN_ID:001, LVL:85%`) through the UART (Serial) pins, which could then be connected to a Wi-Fi or LoRa module for city-wide monitoring.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Distance Sensor**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Category |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Sonar Trig/Echo** | GP14 / GP15 | Smart Sensors |
| **UART TX** | GP0 | Smart IO |
| **GND** | GND | Power |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `map_value_scaled`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `uart_write_text`.
*   From **Text**, drag `create_text_with` (join).

### 7. Variables
*   **raw_dist**: Number (The air gap).
*   **fill_pct**: Number (Scale 0-100).
*   **BIN_DEPTH**: Number (Set to 80cm).
*   **FULL_MARK**: Number (Set to 10cm).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Ullage Calculation**
1.  Read **raw_dist** from the sensor mounted on the lid pointing down.
2.  If `raw_dist > BIN_DEPTH`: Set it to `BIN_DEPTH` (Cap the value).
3.  Calculate the Percentage:
    *   Map `80cm to 10cm` -> `0 to 100`.

**Phase B: Filtering the Sloshing**
4.  Trash or liquids don't stay perfectly flat. Take 5 samples and find the **Median** (refer to Project 1431) to avoid "Jumping" percentages.

**Phase C: Reporting the Status**
5.  Create the message: `BIN_01: [fill_pct]%`.
6.  Use the `uart_write` block to broadcast this message at 9600 baud.
7.  Display the result on the Serial Terminal.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: An empty trash bin (80cm deep).
2.  **Interaction**: User throws a large bag in.
3.  **Process**: Sensor measures the gap is now 45cm.
4.  **Math**: `45cm` is roughly halfway between 80 and 10.
5.  **Action**: The Pico calculates `50%`.
6.  **Output**: UART sends `"BIN_01:50%"`.
7.  **Result**: The "City HQ" (the PC) receives the data and knows that Bin 1 is half full.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Init
sonar = Sonar(14, 15)
uart = machine.UART(0, baudrate=9600, tx=machine.Pin(0), rx=machine.Pin(1))

DEPTH_EMPTY = 80.0 #cm
DEPTH_FULL = 10.0  #cm

while True:
    d = sonar.read()
    
    # Inverted Mapping: (80 -> 0, 10 -> 100)
    # Formula: pct = (Empty - reading) / (Empty - Full) * 100
    pct = ((DEPTH_EMPTY - d) / (DEPTH_EMPTY - DEPTH_FULL)) * 100
    
    # Clamp
    if pct < 0: pct = 0
    if pct > 100: pct = 100
    
    msg = f"BIN_01:{int(pct)}%\n"
    uart.write(msg)
    print("Telemetery Sent:", msg.strip())
    
    utime.sleep(2) # Update every 2 seconds
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Linearity**: If the bin is a cone (narrower at the bottom), 50% height is NOT 50% volume. You would need a more complex formula for volume.
*   **Bag Overhang**: If a trash bag is hanging loosely in front of the sensor, it will report "100% Full" even if the bin is empty. Mounting placement is key.
*   **Serial Speed**: If your UART baud rate (e.g., 9600) doesn't match the receiver, you will see "Gibberish" text.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Lid Open Warning**: If the distance is > 200cm (looking at the ceiling), the lid must be open. Send a "Lid Ajar" alert.
*   **Low Power**: Only wake up and measure once every 10 minutes to save battery.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Compactor**: Run servo to squash trash?

## 1. Project 1439: Advanced Ultrasonic Distance Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Acoustic Lab Bench"
At the end of this project, you will be able to create a professional sensor calibration utility that calculates the "Scale Factor" (Gain) and "Zero Offset" for an ultrasonic sensor, permettant you to compensate for environmental factors or inaccurate mounting geometry.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Two-Point Calibration**: By measuring a known "Close" point (e.g., 10cm) and a "Far" point (e.g., 50cm), we can calculate the linear relationship between what the sensor *thinks* it sees and the *real* physical distance.
*   **Scale Factor (Gain)**: If the sensor measures 10.5cm for a 10cm gap, and 52.5cm for a 50cm gap, it has a "Scale Error" of 5%. We can multiply all future readings by 0.952 to fix this.
*   **Zero Offset**: If the sensor is mounted 2cm deep inside a plastic pipe, every reading will be 2cm too high. We subtract this "Offset" to get the true distance to the tip of the pipe.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **2 x Tactile Buttons** (Cal-Low / Cal-High)
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **Physical Ruler** (for reference)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Sonar** | GP14 / GP15 | Sensor |
| **Btn 1** | GP16 | Calibrate 10cm (Low) |
| **Btn 2** | GP17 | Calibrate 50cm (High) |
| **OLED** | I2C (GP4/5) | UI Display |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable` and `global_variable`.
*   From **Math**, drag `multiplication`, `division`, and `subtraction`.
*   From **Lists**, drag `median` (Project 1431).

### 7. Variables
*   **raw_low / raw_high**: Numbers (Uncalibrated readings).
*   **slope_k**: Number (The gain correction).
*   **offset_b**: Number (The zero correction).
*   **final_dist**: Number (The calibrated result).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Capturing Reference Points**
1.  Place an object exactly **10cm** from the sensor. Press **Button 1**.
    *   Store the result in `raw_low`.
2.  Place the object exactly **50cm** from the sensor. Press **Button 2**.
    *   Store the result in `raw_high`.

**Phase B: Solving the Equation (y = mx + b)**
3.  Calculate `slope_k = (50 - 10) / (raw_high - raw_low)`.
4.  Calculate `offset_b = 10 - (slope_k * raw_low)`.
5.  Save these values to the terminal so you can write them down.

**Phase C: The Transformation Engine**
6.  For every new reading:
    *   `final_dist = (raw_reading * slope_k) + offset_b`.
7.  Compare the `final_dist` against the ruler. It should now be perfectly accurate regardless of sensor drift.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: OLED shows "Raw Data".
2.  **Interaction**: User performs the 10cm and 50cm button presses.
3.  **Process**: The Pico computes the linear transform.
4.  **Math**: If the sensor originally measured 10.5 and 52.5, the gain becomes `40 / 42 = 0.952`.
5.  **Action**: The system switches to "Calibrated Mode".
6.  **Result**: The user has a precision laboratory instrument tuned specifically to their environment.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware
sonar = Sonar(14, 15)
btn_low = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_DOWN)
btn_high = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_DOWN)

# Calibration state
k = 1.0 # Slope
b = 0.0 # Offset

print("Calibration Tool Ready.")

while True:
    raw = sonar.read()
    
    if btn_low.value():
        # User says this is 10cm
        p1_raw = raw
        print(f"Low point captured: {p1_raw}")
        utime.sleep(1)
        
    if btn_high.value():
        # User says this is 50cm
        p2_raw = raw
        print(f"High point captured: {p2_raw}")
        # Solve
        k = (50.0 - 10.0) / (p2_raw - p1_raw)
        b = 10.0 - (k * p1_raw)
        print(f"New Profile: k={k:.4f}, b={b:.4f}")
        utime.sleep(1)
        
    calibrated = (raw * k) + b
    print(f"RAW: {raw:.1f} | CAL: {calibrated:.1f}")
    utime.sleep_ms(200)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Flat Target**: If you use a soft object (like a pillow) for calibration, the point of reflection will be fuzzy, making your math wrong. Always use a hard, flat surface (book/wall).
*   **Reversed Points**: Capturing the 50cm point as the "Low" point and 10cm as "High". This will result in negative distances!
*   **Jitter during Cal**: If you press the button while the object is wobbling, the calibration will be permanent garbage. Use the Median filter from 1431!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Material Study**: Different materials (carpet vs glass) reflect sound differently. Do you need a different Calibration Factor for different surfaces?
*   **Non-Volatile Save**: Save the `k` and `b` values to a file using the logic from Project 1437 so the calibration survives a reboot.

## 1. Project 1440: Advanced Advanced Ultrasonic Distance Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Haptic Nav-Stick"
At the end of this project, you will be able to build a complete assistive navigation prototype for the visually impaired, integrating ultrasonic sensing, haptic vibration feedback, and auditory "pings" to help a user sense obstacles in their environment without sight.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Assistive Technology (UX)**: Designing for users with sensory impairments requires a different type of interface. Instead of screens, we use vibration (Haptics) and sound (Audio) to provide 3D spatial awareness.
*   **Inverse PWM Mapping**: In this project, a *small* distance (10cm) must map to a *strong* vibration (100%), while a *large* distance (150cm) maps to a *zero* vibration (0%).
*   **Multi-Modal Feedback**: Using both sound and touch together increases the user's "Confidence" in the data. You will learn to synchronize a "Tick" sound with a "Vibration Pulse."

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Distance Sensor**
*   **Vibration Motor** (with MOSFET or Transistor Driver)
*   **Active Buzzer**
*   **Push Button** (On/Off Switch)
*   **Lipo Battery & Charger** (for portability)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Sonar** | GP14 / GP15 | Input |
| **Vibe Motor** | GP18 (PWM) | Output |
| **Buzzer** | GP19 | Output |
| **Switch** | GP20 | Input |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `map_value_scaled`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `set_pwm_duty`.

### 7. Variables
*   **proximity**: Number (Measurement in cm).
*   **intensity**: Number (PWM value 0-65535).
*   **is_running**: Boolean (System power state).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Proximity Mapping**
1.  Read the **proximity** from the sensor.
2.  If the proximity is > 200cm, set `intensity` to 0.
3.  Otherwise, use `map_value`:
    *   Map `10cm to 150cm` -> `65535 down to 10000`.

**Phase B: Haptic Pulses**
4.  Instead of a constant vibration, create a "Pulsing" effect.
    *   Use `intensity` to set the power of the pulse.
    *   Make the frequency of the pulse increase as the user gets closer to an object.

**Phase C: Auditory Assistance**
5.  If `proximity < 50cm`, play a short "Click" (2000Hz for 5ms) on the buzzer every time a vibration pulse occurs.
6.  This creates a "Geiger Counter" effect that is very intuitive for navigation.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: System is off. 
2.  **Interaction**: User clicks the button. System chirps to signal "Power On".
3.  **Process**: The user sweeps the stick from side to side.
4.  **Math**: A wall is detected at 80cm. Intensity is calculated at ~50%.
5.  **Action**: The handle vibrates with moderate strength and steady pulses.
6.  **Result**: As the user walks closer to the wall, the vibration gets stronger and the clicking gets faster, warning them to turn.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware
sonar = Sonar(14, 15)
vibe = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(18))
vibe.freq(1000)
buzz = machine.Pin(19, machine.Pin.OUT)
btn = machine.Pin(20, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_DOWN)

system_on = False

while True:
    # Toggle Power
    if btn.value():
        system_on = not system_on
        buzz.value(1); utime.sleep(0.1); buzz.value(0)
        utime.sleep(0.5)
        
    if system_on:
        d = sonar.read()
        
        if 5 < d < 150:
            # Phase A: Map Distance to Power
            # 10cm -> 65535 (Max), 150cm -> 0 (Min)
            power = int((150 - d) / 140 * 65535)
            if power < 0: power = 0
            
            # Phase B & C: Haptic Pulse
            vibe.duty_u16(power)
            buzz.value(1)
            utime.sleep_ms(30) # Very short pulse
            vibe.duty_u16(0)
            buzz.value(0)
            
            # Delay proportional to distance (faster clicks when closer)
            utime.sleep_ms(int(d * 5))
        else:
            vibe.duty_u16(0)
            utime.sleep_ms(100)
    else:
        vibe.duty_u16(0)
        utime.sleep_ms(200)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Mechanical Feedback**: If the vibration motor is mounted too close to the ultrasonic sensor, the sonar might "hear" the motor's buzzing as a fake obstacle. Use foam to isolate the sensor and motor.
*   **Transistor Omission**: A vibration motor takes 60mA-100mA. The Pico pins can only output 12mA. **If you don't use a transistor (S8050 or similar), you will fry your Pico.**
*   **Minimum Threshold**: If the sensor reads < 3cm, it might report 0 or a very large number. Always add a "Minimum Safe Distance" check (e.g., `if d > 5`).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Night Mode**: Add a Light Sensor. If it's dark, increase the buzzer volume to compensate for the lost visual cues.
*   **Orientation Check**: Use an IMU (Project 1421) to detect if the user has dropped the stick or is holding it at the wrong angle.

## Batch 145: Advanced Servo Kinematics 5 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1441: Advanced Servo Kinematics Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Dual-Rate Arm"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement multi-speed "Dual Rate" control for a servo motor using a joystick, including a toggle for "Precision Mode" (10% speed) vs. "Turbo Mode" (100% speed) for delicate maneuvers.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Dual Rate Scaling**: Professional radio controllers use "Rates" to change how much an input affects a motor. We can multiply our joystick input by 1.0 (Turbo) or 0.1 (Precision) to change the "gearing" of the digital controls.
*   **Incremental Positioning**: Instead of mapping the joystick center to 90 degrees, we treat the joystick displacement as "Speed." If you push the stick 5% forward, the servo slowly creeps forward. If you push it 100%, it zips.
*   **Boundary Guarding**: When using incremental motion, you must program "Hard Stops" so the code doesn't try to move the servo to 190 or -10 degrees, which would cause physical damage.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Servo Motor (Standard or Micro)**
*   **Analog 2-Axis Joystick**
*   **Push Button** (Mode Toggle)
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo PWM** | GP15 | Motion & Motors |
| **Joy X-Axis** | GP26 (ADC0) | Input |
| **Toggle BTN** | GP16 | Digital Input |
| **Power** | VBUS (5V) | For Servo |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `multiplication` and `if_item_between`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else`.
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag `set_servo_angle`.

### 7. Variables
*   **current_angle**: Number (Storage for the servo 0-180).
*   **delta**: Number (How much to move this frame).
*   **is_turbo**: Boolean (Mode flag).
*   **deadzone**: Number (Set to 2000 - stops joystick drift).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Reading the Displacement**
1.  Read the **Joy X** value (0 to 65535).
2.  Subtract `32768` to get a range of `-32k to +32k`.
3.  If the absolute value is less than `deadzone`, set it to `0`. (This keeps the arm still when you let go).

**Phase B: Scaling the Delta**
4.  If `is_turbo` is **True**:
    *   Set `delta = raw_joy / 1000`.
5.  Else (Precision Mode):
    *   Set `delta = raw_joy / 10000`. (10x slower).

**Phase C: Boundary Guarding**
6.  `increment current_angle` by `delta`.
7.  Check:
    *   If `current_angle > 180`: Set it to `180`.
    *   If `current_angle < 0`: Set it to `0`.
8.  Update the Servo position using `current_angle`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The arm is at 90 degrees.
2.  **Interaction**: User pushes the stick fully right in Turbo mode.
3.  **Process**: Delta is `+32`. The arm zips at 32 degrees per update.
4.  **Interaction**: User clicks the button to enter Precision mode and pushes fully right.
5.  **Action**: Delta is now `+3.2`. The arm moves gently and smoothly.
6.  **Result**: The user can perform high-speed repositioning AND surgical-level precision with the same stick.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Setup
servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
servo.freq(50)
joy = machine.ADC(26)
btn = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_DOWN)

angle = 90.0
is_turbo = True

while True:
    # Mode Toggle
    if btn.value():
        is_turbo = not is_turbo
        utime.sleep(0.3) # Debounce
        
    # Read Joy and centered
    raw = joy.read_u16() - 32768
    
    # Deadzone
    if abs(raw) < 2000:
        delta = 0
    else:
        # Scale Delta
        rate = 1500 if is_turbo else 15000
        delta = raw / rate
        
    # Update Angle
    angle += delta
    
    # Boundary Guard
    if angle > 180: angle = 180
    if angle < 0: angle = 0
    
    # Write to Servo (Simplified mapping)
    # duty = 1638 to 8192 for 0-180
    duty = int(1638 + (angle / 180) * 6554)
    servo.duty_u16(duty)
    
    utime.sleep_ms(20)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Integers vs Floats**: If you use integers for the `angle`, the Precision mode (adding 0.3) will be rounded to 0, and the arm won't move at all! Always use floats for the internal math.
*   **The Grinding Servo**: If you don't use Phase C, the code might tell a 180-degree servo to go to 200 degrees. The motor will strain against its internal plastic stop and melt.
*   **Deadzone Drift**: Every joystick is different. If your arm "crawls" when you aren't touching the stick, increase the `deadzone` variable.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Expo Rates**: Can you square the joystick input? `Out = X * X`. This makes the center even softer while keeping the edges fast.
*   **Visual Status**: Light up an RGB LED Red for Turbo and Green for Precision.

---

## 1. Project 1442: Advanced Servo Kinematics Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Virtual Twin"
At the end of this project, you will be able to create a digital twin of your servo arm on an OLED display, using basic trigonometry (Sine/Cosine) to draw a real-time needle gauge that tracks the physical angle of the motor.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Digital Twins**: In advanced manufacturing, every physical machine has a "Virtual Twin" in the computer. We can see what the machine is doing without looking at it.
*   **Vector Rendering**: Instead of just printing the number "45", we can draw a line at a 45-degree angle. This requires us to calculate the (X, Y) coordinates of the tip of the needle using the formulas: `X = CenterX + (Length * cos(Angle))` and `Y = CenterY - (Length * sin(Angle))`.
*   **Radians**: Computers do not understand "Degrees" (0-360). They use "Radians" (0-2π). You will learn how to convert degrees to radians using the formula `Rad = Deg * (π / 180)`.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Servo Motor**
*   **0.96" SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **10k Potentiometer** (to control the arm)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo** | GP15 | PWM Output |
| **Potentiometer** | GP26 (ADC0) | Analog Input |
| **OLED SDA** | GP4 | I2C0 |
| **OLED SCL** | GP5 | I2C0 |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `sine`, `cosine`, and `to_radians`.
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `draw_line` and `draw_semi_circle`.

### 7. Variables
*   **angle**: Number (0 to 180).
*   **rads**: Number (The radian equivalent).
*   **x_tip / y_tip**: Numbers (Target coordinates).
*   **radius**: Number (Length of the virtual arm, e.g., 25 pixels).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Angle Acquisition**
1.  Read the **Potentiometer** value.
2.  Map it to a range of `0 to 180` and set it to `angle`.
3.  Set the **Servo** position to this `angle`.

**Phase B: Trigonometric Conversion**
4.  Convert the `angle` to `rads` using the `to_radians` block.
5.  State the center of your gauge as `64, 50` (bottom-middle of the OLED).
6.  Calculate the tip coordinates:
    *   `x_tip = 64 + (radius * cos(rads))`
    *   `y_tip = 50 - (radius * sin(rads))` (Note: Y is subtracted because 0 is at the top of the screen).

**Phase C: Drawing the Twin**
7.  Clear the OLED.
8.  Draw a semi-circle from 0 to 180 as the "Scale."
9.  Draw a line from `64, 50` to `x_tip, y_tip`.
10. Update the display.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The OLED shows a blank gauge.
2.  **Interaction**: User turns the knob to the 45-degree mark.
3.  **Process**: The physical arm moves to 45 degrees.
4.  **Math**: The Pico calculates `cos(45) = 0.707` and `sin(45) = 0.707`.
5.  **Action**: The computer draws a line pointing diagonally up and right.
6.  **Result**: The user sees the virtual needle move in perfect sync with the plastic arm.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import math

# Hardware
pot = machine.ADC(26)
servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
servo.freq(50)
# oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)

cx, cy = 64, 55 # Center of gauge
r = 30 # Radius

while True:
    # 1. Input
    val = pot.read_u16()
    angle = (val / 65535) * 180
    
    # 2. Physical Action
    duty = int(1638 + (angle / 180) * 6554)
    servo.duty_u16(duty)
    
    # 3. Virtual Twin Calculation
    # We subtract angle from 180 because OLED X increases L->R
    rads = math.radians(180 - angle) 
    tx = int(cx + r * math.cos(rads))
    ty = int(cy - r * math.sin(rads))
    
    # 4. Render
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text(f"Ang: {int(angle)}", 0, 0)
    oled.line(cx, cy, tx, ty, 1) # The Needle
    # Draw arc/bottom line
    oled.line(cx-35, cy, cx+35, cy, 1)
    oled.show()
    
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Upside-Down Needle**: If you *add* the Sine value to Y, the needle will point down into the bottom of the screen. Remember: On screens, a larger Y number means "Lower Down."
*   **Coordinate Misalignment**: If the servo is mounted upside down, the physical 0 degrees might be the screen's 180 degrees. You may need to use `180 - angle` in your math.
*   **Screen Flicker**: If you `oled.show()` inside the loop without a small `sleep`, the screen might flicker. 20Hz (50ms) is the "Sweet Spot" for smooth motion.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **The Sweep Effect**: Can you draw 5 faint lines behind the needle to show the "Path" it just took?
*   **Dynamic Colors**: If you have a color OLED, make the needle turn Red if the angle is > 160 (Danger Zone).

## 1. Project 1443: Advanced Servo Kinematics Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Jam-Sensing Arm"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an intelligent fault-detection system that detects a mechanical "jam" by comparing the commanded angle with the actual feedback, automatically cutting power to "relax" the motor and prevent gear damage or electrical burnout.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Closed-Loop Fault Detection**: Most servos are "Open Loop" (the Pico sends a signal and *hopes* the motor moves). By using a Feedback Servo (which has an extra wire), we can monitor if the arm actually moved. If it didn't, we know there is a problem.
*   **Over-Current Protection**: When a motor is stalled (trying to move but blocked), it draws maximum current and generates extreme heat. This can melt the motor casing.
*   **Detaching (PWM Cut)**: You will learn to use `servo_detach` which stops the pulses. This makes the motor go "Limp," allowing the user to safely manually remove the obstruction without the motor fighting them.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Feedback Servo Motor** (Standard servos can be used with a 10-ohm sense resistor for advanced users).
*   **Red LED** (Fault Indicator)
*   **Active Buzzer**
*   **Reset Button**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo PWM** | GP15 | Output |
| **Servo Feedback**| GP26 (ADC0) | Input |
| **Fault LED** | GP16 | Digital Output |
| **Reset BTN** | GP17 | Input |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_absolute_greater_than`.
*   From **Math**, drag `subtraction` and `increment`.
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag `detach_servo`.

### 7. Variables
*   **target_angle**: Number (Where we want to be).
*   **feedback_angle**: Number (Where we actually are).
*   **stall_timer**: Number (Counter for how long we've been stuck).
*   **fault_active**: Boolean (Lockout flag).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Commanding vs Sensing**
1.  Map the **Feedback ADC** value to a range of `0 to 180`.
2.  Continuously update `target_angle` based on a pot or joystick.
3.  Command the servo to move to `target_angle`.

**Phase B: The 5-Degree Deviation Check**
4.  Calculate `error = abs(target_angle - feedback_angle)`.
5.  If `error > 10`: (The arm is significantly behind where it should be).
    *   `increment stall_timer`.
6.  Else:
    *   Set `stall_timer = 0`. (The path is clear).

**Phase C: The Self-Protection Trigger**
7.  If `stall_timer > 500ms`:
    *   **Detach** the servo (Stop PWM).
    *   Turn on the Red LED and Beep.
    *   Set `fault_active` to **True**.
8.  **Pro Tip**: The system must stay in this state until the **Reset Button** is pressed.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The arm is moving freely. `stall_timer` is 0.
2.  **Interaction**: A user places a heavy book on the arm while it's trying to lift.
3.  **Process**: Target is 90. Feedback stays at 20. Error is 70.
4.  **Math**: `stall_timer` starts climbing. After 0.5s, it hits the limit.
5.  **Action**: The high-pitched humming stops as the PWM is cut. The LED turns red.
6.  **Result**: The motor and gears are saved from destruction.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Pins
servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
servo.freq(50)
feedback = machine.ADC(26)
alert = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
btn = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_DOWN)

# Global State
stall_ms = 0
fault = False
target = 90.0

while True:
    if fault:
        if btn.value():
            fault = False
            alert.value(0)
            print("System Reset.")
            utime.sleep(0.5)
        continue # Lock loop while faulted
        
    # 1. Drive
    duty = int(1638 + (target / 180) * 6554)
    servo.duty_u16(duty)
    
    # 2. Monitor
    # Map ADC 0-65535 to 0-180 (depends on servo spec)
    actual = (feedback.read_u16() / 65535) * 180
    
    if abs(target - actual) > 10.0:
        stall_ms += 50
    else:
        stall_ms = 0
        
    # 3. Trip
    if stall_ms > 1000:
        servo.duty_u16(0) # Detach
        alert.value(1)
        fault = True
        print("JAM DETECTED! Power cut for safety.")
        
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Travel Lag**: Servos aren't instant! If you check for a jam the *exact millisecond* you change the target, it will always report a fault because the arm hasn't had time to move yet. Always allow a short "Travel Grace Period."
*   **ADC Noise**: The feedback voltage can jump slightly. A threshold of `10 degrees` is usually safer than `1 degree` to avoid "Ghost Jams."
*   **Auto-Reset**: Never allow the program to auto-start. If the obstruction is still there, it will just stall and heat up again. Force a human to press Reset.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Reverse**: Can you make the arm back up by 20 degrees automatically if it hits a jam, then try again?
*   **Fault Logger**: Write the timestamp of the jam to a file (Project 1437).

## 1. Project 1444: Advanced Servo Kinematics Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Keyframe Editor"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a professional motion recording and playback system, allowing you to "Teach" your robot arm a sequence of movements by saving keyframes to a list and playing them back with smooth interpolation.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Keyframe Animation**: Used in Hollywood and video games, keyframes are "snapshots" of a position at a specific time. You define the start and end points, and the computer fills in the gaps.
*   **Sequential Programming**: You will learn how to iterate through a **List** of data points to perform the same task (moving a motor) over and over with different values.
*   **Mode-Based Logic (State Machine)**: The project switches between "RECORD" mode (where the motor follows your knob) and "PLAY" mode (where the motor ignores you and follows the list).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Servo Motor**
*   **10k Potentiometer** (to "Pose" the arm)
*   **2 x Push Buttons** (Record / Play)
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo** | GP15 | Arm |
| **Potentiometer** | GP26 | Posing Knob |
| **RECORD BTN** | GP16 | Store Frame |
| **PLAY BTN** | GP17 | Run Sequence |
| **OLED** | I2C (GP0/1) | Status Display |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Lists**, drag `create_empty_list` and `add_to_list`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `repeat_for_each_item_in_list`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`.

### 7. Variables
*   **timeline**: List (Storage for recorded angles).
*   **current_angle**: Number (Real-time posing).
*   **edit_mode**: Boolean (Record vs. Play).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Manual Posing**
1.  Read the **Potentiometer** and map it to `0-180`.
2.  If in "EDIT" mode, set the servo to this position.
3.  Display "EDITING" on the OLED along with the current count of frames.

**Phase B: Keyframe Capture**
4.  If the **RECORD BTN** is pressed:
    *   `Add current_angle to list timeline`.
    *   Flash the onboard LED once.
    *   Display "CAPTURED FRAME #[length of timeline]".

**Phase C: The Sequence Replay**
5.  If the **PLAY BTN** is pressed:
    *   Switch to "PLAYBACK" mode.
    *   For `each angle in timeline`:
        *   Move the servo to `angle`.
        *   Wait `500ms`.
    *   Switch back to "EDIT" mode when done.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The OLED says "IDLE. 0 Frames".
2.  **Interaction**: User turns the knob to 10 degrees and hits "Record". Then turns to 170 degrees and hits "Record".
3.  **Process**: The `timeline` list now contains `[10, 170]`.
4.  **Interaction**: User hits the "Play" button.
5.  **Action**: The arm immediately shoots to 10 degrees, waits, then shoots to 170 degrees.
6.  **Result**: You have "Taught" the robot a simple waving motion without writing any new code!

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware
pot = machine.ADC(26)
servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
servo.freq(50)
btn_rec = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_DOWN)
btn_play = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_DOWN)

timeline = []
recording = True

while True:
    if recording:
        # Phase A: Manual Pose
        val = pot.read_u16()
        angle = (val / 65535) * 180
        duty = int(1638 + (angle / 180) * 6554)
        servo.duty_u16(duty)
        
        # Phase B: Capture
        if btn_rec.value():
            timeline.append(angle)
            print(f"Frame {len(timeline)} stored: {int(angle)} deg")
            utime.sleep(0.4) # Debounce
            
        # Switch to Play
        if btn_play.value():
            recording = False
            utime.sleep(0.4)
            
    else:
        # Phase C: Replay
        print("--- REPLAYING SEQUENCE ---")
        for frame_angle in timeline:
            duty = int(1638 + (frame_angle / 180) * 6554)
            servo.duty_u16(duty)
            print(f"Moving to {int(frame_angle)}...")
            utime.sleep(0.8)
        
        print("Sequence Complete.")
        recording = True # Back to edit
        
    utime.sleep_ms(20)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The List Explosion**: If you don't use a debounce (Project 1401), holding the Record button for 1 second might store 50 identical frames in your list!
*   **Sudden Jumps**: Moving from 10 to 170 degrees in one frame can be violent and even tip over a lightweight robot. Professional systems use 10 mini-steps (Interpolation) between frames.
*   **Memory Loss**: Lists are stored in RAM. If you unplug the Pico, your animation is deleted! You would need to use `with open("save.txt", "w")` to save it permanently.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Loop Mode**: Can you make the sequence play forever until a button is pressed?
*   **Undo**: Add a third button that removes the *last* item from the list if you made a mistake.

## 1. Project 1445: Automated Advanced Servo Kinematics Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"The 3D Sight System"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an Inverse Kinematics (IK) "Look At" system for a 2-Axis (Pan-Tilt) head, using trigonometry (atan2) to automatically point the servos at a specific [X, Y, Z] coordinate in 3D space.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Pan-Tilt Kinematics**: A "Pan" motor rotates horizontally (Left/Right), and a "Tilt" motor rotates vertically (Up/Down). Together, they can point at any hemisphere in front of them.
*   **Inverse Kinematics (IK)**: In standard robotics, you tell a motor to move to an angle. In IK, you tell the robot a *location* (e.g., "The Apple is at X=10, Y=2"), and the robot calculates the angles itself.
*   **Vector Decomposition**: You will use `math.atan2(y, x)` to find the horizontal angle and `math.atan2(z, distance)` to find the vertical tilt.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2 x Servo Motors** (Standard or Micro)
*   **Pan-Tilt Bracket Assembly**
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Category |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pan Servo (L/R)**| GP14 | Motion & Motors |
| **Tilt Servo (U/D)**| GP15 | Motion & Motors |
| **Joystick X** | GP26 | Input (Target X) |
| **Joystick Y** | GP27 | Input (Target Y) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `atan2`, `square_root`, and `to_degrees`.
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag `set_servo_angle` (multiple).

### 7. Variables
*   **tx / ty / tz**: Numbers (Target coordinates in cm).
*   **dist_ground**: Number (Distance from base to target).
*   **pan_deg / tilt_deg**: Numbers (The calculated answers).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Reading the Target**
1.  Use the Joysticks to set the `tx` and `ty` coordinates (e.g., `-50 to 50`).
2.  Assume `tz` (height) is constant for now (e.g., `20cm`).
3.  Display the coordinates on the Serial Monitor.

**Phase B: Solving the Azimuth (Pan)**
4.  Calculate `pan_rads = atan2(ty, tx)`.
5.  Convert `pan_rads` to degrees and add `90` (to center the servo).
6.  Set the **Pan Servo** to this value.

**Phase C: Solving the Elevation (Tilt)**
7.  Calculate the ground distance: `dist_ground = sqrt(tx*tx + ty*ty)`.
8.  Calculate `tilt_rads = atan2(tz, dist_ground)`.
9.  Convert `tilt_rads` to degrees and set the **Tilt Servo**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The servos center at [0, 0, 0].
2.  **Interaction**: You move the joystick to represent a point far to the right (X=10, Y=50).
3.  **Process**: The Pico math engine triggers.
4.  **Math**: `atan2(50, 10)` returns approximately 78 degrees.
5.  **Action**: Both motors move simultaneously. The Pan motor swings right, and the Tilt motor adjusts for the depth.
6.  **Result**: The head "locks on" to the invisible target point perfectly.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import math

# Hardware
serv_p = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(14))
serv_t = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
serv_p.freq(50); serv_t.freq(50)

# Target Coords (In CM)
tx, ty, tz = 20.0, 5.0, 10.0

def set_angle(s, a):
    duty = int(1638 + (a / 180) * 6554)
    s.duty_u16(duty)

while True:
    # 1. Pan Calculation (Horizontal)
    # atan2(y, x)
    pan_rad = math.atan2(ty, tx)
    pan_deg = math.degrees(pan_rad) + 90
    
    # 2. Tilt Calculation (Vertical)
    # distance = sqrt(x^2 + y^2)
    dist_2d = math.sqrt(tx**2 + ty**2)
    tilt_rad = math.atan2(tz, dist_2d)
    tilt_deg = math.degrees(tilt_rad) + 90
    
    # 3. Execution
    set_angle(serv_p, pan_deg)
    set_angle(serv_t, tilt_deg)
    
    print(f"Tracking Target: [{tx}, {ty}, {tz}] -> Pan:{int(pan_deg)} Tilt:{int(tilt_deg)}")
    utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Coordinate Swapping**: If Pan is moving when you change TZ, you have swapped your axes in the `atan2` block. X is depth, Y is width, Z is height.
*   **Division by Zero**: If X and Y are both 0, `atan2` is undefined. Always ensure a minimum distance (e.g., 1mm).
*   **Servo Limits**: Your math might return 195 degrees. Servos can't reach that. Always use a `clamp` block to keep angles between 0 and 180.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dynamic Target**: Connect an Ultrasonic sensor (Project 1431). Can the head automatically "Tilt" down as an object gets closer to keep it centered?
*   **Laser Pointer**: Mount a laser on the tilt bracket. It should stay pointed at a fixed spot on the floor even as you rotate the Pan servo.

### 7. Variables
*   **pan, tilt**

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Target**: dx=10, dy=5, dz=0.
2.  **Pan**: `atan2(dy, dx)`.
3.  **Tilt**: `atan2(dz, dist_xy)`.
4.  **Move**: Convert deg to PWM.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Coord**: "Look at tree".
2.  **Action**: Head turns to tree.

### 10. Generated Code
``python
pan = math.atan2(y, x) * 57.3
servo_pan.angle(pan)
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Frame**: Coordinate frame logic. Is Z up or Y up?

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Face**: Face tracking (Sim coordinates).

## 1. Project 1446: Advanced Servo Kinematics Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"The No-Go Zone"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a "Virtual Fence" for your robot arm, defining a keep-out coordinate in 3D space and using distance-checking logic to automatically stop or deviate the arm before it hits a physical obstacle.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Safety Envelopes**: In industrial robotics (like Tesla factories), robots are programmed with "Keep-out Zones" so they don't hit the human workers or other machines. This is called a "Virtual Fence."
*   **Euclidean Proximity**: You will learn to use the Pythagorean theorem in 2D space (`distance = sqrt(dx^2 + dy^2)`) to constantly monitor how close the "Tip" of your robot arm is to a forbidden point.
*   **Predictive Braking**: Instead of stopping *at* the wall, you will learn to implement a "Warning Zone" where the robot slows down as it gets closer to the obstacle, providing a smoother and safer interaction.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Servo Arm** (Pan-Tilt or single axis)
*   **2 x Potentiometers** (to move the arm tip)
*   **Red LED and Buzzer** (Collision Alert)
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo Pan/Tilt** | GP14 / GP15 | Arm Motors |
| **Pot X/Y** | GP26 / GP27 | Input Coordinates |
| **Alert LED** | GP16 | Visual Warning |
| **Buzzer** | GP17 | Audio Warning |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `square_root`, `sum_of_squares`, and `subtraction`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_less_than`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_global`.

### 7. Variables
*   **tx / ty**: Numbers (The user's desired tip position).
*   **ox / oy**: Numbers (The coordinates of the obstacle, e.g., 30, 30).
*   **limit**: Number (The "Danger Radius," e.g., 10cm).
*   **dist_to_obj**: Number (The calculated gap).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Defining the World**
1.  Assume your robot arm is at `(0, 0)`.
2.  Set the **Obstacle** position. Let's say there is a "Water Cup" at `X=20, Y=20`.
3.  Set the `limit` to `15` (15cm safety bubble).

**Phase B: The Pythagorean Check**
4.  Read the joystick/pot to get the current **tx** and **ty**.
5.  Calculate `dx = tx - ox` and `dy = ty - oy`.
6.  Calculate `dist_to_obj = sqrt(dx*dx + dy*dy)`.

**Phase C: The Override**
7.  Check: `If dist_to_obj < limit`:
    *   Set **Fault LED** to ON.
    *   **Freeze** the servos at their last safe position.
    *   Display "COLLISION PREVENTED" on the terminal.
8.  Else:
    *   Turn LED OFF.
    *   Allow the servos to move to the new `tx, ty`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The arm moves freely in the open area.
2.  **Interaction**: User tries to move the arm directly towards the "Water Cup."
3.  **Process**: As the tip gets within 15cm, the math detected the violation.
4.  **Math**: `sqrt((20-10)^2 + (20-10)^2) = 14.1`. This is less than the 15cm limit!
5.  **Action**: The arm stops instantly, even though the user is pushing the stick forward.
6.  **Result**: The robot refuses to hit the obstacle, ensuring workplace safety.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import math

# Hardware
serv_x = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(14))
serv_y = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
serv_x.freq(50); serv_y.freq(50)
alert = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

# Config
OBSTACLE_X, OBSTACLE_Y = 20.0, 20.0
LIMIT = 15.0

def drive(x, y):
    # Simplified angle mapping
    dx = int(1638 + (x / 180) * 6554)
    dy = int(1638 + (y / 180) * 6554)
    serv_x.duty_u16(dx); serv_y.duty_u16(dy)

while True:
    # 1. Get raw input from user (Simulated)
    # real code would read ADC here
    target_x, target_y = 18.0, 18.0 
    
    # 2. Check Distance to No-Go Zone
    dx = target_x - OBSTACLE_X
    dy = target_y - OBSTACLE_Y
    dist = math.sqrt(dx**2 + dy**2)
    
    if dist < LIMIT:
        # COLLISION IMMINENT
        alert.value(1)
        # Power down or hold position
        print(f"Danger! Tip at {dist:.1f}cm from obstacle. Movement Blocked.")
    else:
        alert.value(0)
        drive(target_x, target_y)
        
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Point-Tip Fallacy**: This code treats the arm as a single point. Real robot arms have "Width." You should always add the width of your robot's "Gripper" to the `limit` variable to avoid clipping the edge of the object.
*   **Center of Mass**: If your robot arm is long, it might tip over if it stops too suddenly. Professional systems use "Deceleration Ramps" rather than an instant freeze.
*   **Stuck in the Zone**: If the arm is *already* inside the zone on startup, the code might block it from moving *out* of the zone. You need logic that allows "Retreating" movement.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dynamic Exclusion**: Connect an Ultrasonic sensor to GP18. Let the sensor detect where the wall is, and update the `OBSTACLE` coordinates in real-time.
*   **Slide Logic**: Instead of stopping, can you make the arm "Slide" along the edge of the circle?
