## 1. Project 1447: Advanced Servo Kinematics Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Black Box Recorder"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an industrial circular buffer (Black Box) that constantly records the last 10 seconds of robot movement in RAM, dumping the data to the console or a file only when a mechanical fault occurs for forensic analysis.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Circular Buffers (Ring Buffer)**: When you have limited memory (like on a Pico), you can't record forever. A circular buffer uses a fixed-size list (e.g., 100 spots). When you reach spot 101, you loop back to spot 1 and overwrite the oldest data.
*   **Forensic Analysis**: Engineers use "Black Box" data to see what happened *right before* a crash. Did the arm oscillate? Did the command spike?
*   **Modulo Operator (%)**: You will use the "Remainder" math block to automatically wrap your counter back to zero when it hits the end of the list.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Servo Motor**
*   **Feedback Sensor (or Potentiometer)**
*   **Fault Trigger Button** (to simulate a crash)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Category |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo** | GP15 | Motion & Motors |
| **Feedback** | GP26 | Smart Sensors |
| **Crash Btn** | GP16 | Digital Input |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Lists**, drag `set_list_item` and `get_list_item`.
*   From **Math**, drag `remainder_of` (Modulo).
*   From **Control**, drag `repeat_times`.

### 7. Variables
*   **log_buffer**: List (Size 100).
*   **write_ptr**: Number (Current index).
*   **BUFF_SIZE**: Number (100).
*   **actual_pos**: Number (Sensor reading).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Initializing the Ring**
1.  Create an empty **log_buffer**.
2.  Use a `repeat 100 times` loop to fill it with `0`. (Pre-allocating memory prevents lag during recording).

**Phase B: The Silent Record**
3.  Enter a high-speed loop.
4.  Read the **actual_pos** of the servo.
5.  Set `log_buffer` at index `write_ptr` to `actual_pos`.
6.  Set `write_ptr = (write_ptr + 1) % BUFF_SIZE`. (This is the MAGIC logic that makes it circular).

**Phase C: The Crash Dump**
7.  If the **Crash Btn** is pressed:
    *   Stop the recording.
    *   Iterate through the buffer from `write_ptr` all the way around back to `write_ptr - 1`.
    *   Print each value. This shows the movement history leading up to the button press.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico begins silently filling the ring buffer in RAM.
2.  **Interaction**: You move the servo arm in circles for 30 seconds.
3.  **Process**: Index 0-99 is filled, then overwritten, then filled again.
4.  **Action**: You press the "Crash" button.
5.  **Output**: The terminal suddenly spits out the last 100 readings.
6.  **Result**: You can copy this data into Excel to graph exactly how the arm was moving 5 seconds ago!

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware
serv = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
sens = machine.ADC(26)
btn = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_DOWN)

# Buffer Setup (RAM)
LIMIT = 100
history = [0.0] * LIMIT
ptr = 0

print("System Recording (10s ring buffer)...")

while True:
    # 1. Record in background
    val = (sens.read_u16() / 65535) * 180
    history[ptr] = val
    ptr = (ptr + 1) % LIMIT
    
    # 2. Crash Detection (Manual Trigger)
    if btn.value():
        print("!!! CRASH LOG TRIPPED !!!")
        print("Sequence (Oldest -> Newest):")
        
        # Read the ring back starting from the next pointer
        for i in range(LIMIT):
            read_idx = (ptr + i) % LIMIT
            print(f"T-{LIMIT-i}: {history[read_idx]:.1f} deg")
            
        print("End of Log.")
        break # Stop system
        
    utime.sleep_ms(100) # 10Hz Capture = 10 Seconds total
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Flash Fatigue**: Never write to a file *inside* your recording loop. Recording is fast, but Flash memory is slow and has a limited life. Record in RAM (the list) and only save to Flash (the file) once during the crash event.
*   **The Modulo Offset**: If you start reading from 0 after a crash, the data won't be in chronological order. You must start at the `ptr` and loop around for it to make sense.
*   **Timing Drift**: If you wait exactly 100ms, the log is 10 seconds. If you wait 10ms, it's only 1 second. Pick your frequency based on how fast your robot moves.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Multi-Channel**: Can you record the Command (Target) AND the Actual (Sensor) in the same log to see if the motor was lagging?
*   **Replay Mode**: Can the Pico play back the recorded list to the servo once the crash is cleared?

---

## 1. Project 1448: Smart Advanced Servo Kinematics System

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Remote Puppeteer"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a remote-controlled telepresence system where a "Controller" (Pico 1) with sensors transmits motion data over UART to a "Robot Hand" (Pico 2) with servos, mimicking the human's movements in real-time using data framing.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Telepresence**: This is the tech used by surgeons to operate from across the ocean. A human wears sensors, and a robot replicates those movements exactly. 
*   **Data Framing**: When sending numbers over a wire, the receiver might get "90" and "45," but if it misses one, it might think the "45" is the first number! We use "Start" and "End" characters (like `<` and `>`) to wrap our data so the receiver always knows where a message starts.
*   **Synchronization**: Running two Picos at the same "Heartbeat" ensures the robot doesn't "Lag" behind the human.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **2 x Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **5 x Flex Sensors** (or 5 Potentiometers as a "Glove" mockup)
*   **5 x Micro Servos** (The Hand)
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Type | Transmitter (Pico 1) | Receiver (Pico 2) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Data Out** | UART TX (GP0) | - |
| **Data In** | - | UART RX (GP1) |
| **Sensors** | ADC (GP26-GP28) | - |
| **Servos** | - | PWM (GP10-GP14) |
| **GND** | GROUND | GROUND (Must Connect!) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Text**, drag `create_text_with` and `split_at_delimiter`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `uart_write` and `uart_read_all`.
*   From **Control**, drag `wait`.

### 7. Variables
*   **raw_data**: Text (The incoming serial string).
*   **servo_list**: List (The parsed angles).
*   **packet**: Text (The framed message, e.g. `<180,90,45>`).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Reading the Glove (TX)**
1.  Read the values of the **Flex Sensors**.
2.  Map them to `0-180`.
3.  Create a "Packet": `Text Join ("<", Angle1, ",", Angle2, ">")`.
4.  Write this packet to the **UART** once every 50ms.

**Phase B: Receiving the Stream (RX)**
5.  On Pico 2, check if **UART** has data.
6.  Read until the `>` character is found.
7.  Clean the text by removing the `<` character.
8.  **Split** the text by the comma `,` to get a list of numbers.

**Phase C: The Mimicry**
9.  Iterate through the `servo_list`.
10. Set each **Servo** to the corresponding angle in the list.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Both Picos are powered. Transmitter shows "Glove Ready."
2.  **Interaction**: User curls their index finger. 
3.  **Process**: Pico 1 generates the string `"<120,0,0,0,0>"`.
4.  **Action**: Pico 2 receives the string, splits it, and sees the first number is 120.
5.  **Output**: Servo 1 on the mechanical hand curls exactly 120 degrees.
6.  **Result**: The mechanical hand mirrors the human hand with less than 20ms of delay.

### 10. Generated Code (Receiver Side)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Setup
uart = machine.UART(0, baudrate=115200, tx=machine.Pin(0), rx=machine.Pin(1))
servos = [machine.PWM(machine.Pin(10+i)) for i in range(5)]
for s in servos: s.freq(50)

def move_hand(angles):
    for i in range(min(len(angles), 5)):
        angle = float(angles[i])
        duty = int(1638 + (angle / 180) * 6554)
        servos[i].duty_u16(duty)

while True:
    if uart.any():
        # Read until end of packet
        msg = uart.read().decode('utf-8')
        
        # Framing Check
        if "<" in msg and ">" in msg:
            try:
                # Extract clean data: e.g. "180,90,45"
                content = msg.split("<")[1].split(">")[0]
                angles = content.split(",")
                move_hand(angles)
            except:
                pass # Corrupt packet
                
    utime.sleep_ms(10)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Shared Ground**: If you don't connect the GND pin of Pico 1 to the GND pin of Pico 2, the UART signals will have no reference and will appear as "Gibberish" or nothing at all.
*   **Buffer Bloat**: If Pico 1 sends data faster than Pico 2 can read it, the UART buffer will fill up with old data. Ensure the "Wait" times on both sides are synchronized.
*   **String to Float**: UART data is always text ("180"). You must use the `to_number` block before sending it to a servo.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Wireless Upgrade**: Replace the UART wires with two HC-05 Bluetooth modules or NRF24L01 radios for true remote "Puppeteering."
*   **Haptic Return**: Use the unused RX/TX pins to send a signal BACK. If the robot hand touches something, make a vibration motor on the glove buzz.

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
*   **ADC**

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Comm, drag `send_map`**

### 7. Variables
*   **finger_pos**

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Glove**: Read Flex. `0 (Flat) -> 100 (Fist)`.
2.  **Send**: Packet `[10, 50, 90, 0, 0]`.
3.  **Hand**: Set Servos.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **User**: Makes fist.
2.  **Robot**: Makes fist.

### 10. Generated Code
``python
# Tx
val = adc.read_u16()
send(val)
# Rx
servo.duty_u16(recv())
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Range**: Flex sensors vary. Calibration (Open hand/Closed hand) needed per user.

### 12. Try This Next

## 1. Project 1449: Advanced Servo Kinematics Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Servo Harmonizer"
At the end of this project, you will be able to create an interactive calibration utility that identifies the exact "Electrical-to-Mechanical" limits of your specific servo motor, mapping the raw PWM microseconds to physical angles to ensure your robot moves exactly 90 degrees when you tell it to.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **PWM Pulse Widths**: Standard servos expect a pulse between 500μs (0°) and 2500μs (180°). However, "No-Name" servos often vary (e.g., 600μs to 2400μs). If you use the wrong numbers, your 90° command might actually result in 85°.
*   **Linearity Characterization**: You will learn how to test if a servo is "Linear." If you move from 0 to 10 degrees, does the arm move the same distance as from 170 to 180 degrees? 
*   **User-Assisted Calibration**: In professional robotics, a human "Teaches" the zero point. You will learn to use keys to "Nudge" the servo until it is perfectly vertical, then save that exact number as the new "Home."

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Servo Motor**
*   **Physical Protractor** (or paper printout)
*   **2 x Tactile Buttons** (Nudge Left / Nudge Right)
*   **USB Cable to PC** (for Data Logging)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo** | GP15 | Motor under test |
| **BTN Left** | GP16 | Decrease Pulse |
| **BTN Right** | GP17 | Increase Pulse |
| **Serial** | USB | Data Report |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `multiplication` and `subtraction`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `set_pwm_duty`.

### 7. Variables
*   **pulse_width**: Number (Current pulse in microseconds).
*   **actual_deg**: Number (Entered by user).
*   **error_margin**: Number (Calculation).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Finding the Physical Zero**
1.  Initialize the servo.
2.  Use the **Nudge Buttons** to increment/decrement the `pulse_width` by 10μs.
3.  When the arm is point exactly at the **0-degree** mark on your protractor, press BOTH buttons to save this as `Pulse_Min`.

**Phase B: Mapping the Spectrum**
4.  Standardize the sweep: move from `Pulse_Min` to `2500μs` in 10 steps.
5.  At each step, look at the physical arm.
6.  Input the "Physical Angle" into the Serial Terminal.
7.  The Pico will calculate the `error = Target - Actual`.

**Phase C: The Correction Table**
8.  The system will output a CSV list: `Pulse, Expected, Actual, Error`.
9.  Copy this into Excel to see the "Health" of your servo motor.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico moves servo to 1500μs (Center).
2.  **Interaction**: User nudges the arm until it is perfectly aligned with a 90-degree square.
3.  **Process**: The system records that 1540μs is the TRUE center for this specific motor.
4.  **Math**: The computer calculates the "Shift" (40μs offset).
5.  **Action**: The system sweeps to the ends of the range.
6.  **Result**: The user discovers that their "180-degree" servo actually only reaches 172 degrees. They now know to limit their code to 172 to prevent motor damage.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware
serv = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
serv.freq(50)
btn_l = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_DOWN)
btn_r = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_DOWN)

# Microseconds (approx 1638 to 8192 duty)
# 1 degree is roughly 36 units of duty_u16
current_duty = 4915 # 90 degrees approx

print("--- SERVO CALIBRATOR ---")
print("Use buttons to align arm to 90 degrees.")

while True:
    # 1. Manual Nudge
    if btn_l.value():
        current_duty -= 20
        utime.sleep(0.05)
    if btn_r.value():
        current_duty += 20
        utime.sleep(0.05)
        
    serv.duty_u16(current_duty)
    
    # 2. Capture Point
    if btn_l.value() and btn_r.value():
        print(f"CALIBRATION POINT: 90 deg = {current_duty} duty")
        # Now sweep and report
        for test_angle in [0, 45, 90, 135, 180]:
            # Guessing based on the 90-degree anchor
            # (Simplified linear math)
            offset = (test_angle - 90) * 36 
            test_duty = current_duty + offset
            serv.duty_u16(test_duty)
            print(f"Testing {test_angle} deg...")
            utime.sleep(1.5)
        
        print("Calibration test complete.")
        break

    utime.sleep_ms(10)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Mechanical Slop**: If your servo horn is loose on the shaft, the calibration will change every time it moves. Tighten the center screw!
*   **Parallax Error**: Looking at the arm from an angle. Always look perfectly top-down at the protractor for accurate readings.
*   **Under-Powering**: If the Pico is on a weak USB port, the servo might "Drop" position during a sweep. Use an external 5V supply if possible.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Map**: Create a function `get_duty(angle)` that uses your new calibration numbers instead of the default 1638-8192.
*   **Torque Test**: Hang a small weight on the arm. Does the "Zero Point" change when the motor is under load?

## 1. Project 1450: Advanced Advanced Servo Kinematics Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Robotic Hand Hub"
At the end of this project, you will be able to build a fully integrated multi-axis robotic hand prototype, featuring individual finger control via keyframes, a "Haptic feedback" safety trigger that stops motion if a jam is detected, and an OLED status dashboard.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **System Integration**: You will learn how to coordinate five different servos, two sensors, and an OLED display into a single "Main Loop" without the code becoming disorganized or slow.
*   **Human-Machine Interface (HMI)**: Designing a dashboard that shows the "Health" of the robot (Current Position, Safety Status, and Battery Level) so the operator can monitor the machine at a glance.
*   **Asynchronous-Style Loops**: Even though the Pico usually does one thing at a time, you will learn to structure your code so that the "Safety Watchdog" (Project 1443) checks for jams every 10ms while the "Animation Player" (Project 1444) moves the fingers.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **5 x Micro Servos** (Finger Motors)
*   **1 x Feedback Servo** (The Wrist)
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **Red LED and Active Buzzer**
*   **External 5V 2A Power Supply** (Essential for 6 motors)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Finger Servos 1-5** | GP10 to GP14 | Output |
| **Wrist Servo (FB)** | GP15 (PWM) / GP26 (ADC) | I/O |
| **OLED Display** | I2C0 (GP0/1) | Output |
| **Fault Indicator** | GP16 | Digital |
| **External VCC (5V)** | Servo Red Wires | Power |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Control**, drag `while_true`.
*   From **Lists**, drag `get_item_from_list`.
*   From **Math**, drag `average` and `map`.

### 7. Variables
*   **hand_state**: Text (IDLE, RECORDING, PLAYING, JAMMED).
*   **target_list**: List (Recorded frame sequence).
*   **safety_active**: Boolean (Master override).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: The Integrated Command Center**
1.  Initialize all 5 finger servos to 0 degrees (Open Hand).
2.  Initialize the OLED to show "ROBOT HAND V1.0 - INITIALIZING".
3.  Set `hand_state` to "IDLE".

**Phase B: The Safety Watchdog**
4.  Inside the loop, always read the **Wrist Feedback**.
5.  If the wrist is > 10 degrees away from its `target_pose` for more than 500ms:
    *   Set `hand_state` to "JAMMED".
    *   **Detach all 6 servos** immediately.
    *   Flash the Red LED and sound the Buzzer.

**Phase C: Multi-Finger Performance**
6.  If in "PLAY" mode:
    *   Iterate through the `target_list`.
    *   Each frame in the list should be another list of 5 numbers (one for each finger).
    *   Update all 5 servos simultaneously.
    *   Update the OLED with a progress bar: `PLAYING: [####----] 50%`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Hand is open. OLED shows "SYSTEM READY".
2.  **Interaction**: User triggers a pre-recorded "Grasp" sequence.
3.  **Process**: The fingers begin to curl together smoothly.
4.  **Math**: The safety loop confirms the wrist isn't straining.
5.  **Action**: A dummy object (a stick) blocks the fingers. 
6.  **Output**: The Wrist sensor detects the resistance. The system "Emergency Stops".
7.  **Result**: The OLED flashes "EMERGENCY JAM - CLEAR AND RESET" and the hand release all tension.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware
servos = [machine.PWM(machine.Pin(10+i)) for i in range(5)]
for s in servos: s.freq(50)
wrist_p = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
wrist_p.freq(50)
wrist_f = machine.ADC(26)
alert = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

# State
state = "IDLE"
frames = [[0,0,0,0,0], [90,90,90,90,90], [0,0,0,0,0]] # Open -> Fist -> Open

def check_safety():
    # Placeholder for Jam Detect logic from Project 1443
    actual = (wrist_f.read_u16() / 65535) * 180
    return True # Assume safe for demo

while True:
    if state == "IDLE":
        # Wait for trigger (Btn)
        state = "PLAYING"
        
    elif state == "PLAYING":
        for pose in frames:
            if not check_safety():
                state = "JAMMED"; break
            
            # Update all fingers
            for i in range(5):
                duty = int(1638 + (pose[i] / 180) * 6554)
                servos[i].duty_u16(duty)
            
            utime.sleep(0.5)
        
        if state != "JAMMED": state = "IDLE"

    elif state == "JAMMED":
        for s in servos: s.duty_u16(0)
        wrist_p.duty_u16(0)
        alert.value(1)
        print("CRITICAL FAULT: Hand Disengaged.")
        break # Lock out
        
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Brownout Trip**: 6 servos moving at once can draw 1.5 Amps. If you power them from the Pico's 5V pin, the voltage will dip, and the Pico will reboot itself in a loop. **Use a separate power supply for the motors!**
*   **Array Mismatch**: If your `target_list` has 5 numbers but your hand has 6 servos, the code will crash with an "Index Error."
*   **Spaghetti Wiring**: Use zip-ties! Mechanical movements can pull jumper wires out of breadboards mid-motion.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Voice Control**: Link the UART to an HC-05 (Bluetooth) and send commands like "GRASP" or "POINT" from a smartphone app.
*   **Pressure Sensitivity**: Add a Force Sensitive Resistor (FSR) to the fingertips. Stop the grasp when a specific pressure is reached (Project 1301).

## Batch 146: Advanced Light Sensing (LDR) 5 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1451: Advanced Light Sensing (LDR) Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Signal Purifier"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement "Signal Integration" for a Light Dependent Resistor (LDR), using a software-based accumulator to increase measurement resolution and eliminate high-frequency flicker (noise) from artificial lighting.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Integration Time**: Simple sensors jump around a lot. By "Integrating" (summing) many readings over a set time (e.g., 100ms), we can catch tiny light variations that a single sample would miss. This is how high-end laboratory light meters work.
*   **Averaging vs. Accumulating**: While an average gives you a "clean" number, an accumulator gives you a "deep" number. We can use this to detect extremely faint light sources in a dark room.
*   **Flicker Rejection**: Indoor LED or Fluorescent lights flicker at 50Hz or 60Hz. If we sample at the wrong time, our reading will be "jittery." Sampling over 100ms naturally "smooths" these ripples into one steady number.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Photoresistor (LDR)**
*   **10k Ohm Resistor** (Fixed pull-up/pull-down)
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR Side A** | 3.3V | Power |
| **LDR Side B / Resistor**| GP26 (ADC0) | Signal Junction |
| **Resistor Side B** | GND | Reference |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `sum` and `division`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `repeat_times`.
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `ldr_read`.

### 7. Variables
*   **acc**: Number (The storage for all readings).
*   **samples**: Number (Set to 100).
*   **clean_val**: Number (The result).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: The Sampling Burst**
1.  Initialize the ADC on `GP26`.
2.  Set `acc = 0`.
3.  Use a `repeat 100 times` loop:
    *   Read the LDR voltage.
    *   `increment acc` by the reading.
    *   Wait `1ms`. (100 repeats * 1ms = 100ms total integration).

**Phase B: Normalizing the Signal**
4.  After the loop, set `clean_val = acc / samples`.
5.  Print `clean_val` to the Serial Monitor.

**Phase C: Sensitivity Observation**
6.  Open the Serial Plotter.
7.  Cover the sensor slightly. Notice how the line is perfectly smooth and flat, without the "fuzz" (noise) seen in basic projects.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico prepares to "Listen" to the light.
2.  **Process**: 100 snapshots are taken very quickly.
3.  **Math**: The snapshots are stacked on top of each other. Random noise (+/- 1) cancels itself out across the 100 samples.
4.  **Action**: The computer calculates the middle ground.
5.  **Result**: The user gets a rock-solid light reading that can detect the difference between 5:00 PM and 5:05 PM light levels.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

ldr = machine.ADC(26)

def get_integrated_light(num_samples=100):
    accumulator = 0
    for _ in range(num_samples):
        accumulator += ldr.read_u16()
        utime.sleep_ms(1) # Integration step
        
    # Return average and total magnitude
    return accumulator // num_samples

while True:
    stable_light = get_integrated_light()
    print(f"LDR Signal (Integrated): {stable_light}")
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Integer Overflow**: If you sample 10,000 times, the total (65,535 * 10,000) will be a very large number. Python handles this automatically, but other languages might "Wrap around" to zero.
*   **Sampling Too Fast**: If you don't include the `1ms` wait, the integration period is too short to catch the 50/60Hz light flicker, and your readings will still be unstable.
*   **Floating Point Loss**: If you divide by a float `100.0`, you might lose binary precision. For the cleanest signal, keep it as a large raw integer as long as possible.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **The Light Cloud**: Can you calculate the **Peak-to-Peak** difference during the integration? If the difference is high, it means the light is flickering (Artificial). If it's low, it means the light is steady (Natural Sun).
*   **Sliding Window**: Change the code so it always keeps the *last* 100 readings in a list, updating only the oldest one each time (Project 1447).

## 1. Project 1452: Advanced Light Sensing (LDR) Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Exposure Master"
At the end of this project, you will be able to create a professional photography exposure meter, using logarithmic math to convert raw light levels into "Exposure Values" (EV) and suggesting Shutter Speeds and Aperture settings based on the lighting.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Logarithmic Perception**: Light intensity doubles at every "Step" (Step 1 is twice as bright as Step 0; Step 2 is twice as bright as Step 1). To calculate these steps, we use the `Log2` function. 
*   **The Exposure Triangle**: Photography relies on balance. If the room is dark, you need a larger "Aperture" (hole) or a longer "Shutter Speed" (time) to get a clear picture.
*   **Lux vs. EV**: You will learn how to convert the Pico's 0-65535 voltage signal into "Lux" (a scientific measurement of light) and then into EV (the photographer's measurement).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **LDR (Photoresistor)**
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **10k Ohm Resistor**
*   **Push Button** (Exposure Lock)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR Junction** | GP26 | Analog Input |
| **OLED SDA/SCL**| GP4 / GP5 | I2C0 |
| **BTN1** | GP16 | Digital Input |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `log_base_2` and `power_of`.
*   From **Text**, drag `join_text` and `to_string`.
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `write_line` (multiple).

### 7. Variables
*   **lux**: Number (Calculated intensity).
*   **ev**: Number (Exposure Value).
*   **shutter**: Text (The calculated timing, e.g., "1/60s").
*   **iso**: Number (Sensitivity, default 100).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Light to Lux**
1.  Read the LDR value.
2.  Use the "LDR to Lux" formula (Estimated): `lux = (65535 - ADC) / 100`. (Note: Real world Lux math depends on your specific LDR's Gamma value).

**Phase B: Lux to EV**
3.  Calculate the Exposure Value: `ev = log2(lux / 2.5)`.
4.  Round the `ev` to the nearest whole number.
5.  If `ev < 0`, set it to `0`.

**Phase C: The Camera Assistant**
6.  Suggest a Shutter Speed for a fixed Aperture of `f/2.8`:
    *   `speed = 2^(ev - 3)`.
    *   Format as a fraction (e.g., "1 / 60").
7.  Display "EV: [Value]", "Lux: [Value]", and "Suggested: 1/60s @ f2.8" on the OLED.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The OLED shows "READY - WAITING FOR LIGHT".
2.  **Interaction**: Point the sensor at a desk lamp. 
3.  **Process**: The Pico reads a high voltage.
4.  **Math**: Lux calculates as 1000. `log2(1000/2.5)` is approx `EV 8.6`.
5.  **Action**: The computer calculates that at EV 9, you need a shutter speed of 1/250s.
6.  **Result**: The user sees "EV 9 - 1/250s @ f/2.8" - professional settings for a perfect photo!

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import math

# Hardware
ldr = machine.ADC(26)
# oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)

def calculate_exposure():
    # 1. Raw to Lux (Simplified Curve)
    raw = ldr.read_u16()
    lux = (65535 - raw) / 50
    if lux <= 0: lux = 0.1
    
    # 2. Lux to EV (ISO 100)
    # Formula: EV = log2(lux * ISO / 250)
    ev = math.log2(lux / 2.5)
    
    # 3. EV to Shutter (at f/2.8)
    # T = N^2 / (2^EV) -> T = 2.8^2 / 2^ev
    shutter_sec = (2.8**2) / (2**ev)
    
    # Format Shutter String
    if shutter_sec < 1.0:
        shutter_str = f"1/{int(1/shutter_sec)}"
    else:
        shutter_str = f"{shutter_sec:.1f}s"
        
    return int(ev), int(lux), shutter_str

while True:
    e, l, s = calculate_exposure()
    
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text(f"Lux: {l}", 0, 0)
    oled.text(f"EV:  {e}", 0, 15)
    oled.text(f"f/2.8 @ {s}", 0, 35)
    oled.show()
    
    utime.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Linear Trap**: If you double the light hitting an LDR, the ADC value doesn't double—it might only change by 20%. You *must* use Logarithms to get an accurate photography reading.
*   **Infinity Errors**: In pitch black, Lux is 0. `log2(0)` is impossible (Infinity). Always use a `max(0.1, lux)` check to prevent the Pico from crashing.
*   **Bouncing Numbers**: Light flickers. Use the "Signal Integration" technique from Project 1451 before doing the EV math for a stable display.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Variable ISO**: Add a potentiometer to GP27. Turn the knob to change the ISO from 100 to 1600. Watch how the Shutter Speed gets faster as you increase ISO.
*   **Exposure Lock**: Program the button to "Pause" the screen so you can read the values after taking a measurement in a hard-to-reach spot.

## 1. Project 1453: Advanced Light Sensing (LDR) Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Smart Motion Trigger"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement "Adaptive Thresholding" (Background Subtraction), allowing your sensor to ignore slow changes in natural light (like sunset) completely but instantly trigger an alarm if a sudden "Shadow" (a person) or "Flash" (a flashlight) occurs.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Static vs. Adaptive Thresholds**: A "Static" threshold (e.g., `if light > 500`) fails because 500 might be "Bright" at night but "Dark" at noon. An "Adaptive" threshold continuously recalculates what "Normal" looks like.
*   **Moving Average**: We calculate the average light level of the last 60 seconds to establish a "Baseline."
*   **Deviation Detection**: The trigger happens only if the *current* reading is significantly different (+/- 20%) from the *baseline*. This logic is used in professional security cameras.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **LDR (Photoresistor)**
*   **Red LED** (Intruder Alert)
*   **Active Buzzer**
*   **10k Ohm Resistor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR Input** | GP26 | Analog |
| **Alarm LED** | GP16 | Digital Output |
| **Buzzer** | GP17 | Digital Output |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Lists**, drag `insert_at_start` and `get_average`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else`.
*   From **Math**, drag `multiplication`.

### 7. Variables
*   **history**: List (Stores last 20 readings).
*   **baseline**: Number (The calculated average).
*   **current**: Number (The specific reading right now).
*   **pct_diff**: Number (How distinct the event is).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Building the Baseline**
1.  Create an empty list `history`.
2.  Fill it with 20 readings of the *current* light level (Calibration Phase).
3.  Calculate `baseline = average(history)`.

**Phase B: The Continuous Watch**
4.  Inside the main loop:
    *   Read `current` light level.
    *   Compare: `if current < (baseline * 0.8)`: (20% Drop = Shadow).
    *   OR `if current > (baseline * 1.2)`: (20% Rise = Flashlight).
    *   If True -> Trigger Alarm.

**Phase C: The Drift update**
5.  If NO alarm is triggered:
    *   Remove the oldest item from `history`.
    *   Add `current` to the start of `history`.
    *   Recalculate `baseline`. This allows the system to "Learn" that the sun is setting.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The room is brightly lit (Value 50,000). The Baseline is 50,000.
2.  **Event (Slow)**: The sun sets over 1 hour. The value drops to 10,000.
3.  **Reaction**: The Baseline slowly follows the drop. No alarm sounds because `50,000 -> 49,900` is less than 20% diff.
4.  **Event (Fast)**: A thief walks past, casting a shadow. Value drops from 10,000 to 5,000 instantly.
5.  **Reaction**: `5,000` is 50% of the `10,000` baseline. **ALARM TRIPPED!**

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware
ldr = machine.ADC(26)
led = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
buz = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.OUT)

# Logic State
history = []
max_hist = 20

print("Calibrating environment...")
for _ in range(max_hist):
    history.append(ldr.read_u16())
    utime.sleep(0.1)
    
print("System Armed.")

while True:
    current = ldr.read_u16()
    
    # Calculate Baseline
    baseline = sum(history) / len(history)
    
    # Thresholds (20% sensitivity)
    lower_limit = baseline * 0.8
    upper_limit = baseline * 1.2
    
    # Check
    if current < lower_limit:
        print(f"SHADOW DETECTED! Val: {current} Base: {baseline}")
        led.value(1); buz.value(1)
        utime.sleep(1) # Alarm duration
        # DO NOT update history during alarm event
        
    elif current > upper_limit:
        print(f"FLASH DETECTED! Val: {current} Base: {baseline}")
        led.value(1); buz.value(1)
        utime.sleep(1)
        
    else:
        # Peace - Update Baseline logic
        led.value(0); buz.value(0)
        history.pop(0) # Remove old
        history.append(current) # Add new
        
    utime.sleep(0.2)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Initialization Shock**: If you start the `history` list with Zeros, the `baseline` will be very low at startup. The first real reading will look like a "Massive Flash" (0 -> 50,000) and trip the alarm. Always pre-fill the history.
*   **Polluting the Baseline**: If you add the "Shadow" reading to your history list, the baseline will drop, and the system effectively "learns" to ignore the thief. *Never* update the background model while an active event is occurring.
*   **Sensitivity**: 20% is a good starting point. If it triggers falsely, try 30% (`* 0.7` and `* 1.3`).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dynamic Sensitivity**: Use a potentiometer to adjust the percentage sensitivity (10% to 50%) in real-time.
*   **Directional Motion**: Use two LDRs. If LDR_A dips before LDR_B, the person is walking Left-to-Right!

---

## 1. Project 1454: Advanced Light Sensing (LDR) Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Day/Night Cycle"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement "State Hysteresis" to prevent system rapid-switching at twilight, while rendering graphical icons (Sun/Moon) on an OLED display to indicate the current mode.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Hysteresis (The Toggle Gap)**: If you turn a light ON at 100 and OFF at 100, then when the value is 100, the light will flicker uncontrollably. We use *Hysteresis* to separate the thresholds: Turn ON at 80, Turn OFF at 120. The gap in the middle prevents jitter.
*   **Persistent State**: The system remembers "I am currently in NIGHT mode" and refuses to switch until a strong signal confirms the change.
*   **Bitmap Icons**: You will learn how to verify the mode visually by drawing a filled circle (Sun) or a crescent (Moon) on the screen.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **LDR (Photoresistor)**
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **10k Ohm Resistor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR Input** | GP26 | Analog |
| **OLED** | I2C0 (GP0/GP1) | Output |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `draw_icon`, `draw_circle` (or `fill_rect`).
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else_if`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_global`.

### 7. Variables
*   **is_daytime**: Boolean (True/False).
*   **lux_level**: Number (Filtered light reading).
*   **THRESHOLD_LOW**: Number (e.g., 2000 - Transition to Night).
*   **THRESHOLD_HIGH**: Number (e.g., 4000 - Transition to Day).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Determining the State**
1.  Read the light level (use integration from Project 1451).
2.  If **light > THRESHOLD_HIGH**:
    *   Set `is_daytime = True`.
3.  Else If **light < THRESHOLD_LOW**:
    *   Set `is_daytime = False`.
4.  (Note: If the light is between LOW and HIGH, we do nothing. We stay in the *previous* state).

**Phase B: Render Logic**
5.  Clear the OLED.
6.  If `is_daytime` is **True**:
    *   Draw a filled circle at `(64, 32)` with radius `20` (The Sun).
    *   Draw lines radiating out (Rays).
7.  Else:
    *   Draw a filled circle at `(64, 32)`.
    *   Draw a black circle at `(54, 32)` to "Cut out" the crescent shape (The Moon).
8.  Display text "MODE: DAY" or "MODE: NIGHT".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Dawn (Light = 1000)**: Start in NIGHT mode.
2.  **Sunrise (Light = 3000)**: Light is increasing, but still below HIGH (4000). System stays NIGHT.
3.  **Morning (Light = 4500)**: Light crosses HIGH. System clicks to DAY mode.
4.  **Cloud passes (Light = 3500)**: Light drops, but stays above LOW (2000). System stays DAY.
5.  **Dusk (Light = 1900)**: Light crosses LOW. System clicks to NIGHT mode.
6.  **Result**: The system is rock-stable and never flickers, even on cloudy days.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware
ldr = machine.ADC(26)
# oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)

# Hysteresis Config
THRESH_NIGHT = 20000 
THRESH_DAY = 40000 
is_day = True # Default state

while True:
    # 1. Read Light
    val = ldr.read_u16()
    
    # 2. Update State (Hysteresis)
    if val > THRESH_DAY:
        is_day = True
    elif val < THRESH_NIGHT:
        is_day = False
    # Else: Keep current state
    
    # 3. Render
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text(f"Light: {val}", 0, 0)
    
    if is_day:
        oled.text("MODE: DAY", 0, 55)
        # Draw Sun
        oled.fill_rect(50, 20, 28, 28, 1) # Square Sun for simplicity
        oled.line(64, 10, 64, 50, 1) # Rays
        oled.line(44, 34, 84, 34, 1)
    else:
        oled.text("MODE: NIGHT", 0, 55)
        # Draw Moon (Square with a bite taken out)
        oled.fill_rect(50, 20, 28, 28, 1)
        oled.fill_rect(45, 25, 20, 20, 0) # Black box erases part of moon
        
    oled.show()
    utime.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Single Threshold**: Students often write `if val > 3000: Day else: Night`. At exactly 3000, noise will make it flip-flop `Day..Night..Day..Night` 50 times a second. Always use two separate thresholds.
*   **OLED Burn-in**: If you leave the "Sun" on the screen for 2 years, it will burn a permanent ghost image. Screen savers or dimming logic are needed for real products.
*   **Inverted Logic**: LDRs can be wired "Pull Up" or "Pull Down." Check if `Bright` means `65535` or `0` on your specific board!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Streetlamp**: Add an LED on GP15. Turn it ON when it becomes Night, but ensure it doesn't turn OFF if a car's headlights (temporary flash) hit the sensor. (Combine with Project 1453).
*   **Smooth Transition**: Instead of a binary Day/Night, can you make a "Sunset" icon that slowly lowers on the screen based on light level?

## 1. Project 1455: Automated Advanced Light Sensing (LDR) Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Helios Field"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a "Hill Climbing" optimization algorithm to control a mirror (Heliostat), making it automatically search for the angle that reflects the most sunlight onto a central "Tower" target.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Concentrated Solar Power (CSP)**: Large power plants use thousands of mirrors to reflect sunlight onto a single steam generator. They must track the sun with extreme precision.
*   **Feedback Loops (External)**: Unlike previous projects where the sensor was ON the robot, here the sensor is on the TARGET (The Tower). The robot must move its mirror blindly until the Tower says "That's bright!"
*   **Hill Climbing Algorithm**: A strategy where the robot tries moving a little bit Left. If the signal gets better, it keeps going Left. If it gets worse, it goes Right. It "Climbs the hill" of intensity until it finds the peak.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Servo Motor** (The Mirror mount)
*   **LDR (Photoresistor)** (The "Tower" Target)
*   **Laser Pointer** (Optional, to simulate the sun reflection)
*   **Breadboard and Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Mirror Servo** | GP15 | Output |
| **Tower LDR** | GP26 | Input |
| **Status LED** | GP16 | Indicator |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Variables**, drag `change_by`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else`.
*   From **Control**, drag `repeat_forever`.

### 7. Variables
*   **current_angle**: Number (0-180).
*   **last_brightness**: Number (Memory of the previous reading).
*   **step_direction**: Number (+1 or -1).
*   **new_brightness**: Number.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: The Initial Scan**
1.  Set `current_angle = 90`.
2.  Set `step_direction = 1` (Moving Clockwise).
3.  Read the **Tower LDR** and save it to `last_brightness`.

**Phase B: The Step-and-Check**
4.  Move the servo by `step_direction` (add 1 degree).
5.  Wait `100ms` for the mirror to settle.
6.  Read the **Tower LDR** as `new_brightness`.

**Phase C: The Optimization Choice**
7.  Check: `If new_brightness > last_brightness`:
    *   **Good Move!** Keep `step_direction` as is. (We are climbing the hill).
    *   Set `last_brightness = new_brightness`.
8.  Else (`new_brightness < last_brightness`):
    *   **Bad Move!** We passed the peak or went the wrong way.
    *   Flip the direction: `step_direction = step_direction * -1`.
    *   (Optional) Move back 1 degree immediately.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The mirror points at 90. The Tower reads "Dim" (500).
2.  **Move 1**: Mirror goes to 91. Tower reads "Refection hit me!" (2000).
3.  **Result**: 2000 > 500. Keep going +1.
4.  **Move 2**: Mirror goes to 92. Tower reads "Bright!" (3000).
5.  **Move 3**: Mirror goes to 93. Tower reads "Less Bright" (2500).
6.  **Result**: 2500 < 3000. We went too far. Reverse direction to -1.
7.  **Final**: The mirror oscillates slightly between 91-93, keeping the beam locked on the target.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware
serv = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
serv.freq(50)
tower = machine.ADC(26)
led = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

# State
angle = 90
step = 1
last_lux = 0

def move_servo(deg):
    duty = int(1638 + (deg / 180) * 6554)
    serv.duty_u16(duty)

print("Searching for Solar Peak...")

while True:
    # 1. Make a move
    angle += step
    
    # Mechanical Limits
    if angle > 160: angle = 160; step = -1
    if angle < 20: angle = 20; step = 1
        
    move_servo(angle)
    utime.sleep(0.1) # Wait for mechanical movement
    
    # 2. Check result
    current_lux = tower.read_u16()
    
    # 3. Hill Climb Logic
    # Hysteresis: Only change if meaningful difference (>1000)
    if current_lux > (last_lux + 500):
        # Brighter! Keep going this way
        last_lux = current_lux
        led.value(1) # Locked on
    elif current_lux < (last_lux - 500):
        # Encoder: We walked down the hill. Turn around!
        step *= -1
        last_lux = current_lux
        led.value(0)
    
    # Debug
    print(f"Ang:{angle} Lux:{current_lux} Dir:{step}")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Infinite Spin**: If your LDR reading is noisy, the code might flip direction randomly. Project 1451's "Integration" is crucial here to get a stable signal.
*   **Moving Too Fast**: Servos take time to travel. If you read the light sensor 1ms after sending the command, the mirror hasn't moved yet! Always `sleep` at least 100ms.
*   **Local Maxima**: If there are two bright lights, the mirror might get stuck on the "smaller" one and never find the "brighter" one. A full sweep scan at startup solves this.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual Axis**: Add a second servo for Tilt. Can you hill-climb X and Y at the same time? (Hint: Maximize X for 1 second, then Maximize Y for 1 second).
*   **Wireless Tower**: Put the LDR on a second Pico and send the brightness value back to the Mirror Pico via UART or Bluetooth (Project 1448).

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Cloud**: If cloud blocks sun, system gets confused. Stop tracking.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Calculated**: Use RTC and GPS to calc sun position instead.

---

## 1. Project 1456: Advanced Light Sensing (LDR) Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Arc Flash Breaker"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a high-speed "Slope Detection" safety system that identifies an instantaneous, blinding spike of light (like an electrical arc flash or welding spark) and trips a relay within milliseconds to cut power.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Rate of Change (The First Derivative)**: Instead of asking "Is it bright?", we ask "Did it get bright *fast*?" A slow sunrise is safe. A 0ms-to-10ms jump from dark to blinded is dangerous.
*   **Reaction Latency**: In safety systems, `sleep(1)` is forbidden. You will learn to write tight, non-blocking loops that check sensors thousands of times per second.
*   **Fail-Safe Latching**: Once the breaker trips, it must *stay* tripped until a human physically presses a Reset button. It cannot auto-reset.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **LDR (Photoresistor)**
*   **Relay Module** (Controls the "High Voltage" - simulated with an LED)
*   **Reset Button**
*   **Piezo Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Function |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR Input** | GP26 | Arc Sensor |
| **Relay Signal**| GP15 | Power Control |
| **Reset Btn** | GP14 | Safety Reset |
| **Status LED** | GP25 (Onboard)| Heartbeat |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `subtraction` and `absolute`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `set_pin_state`.

### 7. Variables
*   **prev_light**: Number (The reading 1ms ago).
*   **curr_light**: Number (The reading now).
*   **slope**: Number (The speed of the flash).
*   **tripped**: Boolean (System state).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: The High-Speed Loop**
1.  Initialize the Relay to **ON** (System Normal).
2.  Read `prev_light` once at checking.
3.  Enter a `while True` loop.
4.  Read `curr_light`.

**Phase B: The Derivative Calculation**
5.  Calculate `slope = curr_light - prev_light`.
6.  If `slope > 5000` (A massive jump in one cycle):
    *   **TRIP!** Turn Relay **OFF**.
    *   Turn Buzzer **ON**.
    *   Set `tripped = True`.
7.  Update `prev_light = curr_light`.

**Phase C: The Latch**
8.  If `tripped` is True:
    *   Lock the loop. Do not check sensor.
    *   Check **Reset Button**.
    *   If pressed: Reset `tripped = False`, turn Relay **ON**, Silence Buzzer.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Normal**: Light is steady (20,000). Slope is ~10. Relay is Closed.
2.  **Event**: A camera flash fires.
3.  **Physics**: Value jumps 20,000 -> 60,000 in 1ms.
4.  **Math**: Slope is 40,000. This is > 5,000.
5.  **Action**: Pico cuts power instantly.
6.  **Result**: The "Machinery" stops before the arc can cause damage. The system waits for an operator.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware
ldr = machine.ADC(26)
relay = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)
reset_btn = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_DOWN)
buzzer = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

# Config
relay.value(1) # Power ON
tripped = False
last_val = ldr.read_u16()

print("System Active. Monitoring for Arc Flash...")

while True:
    if tripped:
        # Latch Mode
        if reset_btn.value():
            print("Manual Reset Confirmed.")
            tripped = False
            relay.value(1) # Power ON
            buzzer.value(0)
            utime.sleep(1) # Debounce
            last_val = ldr.read_u16() # Reset baseline
        continue

    # Fast Monitoring
    curr_val = ldr.read_u16()
    slope = curr_val - last_val
    
    # 5000 is an empirical threshold for "Instant Flash"
    if slope > 5000:
        print(f"ARC DETECTED! Slope: {slope}. TRIPPING BREAKERS!")
        relay.value(0) # Cut Power
        buzzer.value(1)
        tripped = True
    
    last_val = curr_val
    # minimal delay for max speed
    utime.sleep_us(500) 
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Negative Slope**: If you turn the lights OFF quickly, the slope is `-5000`. If you only check `abs(slope) > 5000`, the breaker will trip when you go home for the day! Only check for positive spikes (`> 5000`).
*   **Sensor Saturation**: If the room is already super bright (65,000), a flash won't register because the sensor is maxed out. These systems work best in dimmer industrial environments.
*   **Blocking Code**: If you put `print()` inside the high-speed loop, the USB communication will slow the Pico down, and you might miss the flash peak. Only print *after* the trip.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual Channel**: Arc flashes are often blue/UV. Use a UV photodiode alongside the LDR. Only trip if *both* spike at once (Reduces false alarms from flashlights).
*   **Reaction Timer**: Connect an LED to another pin. Turn it on at the exact moment of the flash, and measure the time difference on an oscilloscope. Can you get under 1ms?
## 1. Project 1457: Advanced Light Sensing (LDR) Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Bulb Life Monitor"
At the end of this project, you will be able to create a "Usage Tracker" that logs exactly how many hours, minutes, and seconds a light source has been turned ON, saving this data to non-volatile memory so it persists even if the Pico loses power.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Predictive Maintenance**: Real-world machines (like projector bulbs or UV curing lamps) have a rated lifespan (e.g., 2000 hours). Tracking usage helps replace them *before* they fail.
*   **Non-Volatile Storage (NVS)**: Writing data to a text file on the Pico's internal flash memory ensures the "Total Hours" count isn't lost when you unplug the USB.
*   **State Integration**: We only count time when the LDR confirms the light is physically ON, avoiding "False Run" hours if a relay clicks but the bulb is burnt out.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **LDR (Photoresistor)**
*   **OLED Display** (To show the counter)
*   **Reset Button** (To reset counter after bulb replacement)
*   **10k Ohm Resistor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Function |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR Input** | GP26 | Light Sensor |
| **Reset Btn** | GP14 | Maintenance Reset |
| **OLED** | I2C0 | Dashboard |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **File**, drag `append_to_file` and `read_file`.
*   From **Time**, drag `time_seconds`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `change_by`.

### 7. Variables
*   **total_seconds**: Number (Loaded from file at startup).
*   **session_start**: Number (Time when light turned on).
*   **is_lit**: Boolean (Current state).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: The Memory System**
1.  On startup, try to read `usage.txt`.
2.  If it exists, parse the number into `total_seconds`.
3.  If not, create it and write "0".

**Phase B: The Counting Logic**
4.  Check LDR.
5.  If Light > 1000 AND `is_lit` is False:
    *   Light just turned ON. Set `is_lit = True`.
    *   Record `session_start = time.time()`.
6.  If Light < 1000 AND `is_lit` is True:
    *   Light just turned OFF. Set `is_lit = False`.
    *   Calculate `duration = time.time() - session_start`.
    *   Add `duration` to `total_seconds`.
    *   **Save** `total_seconds` to `usage.txt`.

**Phase C: The Dashboard**
7.  Display "TOTAL HOURS: X" on the OLED.
8.  If the Reset Button is held for 5 seconds, clear the file to "0".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Status**: Counter reads "10h 0m".
2.  **Action**: User turns on the lamp.
3.  **Process**: Pico detects light. It starts a stopwatch.
4.  **Action**: User runs lamp for 1 hour, then turns if off.
5.  **Process**: Pico detects darkness. It stops the stopwatch.
6.  **Saving**: Pico adds 3600 seconds to the total and writes "11h 0m" to the Flash storage.
7.  **Power Cut**: User unplugs the Pico.
8.  **Reboot**: User plugs it back in. The counter correctly resumes at "11h 0m".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import os

# Hardware
ldr = machine.ADC(26)
btn_reset = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_DOWN)

# File Management
filename = "bulb_life.txt"
total_sec = 0

# 1. Load previous Data
try:
    with open(filename, "r") as f:
        data = f.read()
        if data:
            total_sec = int(data)
            print(f"Loaded Usage: {total_sec}s")
except OSError:
    print("New installation. Creating log file.")
    with open(filename, "w") as f:
        f.write("0")

# State
is_on = False
start_time = 0
SAVE_INTERVAL = 60 # Save every minute to be safe

while True:
    # 2. Reset Logic
    if btn_reset.value():
        print("RESETTING COUNTER...")
        total_sec = 0
        with open(filename, "w") as f: f.write("0")
        utime.sleep(2)

    # 3. Monitor Light
    light_val = ldr.read_u16()
    
    if light_val > 10000: # Light is ON
        if not is_on:
            is_on = True
            start_time = utime.time()
            print("Bulb ON - Timer Started")
        else:
            # Already ON - Update temporary visual
            current_session = utime.time() - start_time
            # Every 60s, commit to disk to prevent data loss on crash
            if current_session % SAVE_INTERVAL == 0:
                 with open(filename, "w") as f:
                    f.write(str(total_sec + current_session))
                    
    else: # Light is OFF
        if is_on:
            is_on = False
            elapsed = utime.time() - start_time
            total_sec += elapsed
            print(f"Bulb OFF. Added {elapsed}s. Total: {total_sec}s")
            # Final Save
            with open(filename, "w") as f:
                f.write(str(total_sec))
    
    utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Flash Wear**: Flash memory has a lifespan (approx 100,000 writes). If you write the time to the file *every second*, you will destroy the Pico chip in a few days. Only save when the light turns OFF or at long intervals (e.g., every 15 mins).
*   **Corrupted Files**: If you unplug the Pico *while* it is writing (the `f.write()` line), the file might become blank. Robust systems use two files (Backup A and Backup B).
*   **Voltage Sag**: LDRs change resistance slightly as temperature changes. Ensure your ON/OFF threshold (10000) has a wide safety margin.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Maintenance Alert**: Add an LED that starts blinking Red if `total_seconds > 7,200,000` (2000 hours), signaling the user to buy a new bulb.
*   **Efficiency**: Use the `RTC` (Real Time Clock) to log the exact Date/Time the bulb failed.
## 1. Project 1458: Smart Advanced Light Sensing (LDR) System

### 2. Learning Objective
"The DALI Simulator"
At the end of this project, you will be able to simulate a DALI (Digital Addressable Lighting Interface) ballast, allowing your Pico to be "Addressed" individually on a shared bus to control brightness, scene recall, and status reporting.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Addressable Buses**: In a large office building, 64 lights might share ONE pair of wires. To control just *one* light, we send a digital message: `[Address 5] [Go to 50% Example]`.
*   **Logarithmic Dimming**: DALI uses a specific curve because human eyes are logarithmic. 50% electrical power looks like 80% brightness. DALI compensates for this.
*   **Manchester Encoding**: The DALI protocol doesn't use simple "High/Low." It uses "Bit Transitions" (Rising/Falling edges) to ensure signals travel reliably over 300 meters of wire. (We will simulate the logic layer here).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **2 x Raspberry Pi Pico**
    *   **Pico A**: The "Controller" (Wall Switch).
    *   **Pico B**: The "Ballast" (Light Fixture).
*   **High-Power LED** (or standard LED for sim)
*   **UART Connection** (Crossed TX/RX)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico A (Master) | Pico B (Slave 05) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Bus TX** | GP0 | GP1 (RX) |
| **Bus RX** | GP1 | GP0 (TX) |
| **LED Output**| - | GP15 (PWM) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Text**, drag `create_text_with` and `split`.
*   From **Math**, drag `power`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `uart_write`.

### 7. Variables
*   **my_address**: Text (e.g., "05").
*   **target_address**: Text.
*   **command**: Text (e.g., "DIM", "OFF", "MAX").
*   **arc_power**: Number (0-254 DALI steps).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: The Master Commander (Pico A)**
1.  Initialize UART.
2.  Send commands in format: `ADDR:CMD:VAL`.
3.  Simulate a "Scene":
    *   `05:SET:200` (Light 5 to High).
    *   `05:SET:10` (Light 5 to Dim).

**Phase B: The Intelligent Ballast (Pico B)**
4.  Set `my_address = "05"`.
5.  Check UART. If message received:
    *   Split text by `:`.
    *   If `Part[0] == my_address` (or "FF" for Broadcast):
        *   Execute `Part[1]`.
        *   If `Part[1] == "SET"`, set `arc_power = Part[2]`.

**Phase C: The Logarithmic Driver**
6.  The `arc_power` (0-254) must be converted to PWM (0-65535).
7.  Use formula: `PWM = 10 ^ ((arc_power - 1) / 253 * 3)`. (Simplified Log Curve).
8.  Update LED Duty Cycle.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico B is waiting. LED is Off.
2.  **Command**: Pico A sends `05:SET:128`.
3.  **Process**: Pico B sees "05" matches. It takes "128".
4.  **Math**: DALI level 128 is roughly 15% brightness (due to log curve), not 50%.
5.  **Output**: Pico B sets PWM to roughly 10,000.
6.  **Broadcast**: Pico A sends `FF:OFF:0`.
7.  **Result**: Pico B (and all other Picos) turns off because "FF" is the "Everyone" address.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import math

# Ballast Setup (Pico B)
uart = machine.UART(0, baudrate=9600, tx=machine.Pin(0), rx=machine.Pin(1))
led = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
led.freq(1000)

MY_ADDR = "05"
arc_level = 0

def dali_curve(level):
    if level == 0: return 0
    # DALI Standard IEC 62386 curve approx
    # output = 10^((level-1)/253 * 3) scaled to 65535
    exponent = (level - 1) / 253 * 3
    brightness_percent = math.pow(10, exponent) / 1000 
    return int(brightness_percent * 65535)

print(f"Ballast {MY_ADDR} Ready.")

while True:
    if uart.any():
        try:
            msg = uart.read().decode('utf-8').strip()
            # Format: "ADDR:CMD:VAL"
            parts = msg.split(":")
            
            target = parts[0]
            cmd = parts[1]
            val = int(parts[2])
            
            if target == MY_ADDR or target == "FF":
                print(f"Executing {cmd} with {val}")
                
                if cmd == "SET":
                    arc_level = val
                elif cmd == "OFF":
                    arc_level = 0
                elif cmd == "MAX":
                    arc_level = 254
                    
                # Apply Physics
                duty = dali_curve(arc_level)
                led.duty_u16(duty)
                
        except Exception as e:
            print("Bus Error:", e)
            
    utime.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Linear Dimming**: If you just map 0-254 directly to 0-65535, the dimming will look "Wrong." It will drop from Bright to Half-Bright instantly, then spend forever fading the last tiny bit. The Log curve is essential for "Natural" lighting.
*   **Baud Rate Mismatch**: DALI is slow (1200 baud), but UART can be fast. Ensure Master and Slave agree (e.g., 9600).
*   **Voltage Levels**: Real DALI runs at 16V. **DO NOT** connect a Pico to a real DALI bus wires without an Optoisolator circuit, or you will fry the chip!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Fade Time**: DALI lights don't snap on; they fade. Add a function that smoothly transitions `current_level` to `target_level` over 2 seconds.
*   **Query Status**: Implement a `QUERY` command. If Master sends `05:QUERY:0`, Pico B should reply `05:OK:128` (Current Level) back on TX.
## 1. Project 1459: Advanced Light Sensing (LDR) Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Lux Reference"
At the end of this project, you will be able to calibrate an inexpensive LDR using "Two-Point Calibration" (Gain and Offset) to make it agree with a professional Lux Meter, creating a reliable instrument for scientific experiments.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Sensor Characterization**: LDRs are not identical. One might read 500 at noon, while another reads 600. We must characterize the specific sensor we are holding.
*   **Gain vs. Offset**:
    *   **Offset (The Shift)**: If the sensor reads 10 in total darkness, we subtract 10. `y = x - 10`.
    *   **Gain (The Slope)**: If the sensor reads 100 when the real Lux is 200, we multiply by 2. `y = x * 2`.
*   **Linear Regression**: The mathematical process of finding the "Line of Best Fit" that connects our calibration points.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **LDR (Photoresistor)**
*   **Reference Lux Meter** (Use a phone app like "Lux Light Meter" as a substitute)
*   **OLED Display**
*   **Potentiometer** (Used to establish different light levels) - *Optional, or just move closer/further from a lamp.*

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR Input** | GP26 | Raw Signal |
| **OLED** | I2C0 | User Interface |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `arithmetic` (+, -, *, /).
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_global`.
*   From **Text**, drag `join`.

### 7. Variables
*   **raw_dark_ref**: Number (ADC reading in darkness).
*   **raw_bright_ref**: Number (ADC reading in brightness).
*   **lux_bright_ref**: Number (Real Lux value from reference meter).
*   **m_slope**: Number (Gain).
*   **c_intercept**: Number (Offset).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Zeroing (Dark Calibration)**
1.  Cover the sensor completely (Blackout).
2.  Read `raw_dark_ref`. (Example: 500).
3.  We define "Dark" as 0 Lux. Therefore, our Offset (c) = `-500`.

**Phase B: Spanning (Bright Calibration)**
4.  Expose the sensor to a bright lamp.
5.  Read `raw_bright_ref`. (Example: 60,500).
6.  Use your Reference Meter to measure the REAL value. (Example: 1000 Lux).
7.  Calculate Gain (m):
    *   `Change in Lux = 1000 - 0 = 1000`.
    *   `Change in Raw = 60500 - 500 = 60000`.
    *   `m = 1000 / 60000 = 0.0166`.

**Phase C: The Correction Engine**
8.  In the loop: `ADC = Read()`.
9.  `Lux = (ADC - offset) * gain`.
10. Display `Lux` on OLED.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: System uses default calibration.
2.  **Calibrate**: User enters calibration mode, records Dark and Bright points.
3.  **Process**: Pico computes `m` and `c`.
4.  **Verify**: User points sensor at a window.
5.  **Result**: Pico reads 500 Lux. Phone App reads 505 Lux. The error is < 1%. (Success).

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

ldr = machine.ADC(26)

# Default Calibration (Approximation)
OFFSET = 500   # Raw value in dark
GAIN = 0.0166  # Slope

def calibrate():
    global OFFSET, GAIN
    print("--- CALIBRATION START ---")
    
    # 1. Dark Point
    print("Cover Sensor! Waiting 5s...")
    utime.sleep(5)
    OFFSET = ldr.read_u16()
    print(f"Dark Level (Offset): {OFFSET}")
    
    # 2. Bright Point
    print("Expose to Lamp! Waiting 5s...")
    utime.sleep(5)
    raw_bright = ldr.read_u16()
    
    # 3. Enter Reference
    real_lux = float(input("Enter Ref Lux Meter Value: "))
    
    # 4. Calculate Slope
    # Lux = (Raw - Offset) * Gain
    # Gain = Lux / (Raw - Offset)
    if raw_bright > OFFSET:
         GAIN = real_lux / (raw_bright - OFFSET)
         print(f"New Gain: {GAIN}")
         print("Validation: (Bright - Offset) * Gain = Matches Ref?")
    else:
         print("Error: Bright < Dark. Check Sensor.")

# Uncomment to run interactive calibration once
# calibrate()

while True:
    raw = ldr.read_u16()
    
    # Apply Calibration
    # Ensure we don't go negative if raw < OFFSET due to noise
    if raw < OFFSET: raw = OFFSET
        
    cal_lux = (raw - OFFSET) * GAIN
    
    print(f"Raw: {raw} -> Lux: {cal_lux:.1f}")
    utime.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Assuming Linearity**: LDRs are *not* linear devices! They are approximately logarithmic. This 2-point linear calibration assumes we are working within a small range (e.g., typical room lighting). For wide-range accuracy (Moonlight to Sunlight), you need a Lookup Table (LUT).
*   **Temperature Drift**: If the room is hotter, the LDR resistance changes, invalidating a precise calibration.
*   **Shadows**: When calibrating, ensure your hand isn't casting a shadow on the sensor or the reference meter.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Multi-Point Maps**: Measure 5 points (Dark, Dim, Medium, Bright, Sun). Store them in a Python dictionary. Write a function that interpolates between the two closest points for better accuracy.
*   **Save to Flash**: Save your `GAIN` and `OFFSET` to a file (Project 1457) so you don't have to recalibrate every time you reboot.

## 1. Project 1460: Advanced Advanced Light Sensing (LDR) Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Li-Fi Transceiver"
At the end of this project, you will be able to build a "Visible Light Communication" (VLC) system, allowing two Raspberry Pi Picos to send text messages wirelessly across a room using nothing but a blinking Torch LED and a light sensor.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Li-Fi (Light Fidelity)**: Radio waves (Wi-Fi) are crowded. Light waves (Li-Fi) are free and secure. If you can see the light, you can get the data. If you close the curtains, the signal stops (perfect security).
*   **Modulation Schemes**: We cannot just turn the light ON for "1" and OFF for "0" because ambient light (Room Lights) would confuse the sensor.
*   **OOK (On-Off Keying) with Preamble**: We send a known pattern "10101010" (The Preamble) to tell the receiver "Hey! A message is coming!" before sending the actual data.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **2 x Raspberry Pi Pico**
    *   **Pico TX (Transmitter)**: Controls the LED.
    *   **Pico RX (Receiver)**: Reads the LDR.
*   **High-Power White LED** (The data beam).
*   **Fast LDR or Phototransistor** (The eye).
*   **Cardboard Tube** (To shield LDR from room lights).

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Type | Transmitter (Pico A) | Receiver (Pico B) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Light Source** | GP15 (LED Anode) | - |
| **Light Sensor** | - | GP26 (ADC) |
| **Status LED** | GP25 (Onboard) | GP25 (Onboard) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Text**, drag `to_bytes`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `pwm_set_duty` (for TX) and `adc_read` (for RX).
*   From **Control**, drag `wait_us` (Microseconds).

### 7. Variables
*   **message**: Text ("HELLO").
*   **bit_stream**: List (0,1,0,0,1,0,0,0...).
*   **threshold**: Number (Dynamic Decision Level).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: The Transmitter (TX)**
1.  Take a text string "ABC".
2.  Convert it to ASCII binary (A = 01000001).
3.  Add a "Start Bit" (1) and "Stop Bit" (0) to each byte.
4.  Flash the LED: 
    *   **1**: High Brightness (65535).
    *   **0**: Low Brightness (30000). (Note: We don't turn off completely to keep the link "Alive").
    *   Speed: 10ms per bit (100 Baud).

**Phase B: The Receiver (RX)**
5.  Continuously read the LDR.
6.  Calculate `Dynamic Threshold = Average(Last 10 readings)`.
7.  If `Read > Threshold + Margin`: Record a **1**.
8.  If `Read < Threshold - Margin`: Record a **0**.
9.  Assemble bits into bytes and print the characters.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Setup**: Align the LED pointer to hit the LDR inside the tube.
2.  **Idle**: The LED glows dimly (Idle State). The RX sees "Silence".
3.  **Transmission**: TX flashes brightly: "1-1-1-1" (Preamble). RX wakes up.
4.  **Data**: LED flickers like a Morse code strobe.
5.  **Reception**: RX Console prints: "H...E...L...L...O...".
6.  **Interruption**: You wave your hand in the beam.
7.  **Result**: RX prints "H...E...L...L...[Packet Lost]". Security confirmed.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
# --- TRANSMITTER CODE (Pico A) ---
import machine
import utime

led = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
led.freq(1000)

BIT_TIME = 0.05 # 50ms per bit (Very slow for LDR)
HIGH_POWER = 65000
LOW_POWER = 10000

def send_char(c):
    val = ord(c)
    # Start Bit
    led.duty_u16(HIGH_POWER); utime.sleep(BIT_TIME)
    
    # 8 Data Bits
    for i in range(8):
        if (val >> i) & 1:
            led.duty_u16(HIGH_POWER)
        else:
            led.duty_u16(LOW_POWER)
        utime.sleep(BIT_TIME)
        
    # Stop Bit
    led.duty_u16(LOW_POWER); utime.sleep(BIT_TIME)

# Main Loop
while True:
    msg = "HELLO "
    for char in msg:
        send_char(char)
    utime.sleep(1)

# --- RECEIVER CODE (Pico B) ---
# (To run on second Pico)
# import machine
# ldr = machine.ADC(26)
# while True:
#    val = ldr.read_u16()
#    # Logic to decode High/Low transitions would go here
#    # (Simplified for context)
#    print(val) 
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The LDR Latency**: LDRs act like capacitors. They take ~20ms to "Fade" from resistance A to B. This limits your speed to about 20-50 bits per second. For real WiFi speeds, you must use a Photodiode (which reacts in microseconds).
*   **Ambient Noise**: If you turn the room lights ON, the "Low Power" signal might be overwhelmed. Use the "Cardboard Tube" tunnel to physically block 99% of room light.
*   **Sync Drift**: Without a shared clock wire, the RX doesn't know *exactly* when a bit starts. This simple code works for 1 character, but for long strings, you need Manchester Encoding to keep them synchronized.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Audio Link**: Connect an Aux cable to the LED (with a transistor). Play music. Connect a speaker to the Solar Panel (or Photodiode). You will HEAR the music carried by the light beam!
*   **Remote Control**: Use the TV Remote protocol (NEC) to send standard IR codes over the Visible LED.

## Batch 147: Advanced Temp/Humidity (DHT)

## 1. Project 1461: Advanced Temp/Humidity (DHT) Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Non-Blocking Observer"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a "State Machine" timing loop to read a DHT11 sensor exactly once every 2 seconds *without* using `sleep()`, allowing the Pico to perform other tasks (like blinking an LED) smoothly in between reads.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Sensor Saturation**: DHT sensors are slow chemical devices. If you ask them for data faster than once every 2 seconds, they return garbage or old cached values.
*   **Non-Blocking Logic**: Instead of saying "Wait 2 seconds", we say "Has 2 seconds passed since the last time we checked?". This frees up the processor.
*   **Single-Wire Protocol**: The DHT11 uses one pin for both TX and RX, relying on precise microsecond timing pulses (Bit-Banging) which the driver handles for us.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT11 Sensor** (Blue module) or DHT22 (White module)
*   **Status LED** (To prove the code isn't blocking)
*   **3x Male-to-Male Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **DHT Signal** | GP15 | Single-Wire Data |
| **Power** | 3V3 (Pin 36) | VCC |
| **Ground** | GND (Pin 38) | GND |
| **Status LED** | GP25 | Output |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `dht_read`.
*   From **Time**, drag `ticks_ms` and `ticks_diff`.
*   From **Control**, drag `if_else`.

### 7. Variables
*   **next_read_time**: Number (Timestamp for when we are allowed to read again).
*   **temperature**: Number.
*   **humidity**: Number.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: The Heartbeat (Proof of Life)**
1.  Setup the Onboard LED (GP25).
2.  In your `while True` loop, toggle the LED every 100ms.
3.  If we used `sleep(2)` for the sensor, this LED would freeze for 2 seconds. We want it to blink fast!

**Phase B: The Scheduler**
4.  Initialize `next_read_time = 0`.
5.  Inside the loop, check: `if ticks_ms() > next_read_time`.
6.  **IF TRUE**:
    *   Read the DHT Sensor.
    *   Print the Temperature and Humidity.
    *   Update `next_read_time = ticks_ms() + 2000` (Schedule next read for 2s later).
7.  **IF FALSE**:
    *   Do nothing (Skip the read). The loop continues immediately to blink the LED.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: LED blinks rapidly (10Hz).
2.  **Event (T=0s)**: Timer expires. Pico sends signal to DHT.
3.  **Process**: DHT responds with "24°C, 50%". Pico prints it.
4.  **Schedule**: Next read set for T=2000ms.
5.  **Loop**: LED continues blinking smoothly.
6.  **Event (T=2s)**: Timer expires again. New data is fetched.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import dht

# Hardware
sensor = dht.DHT11(machine.Pin(15))
led = machine.Pin(25, machine.Pin.OUT)

# State
next_read = 0
READ_INTERVAL = 2000 # 2 seconds

print("Starting Non-Blocking DHT Monitor...")

while True:
    current_time = utime.ticks_ms()
    
    # 1. High Priority Task: Blink LED fast (Animation)
    # We toggle every 100ms loop iteration (approx)
    led.toggle()
    utime.sleep(0.1) 
    
    # 2. Low Priority Task: Read Sensor
    # We only assume control if the time is right
    if utime.ticks_diff(current_time, next_read) > 0:
        try:
            sensor.measure()
            temp = sensor.temperature()
            hum = sensor.humidity()
            
            print(f"Time {current_time/1000:.1f}s | T: {temp}C  H: {hum}%")
            
            # Reset Timer
            next_read = utime.ticks_add(current_time, READ_INTERVAL)
            
        except OSError as e:
            print("Sensor Error (Try again)")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Freeze**: Writing `utime.sleep(2)` stops everything. If you had a button to turn off an alarm, it wouldn't work for 2 seconds!
*   **Incorrect Pull-Up**: DHT modules usually have a built-in resistor. If you are using a raw DHT sensor (just the plastic case), you MUST add a 4.7k resistor between Data and 3.3V, or it will read nothing.
*   **Sampling Too Fast**: If you set `READ_INTERVAL = 500`, the DHT11 will heat up from self-heating and give you incorrect temperatures (showing 28°C in a 20°C room).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Comfort Meter**: Add an RGB LED.
    *   Green: 20-25°C.
    *   Blue: < 20°C.
    *   Red: > 25°C.
*   **Min/Max Tracker**: Add variables to track `max_temp` and `min_temp` seen since startup.


## 1. Project 1462: Advanced Temp/Humidity (DHT) Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Data Stopwatch"
At the end of this project, you will be able to display the "Data Age" (e.g., "Updated: 3s ago") alongside your sensor readings. This is a critical feature in professional dashboards to warn users if the sensor has stopped communicating.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Data Freshness**: In safety-critical systems, seeing "25°C" is dangerous if that reading is from yesterday. We must know *when* that reading was taken.
*   **Relative Time**: Calculating the difference between `Current Time` and `Last Successful Read Time`.
*   **Display Flicker**: Updating the screen too fast (every 10ms) makes it unreadable. Updating too slow makes it laggy. We update the "Seconds Ago" counter fast (10Hz), but the Temp/Hum Sensor slow (0.5Hz).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT11 Sensor**
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display** (The Dashboard)
*   **Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **DHT Data** | GP15 | Input |
| **OLED SDA** | GP0 (I2C0) | Data |
| **OLED SCL** | GP1 (I2C0) | Clock |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `draw_text`.
*   From **Time**, drag `ticks_diff`.
*   From **Text**, drag `join`.

### 7. Variables
*   **last_success_time**: Number (Timestamp of the last valid DHT measurement).
*   **data_age**: Number (Seconds elapsed since last success).
*   **temp_cached**: Number (Stored value to allow screen refresh without re-reading sensor).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Setup**
1.  Initialize the OLED and DHT.
2.  Create a variable `last_success_time` set to `ticks_ms()`.
3.  Create `next_sensor_check` set to `0`.

**Phase B: The Dual Loop**
4.  Inside `repeat forever`:
    *   **Task 1: Sensor Check (Every 2s)**
    *   If `ticks_ms > next_sensor_check`:
        *   Try to read DHT.
        *   If successful: Update `temp_cached`, set `last_success_time = ticks_ms()`.
        *   Set `next_sensor_check = ticks_ms() + 2000`.
    
    *   **Task 2: UI Refresh (Every 100ms)**
    *   Clear Screen.
    *   Draw "Temp: [temp_cached] C".
    *   Calculate `data_age = (ticks_ms() - last_success_time) / 1000`.
    *   Draw "Age: [data_age]s ago".
    *   Show Screen.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Measurements taken. Screen shows "Temp: 24C, Age: 0s".
2.  **Tick**: The code loops. Config check false. Age updates: "0.1s", "0.2s"...
3.  **Update**: After 2 seconds, new data arrives. Age resets to "0s".
4.  **Failure**: If you unplug the DHT, the Temp stays "24C" (last known good), but the Age climbs: "5s.. 10s.. 60s..". The user instantly knows the data is stale.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import dht
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

# Hardware Setup
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(0), scl=machine.Pin(1), freq=400000)
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
dht_sensor = dht.DHT11(machine.Pin(15))

# State
temp_val = 0
hum_val = 0
last_success = utime.ticks_ms()
next_read = 0

print("Dashboard Active.")

while True:
    now = utime.ticks_ms()
    
    # --- TASK 1: Read Sensor (Slow) ---
    if utime.ticks_diff(now, next_read) > 0:
        try:
            dht_sensor.measure()
            temp_val = dht_sensor.temperature()
            hum_val = dht_sensor.humidity()
            last_success = now # Reset the "Age" clock
            next_read = utime.ticks_add(now, 2000)
        except OSError:
            pass # Keep old values, let the Age counter climb
            
    # --- TASK 2: Update UI (Fast) ---
    age_seconds = utime.ticks_diff(now, last_success) // 1000
    
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text("ENV MONITOR", 20, 0)
    
    oled.text(f"Temp: {temp_val} C", 0, 20)
    oled.text(f"Hum:  {hum_val} %", 0, 30)
    
    # Context-aware warning
    if age_seconds > 5:
        oled.text(f"OLD DATA! {age_seconds}s", 0, 50)
    else:
        oled.text(f"Updated: {age_seconds}s ago", 0, 50)
        
    oled.show()
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Screen Flicker**: Drawing to the OLED takes time (approx 40ms over I2C). If you redraw too fast without a `sleep(0.1)`, the screen might tear or slow down the sensor read.
*   **Integer Division**: Using `// 1000` gives whole seconds. If you want decimals (`2.5s`), use `/ 1000`.
*   **Negative Time**: If `ticks_ms()` rolls over (after ~40 days), simple subtraction fails. `ticks_diff()` handles this wrap-around logic safely.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Stale Data Alarm**: If `age > 60` seconds, flash the status LED or beep a buzzer to alert the engineer that the sensor is broken.
*   **Quality of Service**: Add a "Success Rate" counter. `Success / Attempts * 100%`.
