## 1. Project 1463: Advanced Temp/Humidity (DHT) Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Mold Prevention Engine"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a "Cumulative Risk Timer" that distinguishes between harmless temporary humidity spikes (like taking a shower) and dangerous long-term saturation (which causes mold growth), triggering an alarm only when a threshold is breached for a sustained period.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Time-Weighted Thresholds**: A standard "If Humidity > 70%" rule is annoying because it beeps every time you boil a kettle. A "Time-Weighted" rule says "If Humidity > 70% FOR 24 Hours".
*   **Accumulator Pattern**: A variable that counts up when the condition is failing and counts down (or resets) when the condition is safe.
*   **Reset Hysteresis**: If humidity drops to 69% for 1 second, should we reset the 24-hour timer? No. We demand it drops to a "Safe Zone" (e.g., < 60%) before giving the "All Clear".

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT11 Sensor**
*   **Active Buzzer** (The Alarm)
*   **RGB LED** (Visual Status)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Function |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **DHT Data** | GP15 | Input |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | Alarm |
| **RGB Red** | GP13 | Danger |
| **RGB Green** | GP12 | Safe |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Variables**, drag `change_by`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else`.
*   From **Control**, drag `wait`.

### 7. Variables
*   **danger_seconds**: Number (Total time spent in "Danger Zone").
*   **ALARM_LIMIT**: Number (Threshold in seconds, e.g., 3600 for 1 hour).
*   **is_high_humidity**: Boolean.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Configuration**
1.  Define `HUMIDITY_LIMIT = 70` (Percent).
2.  Define `SAFE_LIMIT = 60` (Percent).
3.  Set `danger_seconds = 0`.

**Phase B: The Logic Loop (1Hz)**
4.  Every 1 second:
    *   Read Humidity.
    *   **Condition Check**:
        *   If `Humidity > HUMIDITY_LIMIT`:
            *   Increment `danger_seconds` by 1.
            *   Set RGB to **RED** (Warning).
        *   Else If `Humidity < SAFE_LIMIT`:
            *   Set `danger_seconds = 0` (Reset).
            *   Set RGB to **GREEN** (Safe).
        *   Else (Between 60-70%):
            *   Do nothing. (Don't reset, but don't count up). This is the "Yellow Zone".
    
    *   **Alarm Check**:
        *   If `danger_seconds > ALARM_LIMIT`:
            *   Turn ON Buzzer.
            *   Print "MOLD RISK DETECTED!".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Morning**: Humidity 55%. System Green. Timer = 0.
2.  **Shower (10 mins)**: Humidity jumps to 90%. RGB turns Red. Timer counts to 600 seconds.
3.  **Dry Off**: Humidity drops to 55%. Timer resets to 0. (No Alarm).
4.  **Leak (All Day)**: Pipe bursts behind wall. Humidity stays at 75%.
5.  **Event**: After 24 hours (86400 seconds), the Alarm Blares. Mold risk is effectively confirmed.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import dht

sensor = dht.DHT11(machine.Pin(15))
buzzer = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
led_red = machine.Pin(13, machine.Pin.OUT)
led_green = machine.Pin(12, machine.Pin.OUT)

# Config
# For demo, we use 10 seconds as "Long Term"
# In reality: 24 * 3600 (86400 seconds)
ALARM_THRESHOLD_SEC = 10 
danger_counter = 0

print("Monitoring Mold Risk...")

while True:
    try:
        sensor.measure()
        hum = sensor.humidity()
        
        print(f"Humidity: {hum}% | Risk Timer: {danger_counter}/{ALARM_THRESHOLD_SEC}")
        
        if hum > 70:
            # DANGER ZONE
            danger_counter += 1
            led_red.value(1); led_green.value(0)
            
        elif hum < 60:
            # SAFE ZONE
            danger_counter = 0
            led_red.value(0); led_green.value(1)
            buzzer.value(0) # Silence alarm if we recover
            
        else:
            # BUFFER ZONE (60-70)
            # Hold current state. Do not reset timer.
            pass

        # Trigger Alarm
        if danger_counter > ALARM_THRESHOLD_SEC:
            print("!!! MOLD ALARM !!!")
            danger_counter = ALARM_THRESHOLD_SEC # Cap the counter
            # Pulse Buzzer
            buzzer.value(1)
            utime.sleep(0.1)
            buzzer.value(0)

    except OSError:
        pass # Ignore sensor errors
        
    utime.sleep(1) # 1Hz Loop
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Flapping" Reset**: If you reset the timer the instant humidity drops to 69.9%, a noisy sensor oscillating 69.9 <> 70.1 will keep resetting the timer forever, effectively hiding the danger. Always use a lower "Safe" threshold (Hysteresis gap) to confirm the event is over.
*   **Variable Overflow**: If you count seconds for a year, you might exceed the variable limit (unlikely in Python which handles large ints, but common in C++ `int16`).
*   **Power Loss**: If the Pico reboots, `danger_counter` resets to 0. For critical monitoring, you should save this value to a file every hour.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dew Point Calculator**: Use the Temperature and Humidity to calculate the "Dew Point". If Wall Temperature (measured by IR sensor) < Dew Point, condensation IS happening right now.
*   **Ventilation Control**: Instead of a buzzer, turn on a specific GPIO to activate a bathroom fan until humidity is safe.

## 1. Project 1464: Advanced Temp/Humidity (DHT) Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Tiny Chart"
At the end of this project, you will be able to visualize historical temperature trends by creating a "Bar Chart" on a standard 16x2 Text LCD, using "Custom Characters" to draw bars of varying heights.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Custom Character Generator (CGRAM)**: Standard LCDs have fixed fonts (A, B, C...). However, they allow you to define up to 8 of your own characters by setting individual pixels in a 5x8 grid. We will use this to create 8 vertical bar levels for a graph.
*   **Data Normalization**: Converting a real-world value (e.g., 20°C to 40°C) into a display coordinate (e.g., Bar Height 0 to 7).
*   **Ring Buffer (Visual)**: As new data arrives, the graph "Scrolls" left. We achieve this by updating a list and redrawing the screen.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT11 Sensor**
*   **16x2 LCD Display (I2C)**
*   **Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **DHT Data** | GP15 | Single-Wire |
| **LCD SDA** | GP0 | I2C Data |
| **LCD SCL** | GP1 | I2C Clock |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `define_custom_char`.
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `show_text`.
*   From **Lists**, drag `add_to_list` and `remove_first`.

### 7. Variables
*   **history_list**: List of Numbers (Stores the last 16 temperature readings).
*   **char_map**: List of Bytes (The bitmap definitions).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Tuning the Font**
1.  Define 8 Custom Characters in the LCD memory (Index 0 to 7).
    *   Char 0: A flat line at the bottom.
    *   Char 1: 2 lines filled.
    *   ...
    *   Char 7: All 8 lines filled (Solid Block).

**Phase B: The History Engine**
2.  Create an empty list `history_list`. Fill it with 16 zeros.
3.  Every 2 seconds:
    *   Read Temperature (e.g., 24°C).
    *   Add 24 to the **End** of `history_list`.
    *   Remove the **First** item of `history_list` (The oldest).

**Phase C: The Renderer**
4.  Clear the LCD.
5.  Loop from `i = 0 to 15` (The 16 columns of the screen):
    *   Get `val = history_list[i]`.
    *   **Normalize**: `bar_height = (val - 20) / 2`. (Assuming discrete steps).
    *   Clamp `bar_height` between 0 and 7.
    *   `LCD.move_to(i, 1)` (Bottom Row).
    *   `LCD.write_char(bar_height)`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Screen shows a flat line `________________`.
2.  **Heat**: You hold the sensor. Bars start rising from the right: `______________--`.
3.  **Scroll**: As time passes, the "Wave" moves left: `________--==####`.
4.  **Result**: You see a visual history of the temperature rise over the last 32 seconds.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import dht
from pico_i2c_lcd import I2cLcd

# Hardware
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(0), scl=machine.Pin(1), freq=400000)
lcd = I2cLcd(i2c, 0x27, 2, 16)
sensor = dht.DHT11(machine.Pin(15))

# Define Custom Chars (Levels 0-7)
# 5 dots wide, 8 dots high. 1 = Pixel On.
levels = [
    [0x00,0x00,0x00,0x00,0x00,0x00,0x00,0x1F], # 0: Baseline
    [0x00,0x00,0x00,0x00,0x00,0x00,0x1F,0x1F], # 1
    [0x00,0x00,0x00,0x00,0x00,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F], # 2
    [0x00,0x00,0x00,0x00,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F], # 3
    [0x00,0x00,0x00,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F], # 4
    [0x00,0x00,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F], # 5
    [0x00,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F], # 6
    [0x1F,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F,0x1F]  # 7: Full
]

# Write Chars to CGRAM
for i in range(8):
    lcd.custom_char(i, levels[i])

history = [20] * 16 # Init with baseline (20C)

print("Logging Temperature...")

while True:
    try:
        sensor.measure()
        temp = sensor.temperature()
        
        # Shift Register Logic
        history.pop(0)
        history.append(temp)
        
        # Render Graph on Row 1 (Bottom)
        lcd.move_to(0, 1) # Col 0, Row 1
        
        for t in history:
            # Map Temp (20-35C) to Char (0-7)
            # 20C -> 0, 35C -> 7. Range = 15. Step ~ 2.
            index = int((t - 20) / 2)
            if index < 0: index = 0
            if index > 7: index = 7
            lcd.putchar(chr(index))
            
        # Text on Row 0 (Top)
        lcd.move_to(0, 0)
        lcd.putstr(f"Temp: {temp}C   ")
        
    except OSError:
        pass
        
    utime.sleep(2)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **I2C Address**: Most I2C LCDs are `0x27` or `0x3F`. If nothing appears, check the address scanner from Project 1341.
*   **Char Overwrite**: CGRAM only stores 8 characters. If you try to define a 9th, it might crash or overwrite the first one.
*   **Refresh Rate**: LCD pixels are slow (ghosting). Don't update faster than 5Hz, or the graph will look blurry.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Scaling**: Instead of hardcoding 20-35°C, make the graph dynamic. Find `min(history)` and `max(history)` and map the 0-7 range to that dynamically, so the graph always looks "full" regardless of the absolute temperature.

## 1. Project 1465: Automated Advanced Temp/Humidity (DHT) Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Smart Dehumidifier"
At the end of this project, you will be able to build a "Hysteresis Controller" that automatically manages a Relay (controlling a Fan or Dehumidifier) to keep humidity stable, while protecting the motor from burning out due to rapid switching.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Hysteresis (Deadband)**: A control method where the "Turn ON" threshold is different from the "Turn OFF" threshold. This creates a "Stable Zone" where the motor can rest.
*   **Short Cycling**: The damaging effect of turning a motor ON and OFF every few seconds. Hysteresis prevents this.
*   **Bang-Bang Control**: A simple On/Off control strategy (unlike PID which is variable speed).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT11 Sensor**
*   **Relay Module** (Controls high power loads)
*   **RGB LED** (Simulates the Fan status if no Relay is available)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **DHT Data** | GP15 | Input |
| **Relay Signal** | GP14 | Output |
| **Status LED** | GP25 | Output |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else_if`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `dht_read`.

### 7. Variables
*   **humidity**: Number.
*   **fan_state**: String ("ON" or "OFF") or Boolean.
*   **ON_SETPOINT**: Number (60%).
*   **OFF_SETPOINT**: Number (50%).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Configuration**
1.  Set `ON_SETPOINT = 60` (Too humid).
2.  Set `OFF_SETPOINT = 50` (Dry enough).

**Phase B: The Logic**
3.  Loop every 2 seconds:
    *   Read Humidity.
    *   **Rule 1**: If `humidity > ON_SETPOINT`:
        *   Turn **ON** the Fan (Relay = 1).
        *   Set `fan_state = ON`.
    *   **Rule 2**: If `humidity < OFF_SETPOINT`:
        *   Turn **OFF** the Fan (Relay = 0).
        *   Set `fan_state = OFF`.
    *   **Rule 3 (Implicit)**: If humidity is between 50% and 60% (The Deadband):
        *   **Do Nothing**. Keep `fan_state` exactly as it is.
        *   (This is the secret sauce).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Humidity is 55%. Fan is OFF.
2.  **Rain**: Humidity rises... 58... 59... 61.
3.  **Trigger**: Logic hits `> 60`. Fan Turns **ON**.
4.  **Drying**: Humidity falls... 59... 58... (Fan stays ON).
5.  **Target**: Humidity falls to 49. Logic hits `< 50`. Fan Turns **OFF**.
6.  **Drift**: Humidity rises to 51. Fan stays **OFF** (Stable).

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import dht

sensor = dht.DHT11(machine.Pin(15))
relay = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT) # Fan Control
led = machine.Pin(25, machine.Pin.OUT)   # Status

ON_LIMIT = 60
OFF_LIMIT = 50

print("Dehumidifier Controller Running...")

while True:
    try:
        sensor.measure()
        h = sensor.humidity()
        current_state = "ON" if relay.value() else "OFF"
        
        print(f"Hum: {h}% | Fan: {current_state}")
        
        # Hysteresis Logic
        if h > ON_LIMIT:
            relay.value(1) # Turn On
            led.value(1)
        elif h < OFF_LIMIT:
            relay.value(0) # Turn Off
            led.value(0)
        else:
            # Deadband: Do nothing
            pass
            
    except OSError:
        pass
        
    utime.sleep(2)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Single Threshold**: If you say `if h > 60: On; else: Off`, a reading of 60.0 -> 61.0 -> 60.0 -> 61.0 will destroy your relay in minutes.
*   **Inductive Kickback**: Real motors generate voltage spikes when turned off. Ensure your Relay Module has a Flyback Diode (most modules do).
*   **Sensor Heat**: Placing the DHT sensor *inside* the device you are controlling (like a drying box) is good, but don't put it directly in the hot air stream or it may overheat.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Safety Timeout**: Add a rule: "Fan cannot run for more than 30 minutes continuous." (Prevents fire if sensor fails).
*   **Manual Override**: Add a button to toggle the Fan regardless of humidity.
---

## 1. Project 1466: Advanced Temp/Humidity (DHT) Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Rate-of-Rise Alarm"
At the end of this project, you will be able to build a "Rate-of-Rise" heat detector that triggers an alarm if the temperature climbs too fast (e.g., > 5°C in 30 seconds), detecting a fire much faster than a standard smoke detector in dusty environments.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Derivative Control (Rate of Change)**: Measuring the *speed* of a change, not just the absolute value.
*   **Delta Calculation**: $\Delta T = T_{current} - T_{previous}$.
*   **False Positive Rejection**: Ensuring the rise is real and not just a sensor glitch.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT11 Sensor**
*   **Active Buzzer**
*   **Red LED** (Danger Indicator)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **DHT Data** | GP15 | Input |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | Output |
| **Red LED** | GP13 | Output |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `arithmetic` (Subtraction).
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.
*   From **Lists**, drag `create_with`.

### 7. Variables
*   **prev_temp**: Number (Temperature at the start of the interval).
*   **curr_temp**: Number (Temperature at the end of the interval).
*   **temp_slope**: Number (Degrees per second).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Setup**
1.  Take an initial reading: `prev_temp = Sensor.Temperature()`.
2.  Wait 10 seconds (The "Review Period").

**Phase B: Analysis Loop**
3.  Take a new reading: `curr_temp = Sensor.Temperature()`.
4.  Calculate Rise: `delta_temp = curr_temp - prev_temp`.
5.  **Check**:
    *   If `delta_temp > 5` (Sudden spike):
        *   **ALARM ON**.
        *   Print "FIRE DETECTED! Rate: [delta_temp]C / 10s".
    *   Else:
        *   **ALARM OFF**.
        *   Update Baseline: `prev_temp = curr_temp`. (This is crucial, the baseline usually chases the current temp).
6.  Wait 10 seconds.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Normal**: Temp is 25C. 10s later, Temp is 25C. Delta = 0. Safe.
2.  **Heater**: Temp rises slowly to 26C. Delta = 1. Safe. Baseline moves to 26C.
3.  **Fire**: You blast the sensor with a hair dryer.
4.  **Spike**: Temp jumps from 26C to 34C in 10s. Delta = 8.
5.  **Trigger**: Delta > 5. Alarm Sounds!

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import dht

sensor = dht.DHT11(machine.Pin(15))
buzzer = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
red_led = machine.Pin(13, machine.Pin.OUT)

# Init
try:
    sensor.measure()
    prev_temp = sensor.temperature()
except:
    prev_temp = 20 # Fallback

print("Fire Watchdog Active...")

while True:
    utime.sleep(5) # Shorter period for testing
    
    try:
        sensor.measure()
        curr_temp = sensor.temperature()
        
        # Calculate Delta
        delta = curr_temp - prev_temp
        print(f"T: {curr_temp}C | Delta: {delta}C/5s")
        
        # Rate-of-Rise Check
        # Threshold: 3 degrees in 5 seconds is massive for ambient air
        if delta >= 3: 
            print("!!! RATE ALARM !!!")
            buzzer.value(1)
            red_led.value(1)
            utime.sleep(2) # Alarm duration
            buzzer.value(0)
            red_led.value(0)
            
        # Update rolling baseline
        prev_temp = curr_temp
        
    except OSError:
        pass
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Baseline Reset**: If you forget `prev_temp = curr_temp` at the end of the loop, the system compares *everything* to the startup temperature. It would alarm just because the day got hotter (e.g. Morning 10C -> Noon 20C). We want *speed*, so simply reset the baseline.
*   **Sensor Glitch**: Sometimes DHT returns 0 or 255. A "0" reading followed by "25" looks like a +25 degree spike! Always validate data range (0-50C) before calculating slope.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual Mode**: Combine "Rate of Rise" AND "Absolute Limit". Alarm if `Rate > 5` OR `Temp > 50`.
*   **Cooling Alarm**: Detect if a freezer door was left open (Rate of Fall? No, Rate of Rise in a cold room).
---

## 1. Project 1467: Advanced Temp/Humidity (DHT) Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Cold Chain Guardian"
At the end of this project, you will be able to build a "Vaccine Monitor" that tracks if a shipment ever leaves the safe temperature range (2°C to 8°C). Crucially, it implements a "Latch Flag": if the temperature violates the rule even once, the display permanently shows "VOID" to prevent the use of spoiled medicine.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Cold Chain Custody**: The logistical process of ensuring temperature-sensitive products (vaccines, food) stay cold from factory to patient.
*   **Latch Logic (Sticky Flag)**: A status that turns TRUE on an event but can only be reset by a specific "Admin" action (or hard flash erase).
*   **Persistent Logging**: Writing events to Non-Volatile Storage (Flash) so the evidence survives a power cycle (e.g., if the truck driver turns off the fridge to hide the failure).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT11 Sensor**
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **Red LED** (Spoiler Indicator)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **DHT Data** | GP15 | Input |
| **OLED SDA** | GP0 | I2C Data |
| **OLED SCL** | GP1 | I2C Clock |
| **Red LED** | GP14 | Output |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **System & Storage**, drag `append_to_file`.
*   From **Control**, drag `if_else`.
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `show_text`.

### 7. Variables
*   **is_spoiled**: Boolean (True if temperature ever exceeded limits).
*   **current_temp**: Number.
*   **LOW_LIMIT**: Number (2.0°C).
*   **HIGH_LIMIT**: Number (8.0°C).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Initialization**
1.  Read the "spoilage_status.txt" file from Flash (if it exists).
2.  If the file says "VOID", set `is_spoiled = True` immediately on startup. (This prevents "power cycling" to reset the alarm).

**Phase B: The Watchdog Loop**
3.  Every 5 seconds:
    *   Read Temperature.
    *   **Check Integrity**:
        *   `if current_temp < LOW_LIMIT OR current_temp > HIGH_LIMIT`:
            *   Set `is_spoiled = True`.
            *   Append "FAILURE at [Time]" to `log.txt`.
            *   Save "VOID" to `spoilage_status.txt`.
    *   **Update Display**:
        *   If `is_spoiled` is True: Show "SHIPMENT VOID!" and turn on Red LED.
        *   Else: Show "Status: OK" and Current Temp.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Factory**: System starts. Temp 5C. Status "OK".
2.  **Truck**: Fridge fails. Temp rises to 9C.
3.  **Event**: System detects 9 > 8. Sets `is_spoiled = True`. Writes to Flash. LED turns RED. Status "VOID".
4.  **Cover-up**: Driver sees Red LED. Unplugs Battery. Plugs it back in.
5.  **Restart**: System boots. Reads `spoilage_status.txt`. Sees "VOID".
6.  **Result**: LED turns RED immediately. The crime is preserved.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import dht
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

# Hardware
sensor = dht.DHT11(machine.Pin(15))
led_red = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(0), scl=machine.Pin(1), freq=400000)
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)

# Limits
MIN_TEMP = 2
MAX_TEMP = 8 # Narrow range for vaccines (Usually 2-8C)
# Note: DHT11 min is 0C, so it works. 

is_void = False

# --- Persistence Check ---
try:
    with open("status.txt", "r") as f:
        if f.read().strip() == "VOID":
            is_void = True
except OSError:
    pass # No file yet, all good

print(f"Boot Status: {'VOID' if is_void else 'OK'}")

while True:
    try:
        sensor.measure()
        t = sensor.temperature()
        
        # Check Compliance
        if (t < MIN_TEMP or t > MAX_TEMP) and not is_void:
            is_void = True
            print(f"EXCURSION: {t}C")
            # Log the crime
            with open("status.txt", "w") as f:
                f.write("VOID")
            with open("log.txt", "a") as f:
                f.write(f"Fail at {utime.time()}, Temp: {t}\n")
        
        # UI
        oled.fill(0)
        if is_void:
            oled.text("!! VOID !!", 20, 10)
            oled.text(f"Last T: {t}C", 20, 30)
            oled.text("Do Not Use.", 20, 50)
            led_red.value(1)
        else:
            oled.text("Status: GOOD", 10, 10)
            oled.text(f"Temp: {t}C", 10, 30)
            oled.text(f"Limit: {MIN_TEMP}-{MAX_TEMP}", 10, 50)
            led_red.value(0)
            
        oled.show()
        
    except OSError:
        pass
        
    utime.sleep(5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sensor Resolution**: DHT11 only gives whole numbers (e.g. 2, 3, 4). You cannot measure 2.5C. For real medical use, use a DS18B20 (0.0625C resolution).
*   **File Corruption**: If you unplug the Pico exactly while it is writing to `log.txt`, the file system can corrupt. Professional loggers use "Circular Buffers" or raw flash sectors.
*   **Self-Heating**: If you read the sensor every 100ms, it generates heat and might falsely trigger the "8C" limit. Keep read intervals > 2s.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Reset Key**: Add a physical button held for 10 seconds to delete the `status.txt` file (Administrative Reset).
*   **Time Tracking**: Add an RTC module (DS3231) to log the *exact* wall-clock time of the failure, not just `utime` seconds since boot.

## 1. Project 1468: Smart Advanced Temp/Humidity (DHT) System

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Cloud Dashboard"
At the end of this project, you will be able to upload your sensor data to a public cloud platform (ThingSpeak) using a REST API. This transforms your local detector into a global "IoT Node" accessible from your phone anywhere in the world.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **IoT (Internet of Things)**: Physical objects (sensors) connecting to the internet to exchange data.
*   **REST API (GET Request)**: A standard way for computers to talk. We "GET" a specific URL that contains our data (e.g., `update?temp=24`), and the server records it.
*   **API Keys**: A secret password that tells the cloud "This data belongs to User X".

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico W** (Must have WiFi) OR Standard Pico + UART Connection to PC.
*   **DHT11 Sensor**
*   **MicroUSB Cable**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **DHT Data** | GP15 | Input |
| **WiFi Antenna** | Internal | Radio |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Network**, drag `connect_wifi`.
*   From **Internet**, drag `http_get`.
*   From **Text**, drag `join`.

### 7. Variables
*   **ssid**: String (Your Node Name).
*   **password**: String.
*   **api_key**: String (From ThingSpeak).
*   **url**: String.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Cloud Setup**
1.  Go to `ThingSpeak.com` (free account).
2.  Create a "New Channel". Name it "Pico Room Monitor".
3.  Copy the "Write API Key" (e.g., `ABC123XYZ`).

**Phase B: The Code**
4.  Connect to WiFi logic (using `network` module).
5.  Read DHT Sensor.
6.  Construct URL: `http://api.thingspeak.com/update?api_key=[KEY]&field1=[Temp]&field2=[Hum]`.
7.  Send HTTP GET Request.
8.  Wait 20 seconds (ThingSpeak requires >15s gap).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Boot**: Pico connects to WiFi (LED flashes).
2.  **Sense**: Reads 22°C, 45%.
3.  **Upload**: Sends data. Thingspeak replies "1" (Entry ID 1).
4.  **Visualize**: You open your phone browser and see a live graph line moving up.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import urequests
import machine
import dht
import utime

# Config
SSID = "Your_WiFi_Name"
PASS = "Your_WiFi_Password"
API_KEY = "YOUR_THINGSPEAK_KEY"

sensor = dht.DHT11(machine.Pin(15))
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)

def connect():
    wlan.active(True)
    wlan.connect(SSID, PASS)
    print("Connecting to WiFi...")
    while not wlan.isconnected():
        utime.sleep(1)
    print("Connected!", wlan.ifconfig())

connect()

while True:
    try:
        sensor.measure()
        t = sensor.temperature()
        h = sensor.humidity()
        
        print(f"Uploading: {t}C {h}%")
        
        # REST API Call
        url = f"http://api.thingspeak.com/update?api_key={API_KEY}&field1={t}&field2={h}"
        response = urequests.get(url)
        print("Server Reply:", response.text)
        response.close()
        
    except Exception as e:
        print("Error:", e)
        
    utime.sleep(20) # Free tier limit
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Http vs Https**: Pico W supports SSL (https), but it's slower. For simple logging, `http` is fine. If you get memory errors, use `http`.
*   **Rate Limiting**: If you loop too fast (every 1s), the server will block you or ignore inputs. Always assume APIs have limits.
*   **Power Hunger**: WiFi uses ~100mA. Do not run this on a small battery for long without implementing Deep Sleep networking.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Two-Way Comms**: Use the `talkback` API to read a command FROM the cloud (e.g., a "Fan Button" on your phone) to control a Pico LED. 
*   **Local Server**: If no internet, run a Python HTTP server on your PC (`python -m http.server`) and log data there.

## 1. Project 1469: Advanced Temp/Humidity (DHT) Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Salt Test" (Standard Calibration)
At the end of this project, you will be able to perform a professional-grade "One-Point Calibration" using the "Saturated Salt Solution" method. You will measure the sensor's error against a known physical constant (75% RH) and apply a software offset to correct all future readings.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Metrology**: The science of measurement. No sensor is perfect out of the box.
*   **Physical Constants**: NaCl (Table Salt) saturated in water creates an equilibrium relative humidity of exactly ~75% at 25°C.
*   **Offset Correction**: `Corrected_Value = Raw_Value + Offset`.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT11 / DHT22 Sensor**
*   **Ziploc Bag** (Airtight)
*   **Bottle Cap**
*   **Table Salt & Water**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
*   (Standard DHT Setup)

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `addition`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_global`.
*   From **System**, drag `file_write_number`.

### 7. Variables
*   **raw_humidity**: Number.
*   **cal_offset**: Number.
*   **final_humidity**: Number.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: The Physics Setup**
1.  Mix salt and a few drops of water in a bottle cap to make "wet sand" (slurry). NOT soup.
2.  Place the Cap and the Sensor into the Ziploc bag.
3.  Seal it tight. Leave it for 12-24 hours. The air inside *will* settle at 75% Humidity.

**Phase B: The Software**
4.  Read the sensor. Let's say it reads 71%.
5.  Calculate Error: `Target (75) - Actual (71) = +4`.
6.  Set `cal_offset = 4`.
7.  In your main code, update logic: `Hum = dht.humidity() + cal_offset`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Setup**: Bag sealed.
2.  **Wait**: Air equilibrates.
3.  **Measure**: Sensor says 71%. We know it's 75%.
4.  **Save**: We store the offset (+4) in the Pico's flash memory.
5.  **Run**: Next day, room is 50% (True). Sensor reads 46%. Code adds 4. Result: 50% (Correct).

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import dht
import utime

sensor = dht.DHT11(machine.Pin(15))

# Load Calibration
offset = 0
try:
    with open("cal_hum.txt", "r") as f:
        offset = int(f.read())
        print(f"Loaded Offset: {offset}%")
except:
    print("No calibration found.")

# Mode Select
# If we hold a button on boot, we enter CALIBRATION mode.
# Ideally, we just hardcode logic for this example.

print("Use 'c' to calibrate to 75%, 'r' to reset.")

while True:
    try:
        sensor.measure()
        raw = sensor.humidity()
        corrected = raw + offset
        print(f"Raw: {raw}% | Corrected: {corrected}%")
        
        # Simulated User Input (via Serial)
        # In Thonny, type logic helps
        
    except OSError:
        pass
        
    utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Wet Sensor**: If the salt water touches the sensor electronics, it will corrode and die. Keep the cap separate from the sensor.
*   **Temperature**: This 75% constant depends slightly on temp. Keep the bag in a stable 25°C room, not in direct sunlight (greenhouse effect).
*   **Patience**: It really takes >12 hours for the air to stabilize. Measuring after 10 mins is useless.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Two-Point Calibration**: Use Silica Gel (Desiccant) to create a ~0% RH environment. Now you have a Low Point (0%) and High Point (75%) to calculate Gain AND Offset (`y = mx + c`).

## 1. Project 1470: Advanced Advanced Temp/Humidity (DHT) Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Smart AC Controller"
At the end of this project, you will be able to build a "Retrofit Smart Thermostat" that sits on a shelf and autonomously turns on your old "Dumb" Wall AC Unit via Infrared (IR) signals when the room gets too hot.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **IR Replay Attack (Good kind)**: Recording a signal from a remote and replaying it.
*   **Bridge Device**: A device that translates one protocol (Temperature Logic) into another (IR Light).
*   **Open Loop Control**: We send the "ON" signal, but we don't *know* if the AC actually turned on (unless we check the temp drop later).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT11 Sensor**
*   **IR Receiver** (VS1838B - to learn codes)
*   **IR Transmitter LED** (to send codes)
*   **Transistor** (2N2222 - to boost IR power)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **DHT Data** | GP15 | Input |
| **IR Recv** | GP16 | Input |
| **IR Transmit** | GP17 (PWM) | Output |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `ir_sniff` (to learn).
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `ir_send_nec`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else`.

### 7. Variables
*   **ac_on_code**: List/Number (The recorded hex code for "Cool 24C").
*   **ac_off_code**: List/Number.
*   **cooldown_timer**: Number (Prevents rapid toggling).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Learning (The Training Phase)**
1.  Run a simple script to print IR codes to console.
2.  Point your REAL AC remote at the Pico. Press "Power ON". Copy the code (e.g., `0xFFA25D`).
3.  Press "Power OFF". Copy the code (e.g., `0xFF629D`).

**Phase B: The Automation**
4.  Set `TARGET_TEMP = 26`.
5.  Loop every 10 seconds:
    *   Read DHT.
    *   If `Temp > 28` AND `AC_Is_Off`:
        *   Send `ac_on_code`.
        *   Wait 5 minutes (Cooldown).
    *   If `Temp < 24` AND `AC_Is_On`:
        *   Send `ac_off_code`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Hot Day**: Room hits 29C.
2.  **Action**: Pico blasts IR LED.
3.  **Result**: The wall AC unit beeps and turns on.
4.  **Cooling**: Temp drops to 23C.
5.  **Cutoff**: Pico sends OFF signal. AC shuts down. Energy saved.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import dht
from ir_tx.nec import NEC # Hypothetical Helper, or raw PWM

# Hardware
sensor = dht.DHT11(machine.Pin(15))
ir_pwm = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.OUT))
ir_pwm.freq(38000) # Carrier Freq
ir_pwm.duty_u16(0) # Off initially

# Simplified NEC Send Function (Bit-banging is complex, usually we use a lib)
def send_nec(address, command):
    print(f"Sending IR: Addr={address} Cmd={command}")
    # ... (Implementation of NEC 38kHz bursts) ...
    # For prototype, we just print intent or toggle LED
    
AC_ON = 0x55
AC_OFF = 0xAA

print("Smart AC Thermostat Ready.")

while True:
    try:
        sensor.measure()
        t = sensor.temperature()
        print(f"Room: {t}C")
        
        if t > 28:
            send_nec(0x00, AC_ON)
            utime.sleep(60) # Don't spam
            
        elif t < 24:
            send_nec(0x00, AC_OFF)
            utime.sleep(60)
            
    except OSError:
        pass
    
    utime.sleep(5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Carrier Frequency**: IR receivers only see light flashing at 38kHz. If you just turn the LED "On", the TV/AC won't see it. You must use PWM at 38kHz.
*   **Range**: A bare LED output from Pico GPIO is weak (3.3V, low current). Use a Transistor (NPN) to pump 100mA through the IR LED for range > 1 meter.
*   **Line of Sight**: IR is light. It cannot go through walls. The Pico must point AT the AC unit.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Universal Remote**: Add a Web Interface (Pico W) to let you click "Turn AC On" from your phone, effectively making your "Dumb" AC smart.

## 1. Project 1471: Advanced Infrared Remote Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Carrier Gen". Generate 38kHz carrier using PWM. Modulate Data on top (AND Gate logic or PWM disable).

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Modulation Theory**.

## Batch 148: Advanced Infrared Remote

## 1. Project 1471: Advanced Infrared Remote Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"The 38kHz Carrier"
At the end of this project, you will be able to generate the fundamental signal used by 99% of TV remotes: a 38kHz Pulsed Wave. You will understand why we cannot just flash the light "On" and "Off" normally (the sun would drown it out) and how "Carrier Frequency" solves this.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Carrier Frequency**: Blinking a light 38,000 times a second creates a unique "signature" that the receiver can distinguish from sunlight or room lights (which are steady or 50/60Hz).
*   **PWM (Pulse Width Modulation) for Freq**: We use PWM not for brightness, but to generate this high-speed clock.
*   **Duty Cycle & Range**: Setting Duty to 50% gives maximum range. Setting it to 10% saves battery but reduces range.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **IR Transmitter LED** (Clear or Blue-ish tint, 940nm)
*   **220Ω Resistor** (Limit current to ~15mA)
*   **IR Receiver Module** (VS1838B - to test your transmitter)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **IR LED Anode (+)** | GP15 (PWM) | Output |
| **IR LED Cathode (-)** | GND | Ground |
| **IR Receiver Out** | GP16 | Input (Verification) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `pwm_set_freq`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `pwm_set_duty`.
*   From **Control**, drag `wait`.

### 7. Variables
*   **freq_38k**: Number (38000).
*   **duty_on**: Number (32768, which is 50% of 65535).
*   **duty_off**: Number (0).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Concept Verification**
1.  Connect the IR LED.
2.  Set PWM Frequency to 2 Hz (Visible blinking).
3.  Look at the LED through your **Phone Camera** (cameras can see IR light as purple). You should see it blink.

**Phase B: The Carrier Generation**
4.  Change PWM Frequency to 38000 Hz.
5.  Set Duty Cycle to 50%.
6.  Look through the camera. It looks like a solid faint purple glow. To the *Receiver*, this is a blindingly loud signal used to say "I am here".

**Phase C: Modulation (Bursts)**
7.  Turn the PWM Duty ON (50%) for 1 second.
8.  Turn the PWM Duty OFF (0%) for 1 second.
9.  This "Blinking of the Carrier" is how we send data bits.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: LED is off. Receiver Output is HIGH (Idle).
2.  **Burst**: Pico generates 38kHz. Receiver Output drops LOW (Active).
3.  **Space**: Pico stops PWM. Receiver Output rises HIGH.
4.  **Observer**: To your eye, nothing happens. To the Receiver, it sees clear digital pulses.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware
ir_led = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
ir_led.freq(38000) # The Magic Number for TV Remotes
receiver_pin = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN)

print("Broadcasting 38kHz Carrier Waves...")

while True:
    print("MARK (Sending Carrier)")
    ir_led.duty_u16(32768) # 50% Duty - LED follows 38kHz clock
    
    # Check if receiver sees it (Receiver logic is inverted: Low = Seen)
    if receiver_pin.value() == 0:
        print(" -> Receiver confirms signal!")
    else:
        print(" -> Receiver sees nothing.")
        
    utime.sleep(1)
    
    print("SPACE (Silence)")
    ir_led.duty_u16(0)     # 0% Duty - LED Off
    utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Wrong Frequency**: If you set 36kHz or 40kHz, it *might* work but range will drop from 10 meters to 1 meter (Receiver filters are tuned sharply to 38k).
*   **Direct Drive**: A standard GPIO pin gives ~20mA. This is fine for testing. For a TV buster remote, you need a Transistor driver to push 100mA+.
*   **Camera Trick**: Not all phone cameras see IR. iPhones have good filters. Front-facing (Selfie) cameras usually have weaker filters and see IR better.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Frequency Sweep**: Write a loop that steps frequency from 30kHz to 50kHz. Monitor the Receiver output. Find the *exact* center frequency where the range is best.
*   **Proximity Sensor**: Put the LED and Receiver side-by-side. If you put your hand in front, the IR reflects off your hand into the receiver.

## 1. Project 1472: Advanced Infrared Remote Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Protocol Sniffer"
At the end of this project, you will be able to build a "Universal IR Analyser" that decodes the invisible flashes from *any* remote control (TV, AC, DVD) and displays their unique hexadecimal "DNA" on an OLED screen. This is the first step in hacking/cloning a remote.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **NEC Protocol**: The most common IR standard. It sends a 9ms lead pulse, a 4.5ms space, and then 32 bits of data.
*   **Hexadecimal Representation**: IR codes are 32-bit binary numbers (e.g., `1111111100000000...`). Humans can't read that. We display it as HEX (e.g., `FF00A25D`).
*   **Repeat Codes**: If you hold a button, the remote doesn't send the full code again. It sends a special short "Repeat" signal (`0xFFFFFFFF`).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **IR Receiver Module (VS1838B)**
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **Any TV/DVD Remote** (to test)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **IR Recv Data** | GP16 | Input |
| **OLED SDA** | GP0 (I2C0) | Data |
| **OLED SCL** | GP1 (I2C0) | Clock |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `ir_sniff` (or equivalent read block).
*   From **Text**, drag `to_hex`.
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `show_text`.

### 7. Variables
*   **ir_value**: Number (The raw 32-bit integer).
*   **hex_string**: String (The formatted text).
*   **protocol**: String ("NEC", "Sony", etc).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Setup**
1.  Initialize the OLED.
2.  Enable the IR Receiver on GP16.

**Phase B: The Sniffer Loop**
3.  Loop forever:
    *   Check `IR.Any_Data_Received?`.
    *   If **YES**:
        *   `ir_value = IR.Read()`.
        *   **Check Repeat**: If `ir_value == -1` (or special code), Print "REPEAT".
        *   **Format**: Convert `ir_value` to Hex String (UpperCase).
        *   Clear Screen.
        *   Draw "PROTOCOL: NEC".
        *   Draw "HEX: [hex_string]".
        *   Draw "DEC: [ir_value]" (Decimal).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Idle**: Screen says "Waiting for signal...".
2.  **Zap**: You press 'Volume Up' on your TV remote.
3.  **Decode**: OLED flashes: "HEX: E0E0E01F".
4.  **Hold**: You keep the button pressed.
5.  **Repeat**: OLED updates: "REPEAT... REPEAT...".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C
# Note: In real MicroPython, you need a library like 'ir_rx'
# We will simulate the logic or assume a helper class exists.

i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(0), scl=machine.Pin(1), freq=400000)
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
ir_pin = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

print("IR Sniffer Active.")

oled.fill(0)
oled.text("IR SNIFFER", 20, 0)
oled.text("Waiting...", 20, 30)
oled.show()

# Simulated Decode Logic (Simplified)
# Real usage requires measuring pulse widths with interrupts or PIO
last_code = 0

def decode_ir(pin):
    # This function would be complex (NEC Protocol)
    # Returning a dummy 'Power Button' code for demo
    return 0xFFA25D

while True:
    if ir_pin.value() == 0: # Active Low
        code = decode_ir(ir_pin)
        
        oled.fill(0)
        oled.text("RX DATA:", 0, 0)
        
        # Hex Formatting
        hex_str = hex(code).upper().replace("0X", "")
        oled.text(f"HEX: {hex_str}", 0, 25)
        oled.text(f"DEC: {code}", 0, 40)
        
        oled.show()
        print(f"Captured: {hex_str}")
        utime.sleep(0.5) # Debounce
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Unknown Protocol**: Not all remotes use NEC. Some use RC5 or Sony. If your code reader shows garbage, your library might not support that remote brand.
*   **Light Noise**: Trying to scan codes under direct sunlight will fail. The sun emits massive IR.
*   **Pull Up**: The VS1838B data line needs a Pull-Up resistor (internal or external) to be stable.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Cloner**: Save the "Volume Up" code to a variable. Then use Project 1471 logic to *send* that same code back 5 seconds later. (The Replay Attack).

## 1. Project 1473: Advanced Infrared Remote Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Double-Tap Filter"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement "Safety Confirmation Logic" for critical IR commands (like "Turn Off System"). The Pico will ignore a single press of the Power button and only act if the button is pressed *twice* within 3 seconds.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Confirmation Window**: A temporary state where the system is "listening" for a second confirmation signal.
*   **State Machine Timeout**: If the second press doesn't happen within X seconds, the system returns to the "Safe" idle state.
*   **Defensive Design**: Preventing accidental shutdowns caused by stray IR reflections or clumsy fingers.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **IR Receiver**
*   **Relay (or LED)** (The load to switch)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
*   (Standard IR Setup on GP16)

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else`.
*   From **Control**, drag `reset_timer`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **state**: Number (0=Idle, 1=Waiting for Confirm).
*   **timer_start**: Number.
*   **incoming_code**: Number.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Definition**
1.  Define `POWER_CODE = 0xFFA25D`.
2.  Set `state = 0` (Idle).

**Phase B: The Logic**
3.  When IR Code is received:
    *   **Check**: Is it `POWER_CODE`?
        *   **If State == 0 (Idle)**:
            *   Print "Press again to confirm...".
            *   Set `state = 1`.
            *   Set `timer_start = ticks_ms()`.
        *   **If State == 1 (Waiting)**:
            *   Print "POWER DOWN CONFIRMED!".
            *   Toggle Relay.
            *   Set `state = 0`.
    *   **Check Timeout**:
        *   If `state == 1` AND `(Now - timer_start > 3000)`:
            *   Print "Timeout. Cancelled.".
            *   Set `state = 0`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Press 1**: "Press again..." (Relay stays ON).
2.  **Wait 4s**: "Cancelled".
3.  **Press 1**: "Press again...".
4.  **Press 2 (immediately)**: "CONFIRMED". Relay turns OFF.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
# Assuming ir_read() exists

POWER_CODE = 0xFFA25D
state = 0 # 0: Idle, 1: Confirming
confirm_timer = 0

print("System Ready. Double-tap Power to switch.")

while True:
    code = ir_read() # Blocking or non-blocking
    
    if code == POWER_CODE:
        if state == 0:
            print("Action Requested! Confirm in 3s...")
            state = 1
            confirm_timer = utime.ticks_ms()
        elif state == 1:
            print(">>> POWER TOGGLED <<<")
            state = 0 # Reset
            
    # Timeout Check
    if state == 1:
        if utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), confirm_timer) > 3000:
            print("Confirmation Timed Out.")
            state = 0
            
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Button Bounce**: If the remote sends the code 3 times for one press (common), the specific "double tap" logic might trigger instantly on a single press. Add a "Debounce" (ignore signals for 500ms after first press).
*   **Blocking Wait**: Do NOT use `sleep(3)` to wait for the second press, or you will miss it. Use timestamp checking (`ticks_ms`).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Long Press**: Instead of Double Click, implement "Hold for 3 seconds". This requires detecting the `REPEAT` code continuously for 3000ms.
---

## 1. Project 1474: Advanced Infrared Remote Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Signal Strength Meter"
At the end of this project, you will be able to assess the "Health" of an IR signal by measuring the stability of its pulse timings. This allows you to verify if a remote has low battery or if the receiver is getting interference.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Jitter**: The variation in timing. A perfect "Lead Pulse" is 9000µs. A weak signal might be 8800µs or 9200µs.
*   **Tolerance Window**: We accept signals within ±10% margin. Calculating this margin is key to robust decoding.
*   **Quality Metric**: Mapping the "Error Rate" to a 0-100% Quality Score.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **IR Receiver**
*   **OLED Display**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
*   (Standard IR Setup)

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `abs` (Absolute Value).
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `ticks_us`.
*   From **Math**, drag `map_range`.

### 7. Variables
*   **pulse_length**: Number (Microseconds).
*   **expected_len**: Number (9000).
*   **deviation**: Number.
*   **quality**: Number.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Pulse Capture**
1.  Wait for the Falling Edge (Start of Lead Pulse).
2.  Wait for the Rising Edge (End of Lead Pulse).
3.  `pulse_length = Time_Rise - Time_Fall`.

**Phase B: Analysis**
4.  `expected_len = 9000` (Standard NEC).
5.  `deviation = abs(pulse_length - expected_len)`.
6.  **Scoring**:
    *   If `deviation < 50` (Perfect): Quality = 100%.
    *   If `deviation > 1000` (Garbage): Quality = 0%.
    *   Map values in between.
7.  Display "Signal Q: 95%" on OLED.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Point Blank**: You fire the remote 10cm from Pico.
2.  **Result**: "Lead: 9002us. Dev: 2us. Quality: 100%".
3.  **Cross Room**: You fire from 5 meters.
4.  **Result**: "Lead: 8950us. Dev: 50us. Quality: 85%".
5.  **Obstructed**: You aim at the ceiling (reflection).
6.  **Result**: "Lead: 8500us. Dev: 500us. Quality: 40%".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

ir_pin = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

print("Signal Quality Meter...")

while True:
    # Wait for Low (Start of pulse)
    while ir_pin.value() == 1:
        pass
    
    start = utime.ticks_us()
    
    # Wait for High (End of pulse)
    while ir_pin.value() == 0:
        pass
        
    end = utime.ticks_us()
    
    width = utime.ticks_diff(end, start)
    
    # NEC Lead pulse is ~9000us
    error = abs(width - 9000)
    
    # Calculate Quality (Invite-Verse Mapping)
    # 0 error = 100%, 1000 error = 0%
    quality = 100 - (error / 10)
    if quality < 0: quality = 0
    if quality > 100: quality = 100
    
    print(f"Width: {width}us | Err: {error}us | Quality: {int(quality)}%")
    
    utime.sleep(0.5) # Reset
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **CPU interruptions**: Python garbage collection or other interrupts can delay the timing measurement, making a perfect signal look "Jittery". Disable interrupts (`machine.disable_irq()`) during measurement for high precision.
*   **Different Remotes**: If you use a Sony remote (Lead = 2400µs) with this code, it will show "Quality: 0%" because it expects NEC (9000µs).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Protocol Guesser**: If Width ~ 9000 -> Print "NEC". If Width ~ 2400 -> Print "Sony". If Width ~ 13000 -> Print "Panasonic".

---

## 1. Project 1475: Automated Advanced Infrared Remote Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Master Macro"
At the end of this project, you will be able to program a single button to execute a complex sequence of IR commands: "Turn on TV -> Wait 5s -> Switch to HDMI 1 -> Wait 2s -> Set Volume to 50". This automated script (Macro) simplifies home theater setup.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Macro Automation**: Grouping multiple low-level commands into one high-level action.
*   **Asynchronous Delays**: Why we must wait between commands (TVs are slow to boot).
*   **Array Iteration**: storing the sequence as a list of instructions.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **IR Transmitter LED**
*   **Push Button** (The "Start" Trigger)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **IR LED** | GP15 | Output (PWM) |
| **Button** | GP14 | Input (Pull Down) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Lists**, drag `create_with` (Array).
*   From **Control**, drag `for_each`.
*   From **Control**, drag `wait`.

### 7. Variables
*   **macro_sequence**: List of Codes.
*   **delays**: List of Numbers (Wait times).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Recording**
1.  Use Project 1472 (Sniffer) to write down the Hex codes for:
    *   TV Power On (`0x111`)
    *   HDMI Input (`0x222`)
    *   Volume Up (`0x333`)

**Phase B: automation Script**
2.  Create a List `commands = [0x111, 0x222, 0x333, 0x333, 0x333]`.
3.  Wait for Button Press.
4.  **Loop** through list:
    *   Send Command.
    *   Wait 2 seconds (Critical! If you send "HDMI" while TV is showing the logo, it ignores it).
    *   Blink onboard LED to show progress.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **User**: Presses the big red button.
2.  **Pico**: Blasts "Power On". LED Blinks.
3.  **Wait**: 5 seconds passed. TV Screen lights up.
4.  **Pico**: Blasts "HDMI 1". LED Blinks.
5.  **Result**: Perfect setup every time.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
# import ir_sender (Hypothetical library)

button = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_DOWN)
led = machine.Pin(25, machine.Pin.OUT)

# Codes (Example NEC)
TV_POWER = 0xFFA25D
TV_HDMI  = 0xFF629D
TV_VOL   = 0xFFE21D

macro = [
    (TV_POWER, 5.0), # Cmd, Wait Time after
    (TV_HDMI,  2.0),
    (TV_VOL,   0.2),
    (TV_VOL,   0.2),
    (TV_VOL,   0.2)
]

print("Macro Ready. Press Button.")

def play_macro():
    print("Executing Macro...")
    for step in macro:
        cmd, wait = step
        print(f"Sending: {hex(cmd)}")
        
        # ir_sender.send_nec(cmd) 
        led.toggle()
        
        print(f"Waiting {wait}s...")
        utime.sleep(wait)
    
    print("Macro Complete.")
    led.value(0)

while True:
    if button.value() == 1:
        play_macro()
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Impatience**: Beginners send all 5 codes in 100ms. The TV CPU is slow; it cannot process "Vol Up" while it is busy switching to HDMI. Always add generous delays.
*   **State Confusion**: Macros are "Open Loop". If the TV was *already* On, sending "Power On" (which is usually a toggle) might turn it *Off*. Professional remotes use "Discrete Codes" (Explicit ON vs Explicit OFF) to solve this.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Abort Button**: If the user presses the button *again* while the macro is running, stop the sequence immediately.

## 1. Project 1476: Advanced Infrared Remote Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Dead Man's Switch (Heartbeat)"
At the end of this project, you will be able to build a "Watchdog Receiver" that expects a regular IR signal (a heartbeat) from a transmitter. If the signal stops for more than 5 seconds (e.g., transmitter battery dies, or range exceeded), the Pico triggers a "Lost Link" alarm.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Heartbeat Signal**: A periodic signal whose only purpose is to say "I am still alive and connected".
*   **Watchdog Timer**: A timer that counts down and triggers a reset/alarm *unless* it is kicked (reset) by a specific event.
*   **Failsafe Logic**: Designing systems that default to a safe state (Alarm ON) when communication is lost.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **IR Receiver**
*   **Active Buzzer** (The Alarm)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **IR Recv** | GP16 | Input |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | Output |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Time**, drag `ticks_ms`.
*   From **Time**, drag `ticks_diff`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else`.

### 7. Variables
*   **last_seen_time**: Number (Timestamp of last packet).
*   **TIMEOUT_MS**: Number (5000).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: The 'Kick'**
1.  Initialize `last_seen_time = ticks_ms()`.
2.  In the main loop, check `IR.Any_Data?`.
3.  If YES:
    *   Update `last_seen_time = ticks_ms()`.
    *   Turn Alarm OFF.
    *   Blink Green LED (Ack).

**Phase B: The 'Bite'**
4.  In the same loop, check `ticks_diff(now, last_seen_time)`.
5.  If `diff > 5000`:
    *   Turn Alarm ON (Link Lost).
    *   Print "CRITICAL: NO SIGNAL".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: System Silent.
2.  **Running**: You point a remote and hold a button. Green led flickers.
3.  **Loss**: You walk out of the room.
4.  **Wait**: 1s... 2s... 3s... 4s...
5.  **Trigger**: 5.01s. BEEP BEEP BEEP.
6.  **Return**: You walk back in. Signal received. Alarm stops.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

ir_pin = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
buzzer = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
led = machine.Pin(25, machine.Pin.OUT)

last_heartbeat = utime.ticks_ms()
TIMEOUT = 5000 # 5 Seconds

print("Watchdog Active. Feed me signals!")

while True:
    # 1. Feed the Dog (Check for IR activity)
    # Simple check: Is pin Low? (Active)
    if ir_pin.value() == 0:
        last_heartbeat = utime.ticks_ms()
        led.value(1) # Visual ACK
        buzzer.value(0) # Silence
    else:
        led.value(0)
        
    # 2. Check for Bite (Timeout)
    age = utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), last_heartbeat)
    
    if age > TIMEOUT:
        print(f"LINK LOST! Age: {age}ms")
        buzzer.value(1) # BEEP!
        utime.sleep(0.1) # Pulse sound
        buzzer.value(0)
        
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Interference as Heartbeat**: If you use "Any IR Signal" as the heartbeat, sunlight flickering through leaves might trick the system into thinking it's connected. A robust system checks for a *specific* code (e.g., `0xA1B2C3`).
*   **Watchdog Freeze**: If your IR reading code is "Blocking" (waits forever for a signal), the Check Loop never runs, and the alarm never triggers. Ensure your IR read command has a timeout or is non-blocking.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Beacon Mode**: Build a second Pico (Project 1471) that just blasts a code every 2 seconds. Put it on a moving robot. If the robot goes out of range, the Base Station alarms.

---

## 1. Project 1477: Advanced Infrared Remote Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Usage Tracker"
At the end of this project, you will be able to build a "Spy Logger" that sits quietly in the living room, listening to IR signals, and recording exactly when the TV was turned ON and OFF, calculating the total daily screen time.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **State Estimation**: Inferring the state of a device (TV is On/Off) based on commands seen (Power Button), even though we can't measure the TV directly.
*   **Session Tracking**: Calculating `Duration = Stop_Time - Start_Time`.
*   **Timestamp Logging**: Writing readable events to a file (`log.txt`).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **IR Receiver**
*   **RTC Module (DS3231)** (Optional, for real time. We will use system time).

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
*   (Standard IR Setup on GP16)

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **System & Storage**, drag `append_to_file`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else`.

### 7. Variables
*   **tv_is_on**: Boolean.
*   **start_time**: Number.
*   **total_seconds**: Number.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Definition**
1.  Define `POWER_CODE = 0xFFA25D`.
2.  Set `tv_is_on = False`.

**Phase B: The Spy Loop**
3.  When `POWER_CODE` is seen:
    *   **Toggle State**: `tv_is_on = NOT tv_is_on`.
    *   **If TV just turned ON**:
        *   `start_time = ticks_ms()`.
        *   Log "Session Started".
    *   **If TV just turned OFF**:
        *   `end_time = ticks_ms()`.
        *   `duration = (end_time - start_time) / 1000`.
        *   Log "Session Ended. Duration: [duration]s".
        *   Print "Kids watched [duration] seconds of TV.".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Event**: Kid presses Power. System logs "Start".
2.  **Event**: Kid watches cartoons for 20 minutes.
3.  **Event**: Kid presses Power. System logs "End. Duration: 1200s".
4.  **Audit**: Later, you read `log.txt` and see the history.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
# Assuming ir_read() helper

POWER_CODE = 0xFFA25D
tv_on = False
start_time = 0

print("TV Logger Active...")

while True:
    code = ir_read() # Blocking or Polling
    
    if code == POWER_CODE:
        # Toggle State
        if not tv_on:
            # Turn ON
            tv_on = True
            start_time = utime.time()
            print(f"TV Turned ON at {start_time}")
            with open("tv_log.txt", "a") as f:
                f.write(f"ON,{start_time}\n")
        else:
            # Turn OFF
            tv_on = False
            duration = utime.time() - start_time
            print(f"TV Turned OFF. Duration: {duration}s")
            with open("tv_log.txt", "a") as f:
                f.write(f"OFF,{duration}\n")
                
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Desynchronization**: If someone walks up to the TV and presses the physical power button on the side, the Pico doesn't see it. The Pico thinks the TV is OFF, but it's ON. Next time you use the remote, the Pico thinks it's turning ON, but it's turning OFF.
*   **Solution**: Use a light sensor taped to the TV power LED, or a current sensor, for true feedback.
*   **Repeat Codes**: Holding the power button might send the code 5 times. You must filter this so the state doesn't toggle On-Off-On-Off-On rapidly.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Parental Control**: If `duration > 3600` (1 hour), use an IR Transmitter to blast the "Power Off" code. Automatic curfew!

---

## 1. Project 1478: Smart Advanced Infrared Remote System

### 2. Learning Objective
"The WiFi Universal Remote"
At the end of this project, you will be able to control your TV, AC, and Stereo from your smartphone. You will build a Web Server on the Pico W that serves a webpage with buttons. When you click a button on the screen, the Pico emits the corresponding IR signal.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Web Server (HTTP)**: Serving HTML pages to a client (Phone).
*   **API Endpoints**: URLs like `/vol_up` that trigger hardware actions.
*   **Bridge Architecture**: Converting WiFi packets (TCP/IP) into Infrared Light pulses.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico W** (Must be the Wireless model)
*   **IR Transmitter LED** (+ Transistor driver recommended)
*   **Smartphone** (connected to same WiFi)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **IR LED** | GP15 | Output (PWM) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Network**, drag `start_server`.
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `ir_send_nec`.

### 7. Variables
*   **html_page**: String (The HTML code for the UI).
*   **request**: String.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: The Frontend**
1.  Create a variable `html` containing:
    `<h1>Remote</h1> <a href="/on"><button>ON</button></a> <a href="/off"><button>OFF</button></a>`

**Phase B: The Backend**
2.  Connect to WiFi.
3.  Start Socket Server (Port 80).
4.  Loop:
    *   Wait for client (`conn, addr = s.accept()`).
    *   Read Request (`GET /on ...`).
    *   **Logic**:
        *   If request contains `/on`: Send `0xFFA25D`.
        *   If request contains `/off`: Send `0xFF629D`.
    *   Send back `html` variable.
    *   Close connection.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Setup**: Serial Monitor prints "Connected. IP: 192.168.1.50".
2.  **User**: Types `192.168.1.50` in phone browser.
3.  **UI**: Sees big buttons "ON" and "OFF".
4.  **Click**: Taps "ON". Browser sends GET request.
5.  **Action**: Pico receives request -> Blasts IR code -> TV turns ON.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import socket
import machine
import utime
# import ir_lib

ssid = "MyWiFi"
password = "password"

# Hardware
ir_led = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
# (IR Setup omitted for brevity, assume send_nec() exists)

# WiFi Connection
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect(ssid, password)
while not wlan.isconnected():
    utime.sleep(1)
print("IP:", wlan.ifconfig()[0])

# Web Server
s = socket.socket()
s.bind(('', 80))
s.listen(5)

while True:
    conn, addr = s.accept()
    request = str(conn.recv(1024))
    
    # Simple Routing
    if '/tv_on' in request:
        print("Sending IR: TV ON")
        # ir_lib.send_nec(0xFFA25D)
    elif '/tv_off' in request:
        print("Sending IR: TV OFF")
        # ir_lib.send_nec(0xFF629D)
        
    # Response
    response = """HTTP/1.1 200 OK\n\n
    <html>
    <h1>Pico Remote</h1>
    <a href="/tv_on"><button style="font-size:50px">ON</button></a><br><br>
    <a href="/tv_off"><button style="font-size:50px">OFF</button></a>
    </html>
    """
    conn.send(response)
    conn.close()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Blocking Sockets**: `s.accept()` waits forever. This means the Pico does nothing else (no physical buttons work) while waiting for a phone. Use `select` or async libraries for multititasking.
*   **Network Lag**: WiFi isn't instant. There might be a 200-500ms delay between tap and IR blast.
*   **Power**: Pico W needs good power (500mA source) during WiFi transmission.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Voice Control**: Use IFTTT + Webhooks. "Alexa, trigger TV ON" -> IFTTT sends HTTP GET to your Pico's public IP (requires port forwarding) -> Pico blasts IR.

## 1. Project 1479: Advanced Infrared Remote Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Beam Profiler"
At the end of this project, you will be able to scientifically measure the "Cone of Emission" of your IR transmitter. You will perform a manual sweep test to determine the effective transmission angle (e.g., ±30°), understanding why pointing the remote at the ceiling works (reflection) vs pointing it backwards (fail).

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Radiation Pattern (Lobe)**: LEDs do not shoot light equally in all directions. They have a "Main Lobe" (Sweet spot).
*   **Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR)**: As you move off-axis, the signal strength drops. At some angle, the "Noise" (Sun) overpowers the "Signal" (IR).
*   **Packet Loss Rate**: The percentage of commands sent that were NOT received.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico** (The Transmitter)
*   **Second Pico OR Arduino** (The Receiver - ideally separate for mobility)
*   **Protractor / Printed Angle Sheet**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
*   (Standard Transmitter Setup on GP15)
*   (Receiver Setup on GP16)

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Control**, drag `for_loop`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `change_by`.
*   From **Data**, drag `log_data`.

### 7. Variables
*   **packets_sent**: Number.
*   **packets_received**: Number.
*   **success_rate**: Number.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Source Code**
1.  **Transmitter**: Sends numbered packets (1 to 100) rapidly.
2.  **Receiver**: Counts how many valid packets it saw.

**Phase B: The Experiment**
3.  Place Receiver at 0 degrees (Dead on). Run Test. (Expect 100/100).
4.  Rotate Receiver to 10 degrees. Run Test. (Expect 100/100).
5.  Rotate to 20, 30, 40...
6.  At 45 degrees, you might see 80/100.
7.  At 60 degrees, 0/100.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **User**: "Testing 30 degrees... Go."
2.  **Tx**: Blasts 100 codes.
3.  **Rx**: "Received 98. Loss 2%."
4.  **Conclusion**: 30 degrees is reliable.

### 10. Generated Code (Transmitter Side)
```python
import machine
import utime
# import ir_lib

packets_to_send = 100
delay = 0.05

print("Burst Mode Ready. Press Enter to Fire.")

while True:
    input()
    print(f"Firing {packets_to_send} packets...")
    
    for i in range(packets_to_send):
        # ir_lib.send_nec(0x1000 + i) # Sending incrementing ID
        utime.sleep(delay)
        
    print("Done.")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Wall Reflection**: If you do this test in a small white room, the IR will bounce off the walls and you will get "100%" everywhere. Professional tests are done in an "Anechoic Chamber" (or a big open field at night).
*   **Battery Voltage**: As the battery drains, the IR LED gets dimmer, shrinking the cone. Use a stable USB power supply.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Wide Angle Mod**: Sand the top of your IR LED with sandpaper to diffuse the light. Retest. You should see a wider angle but shorter range.

## 1. Project 1480: Advanced Advanced Infrared Remote Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"The T9 Retro typer"
At the end of this project, you will be able to type full text messages on an OLED screen using nothing but a standard TV remote (Numeric Keypad), implementing the classic "Multi-Tap" or "T9" input method used on old Nokia phones.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Multi-Tap Logic**: Cycle through characters ('a' -> 'b' -> 'c') on rapid presses.
*   **Commit Timer**: Automatically accepting the character if no button is pressed for X milliseconds.
*   **Input Map (Dictionary)**: Mapping keys (2) to lists of values (['a', 'b', 'c']).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **IR Receiver**
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **TV Remote** (with 0-9 keys)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **IR Data** | GP16 | Input |
| **OLED** | GP0, GP1 | I2C |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Text**, drag `char_at`.
*   From **Lists**, drag `get_item`.
*   From **Time**, drag `ticks_diff`.

### 7. Variables
*   **current_message**: String.
*   **pending_char**: String.
*   **last_key**: Number.
*   **key_press_count**: Number.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Key Mapping**
1.  Map keys: `2=['a','b','c']`, `3=['d','e','f']`...

**Phase B: Event Loop**
2.  **Input**: User presses '2'.
    *   If `key == last_key` AND `time < 1s`:
        *   Increment `count`.
        *   Update `pending_char = map[2][count % 3]`. (Cycles a->b->c->a).
    *   If `key != last_key`:
        *   **Commit** old char to message.
        *   Start new char 'a'.
3.  **Timeout**:
    *   If `time > 1s` AND `pending_char` exists:
        *   **Commit** to message.
        *   Clear `pending_char`.
        *   Advance Cursor.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Press**: '2'. Screen shows "a" (blinking).
2.  **Press**: '2'. Screen shows "b" (blinking).
3.  **Wait**: 1 second.
4.  **Action**: "b" stops blinking. Cursor moves.
5.  **Press**: '3'. Screen shows "b d".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C
# helper for IR

i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(0), scl=machine.Pin(1))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)

key_map = {
    0x02: ['a', 'b', 'c'], # Simulated Hex Code for '2'
    0x03: ['d', 'e', 'f']  # Simulated Hex Code for '3'
}

msg = ""
pending = ""
last_key = -1
last_time = 0
idx = 0

while True:
    code = ir_check() # Non-blocking
    now = utime.ticks_ms()
    
    if code:
        # Check if same key
        if code == last_key and utime.ticks_diff(now, last_time) < 1000:
            idx += 1
        else:
            # Different key or timeout? Commit previous
            msg += pending
            pending = ""
            idx = 0
            
        if code in key_map:
            chars = key_map[code]
            pending = chars[idx % len(chars)]
            last_key = code
            last_time = now
            
    # Timeout Commit
    if pending and utime.ticks_diff(now, last_time) > 1000:
        msg += pending
        pending = ""
        last_key = -1
        idx = 0
        
    # Render
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text(msg + pending + "_", 0, 0)
    oled.show()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Buffer Overflow**: Typing a novel will crash the Pico RAM or screen width. Limit message length to 16 chars (one line).
*   **Backspace**: You *must* implement a delete key (e.g., 'Vol Down'), or mistakes are permanent!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **SMS Sender**: Combine Project 1478 (Web) and 1480. Type a message on the Remote, press "OK", and the Pico sends it to a Discord Webhook over WiFi.

## 1. Project 1481: Advanced Keypad Security Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Pin Saver"
At the end of this project, you will be able to read 8 separate buttons (or a keypad) using only 3 GPIO pins on the Pico. You will master the **74HC165** Parallel-In Serial-Out (PISO) Shift Register, a fundamental chip for IO expansion.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Serialization**: Converting parallel data (8 buttons pressed at once) into a serial stream (10010011) sent over a single wire.
*   **Latching**: Freezing the state of inputs at a specific moment so they don't change while we are reading them.
*   **Clocking**: Using a rhythmic signal to push bits out of the chip one by one.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **74HC165 Shift Register**
*   **8x Push Buttons** (or a DIP Switch block)
*   **10kΩ Pull-up Resistors** (if not internal to chip/buttons)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Shift Data (Q7)** | GP16 | Input (MISO) |
| **Shift Clock (CP)** | GP18 | Output (SCK) |
| **Shift Latch (PL)** | GP17 | Output (CS) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `shift_register_read`.
*   From **Control**, drag `for_loop`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `bit_get`.

### 7. Variables
*   **raw_byte**: Number (The 8-bit integer read from the chip).
*   **button_states**: List of Booleans.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: The Reading Routine**
1.  **Latch Low**: Tell the chip to "Take a photo" of the button inputs.
2.  **Latch High**: Tell the chip to "Hold this photo" and prepare to send.
3.  **Read Loop**:
    *   Read the **Data Pin** (This is Bit 7).
    *   Pulse the **Clock Pin** High then Low. (This shifts the data, moving Bit 6 to the front).
    *   Repeat 8 times.

**Phase B: Bit Masking**
4.  Construct the 8-bit number from the reads.
5.  Print the binary representation (e.g., `0b00001000` means Button 4 is pressed).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Press**: User holds down the 3rd button.
2.  **Scan**: The Pico runs the latch-clock sequence.
3.  **Output**: Terminal prints `Button 3 Active`.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Wiring
data_pin = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN)
clock_pin = machine.Pin(18, machine.Pin.OUT)
latch_pin = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.OUT)

# Init
clock_pin.value(0)
latch_pin.value(1)

def read_shift_reg():
    # 1. Latch the inputs (Pulse Low)
    latch_pin.value(0)
    utime.sleep_us(5)
    latch_pin.value(1)
    
    value = 0
    # 2. Shift them in (MSB First usually)
    for i in range(8):
        bit = data_pin.value()
        value = (value << 1) | bit
        
        # Clock Pulse
        clock_pin.value(1)
        utime.sleep_us(5)
        clock_pin.value(0)
        
    return value

while True:
    keys = read_shift_reg()
    print(f"Raw: {bin(keys)}")
    
    # Check specific bit (Button 0)
    if (keys >> 0) & 1:
        print("Btn 0 Pressed!")
        
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Floating Inputs**: The 74HC165 inputs effectively float if not connected. You MUST use pull-up or pull-down resistors on the button side, or you will get random noise.
*   **Bit Order**: Some chips send MSB (Bit 7) first, some LSB (Bit 0). If your buttons seem reversed (Button 1 triggers Button 8), reverse your loop or shift direction (`value >> 1`).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Daisy Chain**: Connect the `Q7'` (Serial Out) of a *second* 74HC165 to the valid Data input of the first. Now you can read 16 buttons with the same 3 pins!

## 1. Project 1482: Advanced Keypad Security Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Halo Interface"
At the end of this project, you will be able to upgrade your security keypad with a WS2812 "NeoPixel" ring. You will program intuitive lighting feedback: turning blue as digits are entered, spinning green on access granted, and flashing red on access denied.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Visual Feedback Loops**: Giving the user immediate confirmation that their input was registered.
*   **State Indication**: Using color to represent system status (Locked=Red, Enter Mode=Blue, Unlocked=Green).
*   **Non-Blocking Animation**: Updating LED colors without pausing the keypad scan.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **WS2812 LED Ring** (8, 12, or 16 LEDs)
*   **Keypad** (or Shift Register buttons from Project 1481)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LED Data** | GP15 | Output (PWM-like) |
| **Keypad** | (See Prev) | Input |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Light**, drag `neopixel_set_color`.
*   From **Light**, drag `neopixel_show`.
*   From **Control**, drag `wait`.

### 7. Variables
*   **pin_code_buffer**: String (The numbers currently typed).
*   **led_index**: Number.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Entry Feedback**
1.  Initialize LED Ring to OFF (Black).
2.  Wait for Key Press.
3.  On Press:
    *   Add digit to buffer.
    *   Light up `LED[len(buffer)]` in **Blue**.

**Phase B: Validation Animation**
4.  If `len(buffer) == 4`:
    *   Check if correct.
    *   **Success**: Run "Spin Green" loop (Set LED i Green, Show, Wait 50ms, i++).
    *   **Fail**: Run "Flash Red" loop (All Red, Wait 200ms, All Black, Wait 200ms, Repeat 3x).
    *   Clear Buffer and Reset LEDs.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Locked**: Ring is dim red pulsing (optional).
2.  **Typing**: User types "1". LED 1 turns Blue.
3.  **Typing**: User types "2". LED 2 turns Blue.
4.  **Typing**: User types "3". LED 3 turns Blue.
5.  **Success**: User types "4". The Blue fills up, then explodes into Green.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import neopixel
import utime
# import keypad_lib

NUM_LEDS = 12
np = neopixel.NeoPixel(machine.Pin(15), NUM_LEDS)

code = "1234"
entry = ""

def set_color(r, g, b):
    for i in range(NUM_LEDS):
        np[i] = (r, g, b)
    np.write()

def anim_success():
    for i in range(NUM_LEDS):
        np[i] = (0, 255, 0)
        np.write()
        utime.sleep(0.05)
    utime.sleep(1)
    set_color(0, 0, 0)

def anim_fail():
    for _ in range(3):
        set_color(255, 0, 0)
        utime.sleep(0.1)
        set_color(0, 0, 0)
        utime.sleep(0.1)

while True:
    key = keypad_lib.get_key() # Blocking or Polling
    
    if key:
        print(f"Pressed: {key}")
        entry += key
        
        # Visual Ack of Index
        idx = len(entry) - 1
        if idx < NUM_LEDS:
            np[idx] = (0, 0, 255) # Blue
            np.write()
            
        if len(entry) == 4:
            if entry == code:
                print("ACCESS DIRECTED")
                anim_success()
            else:
                print("DENIED")
                anim_fail()
            entry = ""
            set_color(0,0,0)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Power Hunger**: 12 LEDs on full White draw ~720mA! The Pico regulator might overheat or shutdown. Keep brightness low (e.g., `(0, 50, 0)` instead of `(0, 255, 0)`) if powering from USB.
*   **Index Error**: If user types 20 digits, your code tries to light up LED[19] which crashes if you only have 12 LEDs. Always clamp your index logic.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Timeout Clear**: If user enters 2 digits and walks away, the Blue lights stay on forever. Add a timer to auto-clear the buffer after 5 seconds of inactivity.
