## 1. Project 1483: Advanced Keypad Security Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Uncrackable Logic"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement "Constant Time Comparison" to defeat "Timing Attacks." You will understand why sophisticated hackers can guess your PIN simply by measuring how many milliseconds the Pico takes to say "Access Denied."

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Timing Attacks**: A Side-Channel attack where the attacker infers secret data based on the *execution time* of the algorithm.
*   **Short-Circuit Evaluation**: The standard computer logic (`if A and B`) that stops checking as soon as it finds a False. This is fast but insecure.
*   **Constant Time Logic**: Code written to strictly take the same amount of time regardless of whether the password is correct or incorrect.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Keypad** (or Serial Terminal for simulation)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
*   (Same as Project 1481)

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Control**, drag `for_loop`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `boolean_compare`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `change`.

### 7. Variables
*   **input_pin**: String.
*   **secret_pin**: String.
*   **error_count**: Number.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: The Vulnerable Logic**
1.  Loop `i` from 0 to 3.
2.  If `input[i] != secret[i]`, then `return False` (Immediately stop).
    *   *Why this is bad*: If the first digit is wrong, it returns in 1µs. If the first 3 are correct, it returns in 4µs. A hacker measures this.

**Phase B: The Secure Logic**
3.  Set `error_count = 0`.
4.  Loop `i` from 0 to 3.
5.  If `input[i] != secret[i]`, increment `error_count`. (Do NOT stop!).
6.  Wait 100ms (Masking Delay).
7.  Return `True` if `error_count == 0` else `False`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Hacker**: Enters `0000`. Pico waits 100ms. Denies.
2.  **Hacker**: Enters `1234` (Correct). Pico waits 100ms. Grants.
3.  **Result**: Hacker sees no difference in timing.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import utime
# import keypad_lib

SECRET_PIN = "9481"

def check_pin_insecure(attempt):
    for i in range(len(SECRET_PIN)):
        if attempt[i] != SECRET_PIN[i]:
            return False # FAILS FAST
    return True

def check_pin_secure(attempt):
    errors = 0
    # Must compare every single character even if we already failed
    for i in range(len(SECRET_PIN)):
        if attempt[i] != SECRET_PIN[i]:
            errors += 1
            
    # Artificial Delay to flatten timing jitter
    utime.sleep(0.1) 
    
    return (errors == 0)

# Main Loop simulation
while True:
    user_input = input("Enter PIN: ")
    if len(user_input) != 4:
        print("Invalid Length")
        continue
        
    start = utime.ticks_us()
    result = check_pin_secure(user_input)
    end = utime.ticks_us()
    
    if result:
        print(f"Access Granted (Time: {utime.ticks_diff(end, start)}us)")
    else:
        print(f"Access Denied (Time: {utime.ticks_diff(end, start)}us)")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Early Exit**: Using `break` inside the loop defeats the purpose. The loop must run to completion for `N` iterations every time.
*   **Length Leak**: If your code checks `if len(input) != 4: return False`, that is a leak! It tells the hacker "The PIN is 4 digits."

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Lockout Multiplier**: If `Access Denied`, increment `fail_count`. Sleep for `2 ^ fail_count` seconds. (1s, 2s, 4s, 8s...). This prevents Brute Force.

---

## 1. Project 1484: Advanced Keypad Security Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Arming Sequence"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a professional "Exit Delay" sequence for an alarm system. You will program the keypad to check if "Zones" (Doors/Windows) are closed, then start a countdown beep that speeds up as it nears zero, before finally arming the system.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **State Validaton**: Checking prerequisites (Are all windows closed?) before allowing a state change (Arming).
*   **Frequency Modulation**: Changing the speed of a beep (`BEEP... BEEP...` to `BEEP.BEEP.BEEP`) to convey urgency.
*   **System State**: Tracking modes like `DISARMED`, `ARMING_COUNTDOWN`, and `ARMED`.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Active Buzzer**
*   **2x Magnetic Reed Switches** (Zone 1 & Zone 2)
*   **Keypad**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Zone 1 (Door)** | GP26 | Input (Pull Up) |
| **Zone 2 (Window)** | GP27 | Input (Pull Up) |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | Output |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else`.
*   From **Loops**, drag `while`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `toggle`.

### 7. Variables
*   **system_state**: String ("DISARMED", "COUNTDOWN", "ARMED").
*   **timer**: Number.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Ready to Arm?**
1.  User enters PIN to ARM.
2.  Code checks: `if Zone1 == Open OR Zone2 == Open`.
3.  **Fail**: Beep Error tone. Screen says "CLOSE WINDOWS".
4.  **Pass**: Move to countdown.

**Phase B: The Countdown**
5.  Loop from 10 down to 0 (Seconds).
6.  Beep once per second.
7.  **Acceleration**: If `time < 3`, beep 4 times per second.
8.  At 0: Set `system_state = ARMED`. Long Beep to confirm.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **User**: Enters code to leave house.
2.  **Pico**: "Beep... (10) ... Beep... (9)".
3.  **User**: Opens door, walks out, closes door.
4.  **Pico**: "BeepBeepBeepBeep (1) ... BEEEEEEP (Armed)".
5.  **System**: Now scanning sensors for intruders.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
# import keypad...

zone1 = machine.Pin(26, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
buzzer = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)

def beep(duration, count):
    for _ in range(count):
        buzzer.value(1)
        utime.sleep(duration)
        buzzer.value(0)
        utime.sleep(duration)

STATE = "DISARMED"

print("System Ready.")

while True:
    # Simulating Keypad 'A' press to Arm
    input_cmd = input("Cmd? (A=Arm): ")
    
    if input_cmd == "A":
        # 1. Check Zones
        if zone1.value() == 1: # High = Open (Pullup)
            print("ERROR: Zone 1 Open! Close Door.")
            beep(0.5, 3) # Error tone
        else:
            # 2. Countdown
            print("Arming in 10s...")
            STATE = "COUNTDOWN"
            
            for i in range(10, 0, -1):
                print(f"{i}...")
                
                # Fast beep at end
                if i <= 3:
                     beep(0.1, 2)
                     utime.sleep(0.6)
                else:
                     beep(0.2, 1)
                     utime.sleep(0.8)
            
            STATE = "ARMED"
            print("SYSTEM ARMED. GOODBYE.")
            beep(1.0, 1) # Long confirm
            
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Blocking Countdown**: Using `sleep(10)` means you can't CANCEL the arming if you change your mind. Professional systems check the keypad *during* the countdown loop for a cancel code.
*   **Sensor Noise**: A loose door might flicker Open/Closed. Use software debounce logic.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Cancel Feature**: Modify the countdown loop to check `if keypad.get_key() == '#'` -> Abort arming.

## 1. Project 1485: Automated Advanced Keypad Security Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Admin Console"
At the end of this project, you will be able to build a multi-tiered menu system for your security controller. You will move beyond simple "PASS/FAIL" logic to create a "Settings Mode" where an Admin can change the PIN, view access logs, or adjust beep volume, all using the keypad and LCD.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **State Machine (Menu System)**: Managing different "Modes" (e.g., `HOME` vs `MENU_MAIN` vs `MENU_LOGS`).
*   **Context-Sensitive Input**: Why pressing '1' in the *Main Menu* means "Change Settings", but pressing '1' in *Login Mode* means "Digit 1".
*   **Navigation Logic**: Implementing "Back" and "Select" functionality using limited keys.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **LCD Display** (16x2 I2C)
*   **Keypad**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
*   (Standard LCD on I2C0, Keypad on GPIOs)

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable` (for `current_menu`).
*   From **Text**, drag `text_join` (for dynamic display).

### 7. Variables
*   **menu_state**: String (Current screen ID).
*   **cursor_pos**: Number (Current option selected).
*   **admin_pin**: String ("9999").

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Menu Structure**
1.  Define States: `IDLE`, `MAIN_MENU`, `CHANGE_PIN_MODE`, `VIEW_LOGS_MODE`.
2.  Define Transitions:
    *   `IDLE` + press 'A' -> Ask Admin PIN. -> If correct -> `MAIN_MENU`.
    *   `MAIN_MENU` + '1' -> `CHANGE_PIN_MODE`.
    *   `MAIN_MENU` + '2' -> `VIEW_LOGS_MODE`.
    *   Any Menu + 'D' -> `IDLE` (Exit).

**Phase B: Display Logic**
3.  Inside the main loop, check `menu_state`:
    *   **If IDLE**: Show "System Ready".
    *   **If MAIN_MENU**: Show "1.PIN 2.Logs".
    *   **If CHANGE_PIN_MODE**: Show "New PIN: ____".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Screen says "Ready".
2.  **Action**: User presses 'A'. Screen: "Admin PIN?".
3.  **Input**: User types "9999". Screen: "1.PIN 2.Logs".
4.  **Action**: User presses '1'. Screen: "Enter New PIN".
5.  **Action**: User types "1234". Screen: "Saved!", then returns to Main Menu.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
from pico_i2c_lcd import I2cLcd
import utime
# import keypad...

lcd = I2cLcd(machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(0), scl=machine.Pin(1)), 0x27, 2, 16)
STATE = "IDLE"
ADMIN_PIN = "9999"

def display_menu():
    lcd.clear()
    if STATE == "IDLE":
        lcd.putstr("System Armed\nA=Menu")
    elif STATE == "MAIN":
        lcd.putstr("1.Change PIN\n2.View Logs  D=Exit")
    elif STATE == "CHANGE_PIN":
        lcd.putstr("Enter New PIN:\n")
        
while True:
    key = keypad.get_key()
    
    if key:
        # Global Exit
        if key == 'D':
            STATE = "IDLE"
            display_menu()
            continue
            
        # State Handler
        if STATE == "IDLE":
            if key == 'A':
                # (Simplified: Assume direct entry for demo)
                STATE = "MAIN"
                display_menu()
                
        elif STATE == "MAIN":
            if key == '1':
                STATE = "CHANGE_PIN"
                display_menu()
            elif key == '2':
                lcd.clear()
                lcd.putstr("Logs Empty!")
                utime.sleep(1)
                display_menu()
                
        elif STATE == "CHANGE_PIN":
             # Logic to capture numbers would go here
             lcd.clear()
             lcd.putstr(f"Saved: {key}...")
             utime.sleep(1)
             STATE = "MAIN"
             display_menu()
             
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Screen Flicker**: Clearing and redrawing the LCD inside the fast loop makes it flicker or disappear. Only update the screen when `STATE` changes.
*   **Trap States**: Creating a menu you can't exit from. Always enable a "Back" button (usually 'D' or '#').

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Scrollable List**: If you have 10 log entries, use the '2' and '8' keys (Up/Down) to scroll through them on the small screen.

## 1. Project 1486: Advanced Keypad Security Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Panic Chord"
At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a "Duress Trigger" that activates when the user presses and holds two specific keys (e.g., `*` and `#`) simultaneously for 3 seconds. This simulates a silent alarm or panic button typically found on professional alarm panels.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Chorded Input**: Detecting when multiple keys are pressed at the same time (Keyboard Matrix Ghosting limitations apply).
*   **Hold Logic**: differentiating between a "Tap" (normal use) and a "Hold" (special function).
*   **Duress Code**: An alternative methods where typing `PIN + 1` disarms the system but alerts the police.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Matrix Keypad**
*   **Buzzer** (Siren)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
*   **Standard Matrix Wiring** (Rows 0-3, Cols 0-3)

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Control**, drag `timer`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `and`.

### 7. Variables
*   **keys_pressed**: List (Currently active keys).
*   **panic_timer_start**: Number.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: Matrix Limitations**
1.  Understand that standard "1-Key" libraries cannot detect chords. We need a "Multi-scan" function.
2.  *Alternative strategy for standard keypads*: Press `*` then `#` and hold `#` while `*` was the last key?
3.  *Simplified for this Project*: **Long Press '#'.**

**Phase B: The Hold Timer**
4.  Loop:
    *   If Key `#` is DOWN:
        *   If `panic_timer_start == 0`: Set `panic_timer_start = now`.
        *   If `now - panic_timer_start > 3000`: **TRIGGER ALARM**.
    *   Else (Key UP):
        *   Reset `panic_timer_start = 0`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Normal**: User presses `#` (Enter). Duration 100ms. No Alarm.
2.  **Panic**: User holds `#`.
3.  **Wait**: 1s... 2s... 3s...
4.  **Trigger**: **SIREN ON**. Console prints "SENDING SILENT ALARM TO POLICE".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
# import keypad (assuming raw row/col access logic available or simplified lib)

# For this demo, we assume functions get_key_state('#') returns True if held
panic_start = 0
PANIC_DURATION = 3000

print("System Active. Hold '#' for Panic.")

while True:
    # 1. Check Button State (Pseudocode for Matrix)
    # real matrix requires rapid row scanning manually
    is_hash_held = check_matrix_key_held(row=3, col=2) # '#' position usually
    
    if is_hash_held:
        if panic_start == 0:
            panic_start = utime.ticks_ms()
            print("Panic Timer Started...")
            
        elapsed = utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), panic_start)
        
        if elapsed > PANIC_DURATION:
            print("!!! PANIC ALARM TRIGGERED !!!")
            # Latched Alarm - Requires reboot or special code to clear
            while True:
                toggle_siren()
                utime.sleep(0.1)
    else:
        # Reset if released early
        if panic_start != 0:
            print("Panic Aborted.")
        panic_start = 0
        
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Key Bounce vs Hold**: A bouncing switch might "release" for 1ms during a hold. Robust code accounts for this small gap without resetting the timer effectively.
*   **Ghosting**: On a matrix keypad without diodes, pressing 3 keys forming a rectangle corners triggers the 4th key "Ghost". This makes true multi-key chords unreliable on cheap pads. Long Press is safer.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **The Duress PIN**: Modify Project 1483. If the user enters `2581` (Normal `2580` + 1), disarm the buzzer but set a "Silent Alarm" flag to `True`.

## 1. Project 1487: Advanced Keypad Security Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Indestructible PIN"
At the end of this project, you will be able to store your Security PIN in Non-Volatile Memory (NVM) so that it survives a power cut. You will also learn the concept of "Hashing" to store `3A7B9C` instead of `1234` so that even if a hacker dumps your chip memory, they can't reverse the password.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Non-Volatile Storage (Flash)**: File systems on microcontrollers (like `config.txt`).
*   **Hashing**: A one-way mathematical function (`1234` -> `8d969e...`) used for secure storage.
*   **Boot Logic**: Checking "Does a password exist?" on startup and responding accordingly.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico** (Uses Internal Flash)
*   **Keypad**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
*   **Internal Flash Memory** (No external wiring required)

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **System & Storage**, drag `write_file`.
*   From **System & Storage**, drag `read_file`.
*   From **Text**, drag `hash` (Simulated).

### 7. Variables
*   **stored_hash**: String (The saved password signature).
*   **input_hash**: String (The signature of what the user just typed).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**Phase A: "Set PIN" Mode**
1.  Admin types `1234`.
2.  Pico calculates Hash: `Hash("1234") = 99`. (Simplification).
3.  Pico opens `passwd.txt` in Write Mode.
4.  Pico writes `99`. Closes file.

**Phase B: "Verify" Mode**
5.  User types `1234`.
6.  Pico calculates Hash: `99`.
7.  Pico reads `passwd.txt` -> gets `99`.
8.  `99 == 99` -> **Access Granted**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **First Boot**: "No Password Found. Set PIN:".
2.  **User**: Sets "4321". System Saves.
3.  **Power Loss**: You unplug the Pico and plug it back in.
4.  **Reboot**: "Enter PIN:".
5.  **User**: Types "4321". System remembers! "Granted".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import uhashlib
import ubinascii
import os

PIN_FILE = "secret.dat"

def hash_pin(pin_str):
    # Convert string "1234" to SHA256 bytes
    h = uhashlib.sha256(pin_str.encode())
    # Convert bytes to Hex String
    return ubinascii.hexlify(h.digest()).decode()

def save_pin(pin_str):
    try:
        with open(PIN_FILE, "w") as f:
            f.write(hash_pin(pin_str))
        print("PIN Saved to NVM.")
    except Exception as e:
        print("Save Error:", e)

def verify_pin(input_str):
    try:
        # Check if file exists
        with open(PIN_FILE, "r") as f:
            saved_hash = f.read().strip()
            
        current_hash = hash_pin(input_str)
        
        if current_hash == saved_hash:
            return True
        return False
    except OSError:
        print("No PIN set yet!")
        return False

# Simulation Loop
while True:
    mode = input("(S)et or (V)erify? ")
    
    if mode.upper() == "S":
        p = input("New PIN: ")
        save_pin(p)
        
    elif mode.upper() == "V":
        p = input("Enter PIN: ")
        if verify_pin(p):
            print("ACCESS GRANTED")
        else:
            print("DENIED")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Writing Every Loop**: Flash memory wears out (approx 100k writes). NEVER put `file.write()` inside your main `while True` loop. Only write when settings *change*.
*   **Cleartext**: Storing "1234" in a text file is risky. If someone copies your ".py" files, they see the password. Hashing prevents this.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Factory Reset**: Add a physical button (GP15). If held during boot, delete `secret.dat` to reset the PIN.
save(h)
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Clear Text**: Never save password in clear text!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Factory Reset**: Jumper on boot to reset PIN.

## 1. Project 1488: Smart Advanced Keypad Security System

### 2. Project Description
**"The Sentinel 2FA Node"**
Security that relies on a static password is fundamentally vulnerable to replay attacks and theft. In this project, you will upgrade your security system to use **Time-Based One-Time Passwords (TOTP)**, similar to Google Authenticator or banking tokens. The Pico will act as the "Server" (or the Token), generating a new valid 6-digit code every 30 seconds based on a shared secret key and the current time. Users must enter the correct code displayed on their trusted device (or synchronized with the Pico) to gain access. This introduces the concept of "Something you have" (the synchronized time/token) alongside "Something you know."

### 3. Learning Objectives
*   **Time-Based Security**: Understand how 2FA (Two-Factor Authentication) algorithms function using timestamps.
*   **Hashing & HMACS**: Learn practical application of SHA-256 hashing to generate unique, non-reversible tokens.
*   **Temporal Windows**: Implement logic to handle time drift and valid acceptance windows.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico** (The Microcontroller).
*   **4x4 Matrix Keypad** (The Input Device).
*   **0.96" OLED Display** (Optional: To visualize the "Server" time/code for testing).
*   **Neopixel Ring/LEDs** (Visual Status Feedback).

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Interface | Pico Pin | Component Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **GPIO** | GP0 - GP7 | Keypad Rows/Cols | Standard Matrix Scan. |
| **I2C** | GP16 (SDA), GP17 (SCL) | OLED Display | SSD1306 (optional debug). |
| **Power** | 3.3V | VCC | Common rail. |
| **GND** | GND | GND | Common ground. |

### 6. Blocks Used
| Block Category | Block Name | Action | Description |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Logic** | `pico_rtc_get` | **Read Time** | Get current Unix timestamp for calculations. |
| **Math** | `math_modulo` | **Calculate** | Used to truncate hash to 6 digits. |
| **Smart IO** | `pico_keypad_read` | **Read Input** | Capture user entry. |
| **Variables** | `secret_key` | **Store Data** | Holds the static shared secret. |

### 7. Variables
*   **`current_time`**: The current timestamp (in seconds).
*   **`time_step`**: The 30-second window index (`current_time // 30`).
*   **`otp_code`**: The calculated 6-digit valid One-Time Password.
*   **`user_input`**: The string entered by the user.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Initialize Infrastructure**: Set up the Keypad and (optionally) the OLED.
2.  **Define the Secret**: Create a "Shared Secret" string (e.g., "MY_SUPER_SECRET_KEY"). In a real app, this is shared once between client and server.
3.  **Implement TOTP Logic**:
    *   Get Protocol Time: `T = current_seconds // 30` (Blocks of 30s).
    *   Construct Message: Combine `Secret` + `T`.
    *   Hash Message: `Hash = SHA256(Message)`.
    *   Truncate: Take the last bytes of the hash and convert them to a 6-digit number (modulo 1,000,000).
4.  **User Loop**:
    *   Prompt user "Enter Code:".
    *   Wait for Keypad input (accumulate digits).
    *   Compare `Input` vs `Calculated_OTP`.
    *   **Crucial**: Also check `Calculated_OTP_Previous` and `Calculated_OTP_Next` to allow for slight time drift (clock skew).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Boot**: System initializes. RTC time is assumed synchronized (simulated).
2.  **Idle**: System generates internal OTP repeatedly: `123456`, `987654`...
3.  **Interaction**: user presses '#'. System wakes, flashes Blue.
4.  **Input**: User checks their "Phone" (or the debug OLED) for the code. Types `123456`.
5.  **Validation**:
    *   Pico computes hash for *now*. Matches `123456`.
    *   **LED Green**. Relay Clicks.
6.  **Expiry**: If user waits >30s, the code changes. Typing `123456` now fails -> **LED Red**.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import uhashlib
import ubinascii

# --- Configuration ---
SHARED_SECRET = "PICO_SECURE_NODE_01"
STEP_SECONDS = 30

def get_totp(secret, timestamp):
    """
    Generates a 6-digit TOTP code based on secret and time.
    Mechanism: 
    1. Time Step = Current Time / 30
    2. Data = Secret + TimeStep
    3. Hash = SHA256(Data)
    4. Code = Last 3 bytes of Hash % 1,000,000
    """
    t_index = int(timestamp // STEP_SECONDS)
    msg = f"{secret}{t_index}"
    
    # Create Hash
    h = uhashlib.sha256(msg.encode()).digest()
    
    # Dynamic Truncation (Simplification for Pico optimization)
    # Take last 4 bytes -> integer
    offset = h[-1] & 0x0F
    # Safe fallback if strict RFC6238 implementation isn't needed for this demo
    # We just grab the last 4 bytes to form an integer
    val = int.from_bytes(h[-4:], "big")
    code = val % 1000000
    return f"{code:06d}"

def verify_code(input_code):
    now = utime.time()
    
    # Check Current Window
    if input_code == get_totp(SHARED_SECRET, now):
        return True
    
    # Check Previous Window (Allow 30s drift)
    if input_code == get_totp(SHARED_SECRET, now - STEP_SECONDS):
        return True
        
    return False

# --- Main Simulation ---
print("--- TOTP Security Node ---")
# Simulate Time Passing for Demo
start_time = utime.time()

while True:
    current_otp = get_totp(SHARED_SECRET, utime.time())
    print(f"\rDebug OTP: {current_otp}  (Time: {utime.time()})", end="")
    
    # Here you would integrate 'keypad.get_input()'
    # For simulation, we assume manual entry checking logic exists
    
    utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **RTC Drift**: The Pico's internal Reat-Time Clock is not perfect. It will drift over days. In production, re-sync via NTP (WiFi) or a high-precision DS3231 module.
*   **Blocking Input**: Using `input()` blocks the code update loop. Interaction must be non-blocking so the valid code can change *while* the user is typing (handling edge cases).
*   **Security by Obscurity**: Hiding the code isn't enough. The *Secret* must be strong.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **QR Code**: Generate a QR code for the Secret so it can be scanned into a real Authenticator App (requires adhering to standard Base32 implementations).
*   **Brute Force Lockout**: If a user tries 3 wrong codes, lock the system for 5 minutes, even if they guess the right one later.

## 1. Project 1489: Advanced Keypad Security Calibration Tool

### 2. Project Description
**"The Latency Analyzer"**
In high-security or high-speed gaming applications, **Input Latency**—the delay between a physical press and the digital response—is critical. "The Latency Analyzer" is a scientific tool running on the Pico to measure its own reaction time. It timestamps the exact moment a pin state changes (using Interrupts) and compares it to when the processing logic triggers the feedback LED. This project teaches deep optimization and the cost of Python interpretation vs. raw hardware speed.

### 3. Learning Objectives
*   **Microsecond Precision**: Use `time.ticks_us()` for high-resolution profiling.
*   **Interrupts vs Polling**: Quantify the speed difference between looping checks and hardware interrupts (IRQ).
*   **Debounce Tuning**: Visualize how debounce delays (safeguards) directly negatively impact responsiveness.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**.
*   **4x4 Keypad**.
*   **OLED Display** (To show results).

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
Standard Keypad wiring. Optional: Connect **GP15** to **GP16** with a jumper wire to simulate an "instant" external trigger for calibration logic.

### 6. Blocks Used
| Block Category | Block Name | Action | Description |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Smart IO** | `ticks_us` | **Measure** | Get system time in microseconds. |
| **Smart IO** | `ticks_diff` | **Calculte** | Compute time elapsed between two events. |
| **Events** | `pin_irq` | **Trigger** | Hardware interrupt for instant detection. |

### 7. Variables
*   **`t_start`**: Timestamp of event trigger.
*   **`t_detect`**: Timestamp of software registration.
*   **`latency_us`**: The calculated delay in microseconds.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Setup Profiler**: Create a function `measure_scan_speed()`.
2.  **Baseline Loop**: Run a specialized loop that continuously scans the keypad.
3.  **Measurement Logic**:
    *   Record `t1 = ticks_us()`.
    *   Perform one full scan of 16 keys.
    *   Record `t2 = ticks_us()`.
    *   Print `Scan Time = diff(t2, t1)`.
4.  **Reaction Test**:
    *   Set up an Interrupt (IRQ) on a specific Row Pin.
    *   Inside the IRQ handler, record `t_irq`.
    *   Inside the main loop, when key is processed, record `t_proc`.
    *   Diff is the "System Latency".
5.  **Optimization**: Try removing `time.sleep()` calls and observe the scan rate increase.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Screen shows "Ready. Press '5'".
2.  **Action**: User taps '5'.
3.  **Process**:
    *   Hardware sees voltage change: T=0us.
    *   Debounce waits 20ms: T=20,000us.
    *   Code Logic identifies '5': T=20,500us.
    *   LED turns on: T=20,600us.
4.  **Report**: Screen displays: "Lat: 20.6ms".
5.  **Tuning**: User decreases debounce to 5ms. New Result: "Lat: 5.6ms". (Much snappier!).

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# --- Setup ---
# Assume Keypad is initialized on variable 'kp'
row_pin = machine.Pin(2, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_DOWN)

last_trigger = 0

def callback_handler(pin):
    global last_trigger
    # Record the EXACT hardware time of the event
    last_trigger = utime.ticks_us()

# attach IRQ
row_pin.irq(trigger=machine.Pin.IRQ_RISING, handler=callback_handler)

print("--- Latency Profiler ---")

while True:
    # 1. Main Loop Work
    # This represents the "sluggish" application code
    
    if last_trigger != 0:
        # We detected the event in the main loop
        detection_time = utime.ticks_us()
        
        latency = utime.ticks_diff(detection_time, last_trigger)
        print(f"IRQ to Loop Latency: {latency} us ({latency/1000} ms)")
        
        # Reset
        last_trigger = 0
        
    utime.sleep(0.01) # Simulate generic load (10ms)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Print Flooding**: Placing `print()` inside the high-speed scan loop will slow it down by 100x. Printing is very slow (UART/USB speed). Only print results *after* measurement is done.
*   **Garbage Collection**: In Python, automatic GC (Garbage Collection) pauses execution randomly. For consistent latency, run `gc.collect()` manually at safe times.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **The Ghost Key**: Lower the debounce time to 0ms. You will see "Ghost Keys" (multiple registered presses for one physical tap) due to switch bounce. Graph the relationship between Stability vs. Speed.

## 1. Project 1490: Advanced Advanced Keypad Security Prototype

### 2. Project Description
**"The Enigma Escape Room Controller"**
This project transforms the Pico into a master controller for an "Escape Room." Unlike a simple door lock, this system requires a non-linear or multi-stage sequence of codes to unlock the final mechanism. The user must solve three unique puzzles (finding three different codes) and enter them in specific orders or satisfy specific conditions (e.g., "Code B must be entered within 10 seconds of Code A"). This introduces complex **State Machine** logic.

### 3. Learning Objectives
*   **Finite State Machines (FSM)**: programming systems that exist in distinct states (e.g., Locked, Stage 1 Clear, Stage 2 Clear, Unlocked).
*   **Persistence**: Tracking progress even if the user makes a wrong intermediate guess.
*   **Feedback Loops**: Using sound and light to guide the user through the stages without text instructions.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**.
*   **4x4 Keypad**.
*   **3 Generic LEDs** (Red, Yellow, Green - indicating stage progress).
*   **1 Relay Module** (The "Maglock").
*   **Buzzer** (Audio cues).

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Interface | Pico Pin | Component Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **GPIO** | GP18, 19, 20 | LED Anodes | Indicators for Stages 1, 2, 3. |
| **GPIO** | GP21 | Relay In | Controls the final Lock. |
| **PWM** | GP22 | Buzzer + | Sound effects. |
| **Std** | GP0-7 | Keypad | Standard mapping. |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Lists**, drag `create_list_with`.
*   From **Lists**, drag `list_get_item`.
*   From **Sensors**, drag `keypad_read`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `controls_if_else`.
*   From **Math**, drag `math_number`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `change_variable`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_led_switch`.

### 7. Variables
*   **`current_level`**: Integer (0-4). Tracks progress.
*   **`secret_codes`**: List (Stores the 4 answers).
*   **`input_buffer`**: String (Stores current key sequence).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Game State Initialization**
1.  From **Lists**, drag `create_list_with`. **Create** a list of your 4 codes: `["1111", "2222", "3333", "4444"]`. Use the gear icon to add items.
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Initialize** `current_level` to `0`.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Initialize** `input_buffer` to `""` (empty string).

**Phase B: The Security Loop**
4.  From **Loops**, drag `controls_whileUntil`. **Create** an infinite loop (`while True`).
5.  From **Sensors**, drag `keypad_read`. **Get** the latest key press and store it in variable `key`.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `controls_if`. **If** `key` is valid (not null):
    *   From **Text**, drag `text_join`. **Append** `key` to `input_buffer`.

**Phase C: Multi-Stage Validation**
7.  From **Logic**, drag `controls_if`. **If** `key` equals `#` (Enter):
    *   From **Lists**, drag `list_get_item`. **Retrieve** item at index `current_level` from `secret_codes`.
    *   From **Logic**, drag `logic_compare`. **Check** if `input_buffer` equals the retrieved code.
    
**Phase D: Outcome Handling**
8.  **True Branch** (Correct): 
    *   From **Variables**, drag `change_variable`. **Change** `current_level` by `1`.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_led_switch`. **Turn ON** the LED corresponding to the new level.
9.  **False Branch** (Incorrect):
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_buzzer_tone`. **Play** Error Sound.
    *   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Reset** `input_buffer` to `""`.
10. **Win Check**: From **Logic**, drag `controls_if`. **If** `current_level == 4`, **Activate** the Relay.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Room is dark.
2.  **Action**: Player finds note "Four ones start the fun". Enters `1111`.
3.  **Feedback**: **Red LED** turns ON. Beep-Beep. (Progress!).
4.  **Stages**: Player proceeds through codes 2 and 3.
5.  **Win**: Player enters final code `4444`. **Green LED** ON. **CLACK** (Relay opens).
6.  **Reset**: Game requires power cycle or "Admin Code" `9999` to reset for next player.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
from keypad import Keypad # Assumed library

# Hardware
led_stage1 = machine.Pin(18, machine.Pin.OUT) # Red
led_stage2 = machine.Pin(19, machine.Pin.OUT) # Yellow
led_stage3 = machine.Pin(20, machine.Pin.OUT) # Blue
relay = machine.Pin(21, machine.Pin.OUT)      # Green/Lock

# Game State
CODES = ["1111", "2222", "3333", "4444"]
level = 0

def play_win_sound():
    pass

def check_input(input_str):
    global level
    
    target = CODES[level]
    
    if input_str == target:
        print(f"Level {level} Cleared!")
        level += 1
        update_indicators()
    else:
        print("Wrong Code!")
        play_error_sound()

def update_indicators():
    # Persistent Progress Bar Logic
    if level >= 1: led_stage1.on()
    else: led_stage1.off()
    
    if level >= 2: led_stage2.on()
    else: led_stage2.off()
    
    if level >= 3: led_stage3.on()
    else: led_stage3.off()
    
    if level == 4:
        relay.on() # UNLOCK
        print("ESCAPED!")
        play_win_sound()

# Main Loop
input_buffer = ""
while level < 4:
    key = keypad.get_key()
    if key:
        if key == '#':
            check_input(input_buffer)
            input_buffer = ""
        elif key == '*':
            input_buffer = "" # Clear
        else:
            input_buffer += key
            
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```
    # Placeholder for buzzer logic
    pass

def check_input(input_str):
    global level
    
    target = CODES[level]
    
    if input_str == target:
        print(f"Level {level} Cleared!")
        level += 1
        update_indicators()
    else:
        print("Wrong Code!")
        # Optional: Reset level to 0? level = 0
        play_error_sound()

def update_indicators():
    if level >= 1: led_stage1.on()
    else: led_stage1.off()
    
    if level >= 2: led_stage2.on()
    else: led_stage2.off()
    
    if level == 3:
        relay.on() # UNLOCK
        print("ESCAPED!")
        play_win_sound()

# Main Loop
input_buffer = ""
while level < 3:
    key = keypad.get_key()
    if key:
        if key == '#':
            check_input(input_buffer)
            input_buffer = ""
        elif key == '*':
            input_buffer = "" # Clear
        else:

    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **State Confusion**: Ensuring the user knows which Stage they are in. Without LEDs, they might type Code 2 while the system is still waiting for Code 1.
*   **Input Buffering**: Forgetting to clear the input buffer after a successful (or failed) entry ensures subsequent keystrokes aren't corrupted by old data.

## Batch 150: Advanced Joystick Control 5 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1491: Advanced Joystick Control Sensor Setup

### 2. Project Description
**"The Vector Clamp" (Square to Circle Normalization)**
Standard analog joysticks operate in a "square" coordinate system. If you push the stick fully Up (Y=1.0) or fully Right (X=1.0), the magnitude is 1.0. However, if you push diagonally to the corner, both X and Y are 1.0. The resulting magnitude (speed) is $\sqrt{1^2 + 1^2} = 1.414$, or 141%. This causes omnidirectional robots to surge unexpectedly when moving diagonally. This project implements **Vector Normalization** to clamp the output to a perfect circle, ensuring consistent top speed in all directions.

### 3. Learning Objectives
*   **Vector Math**: Apply Pythagorean theorem to calculate magnitude.
*   **Normalization**: Learn how to scale vectors without losing their directional angle.
*   **Input Shaping**: Understand the importance of processing raw sensor data before using it.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**.
*   **2-Axis Analog Joystick** (XY Module).

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Interface | Pico Pin | Component Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **ADC** | GP26 (ADC0) | VRx (Horiz) | X-Axis Input. |
| **ADC** | GP27 (ADC1) | VRy (Vert) | Y-Axis Input. |
| **Power** | 3.3V | VCC | **Important**: Use 3.3V, not 5V, for accurate ADC reading. |
| **GND** | GND | GND | Common Ground. |

### 6. Blocks Used
| Block Category | Block Name | Action | Description |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Math** | `math_number_property` | **Sqrt** | Calculate the square root for magnitude. |
| **Logic** | `if_logic` | **Check** | Verify if Magnitude > 1.0. |
| **Variables** | `mag` | **Store** | Holds the calculated vector length. |
| **Smart IO** | `pico_adc_read` | **Read** | Get raw 0-65535 value. |

### 7. Variables
*   **`raw_x`, `raw_y`**: The ADC readings (0 - 65535).
*   **`norm_x`, `norm_y`**: The center-zeroed, scaled values (-1.0 to 1.0).
*   **`magnitude`**: The length of the vector.
*   **`final_x`, `final_y`**: The clamped output for the motors.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Read & Offset**: Read ADC (0-65535). Subtract 32768 to center at 0. Divide by 32768 to get range -1.0 to 1.0.
2.  **Calculate Magnitude**: $M = \sqrt{X^2 + Y^2}$.
3.  **Check Clamp**:
    *   If $M <= 1.0$: Output is valid.
    *   If $M > 1.0$: We are in the "corner".
4.  **Normalize**: To clamp, divide `X` and `Y` by `M`. This shrinks the vector back to length 1.0 while preserving the angle.
5.  **Output**: Send `final_x`, `final_y` to motors.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Input**: Stick pushed to Top-Right Corner.
2.  **Raw**: X=1.0, Y=1.0.
3.  **Calc**: Magnitude = 1.414.
4.  **Clamp Logic**: $1.414 > 1.0$ is True.
5.  **Process**:
    *   $NewX = 1.0 / 1.414 = 0.707$
    *   $NewY = 1.0 / 1.414 = 0.707$
6.  **Result**: Magnitude is now 1.0. Robot runs at 100% speed, not 141%.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import math
import utime

adc_x = machine.ADC(26)
adc_y = machine.ADC(27)

def get_normalized_vector():
    # 1. Read Raw (0-65535)
    rx = adc_x.read_u16()
    ry = adc_y.read_u16()
    
    # 2. Map to -1.0 to +1.0
    # Center point is approx 32768. 
    # Note: Real hardware usually needs calibration for true center.
    nx = (rx - 32768) / 32768
    ny = (ry - 32768) / 32768
    
    # 3. Calculate Magnitude
    mag = math.sqrt(nx*nx + ny*ny)
    
    # 4. Clamp
    if mag > 1.0:
        nx = nx / mag
        ny = ny / mag
        mag = 1.0 # For display purposes
        
    return nx, ny, mag

while True:
    x, y, m = get_normalized_vector()
    print(f"X: {x:.2f}, Y: {y:.2f} | Speed: {int(m*100)}%")
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Divide by Zero**: If the stick is exactly at center (0,0), Magnitude is 0. Division logic must handle this (though `if mag > 1.0` implicitly handles it since 0 is not > 1).
*   **Deadzone Ignorance**: Without a deadzone (ignoring small values near 0), the robot might creep slowly even when the stick is released due to sensor noise.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Deadzone**: Add logic: `If abs(RX) < 2000: NX = 0`. Implement this *before* normalization.

## 1. Project 1492: Advanced Joystick Control Data Reader

### 2. Project Description
**"Single-Stick Drive" (Split-Axis Mapping)**
In video games (and real cars), controls are often split. This project interprets the Y-axis of a joystick as two separate distinct controls:
*   **Up (Standard 0 to 1)**: Throttle / Acceleration (0-100%).
*   **Center**: Coast / Neutral.
*   **Down (-1 to 0)**: Brake / Reverse (0-100%).
This "Split-Axis" logic is fundamental for creating intuitive vehicle controllers using limited inputs.

### 3. Learning Objectives
*   **Conditional Mapping**: Applying different math formulas based on input ranges.
*   **Deadband Logic**: Creating a safe "Neutral" zone in the center of the stick travel.
*   **UI Feedback**: Visualizing two distinct values (Green Bar for Gas, Red Bar for Brake) from one input.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Pico**.
*   **Joystick** (Y-axis used).
*   **OLED Display** (SSD1306).

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
Standard Joystick on ADC0/1. OLED on I2C0 (Start I2C).

### 6. Blocks Used
| Block Category | Block Name | Action | Description |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Logic** | `if_else` | **Select** | Split logic for positive vs negative input. |
| **Math** | `math_abs` | **Convert** | Turn negative Down value into positive Brake magnitude. |
| **Smart Display** | `oled_rect` | **Draw** | Draw dynamic bars for visual feedback. |

### 7. Variables
*   **`y_val`**: Raw input centered (-100 to 100).
*   **`throttle`**: Calculated acceleration (0-100).
*   **`brake`**: Calculated braking force (0-100).
*   **`deadzone`**: The safe center range (e.g., 5).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Read Input**: Get Y-axis value (-100 to +100).
2.  **Define Deadzone**: If value is between -5 and +5, set both Throttle and Brake to 0.
3.  **Positive Handling**: If `Y > 5`:
    *   `Throttle = Y`.
    *   `Brake = 0`.
4.  **Negative Handling**: If `Y < -5`:
    *   `Throttle = 0`.
    *   `Brake = abs(Y)`.
5.  **Visualize**: Draw two rectangles on the OLED. Height varies with value.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Idle**: Stick at center. Throttle:0, Brake:0. Screen empty.
2.  **Push Up**: Stick at +50. Throttle:50, Brake:0. Green bar rises.
3.  **Pull Back**: Stick at -80. Throttle:0, Brake:80. Red bar rises.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

adc = machine.ADC(26)

def read_stick():
    # Read and map to -100 to 100
    raw = adc.read_u16()
    # Approx center 32768
    val = int((raw - 32768) / 327) # Scale approx to +/- 100
    return val

while True:
    y = read_stick()
    throttle = 0
    brake = 0
    
    # Deadzone of +/- 5
    if y > 5:
        throttle = y
    elif y < -5:
        brake = abs(y)
        
    # Clamp to max 100 just in case
    if throttle > 100: throttle = 100
    if brake > 100: brake = 100
        
    print(f"Y: {y} | GAS: {throttle}% | BRAKE: {brake}%")
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Jitter**: Without a deadzone, the "Brake" might flicker on and off when the stick is resting, causing "drag" in a game or robot.
*   **Output Ranges**: Forgetting to use `abs()` for the brake results in negative values, which most motor drivers (PWM) don't accept directly.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Smart Reverse**: Implement "Double-Tap to Reverse". First pull back is Brake. Return to center, then pull back again quickly to engage Reverse Gear.

---

## 1. Project 1493: Advanced Joystick Control Threshold Logic

### 2. Project Description
**"Drift Cancel" (Adaptive Calibration)**
Analog potentiometers change resistance with temperature ("Thermal Drift") and mechanical wear. A stick calibrated to "32768" at 20°C might read "33000" at 30°C, causing the drone/robot to drift slowly. This project implements **Adaptive Baseline Logic**: if the input remains stable near the center for a long period, the system slowly updates its definition of "Center" to match the current reading, cancelling out the drift.

### 3. Learning Objectives
*   **Moving Averages**: Basic signal filtering.
*   **Adaptive Systems**: Algorithms that change their parameters based on environmental conditions.
*   **Drift vs Input**: Distinguishing between a user holding the stick slightly off-center vs. sensor error (a difficult problem!).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Pico**.
*   **Joystick**.

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
*   Standard ADC connection.

### 6. Blocks Used
| Block Category | Block Name | Action | Description |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Math** | `math_arithmetic` | **Subtract** | Calculate deviation from current center. |
| **Logic** | `if_logic` | **Compare** | Check if deviation is small (Noise vs Intent). |
| **Variables** | `center_calibration` | **Update** | The dynamic center value. |

### 7. Variables
*   **`current_center`**: The assumed zero-point (starts at 32768).
*   **`read_val`**: Current loop reading.
*   **`stable_count`**: Counter for how long the signal has been "steady".

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Init**: Set `Center = ADC_Read()` at boot.
2.  **Loop**: Read `Current`.
3.  **Check Delta**: `Diff = abs(Current - Center)`.
4.  **Classification**:
    *   If `Diff > Deadzone`: User is moving the stick. Reset `stable_count`.
    *   If `Diff < Noise_Threshold`: Signal is "Still". Increment `stable_count`.
5.  **Adapt**: If `stable_count > 100` (e.g., stable for 5 seconds):
    *   Nudge `Center` slightly towards `Current`. `Center = Center * 0.99 + Current * 0.01`.
    *   This gradually accepts the new resting position as the true center.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Morning**: Cold room. Stick reads 32000. System sets Center=32000.
2.  **Noon**: Room heats up. Stick physically drifts to 32500 (without user touching).
3.  **Process**: System sees 32500. It's within the "Noise Threshold" (assuming threshold is wide enough or drift is slow).
4.  **Adaptation**: System slowly shifts Center to 32500 over 10 seconds.
5.  **Result**: Robot stopped drifting.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

adc = machine.ADC(26)
center = adc.read_u16() # Initial guess
stability_counter = 0

DEADZONE_USER = 5000   # If input > this, it's definitely user
NOISE_THRESHOLD = 500  # If input < this deviation, it might be drift
ADAPT_SPEED = 0.05     # How fast to shift (0.0 to 1.0)

print(f"Boot Center: {center}")

while True:
    raw = adc.read_u16()
    deviation = raw - center
    
    if abs(deviation) > DEADZONE_USER:
        # User is actively moving stick
        stability_counter = 0
        status = "Active"
    else:
        # Stick is near center
        stability_counter += 1
        status = "Idle"
        
        # If it has been idle/near-center for a while (1s approx)
        if stability_counter > 20: 
            # Slowly shift center towards current raw
            # This is an Exponential Moving Average (EMA)
            center = (center * (1.0 - ADAPT_SPEED)) + (raw * ADAPT_SPEED)
            
    print(f"Raw: {raw} | Center: {int(center)} | Dev: {int(deviation)} | {status}")
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Aggressive Adaptation**: If `ADAPT_SPEED` is too high, holding the stick slightly forward (to move slowly) will cause the system to "learn" that forward is the new center, causing the robot to stop moving! Adaptation must be very slow and only apply when deviation is minimal.
*   **Save on Shutdown**: This logic resets on boot. For permanent calibration, save to file.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Flash Save**: Write the calibrated values to `config.json` so they persist after reboot.

---

## 1. Project 1494: Advanced Joystick Control Status Monitor

### 2. Project Description
**"The Digital Dashboard" (Analog Gauge)**
Numerical displays (e.g., "Speed: 50%") are precise but hard to read at a glance. Analog gauges (needles) allow operators to instantly gauge rate and acceleration. This project uses the OLED display to draw a retro-style speedometer. The user inputs speed via the Joystick, and the code calculates the `(x,y)` endpoint of the needle using the vector angle.

### 3. Learning Objectives
*   **Trigonometry**: Practical use of `Cos()` and `Sin()` to translate an angle into pixel coordinates.
*   **Graphics Primitives**: Drawing lines and circles to create a UI.
*   **Data Visualization**: Converting raw sensor inputs into meaningful human-readable graphics.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**.
*   **Joystick**.
*   **OLED Display**.

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
*   **I2C** (GP16/17): OLED.
*   **ADC** (GP26/27): Joystick.

### 6. Blocks Used
| Block Category | Block Name | Action | Description |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Math** | `math_trig` | **Cos/Sin** | Calculate needle tip position. |
| **Smart Display** | `oled_line` | **Draw** | Render the needle. |
| **Smart Display** | `oled_circle` | **Draw** | Render the gauge pivot/boundary. |

### 7. Variables
*   **`angle_rads`**: The angle of the needle in radians.
*   **`needle_len`**: Length of the needle in pixels (e.g., 30).
*   **`center_x`, `center_y`**: The pivot point (e.g., 64, 60).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Read Speed**: Get Joystick Magnitude (0-100%).
2.  **Map to Angle**:
    *   0% Speed = 180 degrees (Left, 9 o'clock).
    *   100% Speed = 0 degrees (Right, 3 o'clock).
    *   Formula: `Angle = 180 - (Speed * 1.8)`.
3.  **Calculate Tip**:
    *   `Tip_X = Center_X + Length * cos(Angle)`.
    *   `Tip_Y = Center_Y - Length * sin(Angle)` (Subtract Y because screen Y grows downwards).
4.  **Render**:
    *   Clear Screen.
    *   Draw Frame (Top half of circle).
    *   Draw Line (Center -> Tip).
    *   Update Display.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Idle**: Stick Center. Needle points Left (0 MPH).
2.  **Accelerate**: Push stick. Needle sweeps smoothly clockwise.
3.  **Max**: Stick Max. Needle points Right (100 MPH).

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import math
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(16), scl=machine.Pin(17))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
adc = machine.ADC(26)

CENTER_X = 64
CENTER_Y = 60
LENGTH = 40

while True:
    # 1. Read Speed (0.0 to 1.0)
    raw = adc.read_u16()
    speed = raw / 65535 # Normalize
    
    # 2. Calculate Angle (180 deg to 0 deg)
    # Math.pi radians = 180 degrees
    angle_rad = math.pi - (speed * math.pi)
    
    # 3. Calculate Endpoint
    # X = r * cos(theta), Y = r * sin(theta)
    tip_x = CENTER_X + int(LENGTH * math.cos(angle_rad))
    tip_y = CENTER_Y - int(LENGTH * math.sin(angle_rad))
    
    # 4. Draw
    oled.fill(0)
    # Draw pivot
    oled.fill_rect(CENTER_X-2, CENTER_Y-2, 5, 5, 1) 
    # Draw arc (simplified as just the needle here)
    oled.line(CENTER_X, CENTER_Y, tip_x, tip_y, 1)
    
    oled.text(f"{int(speed*100)} MPH", 40, 0)
    oled.show()
    
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Radians vs Degrees**: Python's `math.cos()` takes Radians. If you pass degrees (0-360), the needle will spin wildly. Always convert: `Rad = Deg * (PI / 180)`.
*   **Screen Coordinates**: Remember that Y=0 is the TOP of the screen. To move "Up", you subtract from Y.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Damping**: Make the needle move slowly (simulate a heavy physical needle) by averaging the input speed over time.

---

## 1. Project 1495: Automated Advanced Joystick Control Controller

### 2. Project Description
**"The Crane Operator" (Multi-Actuator Control)**
Industrial machines often use joysticks to control multiple mechanisms simultaneously. In this project, you simulate a Crane Controller:
*   **Joystick X**: Rotates the Cab (Servo/Motor A).
*   **Joystick Y**: Extends/Retracts the Boom or Winch (Motor B).
*   **Button**: Activates the Electromagnet to pick up scrap.
This introduces the complexity of managing distinct physical sub-systems from a unified input stream.

### 3. Learning Objectives
*   **Systems Integration**: Coordinating Servos (Position control) and DC Motors (Speed control).
*   **State Management**: Toggling the magnet on/off with a momentary button press.
*   **Safety Limits**: Preventing the boom from extending too far.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Pico**.
*   **Joystick**.
*   **Servo Motor** (The "Cab").
*   **DC Motor + Driver** (The "Winch").
*   **LED** (Simulating the Electromagnet).

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
*   **ADC**: Joystick X/Y.
*   **GPIO (PWM)**: Servo control.
*   **GPIO (Out)**: Winch Motor Driver logic.
*   **GPIO (In)**: Joystick Switch (SW pin).

### 6. Blocks Used
| Block Category | Block Name | Action | Description |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Motion** | `servo_write` | **Move** | Set Cab angle 0-180. |
| **Motion** | `motor_speed` | **Run** | Set Winch speed. |
| **Smart IO** | `pin_toggle` | **Switch** | Toggle Magnet state. |

### 7. Variables
*   **`cab_angle`**: 0-180 degrees.
*   **`winch_speed`**: -100 to +100.
*   **`magnet_active`**: Boolean (True/False).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Cab Control**: Map X (0-65535) to Angle (0-180). Update Servo.
2.  **Winch Control**: Map Y (0-65535) to Speed (-100 to 100). Update DC Motor driver.
3.  **Magnet Logic**:
    *   Read Button (Pull-Up).
    *   If LOW (Pressed) AND was HIGH (Released): Toggle `magnet_active`.
    *   Write `magnet_active` to LED.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Setup**: Rotates to position, drops hook.
2.  **Action**: User swings Left (X axis). Cab rotates.
3.  **Action**: User pushes Button. LED turns ON (Magnet energized).
4.  **Action**: User pulls Back (Y axis). Winch raises the load.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware
cab_servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(0)) 
cab_servo.freq(50)
winch_pwm = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(2))
magnet_led = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)
joy_btn = machine.Pin(22, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
adc_x = machine.ADC(26)
adc_y = machine.ADC(27)

magnet_state = False
last_btn = 1

def set_servo(angle):
    # Map 0-180 to duty cycle (approx)
    duty = int((angle / 180 * 2000) + 500) * 50 # Simplified
    cab_servo.duty_u16(duty)

while True:
    # 1. Cab (Position Control)
    x = adc_x.read_u16()
    angle = int(x / 65535 * 180)
    set_servo(angle)
    
    # 2. Winch (Speed Control)
    y = adc_y.read_u16()
    # Map to PWM duty? Or H-Bridge direction.
    # Simulating simple speed for now
    speed = int((y / 65535) * 65535)
    winch_pwm.duty_u16(speed)
    
    # 3. Magnet (Toggle)
    btn = joy_btn.value()
    if btn == 0 and last_btn == 1:
        magnet_state = not magnet_state
        magnet_led.value(magnet_state)
        print(f"Magnet: {magnet_state}")
    last_btn = btn
    
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Servo Power**: Servos draw high current. Don't power a large servo solely from the Pico's 3.3V pin. Use VBUS (5V) or external power.
*   **Deboucing**: Mechanical buttons bounce. Simple toggle logic is prone to "double toggling". A small `sleep` or debounce check is needed.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Ease-In**: Make the Cab servo move smoothly (interpolate) even if the user jerks the joystick, simulating the inertia of a heavy crane.

---

## 1. Project 1496: Advanced Joystick Control Safety Trigger

### 2. Project Description
**"Reverse Safety Beep" (Audible Status)**
In heavy machinery and trucks, a loud "Beep... Beep..." sounds whenever the vehicle is in reverse. This increases safety for bystanders. This project adds a background audio task: monitoring the Joystick Y-axis, and if it exceeds a "Reverse Threshold" (e.g., < -20%), automatically pulsing a buzzer without blocking the main motor control loop.

### 3. Learning Objectives
*   **Non-Blocking Audio**: Creating a pulsing sound pattern using system timestamps (`ticks_ms`) instead of `sleep()`.
*   **Threshold Monitoring**: Triggering events based on analog ranges.
*   **Safety Interlocks**: Automatic activation of safety features.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Pico**.
*   **Joystick**.
*   **Active Buzzer** (Generates sound when powered).

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
*   **PWM/GPIO**: Buzzer logic.
*   **ADC**: Stick.

### 6. Blocks Used
| Block Category | Block Name | Action | Description |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Logic** | `if_logic` | **Check** | Is Stick < Reverse_Threshold? |
| **Smart IO** | `ticks_ms` | **Time** | Get current time for pulse logic. |
| **Math** | `math_modulo` | **Cycle** | Create a repeating beat (e.g., every 1000ms). |

### 7. Variables
*   **`reverse_threshold`**: The point where reverse engages (e.g., 20000).
*   **`is_reversing`**: Boolean status.
*   **`now`**: Current time.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Read Input**: Get Y-Axis.
2.  **Determine State**:
    *   If `Y < Threshold`: `Reversing = True`.
    *   Else: `Reversing = False`, Turn Buzzer OFF using `pwm.off()`.
3.  **Pulse Logic**:
    *   If `Reversing` is True:
    *   Read `Time`.
    *   If `(Time % 1000) < 500`: Turn Buzzer ON.
    *   Else: Turn Buzzer OFF.
    *   This creates a 0.5s ON, 0.5s OFF pattern.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Forward**: Stick Up. Silence.
2.  **Coast**: Stick Center. Silence.
3.  **Reverse**: Stick Pulled Back.
    *   T=0ms: Beep ON.
    *   T=500ms: Beep OFF.
    *   T=1000ms: Beep ON.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

adc = machine.ADC(27)
buzzer = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)

REVERSE_THRESHOLD = 20000 # Below this is reverse
now = 0

while True:
    val = adc.read_u16()
    
    if val < REVERSE_THRESHOLD:
        # We are reversing!
        # Non-blocking pulse logic
        now = utime.ticks_ms()
        cycle = now % 1000 # 1 second cycle
        
        if cycle < 500:
            buzzer.value(1) # Beep
        else:
            buzzer.value(0) # Silence
            
    else:
        # Not reversing
        buzzer.value(0)
        
    utime.sleep(0.02)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Using Sleep**: Writing `buzzer.on(); sleep(0.5); buzzer.off()` would freeze the robot for 0.5 seconds! The driver would lose control. Always use the modulo-time trick for background tasks.
*   **Passive vs Active Buzzer**: Passive buzzers need PWM to make sound. Active buzzers just need 3.3V. This code assumes an Active Buzzer (or uses basic PWM if adapted).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Variable Pitch**: Make the beep pitch (frequency) increase as the vehicle reverses faster! `freq = map(speed)`.

---

## 1. Project 1497: Advanced Joystick Control Data Logger

### 2. Project Description
**"The Motion Recorder" (Input Replay System)**
Automation often starts with "Teaching" a machine by moving it manually, recording the path, and then playing it back. This project implements a **Macro Recorder** for your joystick. You will record a sequence of `(X, Y)` coordinates into the Pico's RAM and then "Play" them back to drive motors (or move servo arms) autonomously. This is the foundation of "Teach Mode" in industrial robots.

### 3. Learning Objectives
*   **Data Structures**: Using Lists/Arrays to store time-series data.
*   **Memory Management**: Understanding the limits of RAM (how many seconds can we record?).
*   **Sampling Rate**: The relationship between recording speed (frame rate) and playback smoothness.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Pico**.
*   **Joystick**.
*   **2 Servos or Motors** (To visualize the playback).
*   **Push Button** (Record/Play Toggle).

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
*   **ADC**: Joystick.
*   **GPIO**: Servos/Motors.
*   **GPIO**: Button.

### 6. Blocks Used
| Block Category | Block Name | Action | Description |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Lists** | `list_create_with` | **Init** | Create empty list `tape = []`. |
| **Lists** | `list_append` | **Save** | Add current `(x,y)` tuple to list. |
| **Loops** | `for_item` | **Replay** | Iterate through list to reproduce moves. |

### 7. Variables
*   **`recording_tape`**: A list of data tuples `[(x1,y1), (x2,y2)...]`.
*   **`mode`**: "Idle", "Recording", or "Playing".
*   **`frame_delay`**: Time between samples (e.g., 0.05s = 20Hz).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Setup**: Initialize Servo and Joystick. Create empty list.
2.  **Record Mode**:
    *   If Button Clicked -> Start Recording. Clear List.
    *   Loop: Read X, Y. Append to List. Move Servos (Live Monitor). Wait 50ms.
    *   Stop on Click.
3.  **Playback Mode**:
    *   If Button Double-Clicked -> Start Playback.
    *   Loop: For each Item in List: Extract X, Y. Move Servos. Wait 50ms.
    *   Stop at end of list.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Teach**: User presses Button. "Recording" LED On. User waives joystick in a fast circle. LED Off.
2.  **Verify**: User Double-clicks. Robot Arm waives in the exact same circle automatically.
3.  **Observation**: The playback might be slightly "steppy" if the sampling rate is too low.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware
adc_x = machine.ADC(26)
adc_y = machine.ADC(27)
servo_x = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(0))
servo_x.freq(50)
btn = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

tape = []
recording = False

def set_angle(angle):
    duty = int((angle / 180 * 2000) + 500) * 50
    servo_x.duty_u16(duty)

print("Ready. Press (record) or Hold (play).")

while True:
    if btn.value() == 0:
        # Simple Logic: Press = Record, Hold long = Play
        start_press = utime.ticks_ms()
        while btn.value() == 0: pass # Wait release
        duration = utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), start_press)
        
        if duration < 500:
            print("Recording... (Press btn to stop)")
            tape = []
            while btn.value() == 1: # Record loop
                x = adc_x.read_u16()
                angle = int(x / 65535 * 180)
                set_angle(angle)
                tape.append(angle) # Save just X for simplicity
                utime.sleep(0.05)
            print(f"Stored {len(tape)} frames.")
            
        else:
            print("Playing...")
            for frame in tape:
                set_angle(frame)
                utime.sleep(0.05)
            print("Done.")

    # Live Feed
    if not recording:
         x = adc_x.read_u16()
         set_angle(int(x / 65535 * 180))
         utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Memory Overflow**: Python Lists consume RAM. Pico has ~200KB free. Saving 2 integers (8 bytes) at 50Hz = 400 bytes/sec. You can record for minutes, but not hours.
*   **Timing Mismatch**: If you record with `sleep(0.1)` but play back with `sleep(0.01)`, the robot moves 10x faster (Speed ramp!).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Save to File**: Write the list to `motion.txt` so the robot remembers the dance move after power loss.

---

## 1. Project 1498: Smart Advanced Joystick Control System

### 2. Project Description
**"PC Flight Simulator Controller" (Serial Bridge)**
Most Pico projects control local hardware. This project controls the **PC**. By sending Joystick data over the USB Serial (UART) connection, a Python script running on your computer can intercept the data and control the Mouse, Keyboard, or vJoy (Virtual Joystick) driver. You will build a physical Flight Stick that flies a plane in a PC game.

### 3. Learning Objectives
*   **Serial Communication**: Sending formatted string data (`"X,Y,Btn"`) over USB.
*   **Host-Device Interface**: Writing a counterpart script (Host) to parse Device data.
*   **Latency**: Optimizing communication to minimize input lag.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Pico**.
*   **Joystick**.
*   **PC** (Running Python).
*   **Micro-USB Cable** (Data capable).

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
*   **USB**: Data flows through the USB cable (no extra wires needed).

### 6. Blocks Used
| Block Category | Block Name | Action | Description |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Text** | `print` | **Send** | Send text to PC via Serial. |
| **Text** | `text_join` | **Format** | Combine numbers into CSV format. |

### 7. Variables
*   **`data_packet`**: String formatted as `"12345,54321,0"`.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Pico Side**:
    *   Read X, Y, Btn.
    *   Format as CSV: `print(f"{x},{y},{btn}")`.
    *   Delay short (e.g., 20ms).
2.  **PC Side (Host)**:
    *   Install `pyserial` and `pyautogui`.
    *   Open COM port.
    *   Loop: `line = serial.readline()`.
    *   Parse X, Y.
    *   `pyautogui.moveRel(x_delta, y_delta)`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Connect**: Plug Pico into PC.
2.  **Run Host**: Start PC script.
3.  **Fly**: Pushing Joystick Left moves the PC Mouse Left.

### 10. Generated Code
**Pico Code (Sender):**
```python
import machine
import utime

adc_x = machine.ADC(26)
adc_y = machine.ADC(27)
btn = machine.Pin(22, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

while True:
    x = adc_x.read_u16()
    y = adc_y.read_u16()
    b = btn.value()
    # CSV Format
    print(f"{x},{y},{b}") 
    utime.sleep(0.02) # 50Hz
```

**PC Code (Receiver - Conceptual):**
```python
# Run this on computer!
import serial
import pyautogui

ser = serial.Serial('COM5', 9600)

while True:
    if ser.in_waiting:
        line = ser.readline().decode().strip()
        parts = line.split(',')
        if len(parts) == 3:
            x_raw = int(parts[0])
            # Map 0-65535 to Mouse Delta -10 to +10
            dx = int((x_raw - 32768) / 3000)
            pyautogui.moveRel(dx, 0)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Print Speed**: Printing too fast can saturate the buffer if the PC doesn't read fast enough.
*   **Throttling**: If you don't map coordinates correctly, the mouse might jump instantly to the edge of the screen. Relative movement (`moveRel`) is safer than Absolute (`moveTo`).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **HID Mode**: The "Correct" way is to use HID (Human Interface Device) libraries (e.g., CircuitPython or C SDK) to appear as a native Gamepad, requiring no PC script. Try converting this project to CircuitPython HID!

## 1. Project 1499: Advanced Joystick Control Calibration Tool

### 2. Project Description
**"Deadzone Visualizer" (Debugging Tool)**
Deadzones are essential but felt-unseen. This project visualizes them. You will draw a "Crosshair" on the OLED. One pixel (dim) represents the raw physical stick position. A second pixel (bright) represents the *Processed* position (after Deadzone, Clamping, and Curve mapping). This lets you "See" exactly how much stick travel is ignored before movement starts.

### 3. Learning Objectives
*   **Visual Debugging**: Using graphics to examine internal algorithm states.
*   **Signal Processing Chains**: Seeing the difference between Raw -> Deadzone -> Output.
*   **Coordinate mapping**: Mapping 16-bit ADC values to 128x64 px screen space.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Pico**.
*   **Joystick**.
*   **OLED Display**.

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
*   **I2C**: OLED.
*   **ADC**: Joystick.

### 6. Blocks Used
| Block Category | Block Name | Action | Description |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Smart Display** | `oled_pixel` | **Plot** | Draw points. |
| **Smart Display** | `oled_line` | **Cross** | Draw center axes. |
| **Logic** | `deadzone_check` | **Filter** | Apply deadzone logic. |

### 7. Variables
*   **`raw_x`, `raw_y`**: ADC inputs.
*   **`proc_x`, `proc_y`**: Outputs after deadzone logic.
*   **`screen_x`, `screen_y`**: Coordinates on OLED.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Draw UI**: Draw logic crosshair at (64, 32).
2.  **Read Raw**: Read ADC. Map to screen XY. Draw 'Dim' pixel (blink or dither).
3.  **Process**:
    *   Center Raw (-32k to +32k).
    *   If `abs(val) < 5000`: `val = 0`.
    *   Else: `val = val`.
4.  **Draw Processed**: Map result to screen XY. Draw 'Solid' pixel.
5.  **Compare**: Move stick slightly. Raw pixel moves. Solid pixel stays center until the threshold is crossed.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Center**: Both pixels at (64,32).
2.  **Nudge**: Move stick 5%. Raw pixel shifts. Solid pixel "Sticks" to center.
3.  **Push**: Move stick 50%. Both pixels trace arc.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(16), scl=machine.Pin(17))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
adc = machine.ADC(26)

DEADZONE = 5000

while True:
    raw = adc.read_u16()
    
    # Map to screen (0-128)
    scr_raw = int(raw / 65535 * 128)
    
    # Apply Deadzone
    centered = raw - 32768
    if abs(centered) < DEADZONE:
        centered = 0
    
    scr_proc = int((centered + 32768) / 65535 * 128)
    
    oled.fill(0)
    # Crosshair
    oled.line(64, 0, 64, 64, 1)
    oled.line(0, 32, 128, 32, 1)
    
    # Raw Dot (Hollow rect for visibility)
    oled.rect(scr_raw-2, 30, 4, 4, 1) # Y is fixed for demo
    
    # Processed Dot (Filled)
    oled.fill_rect(scr_proc-2, 30, 4, 4, 1)
    
    oled.show()
    utime.sleep(0.02)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Scaling Errors**: 65535 doesn't divide evenly into 128. Use float multiplication or careful shift operations.
*   **Ghosting**: Clear screen (`fill(0)`) every frame, or the dots will leave trails.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Sensitivity Curve**: Visualize a Quadratic Curve (`Output = Input^2`). You will see the Raw dot move linearly, while the Processed dot accelerates faster at the edges.

## 1. Project 1500: Advanced Advanced Joystick Control Prototype

### 2. Project Description
**"Sip-and-Puff Interface" (Assistive Technology)**
For individuals with limited hand mobility (e.g., Quadriplegia), a "Sip-and-Puff" interface allows control using breath pressure. This prototype simulates such a device. Using the Joystick Y-axis to simulate Air Pressure, we interpret "Up" as blowing (Puff) and "Down" as sucking (Sip). We map these to different actions (e.g., Click vs Right Click, or Scroll Up vs Down).

### 3. Learning Objectives
*   **Assistive Tech Principles**: Designing interfaces for widely varying physical abilities.
*   **Threshold Triggering**: Converting continuous analog data into discrete digital events (Binary Classification).
*   **Debouncing Analog**: Avoiding "false clicks" when pressure fluctuates near the threshold.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Pico**.
*   **Joystick** (Simulator for Pressure Sensor).
*   **OLED** (To show "SiP" or "PUFF" status).
*   **3 LEDs** (Action indicators).

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
*   **ADC**: Joystick Y-axis.
*   **I2C**: OLED.
*   **GPIO**: LEDs.

### 6. Blocks Used
| Block Category | Block Name | Action | Description |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Logic** | `if_elseif_else` | **Classify** | Sort input into Sip/Puff/Null. |
| **Smart Display** | `oled_text` | **Feedback** | Show status. |
| **Variables** | `pressure_level` | **Read** | Simulated pressure value. |

### 7. Variables
*   **`pressure`**: -100 (Hard Sip) to +100 (Hard Puff).
*   **`action`**: "NONE", "CLICK", "MENU".

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Read**: Get Y input. Center it.
2.  **Classify**:
    *   Use Hysteresis thresholds.
    *   `if Y > 20000`: Action = PUFF.
    *   `if Y < -20000`: Action = SIP.
    *   `else`: Action = IDLE.
3.  **Debounce**: Require the signal to be sustained for 100ms (filtering accidental breath).
4.  **Execute**:
    *   Puff -> Toggle LED 1 (Select).
    *   Sip -> Toggle LED 2 (Back).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Idle**: Sensor neutral. Screen: "---".
2.  **Blow**: User pushes stick Up. Screen: "PUFF". LED Green ON.
3.  **Suck**: User pulls stick Down. Screen: "SIP". LED Red ON.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

adc = machine.ADC(27)
led_puff = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
led_sip = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)

THRESHOLD = 25000 # High threshold implies "Hard Puff"
CENTER = 32768

state = "IDLE"

while True:
    raw = adc.read_u16()
    pressure = raw - CENTER
    
    if pressure > THRESHOLD:
        if state != "PUFF":
            # Rising Edge
            print("PUFF DETECTED - CLICK")
            led_puff.on()
            state = "PUFF"
    elif pressure < -THRESHOLD:
        if state != "SIP":
            print("SIP DETECTED - BACK")
            led_sip.on()
            state = "SIP"
    else:
        # Hysteresis return to zero
        if abs(pressure) < (THRESHOLD - 5000):
            if state != "IDLE":
                state = "IDLE"
                led_puff.off()
                led_sip.off()

    utime.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **No Hysteresis**: If the threshold is hard (e.g., 20000), a user blowing lightly at exactly 20000 will cause the system to "Click" thousands of times a second. Hysteresis (Trigger at 20k, Reset at 15k) prevents this.
*   **Fatigue**: Setting thresholds too high makes the user pass out from hyperventilation. Sensitivity must be adjustable.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Morse Code**: Implement a parser that interprets sequences. "Puff, Puff, Sip" -> Type letter "A". This is how high-speed assistive typing works.
